# MDCAT Biology MCQs — Source: MCQUniverse.pk

## Enzymes

### Q1. Which of the following is a primary distinguishing characteristic of enzymes?
A.) They are consumed during the reaction.
B.) They increase the activation energy of a reaction.
C.) They are always simple proteins.
D.) They are highly specific in their action. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes are biological catalysts known for their high specificity, meaning each enzyme typically acts on a particular substrate or a group of structurally similar substrates. They are not consumed, they lower activation energy, and while most are proteins, some are RNA molecules (ribozymes).

### Q2. Ribozymes are a unique class of enzymes primarily composed of:
A.) Lipids
B.) Carbohydrates
C.) DNA
D.) RNA **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — While most enzymes are proteins, ribozymes are catalytic RNA molecules. A well-known example is the peptidyl transferase activity of ribosomal RNA, which catalyzes peptide bond formation during protein synthesis.

### Q3. Enzymes accelerate biochemical reactions by:
A.) Increasing the kinetic energy of reactants.
B.) Shifting the equilibrium of the reaction.
C.) Increasing the concentration of products.
D.) Lowering the activation energy. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes function by providing an alternative reaction pathway with a lower activation energy. This allows a greater proportion of reactant molecules to possess sufficient energy to undergo the reaction, thereby increasing the reaction rate without affecting the overall equilibrium or the final product concentration.

### Q4. Which statement best describes the quantity of enzymes required for a biochemical reaction?
A.) Large quantities are needed as they are consumed in the reaction.
B.) The quantity needed is equal to the quantity of substrate.
C.) They are only needed in vitro, not in vivo.
D.) They are required in very small quantities and are reusable. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes are biological catalysts, and like all catalysts, they are not consumed during the reaction. They are regenerated after each catalytic cycle, meaning only very small quantities are sufficient to convert large amounts of substrate into product.

### Q5. Enzymes primarily function by:
A.) Increasing the activation energy of the reaction.
B.) Decreasing the stability of the products.
C.) Increasing the temperature of the reaction system.
D.) Providing an alternative reaction pathway with lower activation energy. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The fundamental mechanism of enzyme action involves lowering the activation energy, which is the minimum energy required for a chemical reaction to occur. By doing so, enzymes increase the rate of reaction without being used up in the process.

### Q6. Most enzymes are categorized as which type of macromolecule?
A.) Carbohydrates
B.) Lipids
C.) Nucleic acids
D.) Proteins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vast majority of enzymes are globular proteins. Their specific three-dimensional structures are crucial for their catalytic activity. Ribozymes (RNA enzymes) are a notable exception.

### Q7. The specific region on an enzyme where the substrate binds and catalysis occurs is known as the:
A.) Allosteric site
B.) Regulatory site
C.) Inhibitor site
D.) Active site **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The active site is a unique three-dimensional cleft or pocket on the enzyme molecule where the substrate specifically binds. This binding facilitates the chemical transformation of the substrate into product.

### Q8. Enzymes are highly sensitive to changes in environmental conditions, particularly:
A.) Light intensity and atmospheric pressure.
B.) Humidity and gravitational force.
C.) Substrate concentration and product concentration.
D.) Temperature and pH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes are proteins, and their intricate three-dimensional structures, including the active site, are highly sensitive to changes in temperature and pH. Deviations from optimal conditions can lead to denaturation and loss of activity.

### Q9. Which of the following statements about enzyme activity is INCORRECT?
A.) Enzymes can catalyze reactions both inside and outside living cells.
B.) Enzymes are highly specific for their substrates.
C.) Some enzymes require non-protein components for their activity.
D.) Enzymes change the equilibrium constant of a reaction. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes accelerate the rate at which equilibrium is reached, but they do not change the position of the equilibrium itself. The equilibrium constant remains unchanged whether an enzyme is present or not. Enzymes can work in vivo and in vitro, are highly specific, and often require cofactors.

### Q10. Which of the following is true regarding enzyme action and activation energy?
A.) Enzymes increase the energy required for a reaction to start.
B.) Enzymes do not affect the activation energy.
C.) Enzymes convert all the activation energy into heat.
D.) Enzymes provide an alternative reaction pathway with a lower activation energy. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes catalyze reactions by lowering the activation energy, which is the energy barrier that must be overcome for reactants to be converted into products. They achieve this by stabilizing the transition state, making the reaction more favorable.

### Q11. The tertiary or quaternary structures of enzymes are crucial because they:
A.) Allow for their easy transportation across membranes.
B.) Determine their solubility in water.
C.) Are responsible for their color.
D.) Form the specific active site necessary for catalysis. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The specific three-dimensional folding of the polypeptide chain(s) (tertiary and quaternary structures) creates the unique shape of the active site. This precise geometry is essential for the specific binding of the substrate and the subsequent catalytic event.

### Q12. An enzyme's ability to catalyze a specific reaction efficiently is largely due to its:
A.) Large molecular weight.
B.) Globular shape.
C.) Presence of disulfide bonds only.
D.) Unique active site structure. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The high specificity and catalytic efficiency of an enzyme are primarily attributed to the unique three-dimensional conformation of its active site. This site is precisely shaped to bind only specific substrate molecules, enabling specific chemical transformations.

### Q13. The active site of an enzyme is best described as a:
A.) Linear chain of amino acids.
B.) Flat surface where any molecule can bind.
C.) Storage region for excess substrate molecules.
D.) Three-dimensional, charge-bearing cavity. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The active site is a precisely shaped, three-dimensional region on the enzyme molecule. Its specific spatial arrangement and charge distribution allow it to bind selectively with its complementary substrate, forming the enzyme-substrate complex.

### Q14. According to the Lock and Key Model of enzyme action:
A.) The active site is flexible and changes shape upon substrate binding.
B.) Multiple enzymes can bind to a single substrate.
C.) Substrates induce a conformational change in the enzyme's active site.
D.) The enzyme's active site has a rigid, pre-formed shape complementary to the substrate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Proposed by Emil Fischer, the Lock and Key Model suggests that the active site of an enzyme is rigid and has a fixed shape that perfectly matches the shape of its specific substrate, much like a key fits into a lock.

### Q15. The Induced Fit Model suggests that:
A.) The enzyme's active site is permanently rigid.
B.) The substrate's shape changes to fit the enzyme.
C.) Enzymes are non-regulatory and have absolute specificity.
D.) The active site undergoes a conformational change upon substrate binding. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Developed by Koshland, the Induced Fit Model proposes that the active site is not rigid but flexible. When the substrate binds, it induces a slight conformational change in the enzyme, molding the active site into a more precise fit for catalysis.

### Q16. Cofactors are often required for enzyme activity. They are typically:
A.) Large protein molecules that assist in denaturation.
B.) Products of the enzymatic reaction.
C.) Inhibitors that block the active site.
D.) Non-protein components that aid in catalysis. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cofactors are non-protein chemical components (like inorganic ions or coenzymes) that are essential for the activity of many enzymes. They may participate directly in the catalytic mechanism or help in substrate binding.

### Q17. What is formed immediately after the substrate binds to the active site of an enzyme?
A.) Enzyme-product complex
B.) Free enzyme and product
C.) Denatured enzyme
D.) Enzyme-substrate complex **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The first step in an enzyme-catalyzed reaction is the reversible binding of the substrate(s) to the active site, leading to the formation of the enzyme-substrate (ES) complex.

### Q18. The energy required to initiate a biochemical reaction, even with the presence of an enzyme, is known as:
A.) Potential energy
B.) Kinetic energy
C.) Free energy
D.) Activation energy **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Activation energy is the minimum energy that reactant molecules must possess to undergo a chemical reaction. Enzymes lower this energy barrier, thus accelerating the reaction rate without changing the overall energy difference between reactants and products.

### Q19. Within the active site, the amino acids responsible for chemically transforming the substrate are part of the:
A.) Binding site
B.) Allosteric site
C.) Regulatory site
D.) Catalytic site **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The active site can often be divided into two main parts: the binding site, which holds the substrate in place, and the catalytic site, which contains the amino acid residues directly involved in the chemical conversion of the substrate into product.

### Q20. Which statement correctly describes the effect of an enzyme on a biochemical reaction?
A.) It increases the equilibrium constant.
B.) It makes an unfavorable reaction favorable.
C.) It changes the overall Gibb's free energy (ΔG) of the reaction.
D.) It increases the rate at which the reaction reaches equilibrium. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes accelerate the rate of reaction by lowering the activation energy, thereby allowing the system to reach chemical equilibrium more quickly. However, they do not alter the position of the equilibrium or the overall free energy change of the reaction (ΔG).

### Q21. A holoenzyme is formed when an apoenzyme combines with a:
A.) Substrate
B.) Product
C.) Inhibitor
D.) Cofactor **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An apoenzyme is the protein component of an enzyme that is inactive on its own. It becomes active, forming a holoenzyme, when it binds to its specific non-protein cofactor (which can be an inorganic ion or a coenzyme).

### Q22. Which model of enzyme action is better suited for explaining the action of regulatory enzymes that can bind to more than one related substrate?
A.) Lock and Key Model
B.) Allosteric Model
C.) Competitive Inhibition Model
D.) Induced Fit Model **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Induced Fit Model, with its flexible active site, can better explain the broader specificity of some enzymes that can act on a range of structurally similar substrates, as well as the regulatory mechanisms where substrate binding induces conformational changes.

### Q23. When an enzyme catalyzes a reaction, it achieves this by:
A.) Directly increasing the temperature of the reactants.
B.) Forcing reactants to collide more frequently.
C.) Consuming the reactants to form an intermediate.
D.) Providing a microenvironment that facilitates bond breaking and formation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The active site of an enzyme creates a unique microenvironment (e.g., localized pH, charge distribution, desolvation) that precisely positions substrates and stresses specific bonds, thereby facilitating their conversion to products.

### Q24. Which of the following is NOT a step in the general mechanism of enzyme action?
A.) Formation of an enzyme-substrate complex.
B.) Conversion of substrate to product.
C.) Release of enzyme from the product.
D.) Permanent alteration of the enzyme's active site. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes are biological catalysts that are reused repeatedly. Their active site regains its original conformation after the reaction, ready to bind to another substrate molecule. A permanent alteration would imply the enzyme is consumed or denatured.

### Q25. The term "transition state" in an enzyme-catalyzed reaction refers to:
A.) The initial state of the reactants.
B.) The final state of the products.
C.) The state where the enzyme is denatured.
D.) A high-energy, unstable intermediate formed during the reaction. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The transition state is a transient, high-energy, unstable intermediate structure formed during the conversion of reactants to products. Enzymes stabilize this transition state, thereby lowering the activation energy required to reach it.

### Q26. If an enzyme has an optimum pH of 2.0, it is likely to be found in which part of the human body?
A.) Small intestine
B.) Blood
C.) Muscle cells
D.) Stomach **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An enzyme with an optimum pH of 2.0, such as pepsin, is adapted to function in highly acidic environments. The human stomach contains strong hydrochloric acid, providing the necessary acidic conditions for such enzymes.

### Q27. Which of the following best describes the role of enzymes in metabolic pathways?
A.) They provide the energy needed for the reactions.
B.) They act as structural components of the pathway.
C.) They primarily store genetic information for the pathway.
D.) They direct the pathway towards the formation of specific products. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes are highly specific and catalyze particular reactions in a sequential manner within metabolic pathways. This specificity ensures that the correct products are formed efficiently, directing the flow of metabolites through the pathway.

### Q28. A characteristic common to both the Lock and Key and Induced Fit models of enzyme action is:
A.) The active site remains rigid throughout the reaction.
B.) The enzyme undergoes denaturation during catalysis.
C.) The enzyme is consumed in the reaction.
D.) Substrate binding occurs at a specific active site. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Both models agree that the substrate binds to a specific region on the enzyme called the active site. The main difference lies in whether this active site is rigid (Lock and Key) or undergoes conformational changes (Induced Fit) upon substrate binding.

### Q29. At what approximate temperature do most human enzymes exhibit their maximum activity?
A.) 0°C
B.) 25°C
C.) 88.6°C
D.) 37°C **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Most human enzymes are optimally active at temperatures close to normal human body temperature, which is approximately 37-38°C. Temperatures significantly above or below this range can reduce or abolish their activity.

### Q30. Increasing the temperature of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction typically doubles the reaction rate for every 10°C rise, up to a certain point. This is due to:
A.) Enzyme denaturation.
B.) Decrease in activation energy.
C.) Formation of stronger enzyme-substrate complexes.
D.) Increased kinetic energy and collision frequency between enzyme and substrate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Within the physiological range, an increase in temperature boosts the kinetic energy of both enzyme and substrate molecules. This leads to more frequent and energetic collisions, thus increasing the probability of enzyme-substrate complex formation and subsequent product formation.

### Q31. What happens to an enzyme's activity when the temperature rises significantly above its optimum?
A.) The enzyme becomes more efficient.
B.) The enzyme's tertiary structure is maintained.
C.) The enzyme forms stronger bonds with the substrate.
D.) Denaturation occurs, leading to a loss of catalytic activity. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — High temperatures disrupt the weak bonds that maintain an enzyme's precise three-dimensional structure. This irreversible change in shape, known as denaturation, destroys the active site and abolishes catalytic activity.

### Q32. When an enzyme is exposed to temperatures significantly below its optimum (e.g., near freezing), its activity usually:
A.) Increases rapidly.
B.) Becomes permanently denatured.
C.) Shifts its optimum pH.
D.) Is temporarily inactivated but can regain activity upon warming. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Low temperatures reduce kinetic energy, slowing molecular motion and enzyme activity. However, enzymes generally retain their structure and regain activity when the temperature returns to optimal.

### Q33. The pH at which an enzyme exhibits its maximum activity is called its:
A.) Isoelectric point
B.) Neutral point
C.) Saturation pH
D.) Optimum pH **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Each enzyme has a specific pH range at which it functions most efficiently, and the point of peak activity is its optimum pH.

### Q34. Pepsin, an enzyme found in the stomach, has an optimum pH of approximately 2.0. This indicates that:
A.) It functions best in a neutral environment.
B.) It is inactivated in acidic conditions.
C.) It only functions in the presence of high salt concentrations.
D.) Its active site is adapted to highly acidic environments. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pepsin is adapted to the highly acidic conditions of the stomach, with optimal activity at pH ~2.

### Q35. Extreme changes in pH can lead to irreversible loss of enzyme activity primarily by:
A.) Increasing the enzyme's affinity for its substrate.
B.) Altering the enzyme's primary amino acid sequence.
C.) Reducing the kinetic energy of the enzyme molecules.
D.) Disrupting the ionic and hydrogen bonds that maintain the enzyme's tertiary structure. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Extreme pH values disrupt key bonds that maintain the enzyme's structure, causing denaturation.

### Q36. How does increasing enzyme concentration affect the rate of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction, assuming substrate is not limiting?
A.) It decreases the reaction rate.
B.) It has no effect on the reaction rate.
C.) It decreases the activation energy further.
D.) It directly increases the reaction rate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — With excess substrate, more enzymes mean more active sites and a directly proportional increase in reaction rate.

### Q37. In a reaction with a fixed amount of enzyme, as substrate concentration increases, the reaction rate will initially increase but eventually plateau. This plateau indicates:
A.) The enzyme is denatured.
B.) The reaction has reached its equilibrium.
C.) The temperature is too high.
D.) All active sites of the enzyme are saturated with substrate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — At high substrate concentrations, all active sites are occupied. The enzyme is working at its maximum rate (Vmax).

### Q38. The maximum velocity (Vmax) of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction occurs when:
A.) The enzyme concentration is very low.
B.) The substrate concentration is negligible.
C.) The pH is extremely acidic or basic.
D.) The enzyme is saturated with substrate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Vmax is reached when all enzyme molecules are bound to substrate and catalyzing at maximum efficiency.

### Q39. If the graph of reaction rate versus temperature for a human enzyme shows a sharp decrease after the optimum, this is primarily due to:
A.) Increased substrate inhibition.
B.) Reversible inactivation.
C.) Formation of precipitate.
D.) Permanent denaturation of the enzyme. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The sharp decrease is due to irreversible denaturation of the enzyme beyond its optimum temperature.

### Q40. Which factor's effect on enzyme activity is often depicted by a bell-shaped curve?
A.) Enzyme concentration
B.) Substrate concentration
C.) Product concentration
D.) pH **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzyme activity vs. pH often shows a bell-shaped curve, with peak activity at the optimum pH and reduced activity on either side.

### Q41. Consider an enzyme from thermophilic bacteria. Compared to a human enzyme, its optimum temperature would likely be:
A.) Significantly lower.
B.) The same.
C.) Dependent on the substrate.
D.) Significantly higher. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thermophilic enzymes are adapted to high temperatures, often 70°C or higher, unlike human enzymes (~37°C).

### Q42. If the rate of an enzymatic reaction is plotted against enzyme concentration, assuming unlimited substrate, the graph would show a:
A.) Bell-shaped curve.
B.) Hyperbolic curve.
C.) Decrease followed by a plateau.
D.) Linear increase. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — With unlimited substrate, more enzyme means more active sites and a linear increase in reaction rate.

### Q43. The effect of pH on enzyme activity is primarily related to its influence on the:
A.) Molecular weight of the enzyme.
B.) Covalent bonds within the enzyme.
C.) Overall concentration of water in the reaction.
D.) Ionization state of amino acid residues in the active site. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — pH affects the ionization of key amino acids, especially at the active site, altering the enzyme's structure and function.

### Q44. Why does the rate of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction decrease at very high substrate concentrations in some?
A.) The enzyme becomes denatured.
B.) The active sites are all saturated.
C.) The product starts inhibiting the enzyme.
D.) Excess substrate might bind to an allosteric site and inhibit the enzyme. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In substrate inhibition, excess substrate may bind to non-catalytic sites, distorting the enzyme and reducing activity.

### Q45. The term "Q10 value" in relation to enzyme activity refers to the factor by which the rate of reaction increases for every:
A.) 1°C increase in temperature.
B.) 5°C increase in temperature.
C.) 1 pH unit change.
D.) 10°C increase in temperature. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Q10 is the factor by which reaction rate increases with a 10°C rise in temperature, typically doubling within limits.

### Q46. If an enzyme functions optimally at a pH of 8.0, it is likely to be active in:
A.) The stomach
B.) The lysosome
C.) The vacuole
D.) The small intestine **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes like trypsin, with optimal activity around pH 8.0, function best in the alkaline environment of the small intestine.

### Q47. An enzyme's activity is significantly reduced at a non-optimal pH due to:
A.) A change in the enzyme's primary structure.
B.) Disruption of the enzyme's peptide bonds.
C.) Increased covalent bonding within the active site.
D.) Alterations in the ionization state of amino acid residues. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Non-optimal pH affects ionizable side chains in the enzyme, particularly in the active site, reducing substrate binding and catalysis.

### Q48. Which graph represents the effect of enzyme concentration on the rate of reaction when substrate is abundant?
A.) A bell-shaped curve.
B.) A curve that plateaus at Vmax.
C.) A curve showing initial increase then decrease.
D.) A straight line increasing proportionally. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — With abundant substrate, the rate of reaction increases linearly with enzyme concentration.

### Q49. What happens to enzyme activity when the pH is slightly altered from the optimum, but not to an extreme level?
A.) The enzyme irreversibly denatures.
B.) The enzyme's primary structure changes.
C.) The enzyme becomes more active.
D.) The enzyme's activity is temporarily reduced due to ionization changes. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Minor pH changes may affect ionization of active site residues, temporarily reducing activity, but it's usually reversible.

### Q50. An enzyme from a psychrophilic organism would likely have an optimum temperature:
A.) Much higher than human enzymes.
B.) Similar to human enzymes.
C.) That is highly variable.
D.) Close to 0°C. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Psychrophilic enzymes are adapted for cold environments and function optimally around 0°C.

### Q51. Which statement about the effect of substrate concentration on enzyme activity is correct?
A.) Increasing substrate concentration always increases the reaction rate linearly.
B.) At very high substrate concentrations, the enzyme activity decreases rapidly.
C.) Substrate concentration primarily affects the enzyme's optimal temperature.
D.) The reaction rate reaches Vmax when the enzyme becomes saturated with substrate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Reaction rate increases with substrate concentration until saturation, at which point Vmax is reached.

### Q52. A significant decrease in enzyme activity at very low temperatures is primarily due to:
A.) Denaturation of the enzyme.
B.) Formation of stronger enzyme-substrate bonds.
C.) Competitive inhibition by water molecules.
D.) Reduced kinetic energy of molecules, leading to fewer effective collisions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Low temperature slows molecular motion, reducing collision frequency between enzyme and substrate, thus lowering activity.

### Q53. The effect of enzyme concentration on reaction rate can be understood as:
A.) Each enzyme molecule can only catalyze one reaction.
B.) Enzyme concentration affects the activation energy.
C.) It is only relevant when substrate is limiting.
D.) More enzyme molecules. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — More enzymes mean more active sites are available to bind and convert substrate, increasing reaction rate.

### Q54. If a graph shows enzyme activity sharply declining below its optimum pH, it suggests that:
A.) The enzyme is becoming more stable.
B.) The enzyme is becoming saturated.
C.) The enzyme is forming aggregates.
D.) The active site is gaining more positive charges. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Below optimum pH, increased protonation alters active site charge, impairing substrate interaction and catalysis.

### Q55. Which pair of factors primarily influences the enzyme's three-dimensional structure and, consequently, its active site?
A.) Substrate concentration and product concentration.
B.) Light intensity and atmospheric pressure.
C.) Enzyme concentration and cofactor concentration.
D.) Temperature and pH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Temperature and pH impact the hydrogen, ionic, and hydrophobic bonds maintaining enzyme structure and active site integrity.

### Q56. The initial rate of an enzyme-catalyzed reaction is often measured because:
A.) Product inhibition becomes significant later.
B.) The enzyme is most stable at the beginning.
C.) The temperature is constant only at the beginning.
D.) Substrate concentration is highest at the start. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — At the start, substrate concentration is highest, and product inhibition is negligible, so the initial rate reflects true enzyme activity.

### Q57. A molecule that binds to an enzyme and decreases its activity is called an:
A.) Activator
B.) Cofactor
C.) Substrate
D.) Inhibitor **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An inhibitor is any substance that reduces the activity of an enzyme. Inhibition can be a normal regulatory mechanism or caused by foreign substances like drugs or poisons.

### Q58. In competitive inhibition, the inhibitor typically binds to the:
A.) Allosteric site
B.) Catalytic site
C.) Product binding site
D.) Active site **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Competitive inhibitors compete with the substrate by binding to the enzyme's active site, preventing substrate binding.

### Q59. Which characteristic distinguishes a competitive inhibitor from a non-competitive inhibitor?
A.) Competitive inhibitors bind irreversibly.
B.) Competitive inhibitors are structurally dissimilar to the substrate.
C.) Competitive inhibitors bind to an allosteric site.
D.) Competitive inhibition can often be overcome by increasing substrate concentration. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Competitive inhibition can be overcome by increasing substrate concentration, allowing the substrate to outcompete the inhibitor.

### Q60. Non-competitive inhibitors primarily affect enzyme activity by binding to the:
A.) Active site.
B.) Product.
C.) Substrate itself.
D.) Allosteric site. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Non-competitive inhibitors bind to an allosteric site, inducing conformational changes that reduce catalytic efficiency.

### Q61. Which of the following is an example of a competitive inhibitor mentioned in the provided text?
A.) Cyanide
B.) Heavy metal salts
C.) Threonine
D.) Penicillin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Penicillin blocks the active site of bacterial enzymes involved in cell wall synthesis, making it a competitive inhibitor.

### Q62. Irreversible non-competitive inhibitors typically cause permanent inactivation of an enzyme by:
A.) Mimicking the substrate structure.
B.) Competing for active sites.
C.) Increasing the optimal temperature of the enzyme.
D.) Forming strong covalent bonds with the enzyme. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Irreversible non-competitive inhibitors form covalent bonds with the enzyme, leading to permanent structural changes.

### Q63. Feedback inhibition is a regulatory mechanism where the activity of an enzyme in a metabolic pathway is inhibited by:
A.) An activator molecule.
B.) An intermediate product of the pathway.
C.) A competitive substrate.
D.) The end product of the pathway. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In feedback inhibition, the final product of a pathway inhibits an earlier enzyme to regulate production.

### Q64. In feedback inhibition, the end product typically binds to which site on the enzyme to exert its inhibitory effect?
A.) Active site
B.) Substrate binding site
C.) Catalytic site
D.) Allosteric site **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The end product binds to an allosteric site, causing a conformational change that reduces enzyme activity.

### Q65. Cyanide is a potent poison because it acts as an irreversible non-competitive inhibitor of:
A.) Amylase
B.) Pepsin
C.) Trypsin
D.) Cytochrome oxidase **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cyanide binds to cytochrome oxidase in the electron transport chain, blocking cellular respiration and energy production.

### Q66. Which type of inhibition is often a normal mechanism for regulating metabolic pathways in living organisms?
A.) Irreversible non-competitive inhibition
B.) Competitive inhibition by drugs
C.) Heavy metal inhibition
D.) Feedback inhibition **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Feedback inhibition is a natural regulatory process used by cells to control enzyme activity and conserve resources.

### Q67. Malonate is given as an example of an inhibitor of succinate dehydrogenase. It is chemically similar to succinate. What type of inhibition would malonate likely exhibit?
A.) Non-competitive inhibition
B.) Irreversible inhibition
C.) Allosteric inhibition
D.) Competitive inhibition **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Malonate resembles succinate and competes for the enzyme's active site, making it a competitive inhibitor.

### Q68. If increasing the substrate concentration can completely reverse the effect of an inhibitor, that inhibitor is most likely:
A.) A non-competitive inhibitor.
B.) An irreversible inhibitor.
C.) An allosteric activator.
D.) A competitive inhibitor. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Competitive inhibition can be overcome by increasing substrate concentration, outcompeting the inhibitor for the active site.

### Q69. Heavy metal ions like Hg++ and Ag+ can act as non-competitive inhibitors by:
A.) Binding to the active site.
B.) Increasing the enzyme's optimal temperature.
C.) Competing with the substrate for binding.
D.) Breaking disulfide bridges and causing enzyme denaturation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Heavy metals disrupt enzyme structure by binding to sulfhydryl groups, breaking disulfide bonds and denaturing the enzyme.

### Q70. An enzyme undergoing feedback inhibition typically has:
A.) Only an active site.
B.) Only an allosteric site.
C.) No specific binding sites.
D.) Both an active site and an allosteric site. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enzymes involved in feedback inhibition have both an active site for the substrate and an allosteric site for the product.

### Q71. Sulfa drugs act as inhibitors of bacterial enzyme action by mimicking PABA (para-aminobenzoic acid), a substrate for folic acid synthesis. This mechanism classifies sulfa drugs as:
A.) Non-competitive inhibitors.
B.) Irreversible inhibitors.
C.) Allosteric regulators.
D.) Competitive inhibitors. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sulfa drugs mimic PABA and competitively inhibit the enzyme involved in folic acid synthesis in bacteria.

### Q72. Which type of inhibition involves the inhibitor binding to the enzyme-substrate complex, but not to the free enzyme?
A.) Competitive inhibition
B.) Non-competitive inhibition
C.) Feedback inhibition
D.) Uncompetitive inhibition **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Uncompetitive inhibitors bind only to the enzyme-substrate complex, reducing both Vmax and Km of the reaction.

## Cell Structure & Function

### Q73. Which of the following organelles is primarily responsible for photosynthesis in plant cells?
A.) Mitochondria
B.) Ribosomes
C.) Endoplasmic Reticulum
D.) Chloroplasts **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chloroplasts are unique to plant cells and are the site where light energy is converted into chemical energy through the process of photosynthesis.

### Q74. A rigid outer layer found in plant cells but absent in animal cells is the:
A.) Plasma membrane
B.) Cytoplasm
C.) Nuclear envelope
D.) Cell wall **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cell wall provides structural support and protection to plant cells, which is not found in animal cells.

### Q75. Which of these structures is typically much larger and more prominent in mature plant cells compared to animal cells?
A.) Nucleus
B.) Golgi apparatus
C.) Lysosome
D.) Vacuole **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plant cells usually have a large central vacuole that maintains turgor pressure against the cell wall and stores water, nutrients, and waste products.

### Q76. Centrioles are involved in cell division and are characteristic components of:
A.) Plant cells
B.) Both plant and animal cells
C.) Fungal cells
D.) Animal cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Centrioles are part of the centrosome and are crucial for the formation of the spindle fibers during cell division in animal cells.

### Q77. Which organelle is responsible for synthesizing lipids and detoxifying drugs, found in both animal and plant cells?
A.) RER
B.) Lysosome
C.) Peroxisome
D.) SER **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum (SER) is involved in lipid synthesis, metabolism of carbohydrates, and detoxification of drugs and poisons.

### Q78. Both animal and plant cells possess a selectively permeable outer boundary called the:
A.) Cell wall
B.) Capsule
C.) Cytosol
D.) Plasma membrane **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The plasma membrane is present in both animal and plant cells, regulating the passage of substances in and out of the cell.

### Q79. The primary function of mitochondria, found in both animal and plant cells, is:
A.) Photosynthesis
B.) Protein synthesis
C.) Waste storage
D.) Cellular respiration **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Mitochondria are where cellular respiration occurs to generate ATP (energy) for the cell's activities.

### Q80. Which organelle modifies, sorts, and packages proteins and lipids for secretion or delivery to other organelles?
A.) Nucleus
B.) Ribosome
C.) Vacuole
D.) Golgi apparatus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Golgi apparatus functions in processing, packaging, and secreting cellular products.

### Q81. Lysosomes are more commonly found and prominent in:
A.) Plant cells
B.) Fungal cells
C.) Bacterial cells
D.) Animal cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lysosomes are the primary digestive organelles in animal cells, breaking down macromolecules, old organelles, and foreign substances.

### Q82. The site of protein synthesis in both animal and plant cells is the:
A.) Nucleus
B.) Mitochondria
C.) Chloroplast
D.) Ribosome **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ribosomes are responsible for protein synthesis and are found in both animal and plant cells.

### Q83. Which structure is responsible for controlling the cell's activities by housing the genetic material?
A.) Cytoplasm
B.) Cell membrane
C.) Endoplasmic Reticulum
D.) Nucleus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nucleus contains the cell's genetic material and controls all cellular activities.

### Q84. The network of interconnected membranes involved in protein synthesis and transport, studded with ribosomes, is the:
A.) SER
B.) Golgi apparatus
C.) Peroxisome
D.) RER **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum is involved in the synthesis and transport of proteins.

### Q85. A key difference between typical mature plant cells and animal cells is the permanent presence of:
A.) Cytoplasm
B.) Nucleus
C.) Plasma membrane
D.) Plastids **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plastids, including chloroplasts, are responsible for photosynthesis and are not found in animal cells.

### Q86. What is the primary component of the cell wall in plants?
A.) Peptidoglycan
B.) Chitin
C.) Protein
D.) Cellulose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The plant cell wall is primarily composed of cellulose, a carbohydrate that provides strength and rigidity.

### Q87. Which of the following is involved in maintaining cell shape and facilitating cell movement?
A.) Cell wall
B.) Central vacuole
C.) Chloroplasts
D.) Cytoskeleton **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cytoskeleton provides structural support and is involved in various forms of cell movement and transport.

### Q88. Which of the following best describes the primary difference in cellular support between plant and animal cells?
A.) Animal cells use a cell wall, plant cells use a cytoskeleton.
B.) Both use a cell wall, but plant cell walls are stronger.
C.) Neither cell type requires specific support structures.
D.) Plant cells use a cell wall, animal cells USE a cytoskeleton. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plant cells have a rigid cell wall for structural support. Animal cells lack a cell wall and instead maintain their shape and organization through their cytoskeleton and the extracellular matrix.

### Q89. Plasmodesmata are channels that connect adjacent cells, allowing for communication and transport. These are characteristic features of:
A.) Animal cells
B.) Bacterial cells
C.) Fungal cells
D.) Plant cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plasmodesmata are small channels that traverse the cell walls of plant cells, enabling direct cytoplasmic connections and intercellular communication.

### Q90. Which organelle is responsible for storing water, nutrients, and waste products?
A.) Lysosome
B.) Peroxisome
C.) Golgi apparatus
D.) Central vacuole **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The large central vacuole is a prominent feature of mature plant cells, serving multiple functions including storage and maintaining turgor.

### Q91. A defining characteristic of plant cells that allows them to produce their own food is the presence of:
A.) Mitochondria
B.) Endoplasmic Reticulum
C.) Nucleus
D.) Chloroplasts **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chloroplasts are the organelles in plant cells that contain chlorophyll and are the sites of photosynthesis.

### Q92. Animal cells can change shape and move more readily than plant cells primarily due to the absence of a:
A.) Plasma membrane
B.) Cytoskeleton
C.) Nucleus
D.) Cell wall **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The rigid cell wall in plant cells restricts their shape changes and movement, while the absence of a cell wall in animal cells allows for greater flexibility and motility.

### Q93. Both plant and animal cells contain ribosomes, indicating their shared need for:
A.) Energy production
B.) Photosynthesis
C.) Cell division
D.) Protein synthesis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ribosomes are universal organelles responsible for synthesizing proteins.

### Q94. Which of the following is a common organelle found in abundance in cells with high energy demands, such as muscle cells in animals and actively growing plant cells?
A.) Lysosomes
B.) Vacuoles
C.) Chloroplasts
D.) Mitochondria **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Mitochondria are the primary sites of ATP production, making them abundant in high-energy-demand cells.

### Q95. The internal membrane system involved in packaging and secretion of substances in both animal and plant cells is the:
A.) Nucleus
B.) Endoplasmic Reticulum
C.) Peroxisome
D.) Golgi apparatus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Golgi apparatus modifies, sorts, and packages proteins and lipids for secretion or delivery.

### Q96. Which of these structures is found in animal cells and is involved in organizing microtubules, particularly during cell division, but is generally absent in higher plant cells?
A.) Plasmodesmata
B.) Chloroplast
C.) Cell wall
D.) Centrosome **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Centrosomes, with centrioles, organize microtubules and are crucial in animal cell division.

### Q97. The tough, flexible outer boundary of an animal cell that controls what enters and leaves the cell is the:
A.) Cell wall
B.) Capsule
C.) Nuclear envelope
D.) Plasma membrane **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The plasma membrane serves as the outer boundary of animal cells, regulating transport.

### Q98. Plant cells store excess glucose primarily as starch, and animal cells store it as:
A.) Starch
B.) Lipids
C.) Proteins
D.) Glycogen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plants store glucose as starch. Animals store it as glycogen, especially in liver and muscle cells.

### Q99. Which structure is common to both plant and animal cells and helps in intracellular transport and mechanical support?
A.) Cell wall
B.) Large central vacuole
C.) Chloroplast
D.) Cytoskeleton **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cytoskeleton provides support, maintains cell shape, and helps with movement inside the cell.

### Q100. What is the main structural component providing rigidity and protection to plant cells?
A.) Glycocalyx
B.) Extracellular matrix
C.) Cytoskeleton
D.) Cell wall **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cell wall provides rigidity and protection to plant cells and lies outside the plasma membrane.

### Q101. If a cell contains a cell wall, chloroplasts, and a large central vacuole, it is most likely a:
A.) Bacterial cell
B.) Fungal cell
C.) Animal cell
D.) Plant cell **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — These features — cell wall, chloroplasts, and large central vacuole — are characteristic of plant cells.

### Q102. Which of the following is a defining characteristic of eukaryotic cells that is absent in prokaryotic cells?
A.) Ribosomes
B.) Plasma membrane
C.) Cytoplasm
D.) Membrane-bound organelles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Eukaryotic cells possess a variety of membrane-bound organelles (like the nucleus, mitochondria, ER, Golgi), which are not found in prokaryotic cells.

### Q103. The genetic material of a prokaryotic cell is typically found in a region called the:
A.) Nucleus
B.) Nucleolus
C.) Plasmid
D.) Nucleoid **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prokaryotic cells lack a true nucleus; their genetic material is located in an irregular region called the nucleoid.

### Q104. Which statement accurately describes the nucleus in prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells?
A.) Both have a true nucleus.
B.) Prokaryotic cells have a true nucleus, eukaryotic cells do not.
C.) Neither cell type has a nucleus.
D.) Eukaryotic cells have a true nucleus, prokaryotic cells do not. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A key distinction is the presence of a membrane-enclosed nucleus in eukaryotic cells, which is absent in prokaryotic cells.

### Q105. Ribosomes are present in:
A.) Prokaryotic cells only
B.) Eukaryotic cells only
C.) Neither prokaryotic nor eukaryotic cells
D.) Both prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ribosomes are essential for protein synthesis and are found in both types of cells, though structurally different.

### Q106. The cell wall of most bacteria is primarily composed of:
A.) Cellulose
B.) Chitin
C.) Lignin
D.) Peptidoglycan **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Peptidoglycan is the primary component of the bacterial cell wall, providing structural integrity.

### Q107. Which of the following is characteristic of eukaryotic cell division but not prokaryotic cell division?
A.) Binary fission
B.) Budding
C.) Fragmentation
D.) Mitosis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Eukaryotic cells divide through mitosis or meiosis, unlike prokaryotes which divide by binary fission.

### Q108. In terms of size, prokaryotic cells are generally:
A.) Larger than eukaryotic cells
B.) Approximately the same size as eukaryotic cells
C.) Vary too widely to compare
D.) Smaller than eukaryotic cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prokaryotic cells are typically much smaller (1–10 µm) than eukaryotic cells (10–100 µm).

### Q109. Which structure is common to both prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells and regulates the passage of substances into and out of the cell?
A.) Cell wall
B.) Capsule
C.) Flagella
D.) Plasma membrane **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The plasma membrane is a universal feature that controls the exchange of substances in all cells.

### Q110. The presence of a cytoskeleton for structural support and intracellular transport is a feature of:
A.) Prokaryotic cells only
B.) Both prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells
C.) Viruses
D.) Eukaryotic cells only **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Eukaryotic cells possess a complex cytoskeleton that supports structure and organelle movement.

### Q111. Genetic recombination in prokaryotic cells primarily occurs through process
A.) Meiosis
B.) Sexual reproduction
C.) Mitosis
D.) Horizontal gene transfer **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prokaryotes exchange genes using horizontal gene transfer: conjugation, transformation, and transduction.

### Q112. Which of the following organelles is NOT found in prokaryotic cells?
A.) Cell wall
B.) Cytoplasm
C.) Ribosomes
D.) Mitochondria **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prokaryotic cells lack mitochondria and perform respiration using their cell membrane.

### Q113. The DNA of a prokaryotic cell is typically:
A.) Linear and associated with histones
B.) Linear and not associated with histones
C.) Circular and associated with histones
D.) Circular and not associated with histones **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prokaryotic DNA is circular and not associated with histone proteins.

### Q114. A feature often present in prokaryotic cells for locomotion is a:
A.) Cilia
B.) Pseudopod
C.) Centriole
D.) Flagellum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Many prokaryotes move using a simple flagellum that rotates like a propeller.

### Q115. The primary form of genetic material in eukaryotic cells is:
A.) A single circular chromosome in the cytoplasm
B.) Plasmids outside the nucleus
C.) A single linear chromosome in the nucleoid
D.) Multiple linear chromosomes within a nucleus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Eukaryotic cells have their genetic material organized into multiple linear chromosomes, enclosed in a nucleus.

### Q116. Which type of cell contains a more complex endomembrane system?
A.) Prokaryotic cells
B.) Both prokaryotic and eukaryotic
C.) Viruses
D.) Eukaryotic cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The endomembrane system is a hallmark of eukaryotic cells, allowing compartmentalization and specialized functions.

### Q117. While both cell types have a cell membrane, eukaryotic cell membranes also contain:
A.) Peptidoglycan
B.) Cellulose
C.) Chitin
D.) Sterols **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Eukaryotic membranes contain sterols(CHOLESTROL) that regulate fluidity, which are typically absent in prokaryotic membranes.

### Q118. The genetic material of prokaryotic cells is naked, meaning it is not associated with:
A.) RNA
B.) Enzymes
C.) Ribosomes
D.) Histone proteins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prokaryotic DNA is not associated with histones, unlike eukaryotic DNA which is wrapped around histone proteins.

### Q119. Which of the following is true regarding the organization of DNA in prokaryotes versus eukaryotes?
A.) Prokaryotic DNA is always found in plasmids, eukaryotic DNA in chromosomes.
B.) Prokaryotic DNA is linear, eukaryotic DNA is circular.
C.) Both have multiple linear chromosomes, but prokaryotic ones are smaller.
D.) Prokaryotic DNA is usually a single circular chromosome, eukaryotic DNA is multiple linear chromosomes. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prokaryotic DNA is typically a single circular chromosome; eukaryotic DNA consists of multiple linear chromosomes.

### Q120. Photosynthesis in cyanobacteria occurs in:
A.) Chloroplasts
B.) Mitochondria
C.) Nucleus
D.) Thylakoids **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Photosynthetic prokaryotes use internal thylakoid membranes in the cytoplasm, not chloroplasts.

### Q121. Which type of cell typically has a larger and more complex genome?
A.) Prokaryotic cells
B.) Both have equally complex genomes
C.) Viruses
D.) Eukaryotic cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Eukaryotic genomes are generally larger and more complex, containing more genes and non-coding regions.

### Q122. The process of transcription and translation are coupled (occur simultaneously) in:
A.) Eukaryotic cells
B.) Both cell types
C.) Neither cell type
D.) Prokaryotic cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In prokaryotes, these processes occur together in the cytoplasm due to the absence of a nuclear envelope.

### Q123. The internal compartmentalization by membranes, allowing for specialized metabolic functions, is extensive in:
A.) Prokaryotic cells
B.) Both equally
C.) Viruses
D.) Eukaryotic cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Eukaryotic cells have numerous membrane-bound compartments for specialized metabolic processes.

### Q124. A plasmid is commonly found in:
A.) Eukaryotic cells only
B.) Both prokaryotic and eukaryotic cells
C.) Viruses
D.) Prokaryotic cells only **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plasmids, a small, circular, extrachromosomal DNA molecule, are typical in prokaryotes and provide additional traits like antibiotic resistance.

### Q125. Which of the following is present in the cytoplasm of eukaryotic cells but generally absent in prokaryotic cells?
A.) Ribosomes
B.) Glycocalyx
C.) Cell wall
D.) Endoplasmic Reticulum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Endoplasmic Reticulum is a eukaryotic-specific organelle not found in prokaryotes.

### Q126. Which of the following best describes the primary function of the nucleus?
A.) Protein synthesis
B.) Energy production
C.) Lipid synthesis
D.) Control of cell activities **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nucleus houses the cell's DNA and controls all cellular functions, including gene expression and cell division.

### Q127. The nuclear envelope is perforated by:
A.) Ribosomes
B.) Centrioles
C.) Lysosomes
D.) Nuclear pores **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Nuclear pores regulate the transport of macromolecules between the nucleus and cytoplasm.

### Q128. The nucleolus is primarily involved in the synthesis of:
A.) Messenger RNA (mRNA)
B.) Transfer RNA (tRNA)
C.) DNA
D.) Ribosomal RNA (rRNA) and ribosome assembly **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nucleolus synthesizes rRNA and assembles ribosomal subunits.

### Q129. What is the primary role of the Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum (RER)?
A.) Lipid synthesis and detoxification
B.) ATP production
C.) Packaging and secretion of proteins
D.) Protein synthesis, folding, and modification **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The RER is involved in the synthesis and processing of proteins, particularly those for secretion or membrane insertion.

### Q130. What is one of the main functions of the Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum (SER)?
A.) Digestion of waste materials
B.) Synthesis of proteins for secretion
C.) Packaging of proteins into vesicles
D.) Calcium ion storage and detoxification of drugs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The SER plays roles in lipid metabolism, detoxification, and calcium storage.

### Q131. Which organelle receives proteins and lipids from the ER, modifies them, sorts them, and packages them into vesicles?
A.) Nucleus
B.) Mitochondria
C.) Ribosome
D.) Golgi apparatus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Golgi apparatus is responsible for processing and packaging proteins and lipids for delivery.

### Q132. The Golgi apparatus typically consists of flattened membrane-bound sacs called:
A.) Cristae
B.) Thylakoids
C.) Grana
D.) Cisternae **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cisternae are the stacked, membrane-bound structures that make up the Golgi apparatus.

### Q133. Which organelle is often referred to as the 'powerhouse of the cell'?
A.) Nucleus
B.) Endoplasmic Reticulum
C.) Golgi apparatus
D.) Mitochondria **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Mitochondria are responsible for most of the cell’s ATP production via aerobic respiration.

### Q134. The inner membrane of the mitochondrion is highly folded into structures called:
A.) Cisternae
B.) Thylakoids
C.) Stroma
D.) Cristae **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cristae increase the surface area for ATP-producing reactions in the mitochondria.

### Q135. Where does the citric acid cycle primarily occur within the mitochondrion?
A.) Outer mitochondrial membrane
B.) Intermembrane space
C.) Cristae
D.) Mitochondrial matrix **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The mitochondrial matrix houses enzymes and substrates for the Krebs cycle (citric acid cycle).

### Q136. The main function of ribosomes, whether free or attached to the ER, is:
A.) DNA replication
B.) Lipid breakdown
C.) Carbohydrate storage
D.) Protein synthesis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ribosomes translate mRNA into polypeptide chains (proteins).

### Q137. Which of the following is synthesized on the ribosomes of the Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum?
A.) Proteins destined for the cytoplasm
B.) Steroid hormones
C.) Fatty acids
D.) Secreted proteins and integral membrane proteins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Proteins synthesized on RER ribosomes are destined for membranes, secretion, or specific organelles.

### Q138. Which organelle is responsible for breaking down long-chain fatty acids through beta-oxidation?
A.) Lysosome
B.) Mitochondria
C.) Golgi apparatus
D.) Peroxisome **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Peroxisomes carry out beta-oxidation of long-chain fatty acids and neutralize toxic compounds.

### Q139. Which component of the nucleus is primarily composed of chromatin?
A.) Nucleolus
B.) Nuclear lamina
C.) Chromosome
D.) Nucleoplasm **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nucleoplasm is the substance within the nuclear envelope that contains chromatin and the nucleolus.

### Q140. If a cell is highly active in synthesizing and secreting proteins, which organelle would be extensively developed?
A.) SER
B.) Mitochondria
C.) Lysosomes
D.) RER and Golgi apparatus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Secretory cells have abundant RER and Golgi apparatus for protein production and export.

### Q141. Cellular respiration is primarily carried out by which organelle?
A.) Chloroplast
B.) Ribosome
C.) Nucleus
D.) Mitochondria **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Mitochondria convert glucose into ATP through the process of cellular respiration.

### Q142. Which of the following is a primary function of the nucleoplasm?
A.) Site of ribosomal RNA synthesis
B.) Storage of lipids
C.) Detoxification of drugs
D.) Medium for chromatin and nucleolus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nucleoplasm is the semi-fluid substance within the nuclear envelope, suspending the chromatin and nucleolus.

### Q143. The outer membrane of the nuclear envelope is continuous with the membrane of which organelle?
A.) Golgi apparatus
B.) Mitochondria
C.) Lysosome
D.) Endoplasmic Reticulum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The outer nuclear membrane is connected to the Rough ER, showing their functional continuity.

### Q144. What is the role of ribosomes found attached to the Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum?
A.) Synthesize proteins that remain in the cytoplasm
B.) Synthesize lipids and carbohydrates
C.) Produce ATP for the cell
D.) Synthesize proteins destined for secretion or membrane integration **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ribosomes on the RER synthesize proteins for secretion or membrane integration.

### Q145. Which face of the Golgi apparatus is typically closest to the Endoplasmic Reticulum and receives vesicles from it?
A.) Trans face
B.) Medial face
C.) Luminal face
D.) Cis face **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cis face of the Golgi apparatus is closest to the ER and receives vesicles from it.

### Q146. The final modifications and sorting of proteins and lipids occur primarily in the:
A.) Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum
B.) Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum
C.) Nucleus
D.) Golgi apparatus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The trans face of the Golgi apparatus is the site for final modification and sorting.

### Q147. What is the primary role of the inner mitochondrial membrane in ATP production?
A.) Contains enzymes for the Krebs cycle
B.) Site of glycolysis
C.) Regulates entry of glucose into the mitochondrion
D.) Provides a large surface area for the ETC **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The inner membrane’s cristae hold the electron transport chain (ETC) critical for ATP synthesis.

### Q148. Which of the following is a characteristic of mitochondrial DNA?
A.) It is linear and found in the nucleus.
B.) It is found only in plant cells.
C.) It is the primary genetic material for the cell.
D.) It is circular and inherited maternally. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Mitochondrial DNA is circular and inherited from the mother.

### Q149. If a cell has a high demand for ATP, which organelle would you expect to be numerous and well-developed?
A.) Nucleolus
B.) Ribosomes
C.) Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum
D.) Mitochondria **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cells with high energy needs contain many mitochondria for ATP production.

### Q150. What type of molecules primarily constitute the nuclear lamina?
A.) Lipids
B.) Carbohydrates
C.) Nucleic acids
D.) Intermediate filaments **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nuclear lamina is made of protein filaments that support the nuclear envelope.

### Q151. Which of the following pathways best describes the synthesis and secretion of a protein like insulin?
A.) Ribosomes (free) → Golgi → Plasma membrane
B.) Smooth ER → Ribosomes → Golgi → Secretory vesicles
C.) Nucleus → Ribosomes (free) → Golgi → Lysosomes
D.) Rough ER → Golgi → Secretory vesicles → Plasma membrane **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Insulin is synthesized in the RER, processed in the Golgi, then secreted via vesicles.

### Q152. The cisternal maturation model describes the Golgi apparatus as:
A.) A static organelle with vesicles moving between cisternae.
B.) A single, continuous compartment throughout its length.
C.) An organelle primarily involved in DNA replication.
D.) Cisternae that mature as they move from the cis to the trans face. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In this model, Golgi cisternae mature and move from cis to trans while carrying cargo.

### Q153. Besides ATP production, mitochondria also play a crucial role in:
A.) Detoxification of drugs
B.) Calcium storage in muscle cells
C.) Photosynthesis
D.) Apoptosis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Mitochondria release signals that trigger apoptosis (programmed cell death) during cellular stress or damage.

### Q154. Which of the following is NOT a direct function of the Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum (SER)?
A.) Lipid synthesis
B.) Steroid hormone synthesis
C.) Detoxification of poisons
D.) Protein glycosylation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Protein glycosylation primarily occurs in the RER and Golgi, not the SER.

### Q155. The presence of abundant free ribosomes in the cytoplasm indicates that the cell is primarily synthesizing proteins for use in the:
A.) Secretion pathway
B.) Lysosomes
C.) Plasma membrane
D.) Cytosol **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Free ribosomes produce proteins that function in the cytosol.

### Q156. The function of the nuclear pores is to:
A.) Synthesize proteins within the nucleus.
B.) Store excess genetic material.
C.) Produce ATP for nuclear activities.
D.) Allow passage of small molecules, ions, and selective large molecules. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Nuclear pores regulate selective exchange between nucleus and cytoplasm.

### Q157. The primary role of cholesterol in the inner mitochondrial membrane is to:
A.) Increase its fluidity.
B.) Maintain its rigidity.
C.) Act as an electron carrier.
D.) It is not a significant component. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The inner mitochondrial membrane lacks cholesterol and instead maintains efficiency through a high protein-to-lipid ratio.

### Q158. Which of the following best describes the function of vesicles budding off from the trans-Golgi network?
A.) Bringing new proteins to the ER.
B.) Digesting cellular waste.
C.) Synthesizing ATP.
D.) Transporting materials for secretion. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Vesicles from the trans-Golgi carry processed proteins and lipids to their specific cellular destinations or outside the cell.

### Q159. In the nucleus, the genetic material exists as chromatin during interphase. Chromatin is a complex of DNA and:
A.) Lipids
B.) Carbohydrates
C.) RNA only
D.) Proteins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chromatin consists of DNA wound around histone and non-histone proteins, aiding in packaging and gene regulation.

### Q160. The lumen of the Endoplasmic Reticulum (ER) is:
A.) The space between the inner and outer nuclear membranes.
B.) The space between the two mitochondrial membranes.
C.) The space inside the Golgi cisternae.
D.) The fluid-filled space within the ER network. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The ER lumen is where proteins fold and undergo initial modifications before transport.

### Q161. A cell specializing in detoxification, such as a liver cell, would have an abundance of which organelle?
A.) Rough Endoplasmic Reticulum
B.) Golgi apparatus
C.) Mitochondria
D.) Smooth Endoplasmic Reticulum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Liver cells have extensive SER to detoxify drugs and metabolize lipids.

### Q162. The inner mitochondrial membrane is folded into cristae to:
A.) Increase the surface area for the Krebs cycle.
B.) Store more genetic material.
C.) Facilitate lipid synthesis.
D.) Increase the surface area for oxidative phosphorylation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cristae increase surface area for the proteins involved in the electron transport chain and ATP synthesis.

### Q163. Which of the following cellular components are produced in the nucleolus?
A.) Messenger RNA (mRNA)
B.) Transfer RNA (tRNA)
C.) DNA polymerase
D.) Ribosomal RNA (rRNA) **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nucleolus synthesizes rRNA and assembles ribosomal subunits.

### Q164. Proteins synthesized on free ribosomes typically function in the:
A.) Endoplasmic Reticulum
B.) Golgi apparatus
C.) Secretory vesicles
D.) Cytosol **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Free ribosomes produce proteins that stay in the cytosol, such as metabolic enzymes.

### Q165. The term "oxidative phosphorylation" refers to ATP synthesis that occurs in the mitochondria, coupled with:
A.) Glycolysis
B.) Fermentation
C.) Photosynthesis
D.) Electron transport and proton gradient **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Electron transport drives the proton gradient, powering ATP synthesis during oxidative phosphorylation.

### Q166. Which region of the Golgi apparatus is primarily involved in the initial processing and glycosylation of proteins received from the ER?
A.) Trans face
B.) Medial cisternae
C.) Exit face
D.) Cis face **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cis face receives proteins from the ER and begins their modification.

### Q167. The primary function of the nuclear pores is to:
A.) Synthesize proteins within the nucleus.
B.) Store nuclear waste products.
C.) Replicate DNA.
D.) Regulate the passage of molecules between the nucleus and cytoplasm. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Nuclear pores allow selective transport of RNA, proteins, and small molecules.

### Q168. Proteins targeted for degradation by lysosomes are typically tagged and then processed through which pathway?
A.) Smooth ER → Cytosol → Lysosome
B.) Nucleus → Ribosomes (free) → Lysosome
C.) Mitochondria → Golgi → Lysosome
D.) Rough ER → Golgi → Lysosome **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Proteins for lysosomal degradation follow the RER → Golgi → lysosome pathway.

### Q169. The process of aerobic respiration, which yields a large amount of ATP, occurs mainly in which two parts of the mitochondrion?
A.) Outer membrane and intermembrane space
B.) Cristae and outer membrane
C.) Matrix and intermembrane space
D.) Matrix and inner membrane **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Krebs cycle occurs in the matrix, while oxidative phosphorylation occurs at the inner membrane.

### Q170. What is the role of vesicles that bud off from the Endoplasmic Reticulum and fuse with the Golgi apparatus?
A.) To synthesize new lipids for the Golgi.
B.) To break down waste products.
C.) To generate ATP for the Golgi.
D.) To transport proteins and lipids. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Transport vesicles move cargo from the ER to the Golgi for further processing.

### Q171. Which organelle serves as the main control center of a eukaryotic cell?
A.) Mitochondria
B.) Endoplasmic Reticulum
C.) Golgi apparatus
D.) Nucleus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nucleus controls gene expression and cellular regulation, making it the control center.

## Bioenergetics

### Q172. Which of the following best defines Bioenergetics?
A.) The study of living organisms and their environment.
B.) The process by which plants convert light into chemical energy.
C.) The study of chemical reactions occurring in living organisms.
D.) The quantitative study of energy relationships and conversions in biological systems. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Bioenergetics is specifically defined as the quantitative study of energy relationships and conversions in biological systems, adhering to the laws of thermodynamics.

### Q173. What is the primary source of energy for almost all life on Earth?
A.) Chemical energy from food
B.) Geothermal energy
C.) Electrical energy
D.) Solar energy **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — All life on this planet Earth is powered, directly or indirectly, by solar energy.

### Q174. How do plants primarily convert light energy into chemical energy?
A.) By directly using sunlight for metabolism.
B.) By storing it in water molecules.
C.) By releasing it as heat.
D.) By synthesizing organic molecules like sugars through photosynthesis. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The chloroplasts of plants capture light energy and convert it into chemical energy stored in sugars and other organic molecules.

### Q175. The accumulation of which molecule in the atmosphere made the evolution of respiration possible?
A.) Carbon dioxide
B.) Nitrogen
C.) Methane
D.) Oxygen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — With the emergence of photosynthesis on earth, molecular oxygen began to accumulate slowly in the atmosphere. The presence of free oxygen made possible the evolution of respiration.

### Q176. What is the role of ATP in biological systems as described in the context of bioenergetics?
A.) It is a structural component of cell membranes.
B.) It is a primary genetic material.
C.) It functions as an enzyme in metabolic reactions.
D.) It acts as a chemical link between catabolism and anabolism. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ATP is described as a kind of chemical link between catabolism and anabolism because it couples energy-releasing (catabolic) reactions with energy-requiring (anabolic) reactions.

### Q177. Which of the following processes releases a great deal of energy and couples some of this energy to ATP formation?
A.) Photosynthesis
B.) Transpiration
C.) Active transport
D.) Respiration **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Respiration releases great deal of energy, and couples some of this energy to the formation of adenosine triphosphate (ATP) molecules.

### Q178. What are the energy-poor inorganic compounds that are reduced to energy-rich carbohydrates during photosynthesis?
A.) Oxygen and nitrogen
B.) Glucose and water
C.) ATP and NADPH
D.) Carbon dioxide and water **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Photosynthesis is the process where energy-poor inorganic compounds (carbon dioxide and water) are reduced to energy-rich carbohydrates (glucose).

### Q179. From which reactant does the oxygen released during photosynthesis originate?
A.) Carbon dioxide
B.) Glucose
C.) Light energy
D.) Water **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Oxygen released during photosynthesis originates from water, a concept hypothesized by Van Niel and later confirmed using isotopic tracers.

### Q180. What are the primary sites of photosynthesis in plants?
A.) Mitochondria
B.) Ribosomes
C.) Vacuoles
D.) Chloroplasts **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chloroplasts: These are the primary sites of photosynthesis in plants, found mainly in mesophyll cells.

### Q181. Which part of the chloroplast contains the thylakoids, where light-dependent reactions occur?
A.) Stroma
B.) Outer membrane
C.) Inner membrane
D.) Grana **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chloroplasts contain stroma, and thylakoids, where light-dependent reactions occur on grana. Grana are stacks of thylakoids.

### Q182. Which of the following pigments are primarily responsible for absorbing violet-blue and orange-red wavelengths of light in plants?
A.) Carotenoids
B.) Phycobilins
C.) Xanthophylls
D.) Chlorophylls **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chlorophylls mainly absorb violet-blue and orange-red wavelengths, giving plants their green color.

### Q183. What is the main function of carotenoids in photosynthesis?
A.) To synthesize ATP directly.
B.) To absorb green light and use in photosynthesis.
C.) To release oxygen from water.
D.) To broaden the spectrum of light used and protect chlorophyll from intense light. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Carotenoids broaden the spectrum of light used for photosynthesis and protect chlorophyll from intense light.

### Q184. Which set of products is directly generated from light-dependent reactions of photosynthesis?
A.) Glucose and oxygen
B.) Carbon dioxide and water
C.) Pyruvic acid and ATP
D.) ATP and NADPH **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The light-dependent reactions directly use light energy to produce ATP and NADPH, which are then used in light-independent reactions.

### Q185. Which of the following statements is TRUE regarding photosynthesis and respiration in plants?
A.) Both processes occur continuously throughout day and night.
B.) Respiration occurs only during the daytime, while photosynthesis is continuous.
C.) Both processes occur only in the presence of light.
D.) Photosynthesis occurs primarily during the daytime, while respiration is continuous. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Photosynthesis primarily occurs during the daytime, whereas respiration continues during the day and night.

### Q186. The concept that oxygen released during photosynthesis originates from water was initially hypothesized by:
A.) Melvin Calvin
B.) Daniel Arnon
C.) Robin Hill
D.) C.B. Van Niel **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Oxygen released during photosynthesis originates from water, a concept hypothesized by Van Niel.

### Q187. In plants, chloroplasts are primarily found in which type of cells?
A.) Epidermal cells
B.) Root cells
C.) Xylem cells
D.) Mesophyll cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chloroplasts are found mainly in mesophyll cells.

### Q188. Which part of the chloroplast is the site for light-independent (dark) reactions?
A.) Thylakoid membrane
B.) Outer membrane
C.) Intermembrane space
D.) Stroma **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Dark reactions occur in the stroma of the chloroplast.

### Q189. Photosystem I (PSI) is characterized by a special pair of chlorophyll 'a' molecules known as P700. What does 'P700' refer to?
A.) Its molecular weight.
B.) The number of chlorophyll molecules present.
C.) Its position in the electron transport chain.
D.) The wavelength of light (in nm) it optimally absorbs. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — P700 refers to the reaction-center chlorophyll 'a' that absorbs light energy of 700 nm.

### Q190. What is the primary product of cyclic photophosphorylation?
A.) NADPH
B.) Oxygen
C.) Glucose
D.) ATP **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cyclic photophosphorylation involves only Photosystem I and generates ATP but not NADPH or oxygen.

### Q191. The process of photolysis in Photosystem II involves the splitting of what molecule to replace lost electrons?
A.) Carbon dioxide
B.) ATP
C.) NADPH
D.) Water **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In Photosystem II, electrons lost are replaced by electrons from water (photolysis), releasing oxygen.

### Q192. Which of the following is NOT produced during non-cyclic photophosphorylation (Z-scheme)?
A.) ATP
B.) NADPH
C.) Oxygen
D.) Glucose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Non-cyclic photophosphorylation produces ATP, NADPH, and oxygen. Glucose is produced in the Calvin Cycle.

### Q193. The mechanism by which ATP is synthesized, involving proton pumping across membranes during photosynthesis, is known as:
A.) Substrate-level phosphorylation
B.) Oxidative phosphorylation
C.) Fermentation
D.) Chemiosmosis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chemiosmosis is the mechanism for ATP synthesis in photophosphorylation, using redox reactions to pump protons across thylakoid membranes.

### Q194. Light-independent reactions (Calvin Cycle) primarily occur in which part of the chloroplast?
A.) Thylakoid lumen
B.) Grana
C.) Outer membrane
D.) Stroma **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Light-Independent Reactions (Calvin Cycle) occur in the stroma.

### Q195. What is the main purpose of the light-independent reactions?
A.) To produce ATP and NADPH.
B.) To split water and release oxygen.
C.) To absorb light energy.
D.) To fix carbon dioxide and synthesize sugar. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — light-independent reactions ( Calvin cycle ) use ATP and NADPH from light reactions to fix carbon dioxide and synthesize carbohydrates.

### Q196. The carbon fixation phase of the Calvin Cycle involves the incorporation of CO₂ into an organic molecule. What is the CO₂ acceptor molecule?
A.) Phosphoglycerate
B.) Glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate
C.) ATP
D.) Ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The CO₂ acceptor in the carbon fixation phase is RuBP (Ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate).

### Q197. Which of the following environmental factors is considered most crucial for the process of photosynthesis?
A.) Temperature
B.) Humidity
C.) Wind speed
D.) Light intensity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Light is the energy source for photosynthesis, making light intensity a primary factor.

### Q198. At very low light intensities, what is the relationship between light intensity and the rate of photosynthesis?
A.) The rate of photosynthesis decreases exponentially.
B.) The rate of photosynthesis becomes constant.
C.) Light intensity has no effect on the rate.
D.) The rate of photosynthesis is directly proportional to light intensity. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — At low light intensities, light is a limiting factor; the rate of photosynthesis increases as light intensity increases.

### Q199. What happens to the rate of photosynthesis if the light intensity continues to increase beyond the saturation point for a plant?
A.) The rate continues to increase indefinitely.
B.) The rate starts to decrease rapidly.
C.) The plant begins to respire more.
D.) The rate becomes constant, as other factors become limiting. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Beyond the saturation point, increasing light intensity will not increase the rate because other factors become limiting.

### Q200. Why is water considered an essential factor for photosynthesis?
A.) It provides structural support to the plant.
B.) It acts as a solvent for pigments.
C.) It helps in nutrient absorption from the soil.
D.) It provides electrons and protons for the light-dependent reactions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water is split during photolysis in the light-dependent reactions, providing electrons and releasing oxygen.

### Q201. What is the consequence of severe water shortage on the rate of photosynthesis?
A.) The rate significantly increases.
B.) The rate remains unaffected.
C.) The light-dependent reactions accelerate.
D.) The rate decreases due to stomatal closure, limiting CO₂ intake. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water shortage leads to stomatal closure, reducing CO₂ intake and slowing photosynthesis.

### Q202. Which gas is a necessary raw material for the light-independent reactions of photosynthesis?
A.) Oxygen
B.) Nitrogen
C.) Hydrogen
D.) Carbon dioxide **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Calvin Cycle uses ATP and NADPH from light reactions to fix carbon dioxide and synthesize sugars.

### Q203. If the carbon dioxide concentration in the atmosphere is very low, how would it affect the rate of photosynthesis, assuming other factors are optimal?
A.) The rate would be very high.
B.) The rate would increase steadily.
C.) It would only affect the light-dependent reactions.
D.) The rate would be limited, even with high light intensity. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Low CO₂ concentration limits the Calvin Cycle, even if other factors are optimal.

### Q204. What is the typical atmospheric concentration of carbon dioxide (CO₂)?
A.) Approximately 21%
B.) Less than 0.01%
C.) Greater than 1%
D.) Around 0.04% **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Atmospheric CO₂ is typically around 0.03–0.04%.

### Q205. An increase in carbon dioxide concentration generally leads to what effect on the rate of photosynthesis, up to a certain point?
A.) A decrease in rate.
B.) No significant change in rate.
C.) A shift from aerobic to anaerobic respiration.
D.) An increase in rate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Increased CO₂ enhances photosynthesis until another factor becomes limiting.

### Q206. Why might, very high temperatures negatively affect the rate of photosynthesis?
A.) Increased light absorption.
B.) Increased water uptake.
C.) Faster diffusion of CO₂.
D.) Denaturation of enzymes. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — High temperatures can denature enzymes involved in photosynthesis, slowing the reactions.

### Q207. Which of the following factors, when in short supply, acts as a 'limiting factor' on the rate of photosynthesis?
A.) Oxygen
B.) Nitrogen
C.) The factor that is most abundant.
D.) The factor that is closest to its minimum required level. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — According to Liebig's Law, the scarcest essential factor limits the process.

### Q208. In a closed environment, if a plant is actively photosynthesizing, what happens to the carbon dioxide concentration over time if no new CO₂ is introduced?
A.) It increases.
B.) It remains constant.
C.) It fluctuates randomly.
D.) It decreases. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plants consume CO₂ during photosynthesis, so its concentration decreases.

### Q209. Which statement best describes the interaction between light and water in photosynthesis?
A.) Water absorbs light energy directly.
B.) Water concentration affects the type of light absorbed.
C.) Light converts water into glucose.
D.) Light is needed to split water molecules. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Light energy drives photolysis of water during the light-dependent reactions.

### Q210. If a plant is exposed to green light, how would its photosynthetic rate likely compare to exposure to red or blue light?
A.) Higher, as green light is reflected.
B.) Similar, as all visible light is equally effective.
C.) Lower, as green light causes photoinhibition.
D.) Lower, as chlorophyll absorbs green light poorly. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chlorophyll reflects green light, so photosynthesis is less efficient under green light.

### Q211. Under what conditions would increasing the concentration of carbon dioxide NOT lead to an increased rate of photosynthesis?
A.) When light intensity is very high.
B.) When water availability is abundant.
C.) When temperature is optimal.
