D.) When another factor, like light or temperature, is limiting. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — According to the concept of limiting factors, only the most limiting factor controls the rate.

### Q212. Where do the light-dependent reactions of photosynthesis primarily occur within the chloroplast?
A.) Stroma
B.) Outer membrane
C.) Inner membrane
D.) Thylakoid membrane **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Light-dependent reactions occur on grana, which are stacks of thylakoids within the chloroplast.

### Q213. Which of the following molecules are the direct products of the light-dependent reactions, essential for the light-independent reactions?
A.) Glucose and Water
B.) Oxygen and Carbon Dioxide
C.) Pyruvic Acid and NADH
D.) ATP and NADPH **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Light-dependent reactions produce ATP and NADPH, which are used in the light-independent reactions.

### Q214. Photosystem II (PSII) contains a special pair of chlorophyll 'a' molecules referred to as:
A.) P700
B.) P660
C.) P720
D.) P680 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Photosystem II is associated with P680, named for its absorption of 680 nm light.

### Q215. In non-cyclic photophosphorylation, what is the fate of the electrons excited from Photosystem II?
A.) They return to Photosystem II.
B.) They are used to split water molecules.
C.) They are released as heat energy.
D.) They are captured by an electron acceptor and then passed to Photosystem I. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Electrons from PSII are passed to PSI through an electron transport chain after being captured by an electron acceptor.

### Q216. The process by which water molecules are split during the light-dependent reactions, releasing electrons, protons, and oxygen, is called:
A.) Hydrolysis
B.) Dehydration
C.) Respiration
D.) Photolysis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Photolysis refers to the splitting of water using light in Photosystem II.

### Q217. Which of the following is a direct product of water splitting (photolysis) in Photosystem II?
A.) Carbon dioxide
B.) Glucose
C.) ATP
D.) Oxygen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Photolysis of water in PSII releases oxygen as a direct byproduct.

### Q218. What is the role of the electron transport chain located between Photosystem II and Photosystem I in non-cyclic photophosphorylation?
A.) To directly produce glucose.
B.) To absorb additional light energy.
C.) To split water molecules.
D.) To generate ATP. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Electrons passed through the ETC generate ATP during non-cyclic photophosphorylation.

### Q219. Which Photosystem is involved in cyclic photophosphorylation?
A.) Photosystem II only
B.) Both Photosystem I and Photosystem II
C.) Neither Photosystem I nor Photosystem II
D.) Photosystem I only **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cyclic photophosphorylation involves only Photosystem I.

### Q220. What is the key difference in products between cyclic and non-cyclic photophosphorylation?
A.) Cyclic produces glucose, non-cyclic does not.
B.) Cyclic produces oxygen, non-cyclic does not.
C.) Non-cyclic produces ATP only; cyclic produces NADPH only.
D.) Cyclic produces ATP; non-cyclic produces ATP, NADPH, and oxygen. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cyclic photophosphorylation generates only ATP, whereas non-cyclic also produces NADPH and oxygen.

### Q221. The chemiosmotic mechanism in photosynthesis is responsible for the synthesis of:
A.) Glucose
B.) Water
C.) Oxygen
D.) ATP **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chemiosmosis generates ATP by using the proton gradient created across the thylakoid membrane.

### Q222. Which component is responsible for collecting light energy over a broad area and funneling it to the reaction center in a photosystem?
A.) Electron transport chain
B.) ATP synthase
C.) NADP+ reductase
D.) Antenna complex **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pigments in the antenna complex absorb light and transfer energy to the reaction center.

### Q223. In non-cyclic photophosphorylation, what is the ultimate electron acceptor at the end of the electron transport chain following Photosystem I?
A.) Water
B.) Oxygen
C.) ATP
D.) NADP+ **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Electrons reduce NADP+ to NADPH at the end of the non-cyclic pathway.

### Q224. The Z-scheme describes which of the following processes?
A.) Glycolysis
B.) Cyclic photophosphorylation
C.) Calvin Cycle
D.) Non-cyclic photophosphorylation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Z-scheme represents non-cyclic photophosphorylation involving PSII and PSI.

### Q225. When light energy excites electrons in the reaction center of Photosystem I (P700), what is the immediate consequence?
A.) ATP is immediately synthesized.
B.) Water is split to replace the electrons.
C.) CO2 is fixed.
D.) Electrons are passed to an electron acceptor. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Excited electrons are passed to an acceptor to begin electron transport.

### Q226. The energy released during the transfer of electrons along the electron transport chain in thylakoid membranes is used to:
A.) Directly form glucose.
B.) Split carbon dioxide molecules.
C.) Denature enzymes.
D.) Pump protons into the thylakoid lumen. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This creates a proton gradient for ATP synthesis via chemiosmosis.

### Q227. Which of the following describes a key feature of the Calvin Cycle?
A.) They directly require light energy.
B.) They produce oxygen as a byproduct.
C.) They only involve Photosystem I.
D.) They occur in the stroma and use ATP and NADPH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Calvin Cycle occurs in the stroma using ATP and NADPH from light reactions.

### Q228. What is the first stable organic product formed after carbon fixation in the Calvin Cycle?
A.) Glucose
B.) Ribulose-1,5-bisphosphate
C.) Glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate
D.) Phosphoglycerate **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — CO2 is fixed to RuBP forming an unstable 6C compound which splits into two PGA.

### Q229. The reduction phase of the Calvin Cycle primarily involves the conversion of PGA to G3P using energy from:
A.) ATP only
B.) NADPH only
C.) Light energy directly
D.) Both ATP and NADPH **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Both ATP and NADPH are required to convert PGA to G3P.

### Q230. What is regenerated during the final phase of the Calvin Cycle to allow the cycle to continue?
A.) ATP
B.) NADPH
C.) Glucose
D.) RuBP **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RuBP is regenerated to keep the Calvin Cycle running.

### Q231. How many carbon atoms does the RuBP have before combining with CO2 in the Calvin Cycle?
A.) 3 carbons
B.) 4 carbons
C.) 6 carbons
D.) 5 carbons **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RuBP is a 5-carbon sugar that combines with CO2.

### Q232. Which enzyme is responsible for catalyzing the carbon fixation step in the Calvin Cycle, combining CO2 with RuBP?
A.) ATP Synthase
B.) NADP+ reductase
C.) Phosphofructokinase
D.) RuBisCO **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RuBisCO catalyzes the fixation of CO2 to RuBP.

### Q233. The products of the light-dependent reactions, ATP and NADPH, are consumed in which phase of photosynthesis?
A.) Photolysis
B.) Electron transport chain
C.) Cyclic photophosphorylation
D.) Calvin Cycle **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ATP and NADPH are used in the Calvin Cycle to synthesize carbohydrates.

### Q234. How many phases does the Calvin Cycle primarily consist of?
A.) Two
B.) Four
C.) Five
D.) Three **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Calvin Cycle has three phases: carbon fixation, reduction, and regeneration.

### Q235. In the reduction phase of the Calvin Cycle, what is directly formed from 3-PGA using the energy from ATP and NADPH?
A.) RuBP
B.) Glucose
C.) Oxygen
D.) Glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — 3-PGA is reduced to G3P using ATP and NADPH.

### Q236. What is the primary fate of G3P molecules produced during the Calvin Cycle?
A.) All G3P molecules are immediately converted to glucose.
B.) All G3P molecules are used to regenerate RuBP.
C.) G3P is released as a waste product.
D.) Some G3P molecules are used to synthesize sugars, while others regenerate RuBP. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Some G3P is used to form glucose; the rest regenerates RuBP.

### Q237. Which of the following describes the role of ATP in the regeneration phase of the Calvin Cycle?
A.) It provides energy for CO2 fixation.
B.) It reduces PGA to G3P.
C.) It is formed during this phase.
D.) It is used to convert G3P back into RuBP. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ATP is required to regenerate RuBP from G3P.

### Q238. If the supply of ATP and NADPH from the light reactions suddenly stopped, which phase of photosynthesis would be immediately affected?
A.) Only the light-dependent reactions.
B.) Only the photolysis of water.
C.) Both light-dependent and light-independent reactions.
D.) Only the light-independent reactions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Light-independent reactions depend on ATP and NADPH from the light reactions.

### Q239. Why are the light-independent reactions also known as 'dark reactions'?
A.) They occur only in the absence of light.
B.) They are inhibited by light.
C.) They produce no visible products.
D.) They do not directly require light energy. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Dark reactions are so named because they do not directly use light.

### Q240. Which molecule represents the initial input of carbon into the Calvin Cycle?
A.) Glucose
B.) Pyruvic acid
C.) Oxygen
D.) Carbon dioxide **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Calvin Cycle begins with the fixation of carbon dioxide.

### Q241. Where does the energy required for the light-independent reactions ultimately come from?
A.) From the breakdown of water molecules.
B.) Directly from sunlight.
C.) From stored glucose molecules.
D.) From ATP and NADPH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ATP and NADPH from light reactions supply the energy.

### Q242. Which of the following events occurs FIRST in the light-dependent reactions?
A.) ATP synthesis by ATP synthase.
B.) Reduction of NADP+ to NADPH.
C.) Carbon dioxide fixation.
D.) Excitation of electrons in Photosystem II. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Light energy first excites electrons in Photosystem II (P680), starting the Z-scheme.

### Q243. What is the primary role of Photosystem I (PSI) in non-cyclic photophosphorylation?
A.) To split water molecules.
B.) To generate ATP directly from light.
C.) To fix carbon dioxide.
D.) To receive electrons from PSII and ultimately reduce NADP+. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — PSI accepts electrons from PSII and uses them to reduce NADP+ to NADPH.

### Q244. The flow of electrons in the Z-scheme starts from water and ends at:
A.) ATP
B.) Oxygen
C.) Glucose
D.) NADP+ **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Electrons from water end up reducing NADP+ to NADPH in the Z-scheme.

### Q245. If a plant is unable to synthesize NADP+, which process would be directly inhibited?
A.) Cyclic photophosphorylation.
B.) Photolysis of water.
C.) ATP synthesis in light reactions.
D.) The reduction phase of the Calvin Cycle. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Without NADP+, NADPH can't be formed, halting the reduction phase of the Calvin Cycle.

### Q246. The enzyme ATP synthase plays a vital role in both photosynthesis and respiration. In photosynthesis, where is it located?
A.) Outer chloroplast membrane
B.) Stroma
C.) Inner mitochondrial membrane
D.) Thylakoid membrane **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ATP synthase is located in the thylakoid membrane where chemiosmosis occurs.

### Q247. Which of the following is a key characteristic of a C3 plant's carbon fixation in the Calvin Cycle?
A.) CO2 is first incorporated into a 4-carbon compound.
B.) It primarily occurs at night.
C.) It is highly efficient in hot, dry climates.
D.) The enzyme RuBisCO is involved in initial carbon fixation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — C3 plants use RuBisCO to fix CO2 directly to a 3-carbon compound.

### Q248. If a plant is deprived of carbon dioxide, which of the following molecules would accumulate in the chloroplast?
A.) ATP
B.) NADPH
C.) Glucose
D.) RuBP **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Without CO2, RuBP can't be used in fixation and builds up in the chloroplast.

### Q249. The energy stored in the proton gradient across the thylakoid membrane is directly used by ATP synthase to:
A.) Split water molecules.
B.) Reduce NADP+.
C.) Fix carbon dioxide.
D.) Drive the synthesis of ATP. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The proton motive force powers ATP synthase to produce ATP.

### Q250. Which molecule serves as the energy currency that powers most cellular work, including many steps in the Calvin Cycle?
A.) Glucose
B.) Water
C.) Oxygen
D.) ATP **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ATP provides energy for biosynthesis, including in the Calvin Cycle.

### Q251. Considering the overall process of photosynthesis, which phase is primarily responsible for converting light energy into chemical energy stored in energy carriers (ATP, NADPH), and which phase utilizes these carriers to synthesize organic molecules?
A.) Light-independent phase converts light energy; light-dependent phase synthesizes organics.
B.) Both phases convert light energy; only light-independent synthesizes organics.
C.) Both phases synthesize organics; only light-dependent converts light energy.
D.) Light-dependent phase converts light energy; light-independent phase synthesizes organics. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Light-dependent reactions make ATP/NADPH; light-independent reactions use them to make sugars.

## Biological Molecules

### Q252. What is the primary focus of biochemistry?
A.) The study of plant structures and functions.
B.) The classification of all known biological species.
C.) The historical development of biological theories.
D.) The study of chemical compounds and processes in living organisms. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Biochemistry is defined as the branch of biology that deals with the chemical compounds of living beings and the various chemical reactions (metabolism) that occur within them.

### Q253. Which of the following best describes bioelements?
A.) Elements found exclusively in non-living matter.
B.) Elements that are radioactive and unstable.
C.) Elements used only in industrial processes.
D.) Elements found in living beings. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Bioelements are approximately 25 elements out of 92 naturally occurring elements on Earth that are found in living beings.

### Q254. What percentage of the human body is constituted by the six major bioelements?
A.) 50%
B.) 75%
C.) 100%
D.) 99% **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The six commonest bioelements (Oxygen, Carbon, Hydrogen, Nitrogen, Calcium, Phosphorus) constitute '99%' of the protoplasm in the human body.

### Q255. Which of the following is considered a 'major bioelement' in the human body?
A.) Potassium
B.) Sulphur
C.) Iron
D.) Calcium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The six major bioelements that constitute '99%' of the human body are Oxygen (65%), Carbon (18%), Hydrogen (10%), Nitrogen (3%), Calcium (2%), and Phosphorus (1%). Potassium, Sulphur, and Iron are listed as minor or trace elements.

### Q256. What distinguishes minor bioelements from major bioelements?
A.) Minor bioelements are found in proportions less than '0.01%' of protoplasm.
B.) Minor bioelements are not essential for life.
C.) Minor bioelements are only found in plants.
D.) Minor bioelements are found as less than '1%' of the protoplasm. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Minor bioelements are defined as those found as less than '1%' of the protoplasm, while trace elements are less than 0.01%.

### Q257. Which of the following is NOT one of the four fundamental kinds of biological molecules?
A.) Carbohydrates
B.) Proteins
C.) Nucleic acids
D.) Water **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The four fundamental kinds of biological molecules are carbohydrates, proteins, lipids, and nucleic acids. Water is an inorganic biomolecule, not one of the four fundamental organic macromolecules.

### Q258. What percentage of a mammalian cell is composed of water?
A.) 15%
B.) 4%
C.) 18%
D.) 70% **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — According to Table 2.1, water constitutes '70%' of both bacterial and mammalian cells.

### Q259. Which of the following organic biomolecules has the highest percentage in a mammalian cell?
A.) Carbohydrates
B.) Lipids
C.) DNA
D.) Proteins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In mammalian cells, proteins make up 18%, carbohydrates 4%, lipids 3%, and DNA 0.25%.

### Q260. What is the approximate number of naturally occurring elements found in living beings?
A.) 16
B.) 92
C.) 100
D.) 25 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Approximately 25 elements out of 92 naturally occurring elements on Earth are found in living beings and are called bioelements.

### Q261. What is the branch of biology that deals with the composition and structure of chemical compounds in living organisms and the chemical processes within them?
A.) Anatomy
B.) Physiology
C.) Ecology
D.) Biochemistry **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Biochemistry is defined as the study of the composition and structure of different chemical compounds found in living organisms and the chemical processes taking place within or related to living organisms.

### Q262. Which biological molecule is primarily responsible for providing a quick and readily available source of energy for cellular activities?
A.) Lipids
B.) Proteins
C.) Nucleic acids
D.) Carbohydrates **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Carbohydrates, particularly glucose, are readily broken down by cells to release energy for immediate use, serving as a primary fuel source.

### Q263. The diverse functions of biological membranes, such as transport and cell recognition, are largely attributed to which two types of biological molecules?
A.) Carbohydrates and Nucleic Acids
B.) Lipids and Carbohydrates
C.) Nucleic Acids and Proteins
D.) Proteins and Lipids **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Biological membranes are primarily composed of a lipid bilayer, which provides the structural framework, and various proteins embedded within or associated with it, which carry out diverse functions like transport, enzymatic activity, and cell recognition.

### Q264. Which biological molecule is crucial for carrying genetic information from the nucleus to the ribosomes for protein synthesis?
A.) DNA
B.) Lipids
C.) Carbohydrates
D.) RNA **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RNA (Ribonucleic Acid), specifically messenger RNA (mRNA), is responsible for carrying the genetic code from DNA in the nucleus to the ribosomes in the cytoplasm, where it is translated into proteins.

### Q265. What is a key role of lipids in living organisms, besides energy storage?
A.) Forming the primary structure of enzymes.
B.) Acting as the main component of cell walls in plants.
C.) Storing genetic blueprints for inheritance.
D.) Providing insulation and protection for organs. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lipids, particularly fats, serve as long-term energy reserves, but also play crucial roles in insulating the body and cushioning vital organs against physical shock. They also form cell membranes.

### Q266. Which class of biological molecules is responsible for a vast array of functions including enzymatic catalysis, structural support, transport, and defense?
A.) Carbohydrates
B.) Lipids
C.) Nucleic Acids
D.) Proteins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Proteins are highly versatile macromolecules that perform a wide range of functions, acting as enzymes to speed up reactions, providing structural support (e.g., collagen, keratin), transporting substances (e.g., hemoglobin), and defending the body against pathogens (e.g., antibodies).

### Q267. What is the primary reason for water's high polarity?
A.) The symmetrical arrangement of hydrogen and oxygen atoms.
B.) The equal sharing of electrons between hydrogen and oxygen.
C.) The presence of strong ionic bonds.
D.) The uneven sharing of electrons and the bent molecular shape. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water is highly polar due to the bent shape of the molecule and the unequal sharing of electrons, where oxygen is more electronegative, resulting in a partial negative charge on oxygen and partial positive charges on hydrogen atoms.

### Q268. The ability of water to form hydrogen bonds is due to:
A.) Its linear molecular structure.
B.) The presence of strong covalent bonds within the molecule.
C.) Its nonpolar nature.
D.) The ionization energy difference. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hydrogen bonds form between the partially positive hydrogen atom of one water molecule and the partially negative oxygen atom of an adjacent water molecule due to the polar nature of water.

### Q269. Water is often called the 'universal solvent' due to its ability to dissolve a wide range of substances. This property is primarily linked to its:
A.) Low density.
B.) High specific heat capacity.
C.) Cohesive forces.
D.) High polarity. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water's high polarity allows it to form hydration shells around charged and polar molecules (hydrophilic substances), effectively dissolving them.

### Q270. What term describes the tendency of water molecules to stick to other water molecules?
A.) Adhesion
B.) Hydrolysis
C.) Ionization
D.) Cohesion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cohesion is the property of water molecules to stick to each other due to extensive hydrogen bonding, which is responsible for surface tension.

### Q271. The property of water that allows plants to transport water upwards through xylem vessels is primarily:
A.) High heat of vaporization.
B.) Hydrophobic exclusion.
C.) Lower density of ice.
D.) Cohesion and Adhesion. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cohesion among water molecules and adhesion of water molecules to the xylem walls create a continuous column of water that can be pulled upwards during transpiration.

### Q272. Water has a high specific heat capacity. This property is vital for:
A.) Dissolving nonpolar substances.
B.) Rapidly changing environmental temperatures.
C.) Facilitating hydrophobic interactions.
D.) Maintaining stable internal body temperatures. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water's high specific heat capacity helps living organisms resist drastic temperature changes, thus maintaining a stable internal environment (homeostasis).

### Q273. Why does ice float on liquid water?
A.) Ice has a higher density than liquid water.
B.) Liquid water has a linear molecular arrangement.
C.) Ice molecules have stronger covalent bonds.
D.) Hydrogen bonds in ice form a more open, crystalline structure. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — As water freezes, hydrogen bonds stabilize, forming an open, crystalline lattice that spaces the molecules further apart, making ice less dense than liquid water, allowing it to float.

### Q274. The high heat of vaporization of water is important because it allows organisms to:
A.) Resist freezing in cold environments.
B.) Absorb more heat without changing temperature.
C.) Dissolve more solutes.
D.) Cool down effectively through sweating or transpiration. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A large amount of energy is required to break the hydrogen bonds and convert liquid water to water vapor. This property allows for effective cooling as heat is removed from the body during evaporation (e.g., sweating).

### Q275. Hydrolysis reactions, which involve the breaking of a bond by the addition of a water molecule, demonstrate water's role as a:
A.) Solvent
B.) Insulator
C.) Buffer
D.) Reagent **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In hydrolysis, water actively participates in the chemical reaction by splitting into H+ and OH- and adding across a bond, thus acting as a reactant or reagent.

### Q276. What is the process called when water molecules spontaneously dissociate into hydrogen ions (H+) and hydroxide ions (OH-)?
A.) Hydrolysis
B.) Condensation
C.) Polymerization
D.) Ionization **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water molecules have the ability to spontaneously dissociate or ionize into a hydrogen ion (H+) and a hydroxide ion (OH-).

### Q277. The surface tension of water, allowing insects to walk on its surface, is a direct result of:
A.) Adhesion
B.) High specific heat
C.) Hydrophobic exclusion
D.) Cohesion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cohesion, the strong attraction between water molecules due to hydrogen bonding, creates surface tension at the air-water interface.

### Q278. When a substance like oil separates from water, forming distinct layers, this phenomenon is known as:
A.) Cohesion
B.) Adhesion
C.) Hydrophilic interaction
D.) Hydrophobic exclusion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hydrophobic exclusion describes the tendency of nonpolar substances (like oil) to aggregate and separate from water, as water molecules prefer to hydrogen bond with each other rather than with the nonpolar molecules.

### Q279. Which property of water is crucial for aquatic life in cold climates, preventing entire bodies of water from freezing solid?
A.) High heat of vaporization
B.) High specific heat capacity
C.) Water as a universal solvent
D.) Lower density of ice **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lower density of ice means it floats on the surface, insulating the water below and preventing it from freezing completely, allowing aquatic organisms to survive.

### Q280. What type of bond is responsible for the unique properties of water, such as its high boiling point compared to similar-sized molecules?
A.) Ionic bond
B.) Covalent bond
C.) Peptide bond
D.) Hydrogen bond **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The extensive network of hydrogen bonds between water molecules requires a significant amount of energy to break, leading to its unusually high boiling point.

### Q281. Water has a pH of 7. This neutrality is a result of water's:
A.) Cohesive properties
B.) Solvent properties
C.) Thermal properties
D.) Ionization **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The ionization of water into H+ and OH- ions is fundamental to the pH scale and the concept of neutrality (where [H+] = [OH-]).

### Q282. Water molecules adhere to the walls of narrow tubes, a phenomenon called capillarity. This is an example of:
A.) Cohesion
B.) Surface tension
C.) Hydrophobic effect
D.) Adhesion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Adhesion is the attraction between water molecules and molecules of other substances, such as the cellulose in plant cell walls or glass in a narrow tube.

### Q283. When a plant wilts due to lack of water, which property of water is directly related to its ability to maintain turgor pressure in plant cells?
A.) High specific heat
B.) High heat of vaporization
C.) Lower density of ice
D.) Cohesion and solvent properties **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water, acting as a solvent, fills the plant cells, and its cohesive properties help maintain turgor pressure within the cells, which prevents wilting.

### Q284. Why is water considered an excellent thermal buffer in biological systems?
A.) All of these.
B.) It has a low specific heat capacity.
C.) It easily evaporates at low temperatures.
D.) It can absorb and release large amounts of heat with minimal temperature change. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water's high specific heat capacity allows it to absorb excess heat when metabolic activity is high and release it when the environment is cold, thus buffering temperature fluctuations.

### Q285. What is the approximate pH of pure water at 25°C?
A.) 0
B.) 4.5
C.) 14
D.) 7 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pure water at 25°C is neutral, meaning the concentration of H+ and OH- ions are equal, resulting in a pH of 7.

### Q286. In what state are water molecules most spread out due to hydrogen bonding?
A.) Gaseous water
B.) Liquid water
C.) Supercritical fluid water
D.) Solid water **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In ice, hydrogen bonds form a stable, crystalline lattice where molecules are more spread out compared to the constantly breaking and reforming bonds in liquid water, leading to its lower density.

### Q287. What is the general molecular formula for monosaccharides?
A.) (CH)n
B.) (CHO)n
C.) (C6H12O6)n
D.) (CH2O)n **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Monosaccharides are true carbohydrates represented by the general molecular formula (CH2O)n, where 'n' is the number of carbon atoms, ranging from 3 to 7.

### Q288. Which of the following is a characteristic of monosaccharides?
A.) They are complex carbohydrates that cannot be hydrolyzed further.
B.) They are formed by the linkage of many monosaccharide units.
C.) They are tasteless and insoluble in water.
D.) They are either polyhydroxy aldehydes or polyhydroxy ketones. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Monosaccharides are the simplest sugars, defined as polyhydroxy aldehydes (aldoses) or polyhydroxy ketones (ketoses).

### Q289. Glucose is classified as a:
A.) Disaccharide
B.) Polysaccharide
C.) Oligosaccharide
D.) Monosaccharide **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Glucose is a simple sugar, specifically a hexose monosaccharide, as it cannot be hydrolyzed into simpler sugar units.

### Q290. What type of monosaccharide is glucose, based on its functional group?
A.) Ketose
B.) Triose
C.) Pentose
D.) Aldose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Glucose is an aldose because it contains an aldehyde functional group (-CHO).

### Q291. How many carbon atoms are typically found in monosaccharides?
A.) 1 to 2
B.) 8 to 12
C.) More than 12
D.) 3 to 7 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The number of carbon atoms in monosaccharides ranges from 3 to 7.

### Q292. Which of the following is an example of an oligosaccharide?
A.) Starch
B.) Glycogen
C.) Glucose
D.) Sucrose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Oligosaccharides are carbohydrates that yield 2 to 10 monosaccharide units upon hydrolysis. Sucrose (cane sugar), maltose, and lactose are common disaccharides, a type of oligosaccharide.

### Q293. What type of bond links two monosaccharides together to form a disaccharide?
A.) Peptide bond
B.) Ester bond
C.) Hydrogen bond
D.) Glycosidic bond **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Monosaccharide units are joined together by a covalent bond known as a glycosidic bond, formed by a condensation reaction.

### Q294. Sucrose is a disaccharide formed by the condensation of which two monosaccharides?
A.) Glucose and Glucose
B.) Glucose and Galactose
C.) Fructose and Fructose
D.) Glucose and Fructose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sucrose is commonly known as cane sugar or table sugar and is formed by the condensation of one molecule of glucose and one molecule of fructose.

### Q295. Lactose, the sugar found in milk, is composed of which two monosaccharides?
A.) Glucose and Glucose
B.) Glucose and Fructose
C.) Fructose and Galactose
D.) Glucose and Galactose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lactose is formed by the condensation of one molecule of glucose and one molecule of galactose.

### Q296. Which polysaccharide is the main storage carbohydrate in plants?
A.) Glycogen
B.) Cellulose
C.) Chitin
D.) Starch **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Starch is the principal carbohydrate stored in plant cells as an energy reserve.

### Q297. Which polysaccharide provides structural support in plant cell walls?
A.) Starch
B.) Glycogen
C.) Amylose
D.) Cellulose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cellulose is a fibrous polysaccharide that forms the major structural component of plant cell walls.

### Q298. Glycogen is the main storage polysaccharide in:
A.) Plants
B.) Fungi
C.) Bacteria
D.) Animals **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Glycogen is the primary storage form of glucose in animals, especially abundant in the liver and muscle cells.

### Q299. What is the key difference between alpha-glucose and beta-glucose?
A.) The position of the carbonyl group.
B.) The number of carbon atoms.
C.) The presence of a ring structure.
D.) The orientation of the -OH group on carbon 1. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Alpha-glucose and beta-glucose are anomers, differing in the orientation of the hydroxyl group (-OH) on the anomeric carbon (carbon 1) when in ring form.

### Q300. Which polysaccharide is a component of the exoskeleton of insects and crustaceans?
A.) Cellulose
B.) Glycogen
C.) Starch
D.) Chitin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chitin is a structural polysaccharide found in the cell walls of fungi and the exoskeletons of arthropods (insects, crustaceans).

### Q301. The conversion of a linear monosaccharide into its ring form involves a reaction between the carbonyl group and a hydroxyl group. This ring form is more stable in:
A.) Organic solvents
B.) Acidic conditions
C.) Basic conditions
D.) Aqueous solutions **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In aqueous solutions, monosaccharides with five or more carbon atoms predominantly exist in cyclic (ring) forms, which are more stable than their open-chain counterparts.

### Q302. Maltose is a disaccharide formed from two units of:
A.) Fructose
B.) Galactose
C.) Sucrose
D.) Glucose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Maltose is a disaccharide commonly known as malt sugar, composed of two glucose units joined by an alpha-1,4 glycosidic bond.

### Q303. What type of reaction is involved in the formation of disaccharides from monosaccharides?
A.) Hydrolysis reaction
B.) Oxidation reaction
C.) Reduction reaction
D.) Condensation reaction **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Disaccharides are formed from two monosaccharides by a condensation reaction, where a molecule of water is removed.

### Q304. Which term refers to isomers that are mirror images of each other, like D-glucose and L-glucose?
A.) Anomers
B.) Epimers
C.) Structural isomers
D.) Stereoisomers **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — D-glucose and L-glucose are stereoisomers, specifically enantiomers, meaning they are non-superimposable mirror images of each other.

### Q305. Why are laboratory-manufactured 'left-handed' sugars not metabolized by human enzymes?
A.) They are too large to be absorbed.
B.) They are quickly denatured in the body.
C.) They are insoluble in water.
D.) Human enzymes are 'right-handed'. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Biological systems, including human enzymes, are highly stereospecific. Most naturally occurring sugars are D-isomers, and the enzymes that process them are structured to recognize and act upon these 'right-handed' forms, making L-isomers non-metabolizable.

### Q306. What is the primary role of starch in a potato plant?
A.) Structural support for leaves.
B.) Defense against herbivores.
C.) Facilitating water transport.
D.) Long-term energy storage. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Starch is the primary storage polysaccharide in plants, allowing them to store excess glucose as a readily available energy reserve.

### Q307. Which carbohydrate is sometimes referred to as 'animal starch'?
A.) Cellulose
B.) Amylose
C.) Sucrose
D.) Glycogen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Glycogen is the animal equivalent of starch and is often referred to as 'animal starch' due to its role as the primary glucose storage form in animals.

### Q308. Which of the following is a characteristic of polysaccharides?
A.) They are generally sweet in taste.
B.) They are readily soluble in water.
C.) They are simple sugars like glucose.
D.) They are macromolecules. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Polysaccharides are complex carbohydrates (macromolecules) formed by the polymerization of hundreds to thousands of monosaccharide units linked by glycosidic bonds. They are generally not sweet and are less soluble or insoluble in water.

### Q309. The hydrolysis of a disaccharide yields:
A.) A polysaccharide and water.
B.) Amino acids.
C.) Glycerol and fatty acids.
D.) Two monosaccharide units. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hydrolysis is the reverse of condensation; it involves the addition of a water molecule to break the glycosidic bond, yielding two monosaccharide units from a disaccharide.

### Q310. Which of these properties is true for monosaccharides?
A.) They are generally insoluble in water.
B.) They are large, complex polymers.
C.) They cannot be oxidized.
D.) They are typically sweet in taste. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Monosaccharides are simple sugars, generally sweet in taste, readily soluble in water, and can be oxidized.

### Q311. In the open-chain structure of a monosaccharide, all carbon atoms except one have a:
A.) Carboxyl group
B.) Amino group
C.) Phosphate group
D.) Hydroxyl group **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In the open-chain form of a monosaccharide, all carbon atoms except one (which is part of an aldehyde or ketone group) have a hydroxyl (-OH) group attached.

### Q312. What are the basic building blocks of proteins?
A.) Monosaccharides
B.) Fatty acids
C.) Nucleotides
D.) Amino acids **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Proteins are polymers made up of repeating monomer units called amino acids, linked together by peptide bonds.

### Q313. Which of the following functional groups are characteristic components of an amino acid?
A.) Aldehyde and Ketone
B.) Hydroxyl and Phosphate
C.) Methyl and Carbonyl
D.) Carboxyl and Amino **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Every amino acid contains at least one amino group (-NH2) and one carboxyl group (-COOH), both attached to the central carbon atom (alpha-carbon).

### Q314. The unique characteristic of each amino acid is determined by its:
A.) Amino group
B.) Carboxyl group
C.) Alpha-carbon
D.) R-group **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The R-group, or side chain, differs among the 20 amino acids and gives each its unique properties.

### Q315. What type of bond links amino acids together to form a polypeptide chain?
A.) Glycosidic bond
B.) Ester bond
C.) Hydrogen bond
D.) Peptide bond **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Amino acids are linked by peptide bonds, formed during a condensation reaction between the carboxyl and amino groups.

### Q316. The formation of a peptide bond involves a reaction where:
A.) A water molecule is added.
B.) An oxygen molecule is added.
C.) A carbon dioxide molecule is removed.
D.) A water molecule is removed. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Peptide bond formation is a dehydration synthesis reaction where a water molecule is removed.

### Q317. The primary structure of a protein refers to its:
A.) Three-dimensional shape with alpha-helices and beta-sheets.
B.) Overall globular or fibrous conformation.
C.) Arrangement of multiple polypeptide chains.
D.) Specific sequence of amino acids. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The primary structure is the unique linear sequence of amino acids in a protein.

### Q318. Sickle cell hemoglobin is an example illustrating the significance of:
A.) Protein denaturation.
B.) The role of the R-group in determining protein function.
C.) The primary role of carbohydrates in oxygen transport.
D.) The critical importance of amino acid sequence in protein function. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A single amino acid change in hemoglobin leads to sickle cell disease, showing the importance of amino acid sequence.

### Q319. Which level of protein structure involves the folding of the polypeptide chain into alpha-helices and beta-pleated sheets, stabilized by hydrogen bonds?
A.) Primary
B.) Tertiary
C.) Quaternary
D.) Secondary **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Secondary structure includes localized folding like alpha-helices and beta-sheets, stabilized by hydrogen bonds.

### Q320. The overall three-dimensional shape of a single polypeptide chain, including all its side chain interactions, defines its:
A.) Primary structure
B.) Secondary structure
C.) Quaternary structure
D.) Tertiary structure **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Tertiary structure is the full 3D structure formed by the folding and interactions of the polypeptide chain.

### Q321. What type of protein structure results from the association of two or more separate polypeptide chains?
A.) Primary
B.) Secondary
C.) Tertiary
D.) Quaternary **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Quaternary structure describes the interaction and arrangement of multiple polypeptide subunits in a protein.

### Q322. Enzymes, which catalyze biochemical reactions, are primarily what type of biological molecule?
A.) Carbohydrates
B.) Lipids
C.) Nucleic acids
D.) Proteins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Most enzymes are proteins that catalyze biological reactions.

### Q323. Which of the following is a characteristic of fibrous proteins?
A.) They are generally soluble in water.
B.) They are typically spherical in shape.
C.) They act as enzymes.
D.) They play structural roles in the body. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Fibrous proteins have elongated shapes and provide structural support (e.g., collagen, keratin).

### Q324. Globular proteins typically have which of the following characteristics?
A.) They are insoluble and provide structural support.
B.) They are elongated and form fibers.
C.) They are primarily composed of beta-pleated sheets.
D.) They are usually soluble and have functional roles. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Globular proteins are spherical, soluble, and perform functional roles in the body.

### Q325. The breakdown of a protein into smaller polypeptide fragments or individual amino acids is called:
A.) Condensation
B.) Denaturation
C.) Polymerization
D.) Hydrolysis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hydrolysis is the reaction that breaks peptide bonds by adding water, releasing amino acids.

### Q326. What type of interaction is crucial for maintaining the tertiary and quaternary structures of proteins, often involving the burying of nonpolar side chains away from water?
A.) Hydrogen bonds
B.) Ionic bonds
C.) Hydrophilic interactions
D.) Hydrophobic interactions **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hydrophobic interactions drive protein folding by keeping nonpolar side chains away from the aqueous environment.

### Q327. What is a defining characteristic of lipids?
A.) They are generally water-soluble.
B.) They are polymers of amino acids.
C.) They are primarily composed of monosaccharide units.
D.) They are insoluble in water. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lipids are a diverse group of organic compounds characterized by their insolubility in water and solubility in nonpolar organic solvents due to their nonpolar hydrocarbon nature.

### Q328. Triglycerides (acylglycerols) are formed by the esterification of glycerol with:
A.) Amino acids
B.) Phosphate groups
C.) Monosaccharides
D.) Fatty acids **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Triglycerides are formed when glycerol reacts with three fatty acids via ester bonds in a condensation reaction.

### Q329. What is the primary biological role of triglycerides?
A.) Structural component of cell membranes.
B.) Genetic information carrier.
C.) Catalysis of biochemical reactions.
D.) Long-term energy storage. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Triglycerides serve as the main form of long-term energy storage in many organisms.

### Q330. Phospholipids are crucial components of cell membranes due to their:
A.) Entirely hydrophobic nature.
B.) Entirely hydrophilic nature.
C.) Ability to form strong covalent bonds with water.
D.) Amphipathic nature. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Phospholipids are amphipathic, having both a hydrophilic head and hydrophobic tails, enabling them to form cell membranes.

### Q331. Which of the following is an example of a steroid?
A.) Phospholipid
B.) Triglyceride
C.) Wax
D.) Cholesterol **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cholesterol is a steroid composed of a four-ring structure and is a precursor for many hormones.

### Q332. Waxes are lipids primarily known for their role in:
A.) Providing insulation in animals.
B.) Acting as signaling molecules.
C.) Being a major component of brain tissue.
D.) Forming waterproof coatings on plants and animal surfaces. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Waxes are hydrophobic lipids that serve as waterproof coatings on plant leaves, fruits, and animal surfaces.

### Q333. Terpenes are a diverse group of lipids that include which important biological molecules?
A.) Antibodies and enzymes
B.) Hormones like insulin
C.) Components of bacterial cell walls
D.) Vitamins and pigments **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Terpenes include biologically important molecules like vitamins A, D, E, K and pigments like carotenoids.

### Q334. Which part of a phospholipid molecule is hydrophilic?
A.) The fatty acid tails
B.) The glycerol backbone
C.) The ester bonds
D.) The phosphate group **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The phosphate group is polar and hydrophilic, making it the water-attracting portion of a phospholipid.

### Q335. The conversion of a fatty acid from a saturated to an unsaturated state involves:
A.) Breaking of an ester bond.
B.) Addition of a hydroxyl group.
C.) Removal of a phosphate group.
D.) Formation of one or more double bonds in the hydrocarbon chain. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Unsaturated fatty acids contain double bonds, unlike saturated fatty acids which have only single bonds.

### Q336. Prostaglandins are a group of lipid compounds that act as:
A.) Long-term energy reserves.
B.) Structural components of hair and nails.
C.) Genetic material carriers.
D.) Local signaling molecules involved in inflammation and pain. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prostaglandins are eicosanoids that function as local hormones, playing roles in inflammation and other processes.

### Q337. Which of the following is a key structural difference between RNA and DNA?
A.) RNA contains deoxyribose sugar, while DNA contains ribose sugar.
B.) RNA is typically double-stranded, while DNA is single-stranded.
C.) RNA contains a phosphate group, while DNA does not.
D.) RNA contains uracil in place of thymine, while DNA contains thymine. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RNA differs from DNA by having ribose instead of deoxyribose and uracil instead of thymine. RNA is usually single-stranded; both contain phosphate groups.

### Q338. What is the primary role of messenger RNA?
A.) To form the structural component of ribosomes.
B.) To transport specific amino acids to the ribosome during protein synthesis.
C.) To regulate gene expression.
D.) To carry genetic information from DNA to the ribosomes for protein synthesis. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Messenger RNA (mRNA) carries genetic code from the DNA to ribosomes, where it is translated into proteins.

### Q339. Which type of RNA plays a crucial role in protein synthesis by catalyzing peptide bond formation?
A.) Messenger RNA (mRNA)
B.) Transfer RNA (tRNA)
C.) Small nuclear RNA (snRNA)
D.) Ribosomal RNA (rRNA) **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ribosomal RNA (rRNA) is part of ribosomes and catalyzes the formation of peptide bonds during protein synthesis.

### Q340. What defines a conjugated molecule?
A.) A molecule composed only of carbon and hydrogen.
B.) A molecule formed solely from one type of biological macromolecule.
C.) A molecule that can only function independently.
D.) A molecule formed by the combination of two or more different types of molecules. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Conjugated molecules consist of two or more different biological components, such as protein and carbohydrate.

### Q341. Glycoproteins are crucial components of cell membranes, often involved in:
A.) Energy storage.
B.) Forming the genetic material.
C.) Catalyzing intracellular reactions.
D.) Cell-to-cell recognition and signaling. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Glycoproteins help in cell communication and recognition by acting as molecular identifiers on cell surfaces.

### Q342. Which conjugated molecule is primarily responsible for the transport of lipids (like cholesterol and triglycerides) in the bloodstream?
A.) Glycoprotein
B.) Glycolipid
C.) Nucleoprotein
D.) Lipoprotein **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lipoproteins transport hydrophobic lipids in blood. LDL and HDL are examples of lipoproteins.

### Q343. Glycolipids are found in cell membranes, particularly abundant in nervous tissue, and contribute to:
A.) Structural support within the cytoplasm.
B.) Long-term energy storage in adipose tissue.
C.) The catalytic activity of enzymes.
D.) Cell surface markers for cell recognition. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Glycolipids are involved in cell recognition and communication, especially in neural tissue.

### Q344. Which type of conjugated molecule forms structures like chromosomes and ribosomes, playing roles in genetic information storage and protein synthesis?
A.) Glycolipid
B.) Glycoprotein
C.) Lipoprotein
D.) Nucleoprotein **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Nucleoproteins are DNA/RNA-protein complexes found in chromosomes and ribosomes, essential for gene expression and protein production.

## Evolution

### Q345. According to evolutionists, approximately how long ago did life begin on Earth?
A.) About 1.5 billion years ago
B.) About 5 billion years ago
C.) About 420 million years ago
D.) About 3.5 billion years ago **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that modern biologists believe life began about 3.5 billion years ago.

### Q346. What was the likely atmospheric condition of early Earth, according to evolutionists?
A.) Oxidizing and hot
B.) Reduced and oxygen-rich
C.) Oxidizing and ozone-rich
D.) Reduced and ozone-less **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Evolutionists concluded that the early atmosphere of Earth was reduced (without oxygen), hot, and ozone-less.

### Q347. The hypothesis suggesting that the first living beings originated in hot water springs among inorganic and organic molecules is called the:
A.) Panspermia hypothesis
B.) Biochemical evolution hypothesis
C.) Spontaneous generation hypothesis
D.) Vent hypothesis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text mentions that evolutionists hypothesized the first living being originated in hot water springs, and this hypothesis is called the vent hypothesis.

### Q348. Which group of prokaryotes is believed by evolutionists to be the first living beings on Earth?
A.) Cyanobacteria
B.) Eubacteria
C.) Proteobacteria
D.) Archaea **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Evolutionists believe that the first living being on Earth belonged to a group of prokaryotes, the Archaebacteria, now known as Archaea.

### Q349. What significant change occurred in Earth's atmosphere that allowed life on land to become possible approximately 420 million years ago?
A.) Accumulation of carbon dioxide
B.) Decrease in ultraviolet radiation
C.) Increase in nitrogen levels
D.) Buildup of protective ozone **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that about 420 million years ago, enough protective ozone had built up in the upper atmosphere to make life on land possible.

### Q350. Which of the following describes the initial nutritional mode of the earliest prokaryotes according to the text?
A.) Photosynthetic autotrophs
B.) Chemoautotrophs
C.) Predatory
D.) Absorptive heterotrophs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text mentions that the early prokaryotes were absorptive heterotrophs.

### Q351. Eukaryotes are estimated to have appeared how many years ago, according to fossil records?
A.) 3.5 billion years ago
B.) 420 million years ago
C.) 5 billion years ago
D.) 1.9 to 2.1 billion years ago **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The fossil record indicates that eukaryotes appeared 1.9 to 2.1 billion years ago.

### Q352. Which theory for the evolution of eukaryotes suggests that the prokaryotic cell membrane folded inward to enclose genetic material?
A.) Endosymbiosis theory
B.) Chemiosmotic theory
C.) Vent hypothesis
D.) Membrane invagination theory **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The membrane invagination theory proposes that the prokaryotic cell membrane folded inward (invaginated) to enclose copies of genetic material.

### Q353. Who proposed the Endosymbiosis theory?
A.) Charles Darwin
B.) Jean-Baptiste Lamarck
C.) Alfred Wallace
D.) Lynn Margulis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The endosymbiosis theory was proposed by Lynn Margulis.

### Q354. According to the endosymbiosis theory, what did a large anaerobic amoeboid prokaryote ingest that later developed into a mitochondrion?
A.) A small photosynthetic bacterium
B.) A spirochete
C.) A eukaryotic nucleus
D.) A small aerobic bacterium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The theory states that a large anaerobic amoeboid prokaryote ingested a small aerobic bacterium, which then developed into a mitochondrion.

### Q355. Which of the following is cited as evidence supporting the endosymbiosis theory for mitochondria and chloroplasts?
A.) They have different genetic codes from bacteria.
B.) They are significantly larger than bacteria.
C.) They produce all of their own enzymes and proteins independently.
D.) They have their own DNA and ribosomes similar to bacteria. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that both mitochondria and chloroplasts are similar in size to bacteria, have their own DNA, and have ribosomes similar to those of bacteria, supporting the endosymbiosis theory.

### Q356. What was the critical consequence of the emergence of the eukaryotic cell?
A.) A decrease in biodiversity
B.) The immediate formation of land-based life
C.) The end of all prokaryotic life forms
D.) An increase in biodiversity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text indicates that the emergence of the eukaryotic cell led to a dramatic increase in the complexity and diversity of living organisms on Earth.

### Q357. Which of the following is NOT one of Lamarck's basic assumptions regarding evolution?
A.) Use and disuse of organs
B.) Inheritance of acquired characteristics
C.) Organisms undergo change over time
D.) Natural selection of advantageous traits **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Natural selection was proposed by Darwin, not Lamarck. Lamarck's key assumptions were the use and disuse of organs and the inheritance of acquired characteristics.

### Q358. According to Lamarck, what happens to organs that are more frequently used by an organism?
A.) They diminish and disappear
B.) They become vestigial structures
C.) They are passed on only if beneficial
D.) They develop and become strong **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lamarck believed that organs more frequently used by an organism are developed and become strong.

### Q359. What did Lamarck propose about characteristics acquired by an individual during its lifetime?
A.) They are rarely passed to offspring.
B.) They are only passed if there's a genetic mutation.
C.) They are lost in the next generation.
D.) They are passed on to the offspring. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lamarck believed that characteristics an individual acquired during its lifetime were passed on to its offspring.

### Q360. Which animal's evolution did Lamarck famously use to illustrate his hypothesis of acquired characteristics?
A.) Finches
B.) Rabbits
C.) Snakes
D.) Giraffes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The evolution of the giraffe's long neck from short-necked ancestors is an example often used to illustrate Lamarck's hypothesis.

### Q361. In the Lamarckian explanation of giraffe neck evolution, how did their necks become longer?
A.) Offspring were born with mutations for longer necks.
B.) Giraffes with naturally longer necks survived better.
C.) They evolved through a sudden act of creation.
D.) Each generation stretched its neck to reach higher leaves, and this trait was inherited. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — According to Lamarck, each giraffe stretched its neck to reach leaves, and the results of this stretching (an acquired characteristic) were passed on to future generations, leading to longer necks.

### Q362. Lamarck's contribution to science is considered important because he was the first to propose that:
A.) Species are individually created by God.
B.) Genetic mutations drive evolutionary change.
C.) Evolution occurs through survival of the fittest.
D.) Organisms undergo change due to natural phenomena. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lamarck's contribution is important because he was the first to propose that organisms undergo change over time as a result of some natural phenomena.

### Q363. What is a major drawback of Lamarckism, identified by later scientific understanding?
A.) It did not account for environmental conditions.
B.) It proposed a 'ladder of life.'
C.) It failed to recognize the concept of 'use and disuse.'
D.) Acquired characteristics have no genetic basis and cannot be inherited. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains that characteristics acquired during a lifetime have no genetic basis and therefore cannot be inherited to the next generation, a major drawback of Lamarckism.

### Q364. The anatomical, biochemical, and behavioral characteristics an individual displays as it develops through life are known as its:
A.) Genotype
B.) Allele frequency
C.) Acquired character
D.) Phenotype **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The phenotype is defined as the anatomical, biochemical, and behavioral characteristics that an individual organism displays as it develops through life.

### Q365. Why was Lamarck criticized in his day?
A.) For advocating the idea of evolutionary change.
B.) For his belief in special creation.
C.) For his lack of observations during voyages.
D.) For the mechanism of evolution he proposed. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that Lamarck was criticized less for advocating ideas of evolutionary change and more for the mechanism he proposed for that change.

### Q366. Which of the following examples did Lamarck cite to illustrate the 'use and disuse' principle?
A.) Darwin's finches
B.) Industrial melanism in moths
C.) The evolution of giraffes with shorter necks
D.) A blacksmith developing a bigger bicep **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lamarck cited the blacksmith developing a bigger bicep in the arm that works the hammer as an example of the use and disuse of organs.

### Q367. What was Charles Darwin's position aboard the HMS Beagle?
A.) Captain
B.) Physician
C.) Geologist
D.) Naturalist **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Charles Darwin accepted the position of naturalist aboard the HMS Beagle in 1831.

### Q368. Darwin's observations on the Galapagos Islands were most important in the development of his ideas about:
A.) Geological formations
B.) Human population growth
C.) Plant classification
D.) Evolution **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that Darwin made observations on the Galapagos Islands that were most important in the development of his ideas about evolution.

### Q369. Which concept describes the differential reproductive capacities among individuals of a population, central to Darwin's theory?
A.) Overproduction
B.) Variations
C.) Struggle for existence
D.) Natural selection **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Natural selection refers to the differential reproductive capacities among the individuals of a population.

### Q370. According to Darwin, what is the consequence of each species having the capacity to produce more offspring than will survive to maturity?
A.) Increased variations
B.) Reduced competition
C.) Instant speciation
D.) Overproduction **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Darwin's observation that each species has the capacity to produce more offspring than will survive to maturity is termed 'Overproduction'.

### Q371. Which of Darwin's observations states that individuals in a population exhibit differences in their traits?
A.) Overproduction
B.) Struggle for existence
C.) Survival of the fittest
D.) Variations **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — 'Variations' is the observation that the individuals in a population exhibit variation in their traits.

### Q372. Who proposed the theory of uniformitarianism, influencing Darwin's understanding of Earth's age?
A.) Charles Lyell
B.) Thomas Malthus
C.) Alfred Wallace
D.) James Hutton **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Charles Lyell's book supported a theory of geological change proposed by James Hutton called uniformitarianism.

### Q373. Thomas R. Malthus's work, 'An Essay on the Principle of Population,' primarily highlighted the discrepancy between:
A.) Genetic variation and environmental stability
B.) Species diversity and habitat size
C.) Acquired traits and inherited traits
D.) Human population growth and food supply **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Malthus noted that human populations increase exponentially while food supply increases arithmetically, leading to struggle for existence.

### Q374. Who independently conceived a theory of the origin of species by natural selection?
A.) Charles Lyell
B.) James Hutton
C.) Joseph Dalton Hooker
D.) Alfred R. Wallace **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In 1858, Darwin received a letter from Alfred R. Wallace outlining the theory of natural selection, prompting Darwin to publish.

### Q375. 'Survival of the fittest' means that individuals who are fittest are those that:
A.) Are physically strongest
B.) Live the longest
C.) Can outcompete all other species
D.) Reproduce most successfully **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The fittest individuals are those that reproduce most successfully in their environment.

### Q376. The processes of natural selection cause an increase of which type of alleles within a population?
A.) Unfavourable alleles
B.) Deleterious alleles
C.) Neutral alleles
D.) Favourable alleles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Natural selection causes an increase of favourable alleles and a decrease of unfavourable alleles in a population.

### Q377. Darwin's 'Descent with modification' suggests that all organisms are related through:
A.) Independent creation events
B.) Spontaneous generation
C.) Acquired characteristics
D.) Descent from some common ancestors **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Darwin believed that all organisms are related through descent from some common ancestors.

### Q378. What does 'overproduction' imply about a population's potential growth?
A.) It grows linearly over time.
B.) It remains stable regardless of resources.
C.) It always reaches carrying capacity immediately.
D.) It grows exponentially over time. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Natural populations may exponentially increase in number over time due to overproduction.

### Q379. Which of the following is an example of an 'environmental' limit on population growth mentioned in the text?
A.) Interspecific competition
B.) Intraspecific competition
C.) Variation in traits
D.) Disease causing organisms **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Other limits on population growth include predators and disease-causing organisms.

### Q380. How many pages was Darwin's initial brief sketch of his theory, written for himself in 1842?
A.) 10 pages
B.) 100 pages
C.) 230 pages
D.) 35 pages **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In 1842, Darwin wrote for himself a brief 35-page sketch of his theory.

### Q381. What was the title of Darwin's landmark book published in 1859?
A.) The Evolution by Means of Natural Selection
B.) An Essay on the Principle of Population
C.) Zoological Philosophy
D.) The Origin of Species by Means of Natural Selection **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Darwin published 'The Origin of Species by Means of Natural Selection' in November 1859.

### Q382. Which of the following best describes the belief of supporters of Special Creation?
A.) The universe and man are products of innumerable changes from lower to higher forms.
B.) Life originated spontaneously from inorganic and organic molecules in hot water springs.
C.) Organisms undergo change over time due to natural phenomena.
D.) Every species was individually created by God in its present form and is incapable of change. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Creationists believe every species was individually created by God in the form it exists today and is not capable of undergoing change.

### Q383. According to evolutionists, what is the origin of the universe and man?
A.) A sudden, supernatural act at a particular time.
B.) An unobservable process that cannot be scientifically proven or disproven.
C.) Directly from existing conditions in their present form.
D.) The result of innumerable changes from lower to higher forms. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Evolutionists believe the universe and man result from innumerable changes from lower to higher forms.

### Q384. Scientific truth, concerning creation, is described as tentative, while theological truth to the believer is considered:
A.) Tentative
B.) Observable
C.) Contradictory
D.) Absolute **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Scientific truth is tentative, but theological truth to the believer is absolute.

### Q385. What is biogeography?
A.) The study of ancient life forms through fossils.
B.) The study of the geological distribution of rocks and minerals.
C.) The study of the genetic makeup of populations over time.
D.) The study of the geographical distribution of plants and animals on Earth. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Biogeography is the study of geographical distribution of plants and animals on Earth.

### Q386. Darwin noticed the absence of which animal in South America, despite suitable environmental conditions?
A.) Finches
B.) Tortoises
C.) Llamas
D.) Rabbits **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Darwin noticed South America lacked rabbits despite suitable environment.

### Q387. Which of the following is an example of a physical barrier inhibiting the distribution of organisms?
A.) An unfavourable climate
B.) The presence of competing organisms
C.) Disease-causing organisms
D.) A mountain range **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Physical barriers like mountains inhibit species distribution.

### Q388. Biogeographic studies reveal that life-forms in different parts of the world possess:
A.) Identical evolutionary histories.
B.) Similar genetic potentials.
C.) Universal distribution patterns.
D.) Distinctive evolutionary histories. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Life-forms in different regions have distinctive evolutionary histories.

### Q389. What is the science of discovery, identification, and interpretation of fossils called?
A.) Biogeography
B.) Anatomy
C.) Molecular biology
D.) Paleontology **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Paleontology is the science of discovery, identification, and interpretation of fossils.

### Q390. According to the fossil record, which type of organism represents the oldest known fossils?
A.) Eukaryotes
B.) Amphibians
C.) Fishes
D.) Prokaryotes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The oldest known fossils are prokaryotes.

### Q391. Which of the following represents the correct chronological sequence of vertebrate classes in the fossil record?
A.) Amphibians, Fishes, Reptiles, Mammals, Birds
B.) Reptiles, Fishes, Amphibians, Birds, Mammals
C.) Mammals, Birds, Fishes, Amphibians, Reptiles
D.) Fishes, Amphibians, Reptiles, Mammals and Birds **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Fossil record shows fishes first, then amphibians, reptiles, mammals, and birds.

### Q392. The succession of fossil forms provides strong evidence in favor of evolution because it offers a:
A.) Theoretical framework for genetic inheritance.
B.) Direct observation of ongoing speciation events.
C.) Snapshot of life forms at a single point in time.
D.) Visual record showing the evolution of a species. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Fossil succession provides a visual record of evolutionary change.

### Q393. What does Neo-Darwinism refer to?
A.) Darwin's original theory without any modifications.
B.) A new theory that completely replaced Darwinism.
C.) A theory explaining the origin of life before prokaryotes.
D.) The reappraisal of natural selection in terms of modern population genetics. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neo-Darwinism is natural selection reappraised with population genetics.

### Q394. The emergence of which scientific field has provided firm support for Darwinian theory, leading to Neo-Darwinism?
A.) Paleontology
B.) Biogeography
C.) Comparative anatomy
D.) Population genetics **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Population genetics provided a firm basis for Neo-Darwinism.

### Q395. Which of the following is cited as a source of evidence for evolution?
A.) Astrology
B.) Creationism
C.) Mythology
D.) Comparative Anatomy **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Comparative anatomy is a key evidence source for evolution.

### Q396. The study of similarities and differences in the DNA and proteins of different species provides evidence for evolution through:
A.) Biogeography
B.) Paleontology
C.) Uniformitarianism
D.) Molecular Biology **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Molecular biology studies genetic similarities to provide evidence for evolution.

### Q397. Differentiating between convergent and divergent evolution is based on the inheritance of which types of structures?
A.) Vestigial and analogous structures
B.) Acquired and inherited structures
C.) Primitive and advanced structures
D.) Homologous and analogous structures **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Homologous and analogous structures distinguish convergent and divergent evolution.

### Q398. Which concept involves organisms that share similar traits due to adapting to similar environments, despite not sharing a recent common ancestor?
A.) Divergent evolution
B.) Coevolution
C.) Parallel evolution
D.) Convergent evolution **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Convergent evolution leads to similar traits in unrelated species adapting to similar environments.

### Q399. Which principle describes the conditions under which allelic frequencies in a population remain constant from generation to generation?
A.) Natural Selection
B.) Inheritance of Acquired Characters
C.) Endosymbiosis Theory
D.) Hardy-Weinberg Principle **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Hardy-Weinberg Principle explains when allele frequencies remain stable.

### Q400. What is the concept related to random fluctuations in allele frequencies from one generation to the next, especially in small populations?
A.) Natural selection
B.) Gene flow
C.) Mutation
D.) Genetic drift **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Genetic drift refers to random changes in allele frequencies in small populations.

### Q401. The process by which new and distinct species are formed in the course of evolution is known as:
A.) Adaptation
B.) Hybridization
C.) Extinction
D.) Speciation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Speciation is the formation of new distinct species during evolution.

## Respiration

### Q402. What is the primary characteristic of cellular respiration?
A.) Uptake of oxygen from the environment
B.) Exhaling of carbon dioxide into the environment
C.) Exchange of gases in capillaries
D.) Breakdown of complex organic compounds to release energy **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cellular respiration (internal respiration) is defined as a catabolic process that involves the breakdown of complex organic compounds into simpler molecules with the release of energy.

### Q403. Which of the following processes are collectively referred to as cellular respiration?
A.) Breathing and gas exchange
B.) Inhaling and exhaling
C.) Oxygen uptake and carbon dioxide disposal
D.) Glycolysis, Krebs cycle, and electron transport chain **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cellular respiration involves processes collectively called glycolysis, Krebs cycle, and electron transport chain.

### Q404. What does the term "external respiration" refer to?
A.) Energy production within cells
B.) Breakdown of glucose molecules
C.) Transport of gases by blood
D.) Uptake of oxygen from the environment and disposal of carbon dioxide **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — External respiration is the process that occurs at the organismic level, involving the uptake of oxygen from the environment and the disposal of carbon dioxide into the environment.

### Q405. Which of the following is NOT a characteristic of cellular respiration?
A.) It is a catabolic process.
B.) It occurs at the cellular level.
C.) It releases energy.
D.) It involves the inhaling of oxygen. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cellular respiration is a catabolic process occurring at the cellular level that releases energy from complex organic compounds. Inhaling oxygen is part of external (organismic) respiration.

### Q406. Which property is essential for a respiratory surface to allow effective gas diffusion?
A.) It must be thick.
B.) It must be dry.
C.) It must have a small surface area.
D.) It must be permeable. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — For effective diffusion, the respiratory surface must be moist and permeable so that gases can pass through.

### Q407. Why must a respiratory surface be thin for efficient diffusion?
A.) To increase its surface area.
B.) To prevent water loss.
C.) To ensure a good blood supply.
D.) Diffusion is only efficient over short distances. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A respiratory surface must be thin because diffusion is only efficient over distances of 1 mm or less.

### Q408. What is the significance of a large surface area for a respiratory surface?
A.) It reduces the need for ventilation.
B.) It prevents the entry of foreign particles.
C.) It helps to maintain its structural support.
D.) It allows sufficient amounts of gases to be exchanged. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A large surface area ensures that a sufficient amount of gases can be exchanged according to the organism's needs.

### Q409. What is the role of a good ventilation mechanism across the respiratory surface?
A.) To keep the surface moist.
B.) To provide structural support.
C.) To filter the air.
D.) To maintain a steep diffusion gradient. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A good ventilation mechanism is required to maintain a steep diffusion gradient across the respiratory surface.

### Q410. Why is it advantageous for the respiratory surface to be located internally in air-breathing vertebrates?
A.) To increase the surface area for gas exchange.
B.) To protect it from physical damage.
C.) To enhance the blood supply.
D.) To prevent the surface from losing water to the atmosphere. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The internal location helps prevent the moist respiratory surface from losing water to the atmosphere.

### Q411. Air becomes saturated with water vapor before reaching the respiratory surface due to its passage through a branched tubular way. What is the benefit of this?
A.) It helps in filtering foreign particles.
B.) It warms the air to body temperature.
C.) It increases the oxygen concentration.
D.) It prevents the respiratory surface from becoming too dry. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Air becomes saturated with water vapor before reaching the respiratory surface, which helps maintain the moist condition of the surface.

### Q412. Diffusion of gases across the respiratory surface is most effective over a distance of:
A.) 5 mm or less
B.) 10 mm or less
C.) 2 mm or less
D.) 1 mm or less **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Diffusion is only efficient over distances of 1 mm or less, making a thin respiratory surface crucial.

### Q413. Which of the following is NOT a property of an ideal respiratory surface?
A.) Moist and permeable
B.) Large surface area
C.) Good blood supply
D.) Thick and dry **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An ideal respiratory surface must be moist and permeable, thin, and have a large surface area with a good blood supply.

### Q414. The structural support of a respiratory surface ensures that:
A.) It can expand and contract easily.
B.) It can filter incoming air.
C.) It is well-ventilated.
D.) It remains open and does not collapse. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Structural support is necessary for the respiratory surface to remain open and prevent collapse.

### Q415. The main function of the hair and mucus lining the vestibules of the nasal cavity is:
A.) To detect odors.
B.) To warm the inhaled air.
C.) To moisten the air.
D.) To filter out dust and foreign particles. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The hair and mucus in the vestibules of the nasal cavity act as a primary filter, trapping dust and other foreign particles from the inhaled air.

### Q416. Which of the following is responsible for warming the inhaled air as it passes through the nasal cavity?
A.) Nasal septum
B.) Cilia
C.) Olfactory receptors
D.) Blood capillaries **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The extensive network of blood capillaries lining the nasal mucous membrane helps in warming the inhaled air to body temperature.

### Q417. The nasal cavity is lined with a ciliated mucous membrane primarily for:
A.) Detecting smells.
B.) Adding moisture to the air.
C.) Providing structural support to the nose.
D.) Propelling trapped particles towards the pharynx. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cilia in the mucous membrane continuously beat, sweeping trapped dust and microorganisms along with mucus towards the pharynx, where they are swallowed.

### Q418. The part of the pharynx that connects with the nasal cavity is the:
A.) Oropharynx
B.) Laryngopharynx
C.) Epiglottis
D.) Nasopharynx **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nasopharynx is the superior part of the pharynx, located behind the nasal cavity and extending down to the soft palate.

### Q419. The pharynx serves as a passageway for both:
A.) Air and blood
B.) Food and water
C.) Blood and lymph
D.) Air and food **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pharynx is a cone-shaped passageway that serves as a common pathway for both air (from the nasal and oral cavities to the larynx) and food (from the oral cavity to the esophagus).

### Q420. Which of the following structures is commonly known as the voice box?
A.) Pharynx
B.) Trachea
C.) Bronchi
D.) Larynx **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The larynx is also known as the voice box because it contains the vocal cords responsible for sound production.

### Q421. The opening of the larynx is called the:
A.) Epiglottis
B.) Trachea
C.) Pharynx
D.) Glottis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The glottis is the opening into the larynx, through which air passes.

### Q422. What is the primary function of the epiglottis during swallowing?
A.) To produce sound.
B.) To warm the air.
C.) To filter foreign particles from the air.
D.) To prevent food and liquid from entering the trachea. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The epiglottis is a flap of cartilage that covers the glottis during swallowing, directing food and liquids into the esophagus and preventing them from entering the respiratory tract.

### Q423. The vocal cords are located within the:
A.) Pharynx
B.) Nasal cavity
C.) Trachea
D.) Larynx **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vocal cords, which are two bands of smooth muscles, are situated within the larynx and vibrate to produce sound.

### Q424. Which part of the nasal cavity contains hair that traps large dust particles?
A.) Olfactory region
B.) Respiratory region
C.) Nasal conchae
D.) Vestibules **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vestibules, the anterior part of the nasal cavity, are lined with coarse hairs that act as a first line of defense against large dust particles.

### Q425. The sense of smell is primarily associated with which part of the upper respiratory tract?
A.) Pharynx
B.) Larynx
C.) Trachea
D.) Nasal cavity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The upper part of the nasal cavity contains olfactory receptors, which are responsible for the sense of smell.

### Q426. Which of the following is NOT a division of the pharynx?
A.) Nasopharynx
B.) Oropharynx
C.) Laryngopharynx
D.) Tracheopharynx **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The three divisions of the pharynx are the nasopharynx, oropharynx, and laryngopharynx. Tracheopharynx is not a recognized division.

### Q427. What is the main characteristic of the nasal mucous membrane that helps in moistening the air?
A.) Presence of vocal cords
B.) Rich blood supply
C.) Presence of cartilage
D.) Secretion of mucus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The mucous glands in the nasal lining secrete mucus, which adds moisture to the inhaled air, preventing the delicate lung tissues from drying out.

### Q428. The structure that prevents food from entering the trachea during swallowing is part of the:
A.) Pharynx
B.) Nasal cavity
C.) Oral cavity
D.) Larynx **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The epiglottis, which prevents food from entering the trachea, is a part of the larynx.

### Q429. Which of the following statements about the nasal cavity's function is INCORRECT?
A.) It filters inhaled air.
B.) It moistens inhaled air.
C.) It warms inhaled air.
D.) It cools inhaled air. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nasal cavity warms, moistens, and filters inhaled air, preparing it for the lungs. It does not cool the air.

### Q430. The primary cartilage that forms the Adam's apple and is part of the larynx is the:
A.) Epiglottis
B.) Arytenoid cartilage
C.) Cricoid cartilage
D.) Thyroid cartilage **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The thyroid cartilage is the largest cartilage of the larynx and forms the prominent Adam's apple, particularly noticeable in males.

### Q431. If the epiglottis fails to function correctly, what might be the consequence?
A.) Difficulty in speaking
B.) Air entering the esophagus
