C.) Impaired sense of smell
D.) Food entering the trachea **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The epiglottis closes over the glottis during swallowing to prevent food and liquids from entering the trachea. A malfunction would lead to food entering the windpipe, causing choking.

### Q432. Which part of the pharynx serves as a passageway only for air?
A.) Oropharynx
B.) Laryngopharynx
C.) All parts serve both
D.) Nasopharynx **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nasopharynx is strictly a respiratory passageway; the oropharynx and laryngopharynx are common passageways for both food and air.

### Q433. The two bands of smooth muscles within the larynx that vibrate to produce sound are known as:
A.) Ligaments
B.) Cartilages
C.) Tracheal rings
D.) Vocal cords **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sound production occurs when air passes over and vibrates the vocal cords, which are located in the larynx.

### Q434. Which feature of the nasal cavity helps in trapping fine dust particles and microorganisms?
A.) The presence of vibrissae (hairs)
B.) The rich blood supply
C.) Its connection to the pharynx
D.) The ciliated mucous membrane **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The sticky mucus traps fine particles and microorganisms, and the cilia then sweep this mucus towards the pharynx, preventing them from reaching the lungs.

### Q435. The trachea is commonly known as the:
A.) Voice box
B.) Food pipe
C.) Bronchial tree
D.) Windpipe **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The trachea is a membranous tube connecting the larynx to the lungs, commonly known as the windpipe.

### Q436. What is the primary function of the C-shaped cartilaginous rings in the trachea?
A.) To facilitate gas exchange.
B.) To warm the inhaled air.
C.) To produce mucus.
D.) To prevent its collapse. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The C-shaped cartilaginous rings provide structural support to the trachea, ensuring it remains open and does not collapse, especially during inhalation.

### Q437. The inner lining of the trachea is composed of:
A.) Smooth muscle tissue.
B.) Squamous epithelium.
C.) Cartilaginous tissue.
D.) Ciliated mucous membrane. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The trachea is lined with a ciliated mucous membrane, which traps foreign particles and moves them upwards.

### Q438. The trachea divides into two primary branches called the:
A.) Bronchioles
B.) Alveolar ducts
C.) Terminal bronchioles
D.) Primary bronchi **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The trachea divides at its lower end into two primary bronchi, one entering each lung.

### Q439. How many secondary bronchi typically branch off to supply the right lung?
A.) Two
B.) Four
C.) Five
D.) Three **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The right lung has three lobes, and typically, three secondary bronchi branch off from the primary bronchus to supply each lobe.

### Q440. Which of the following respiratory structures lacks cartilage in its walls?
A.) Trachea
B.) Primary bronchi
C.) Secondary bronchi
D.) Bronchioles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Unlike the trachea and larger bronchi, bronchioles do not have cartilage in their walls, allowing their diameter to be regulated by smooth muscle.

### Q441. The smallest branches of the bronchi that lead to alveolar ducts are called:
A.) Tertiary bronchi
B.) Segmental bronchi
C.) Respiratory bronchioles
D.) Terminal bronchioles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Bronchioles subdivide further into smaller tubes, eventually forming terminal bronchioles which lead to respiratory bronchioles and then alveolar ducts.

### Q442. The tiny air-filled sacs where gas exchange occurs in the lungs are called:
A.) Bronchioles
B.) Alveolar ducts
C.) Tracheal rings
D.) Alveoli **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Alveoli are the microscopic air sacs at the end of the respiratory tree where the exchange of oxygen and carbon dioxide takes place between the air and the blood.

### Q443. Approximately how many alveoli are present in the human lungs?
A.) 50 million
B.) 200 million
C.) 1 billion
D.) 700 million **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The human lungs contain over 700 million alveoli, providing a vast surface area for gas exchange.

### Q444. What is the approximate thickness of the alveolar walls, crucial for efficient gas exchange?
A.) 1 µm
B.) 10 µm
C.) 0.5 mm
D.) 0.1 µm **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The alveolar walls are extremely thin, approximately 0.1 µm thick, which facilitates rapid diffusion of gases.

### Q445. The substance that lines the alveoli and lowers surface tension, preventing their collapse, is:
A.) Mucus
B.) Plasma
C.) Lymph
D.) Surfactant **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Surfactant, a lipoprotein, lines the alveoli and reduces surface tension, preventing the small air sacs from collapsing, especially during exhalation.

### Q446. The principal organs of the respiratory system are the:
A.) Trachea and bronchi
B.) Larynx and pharynx
C.) Diaphragm and intercostal muscles
D.) Lungs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lungs are the primary organs of respiration where gas exchange takes place.

### Q447. How many lobes does the left human lung typically have?
A.) One
B.) Three
C.) Four
D.) Two **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The left lung is typically smaller and has two lobes (superior and inferior) to accommodate the heart.

### Q448. The right human lung typically has how many lobes?
A.) Two
B.) Four
C.) Five
D.) Three **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The right lung is generally larger and is divided into three lobes (superior, middle, and inferior).

### Q449. The double-layered membrane covering the lungs is called the:
A.) Pericardium
B.) Peritoneum
C.) Meninges
D.) Pleura **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lungs are enclosed by a double-layered serous membrane called the pleura, consisting of a visceral layer and a parietal layer.

### Q450. The space between the visceral and parietal pleura is known as the:
A.) Mediastinum
B.) Pericardial cavity
C.) Alveolar space
D.) Pleural cavity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pleural cavity is the potential space between the two pleural layers, containing a small amount of pleural fluid.

### Q451. What is the function of the fluid within the pleural cavity?
A.) To facilitate gas exchange.
B.) To produce surfactant.
C.) To warm the inhaled air.
D.) To lubricate the surfaces. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pleural fluid reduces friction between the pleural layers during respiratory movements.

### Q452. The hilum of the lung is the region where:
A.) Gas exchange primarily occurs.
B.) The lung lobes are separated.
C.) Surfactant is produced.
D.) Bronchi, blood vessels, and nerves enter and exit the lung. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The hilum is a depression or indentation on the medial surface of each lung where the bronchi, pulmonary arteries and veins, and nerves enter and leave the lung.

### Q453. Which structure forms the walls of the alveolar ducts and leads to the alveoli?
A.) Tertiary bronchi
B.) Primary bronchi
C.) Trachea
D.) Terminal bronchioles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Terminal bronchioles branch further to form respiratory bronchioles, which then lead to alveolar ducts, ultimately ending in alveolar sacs containing alveoli.

### Q454. The dense network of capillaries surrounding each alveolus is crucial for:
A.) Maintaining alveolar shape.
B.) Secreting mucus.
C.) Producing sound.
D.) Effective transport of respiratory gases. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The rich capillary network around each alveolus ensures a close proximity between air and blood, facilitating rapid and efficient gas exchange.

### Q455. The main difference between bronchi and bronchioles is that bronchioles:
A.) Are larger in diameter.
B.) Have cartilaginous rings.
C.) Are directly connected to the trachea.
D.) Lack cartilaginous support. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Bronchioles differ from bronchi primarily because they lack the cartilaginous rings found in the trachea and larger bronchi.

### Q456. Which part of the lower respiratory tract is primarily responsible for conducting air to the respiratory zone?
A.) Alveoli
B.) Alveolar ducts
C.) Lungs
D.) Bronchi **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The bronchi and bronchioles form the conducting zone of the respiratory system, leading air to the respiratory zone (alveolar ducts and alveoli) where gas exchange occurs.

### Q457. The visceral pleura is the layer of the pleura that:
A.) Lines the thoracic cavity.
B.) Forms the mediastinum.
C.) Contains the pleural fluid.
D.) Covers the surface of the lungs. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The visceral pleura is the inner layer that adheres directly to the surface of the lungs.

### Q458. The parietal pleura is the layer of the pleura that:
A.) Covers the surface of the lungs.
B.) Is responsible for gas exchange.
C.) Produces surfactant.
D.) Lines the inner wall of the thoracic cavity. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The parietal pleura is the outer layer that lines the thoracic cavity.

### Q459. Which of the following statements about the lungs is TRUE?
A.) They are rigid, non-elastic organs.
B.) The left lung is typically larger than the right lung.
C.) They are directly attached to the ribs.
D.) They are spongy due to the presence of millions of alveoli. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lungs have a spongy texture because they are filled with millions of tiny air sacs (alveoli).

### Q460. The walls of the alveoli are composed of a moist squamous epithelium because:
A.) It provides strength and rigidity.
B.) It secretes protective enzymes.
C.) It filters out impurities from the air.
D.) It facilitates rapid diffusion of gases. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The thin, moist squamous epithelium of the alveolar walls provides a minimal barrier for efficient and rapid gas exchange.

### Q461. What is the consequence if a baby is born with insufficient surfactant in its lungs?
A.) The baby will have difficulty forming vocal sounds.
B.) The trachea will become blocked.
C.) The lungs will be unable to warm inhaled air.
D.) The alveoli will tend to collapse, making breathing difficult. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Insufficient surfactant leads to increased surface tension within the alveoli, making them prone to collapse, a condition often seen in premature infants.

### Q462. The cartilage rings of the trachea are C-shaped rather than complete rings to allow for:
A.) Greater flexibility during neck movements.
B.) Easier passage of air.
C.) More efficient gas exchange.
D.) The esophagus to expand into the tracheal space during swallowing. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The open part of the C-shaped rings faces the esophagus, allowing the esophagus to expand slightly into the tracheal space when a bolus of food is swallowed.

### Q463. Which structures are responsible for increasing the surface area for gas exchange in the lungs?
A.) Trachea and bronchi
B.) Bronchioles and alveolar ducts
C.) Pleural membranes
D.) Alveoli **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vast number of tiny alveoli provides an enormous total surface area, which is essential for efficient gas exchange.

### Q464. The movement of foreign particles trapped in the trachea's lining is primarily due to the action of:
A.) Smooth muscle contractions.
B.) Cartilage rings.
C.) Mucous glands.
D.) Cilia. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cilia in the ciliated mucous membrane of the trachea constantly beat, propelling mucus and trapped particles upwards towards the pharynx to be swallowed or expelled.

### Q465. Which of the following muscles contracts during normal inspiration?
A.) Internal intercostal muscles
B.) Abdominal muscles
C.) Pectoralis minor
D.) Diaphragm **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — During normal inspiration, the diaphragm contracts and flattens, increasing the volume of the thoracic cavity.

### Q466. What happens to the thoracic cavity's volume during inspiration?
A.) It decreases.
B.) It remains unchanged.
C.) It first decreases, then increases.
D.) It increases. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The contraction of the diaphragm and external intercostal muscles leads to an increase in the volume of the thoracic cavity during inspiration.

### Q467. During inspiration, the pressure inside the lungs:
A.) Increases above atmospheric pressure.
B.) Becomes equal to atmospheric pressure.
C.) Fluctuates rapidly.
D.) Decreases below atmospheric pressure. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — As the thoracic cavity volume increases, the pressure inside the lungs (intrapulmonary pressure) decreases, becoming lower than the atmospheric pressure, causing air to rush in.

### Q468. Normal expiration at rest is generally considered a:
A.) Active process
B.) Forceful process
C.) Muscular contraction process
D.) Passive process **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Normal expiration during quiet breathing is largely passive, as it results from the relaxation of the inspiratory muscles and the elastic recoil of the lungs.

### Q469. Which muscles relax during normal quiet expiration?
A.) Internal intercostal muscles and abdominal muscles
B.) Sternocleidomastoid muscles
C.) Pectoralis major muscles
D.) Diaphragm and external intercostal muscles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — During normal expiration, the diaphragm relaxes and becomes dome-shaped, and the external intercostal muscles relax, causing the rib cage to move downward and inward.

### Q470. What happens to the diaphragm during expiration?
A.) It contracts and flattens.
B.) It moves upward and forward.
C.) It remains stationary.
D.) It relaxes and becomes dome-shaped. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Relaxation of the diaphragm causes it to return to its original dome shape, reducing the vertical dimension of the thoracic cavity.

### Q471. Which part of the brain is primarily responsible for initiating and controlling the rhythm of breathing?
A.) Cerebellum
B.) Cerebrum
C.) Pons
D.) Medulla oblongata **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The respiratory center, located in the medulla oblongata, contains neurons that generate the basic rhythm of breathing.

### Q472. The amount of air inhaled or exhaled during a normal, quiet breath is called the:
A.) Residual volume
B.) Vital capacity
C.) Total lung capacity
D.) Tidal volume **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Tidal volume is the volume of air exchanged with each normal breath, typically around 500 mL.

### Q473. What is the approximate average tidal volume for an adult?
A.) 100 mL
B.) 1500 mL
C.) 3000 mL
D.) 500 mL **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The average tidal volume for a resting adult is approximately 500 mL.

### Q474. The extra volume of air that can be inhaled with maximum effort beyond a normal tidal inhalation is the:
A.) Expiratory reserve volume
B.) Residual volume
C.) Vital capacity
D.) Inspiratory reserve volume **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Inspiratory reserve volume (IRV) is the additional volume of air that can be forcibly inhaled after a normal inspiration.

### Q475. The total lung capacity (TLC) of an average adult is approximately:
A.) 2000 mL
B.) 3500 mL
C.) 4500 mL
D.) 6000 mL **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Total lung capacity is the maximum amount of air the lungs can hold, which is about 6000 mL (6 liters) in an average adult.

### Q476. When the external intercostal muscles contract, the rib cage moves:
A.) Downward and inward
B.) Sideways only
C.) Remains stationary
D.) Upward and forward **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Contraction of the external intercostal muscles lifts the rib cage upward and forward, further increasing the volume of the thoracic cavity during inspiration.

### Q477. In forced expiration, which muscles contract?
A.) Diaphragm and external intercostals
B.) Pectoralis major and minor
C.) Sternocleidomastoid and scalenes
D.) Internal intercostals and abdominal muscles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Forced expiration is an active process involving the contraction of the internal intercostal muscles (pulling the rib cage down) and abdominal muscles (pushing the diaphragm up), rapidly decreasing thoracic volume.

### Q478. An increase in the pressure inside the lungs causes air to move:
A.) Into the lungs
B.) Into the blood
C.) Out of the blood
D.) Out of the lungs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Air moves from a region of higher pressure to a region of lower pressure. When intrapulmonary pressure increases above atmospheric pressure, air is forced out.

### Q479. Which of the following describes the correct sequence of events during inspiration?
A.) Diaphragm relaxes, thoracic volume decreases, pressure increases, air flows out.
B.) Diaphragm contracts, thoracic volume decreases, pressure increases, air flows in.
C.) Diaphragm relaxes, thoracic volume increases, pressure decreases, air flows out.
D.) Diaphragm contracts, thoracic volume increases, pressure decreases, air flows in. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Inspiration is an active process where the diaphragm contracts, increasing thoracic volume, which lowers lung pressure below atmospheric pressure, causing air to rush in.

### Q480. The respiratory center that controls breathing is primarily located in the:
A.) Cerebral cortex
B.) Hypothalamus
C.) Cerebellum
D.) Medulla oblongata **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The medulla oblongata houses the primary respiratory control centers responsible for setting the basic rhythm of breathing.

### Q481. When the respiratory center sends impulses to the diaphragm and intercostal muscles, it leads to:
A.) Relaxation of these muscles.
B.) Inhibition of breathing.
C.) Forced expiration.
D.) Contraction of these muscles. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Impulses from the respiratory center stimulate the contraction of the diaphragm and external intercostal muscles, initiating inspiration.

### Q482. What is the effect of the relaxation of the inspiratory muscles during normal expiration?
A.) Increase in thoracic volume
B.) Decrease in thoracic volume
C.) Increased lung pressure
D.) Both b and c **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Relaxation of inspiratory muscles causes the thoracic cavity to decrease in volume, which in turn increases the pressure inside the lungs, forcing air out.

### Q483. The volume of air remaining in the lungs even after a maximal expiratory effort is known as the:
A.) Tidal volume
B.) Vital capacity
C.) Inspiratory reserve volume
D.) Residual volume **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Residual volume is the air that always remains in the lungs, preventing complete collapse and ensuring continuous gas exchange between breaths.

### Q484. A person takes a deep breath in, beyond their normal quiet inhalation. This additional volume is called the:
A.) Expiratory reserve volume
B.) Tidal volume
C.) Functional residual capacity
D.) Inspiratory reserve volume **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Inspiratory reserve volume (IRV) is the amount of air that can be forcibly inhaled after a normal tidal inhalation.

### Q485. How is the majority of oxygen transported in the blood?
A.) Dissolved directly in blood plasma.
B.) As bicarbonate ions.
C.) Bound to plasma proteins.
D.) Bound to hemoglobin in red blood cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Most oxygen (approximately 97-98%) is transported in the blood by binding to hemoglobin molecules within red blood cells, forming oxyhemoglobin.

### Q486. What is the name of the complex formed when oxygen binds to hemoglobin?
A.) Carboxyhemoglobin
B.) Methemoglobin
C.) Deoxyhemoglobin
D.) Oxyhemoglobin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When oxygen binds to the iron atom in the heme group of hemoglobin, the resulting bright red complex is called oxyhemoglobin.

### Q487. In which form is a small amount of oxygen transported in the blood?
A.) Bound to white blood cells.
B.) As carbaminohemoglobin.
C.) Within platelets.
D.) Dissolved in blood plasma. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A small percentage of oxygen (about 1.5-3%) is transported by being dissolved directly in the blood plasma.

### Q488. What is the approximate partial pressure of oxygen (PO2) in the alveoli, which facilitates oxygen loading onto hemoglobin?
A.) 40 mm Hg
B.) 70 mm Hg
C.) 159 mm Hg
D.) 105 mm Hg **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The relatively high partial pressure of oxygen (around 105 mm Hg) in the alveoli drives the loading of oxygen onto hemoglobin.

### Q489. Which of the following factors would cause hemoglobin to release oxygen more readily to the tissues?
A.) Increased pH
B.) Decreased temperature
C.) Increased partial pressure of oxygen
D.) Increased carbon dioxide concentration **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An increase in carbon dioxide concentration, which leads to a decrease in pH (more acidic conditions), reduces hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen, causing it to release oxygen more readily (Bohr effect).

### Q490. The Bohr effect describes the phenomenon where:
A.) Hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen increases with increasing temperature.
B.) Myoglobin stores oxygen in muscles.
C.) Carbon dioxide is transported as bicarbonate ions.
D.) Hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen decreases with decreasing pH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Bohr effect states that a decrease in blood pH (increase in acidity, usually due to increased CO2) causes hemoglobin to unload oxygen more readily, and vice versa.

### Q491. How does an increase in body temperature affect oxygen transport by hemoglobin?
A.) It increases hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen.
B.) It has no effect on oxygen affinity.
C.) It prevents oxygen from binding to hemoglobin.
D.) It decreases hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Increasing temperature weakens the bond between oxygen and hemoglobin, leading to increased oxygen release to the tissues, especially during exercise when muscle temperature rises.

### Q492. What is the color of deoxyhemoglobin?
A.) Bright red
B.) Green
C.) Yellow
D.) Dark red **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When oxyhemoglobin releases oxygen to the tissues, it becomes deoxyhemoglobin, which has a darker red or bluish tinge.

### Q493. In what form is the largest percentage of carbon dioxide transported in the blood?
A.) Dissolved in plasma.
B.) Bound to hemoglobin as carbaminohemoglobin.
C.) As carbonic acid.
D.) As bicarbonate ions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Approximately 70-72% of carbon dioxide is transported in the blood in the form of bicarbonate ions (HCO3-).

### Q494. Carbon dioxide moves from body tissues into the blood because:
A.) The partial pressure of CO2 is lower in the tissues than in the blood.
B.) Hemoglobin has a stronger affinity for CO2 in the blood.
C.) Oxygen is abundant in the tissues.
D.) The partial pressure of CO2 is higher in the tissues than in the blood. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Carbon dioxide diffuses from the tissues, where its partial pressure is high due to cellular metabolism, into the blood, where its partial pressure is lower.

### Q495. Which enzyme facilitates the conversion of carbon dioxide and water into carbonic acid in red blood cells?
A.) Amylase
B.) Pepsin
C.) Lipase
D.) Carbonic anhydrase **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Carbonic anhydrase is an enzyme found in red blood cells that rapidly catalyzes the reversible reaction between carbon dioxide and water to form carbonic acid.

### Q496. The transport of carbon dioxide as bicarbonate ions helps to:
A.) Increase the pH of the blood.
B.) Directly increase oxygen binding to hemoglobin.
C.) Reduce the partial pressure of oxygen.
D.) Maintain the acid-base balance (pH) of the blood. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The bicarbonate buffer system is a crucial mechanism for regulating blood pH by transporting CO2 in a soluble and reversible form.

### Q497. When carbon dioxide binds directly to hemoglobin, it forms:
A.) Oxyhemoglobin
B.) Deoxyhemoglobin
C.) Methemoglobin
D.) Carbaminohemoglobin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Carbon dioxide can bind directly to the amino groups of hemoglobin, forming carbaminohemoglobin.

### Q498. Which statement about carbon dioxide transport is TRUE?
A.) All CO2 is transported dissolved in plasma.
B.) CO2 only binds to hemoglobin when oxygen is absent.
C.) CO2 is transported primarily by white blood cells.
D.) The majority of CO2 is transported in the form of bicarbonate ions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — While CO2 is transported in multiple forms, the largest proportion (around 70-72%) is transported as bicarbonate ions.

### Q499. What is the effect of high CO2 levels on blood pH?
A.) Increases pH (becomes more alkaline).
B.) Has no effect on pH.
C.) Fluctuates pH unpredictably.
D.) Decreases pH (becomes more acidic). **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — High CO2 levels lead to increased formation of carbonic acid, which then dissociates, releasing hydrogen ions and thus decreasing blood pH, making it more acidic.

### Q500. Where does oxygen primarily unload from hemoglobin to enter the body cells?
A.) In the lungs.
B.) In the heart chambers.
C.) In the pulmonary arteries.
D.) In the systemic capillaries. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Oxygen unloads from hemoglobin and diffuses into the body cells at the systemic capillaries, where the partial pressure of oxygen is lower and the tissues require oxygen for metabolism.

### Q501. Which of the following conditions would shift the oxygen-hemoglobin dissociation curve to the right, favoring oxygen release?
A.) Decreased temperature
B.) Increased pH
C.) Increased partial pressure of O2
D.) Increased acidity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Increased acidity (lower pH), increased temperature, and increased CO2 all shift the curve to the right, indicating that hemoglobin has a lower affinity for oxygen and releases it more readily.

### Q502. Why does carbon dioxide move from the blood into the alveoli in the lungs?
A.) The partial pressure of CO2 is higher in the alveoli.
B.) Hemoglobin has a stronger affinity for CO2 in the lungs.
C.) Oxygen is actively transported into the blood.
D.) The partial pressure of CO2 is lower in the alveoli. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Carbon dioxide diffuses from the pulmonary capillaries, where its partial pressure is relatively high, into the alveoli, where its partial pressure is lower, facilitating its exhalation.

### Q503. When oxyhemoglobin reaches the tissues, what causes it to release oxygen?
A.) Higher partial pressure of oxygen in the tissues.
B.) Higher pH in the tissues.
C.) Lower temperature in the tissues.
D.) Lower partial pressure of oxygen in the tissues. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lower partial pressure of oxygen in metabolically active tissues creates a gradient that promotes the dissociation of oxygen from hemoglobin.

### Q504. Which form of carbon dioxide transport plays a significant role in acid-base buffering in the blood?
A.) Dissolved CO2
B.) Carbaminohemoglobin
C.) Carbonic acid directly
D.) Bicarbonate ions **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The conversion of CO2 to bicarbonate ions and their transport in the plasma is a key component of the blood's buffering system, helping to regulate pH.

### Q505. What is the primary function of hemoglobin in the blood?
A.) Transporting carbon dioxide only.
B.) Storing oxygen in muscles.
C.) Fighting infections.
D.) Transporting oxygen from the lungs to the tissues. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hemoglobin is the primary respiratory pigment in red blood cells responsible for binding and transporting oxygen from the lungs to the body's tissues.

### Q506. A hemoglobin molecule is capable of carrying how many molecules of oxygen (O2)?
A.) One
B.) Two
C.) Three
D.) Four **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Each hemoglobin molecule consists of four polypeptide chains, each with a heme group that can bind one O2 molecule, allowing it to carry up to four O2 molecules.

### Q507. Which of the following statements best describes myoglobin?
A.) It is the primary oxygen transport protein in blood.
B.) It has a lower binding affinity for oxygen compared to hemoglobin.
C.) It transports carbon dioxide.
D.) It is an oxygen-storing protein found in muscle cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Myoglobin is an oxygen-binding protein located in skeletal and cardiac muscle cells, primarily serving as an oxygen-storing protein.

### Q508. How many oxygen molecules can a single myoglobin molecule bind?
A.) Zero
B.) Two
C.) Four
D.) One **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Myoglobin is a monomer with a single polypeptide chain and one heme group, so it can bind only one oxygen molecule.

### Q509. Compared to hemoglobin, myoglobin has a:
A.) Lower binding affinity for oxygen.
B.) Similar binding affinity for oxygen.
C.) Variable binding affinity for oxygen.
D.) Higher binding affinity for oxygen. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Myoglobin has a significantly higher binding affinity for oxygen, enabling it to store oxygen efficiently in muscle tissues.

### Q510. An infection of the sinuses, often characterized by headache and facial pain, is known as:
A.) Pneumonia
B.) Otitis Media
C.) Tuberculosis
D.) Sinusitis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sinusitis is an inflammation or infection of the sinuses, often causing facial pressure and pain.

### Q511. Which of the following is an infection of the middle ear, commonly occurring as a complication of upper respiratory infections?
A.) Emphysema
B.) Bronchitis
C.) Lung cancer
D.) Otitis Media **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Otitis media is a middle ear infection often resulting from upper respiratory tract infections.

### Q512. A serious infection that inflames the air sacs in one or both lungs, which may fill with fluid or pus, is called:
A.) Asthma
B.) Emphysema
C.) Sinusitis
D.) Pneumonia **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pneumonia causes inflammation of the alveoli, which may fill with fluid or pus, affecting gas exchange.

### Q513. Pulmonary tuberculosis is a chronic bacterial infection primarily affecting the:
A.) Pharynx
B.) Larynx
C.) Nasal cavity
D.) Lungs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pulmonary tuberculosis is a bacterial lung infection caused by *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*, primarily affecting the lungs.

### Q514. Which lung disorder is characterized by the destruction of alveolar walls, leading to enlarged air spaces and reduced surface area for gas exchange?
A.) Asthma
B.) Bronchitis
C.) Pneumonia
D.) Emphysema **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Emphysema involves permanent damage to alveoli, reducing surface area and impairing respiratory efficiency.

### Q515. Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease (COPD) commonly includes which two conditions?
A.) Sinusitis and Otitis Media
B.) Pneumonia and Tuberculosis
C.) Asthma and allergies
D.) Chronic bronchitis and emphysema **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — COPD is primarily made up of two chronic conditions: chronic bronchitis and emphysema.

### Q516. The leading cause of lung cancer is:
A.) Air pollution
B.) Genetic predisposition
C.) Viral infections
D.) Smoking **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Smoking is the primary cause of lung cancer, responsible for the majority of cases globally.

### Q517. What is a primary effect of smoking on the respiratory system?
A.) It increases the number of cilia.
B.) It enhances lung elasticity.
C.) It increases oxygen absorption capacity.
D.) It paralyzes and destroys cilia. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Smoking damages and destroys the cilia that normally help clear mucus, leading to respiratory congestion and infections.

### Q518. In mouth-to-mouth artificial respiration (CPR), forcing air into the victim's lungs is done to achieve:
A.) Active exhalation
B.) Passive inhalation
C.) Direct gas exchange in the rescuer's mouth
D.) Passive exhalation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The rescuer forces air in (active inhalation), then allows air to leave passively (passive exhalation).

### Q519. Before performing mouth-to-mouth artificial respiration, what is a crucial initial step to ensure effective ventilation?
A.) Administering chest compressions immediately.
B.) Checking the victim's pulse.
C.) Applying pressure to the sternum.
D.) Tilting the head back and lifting the chin to open the airway. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Tilting the head and lifting the chin helps open the airway by moving the tongue away from the back of the throat.

## Reproduction

### Q520. What is the primary significance of reproduction for a species?
A.) Ensures individual survival.
B.) Enables growth and development.
C.) Maintains homeostasis within the organism.
D.) Prevents the species from ceasing to exist. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Reproduction is not essential for the survival of an individual, but it is required for the survival of the species. Without it, the species would die out if all current members perished.

### Q521. Which of the following is a unique characteristic of the human reproductive system compared to other body systems?
A.) It is functional at birth.
B.) It maintains constant functionality throughout life.
C.) It exhibits minimal differences between males and females.
D.) It becomes functional at puberty. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The reproductive system is unique because it does not become functional until it is "turned on" at puberty by the action of sex hormones, unlike other body systems that are functional at or shortly after birth.

### Q522. In terms of structural differences, how does the reproductive system compare to other organ systems in males and females?
A.) All organ systems show significant differences between sexes.
B.) Only the digestive system shows major differences between sexes.
C.) All organ systems are largely identical in males and females.
D.) The reproductive system is quite different, while others show slight differences. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The reproductive system is quite different in male and female, whereas other organ systems of the body exhibit only slight differences.

### Q523. A single cell undergoes meiosis. How many daughter cells are produced, and what is their ploidy level?
A.) Two diploid cells.
B.) Four diploid cells.
C.) Two haploid cells.
D.) Four haploid cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Meiosis is a process where a single cell divides twice to produce four cells containing half the original amount of genetic information, making them haploid.

### Q524. Which of the following events characterizes Prophase I of meiosis?
A.) Sister chromatids separate.
B.) Chromosome pairs line up at the equator.
C.) DNA is copied.
D.) Homologous chromosomes pair up and may exchange DNA. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In Prophase I, copied chromosomes condense and pair up (homologous chromosomes), and they may exchange bits of DNA in a process called recombination or crossing over.

### Q525. During which stage of meiosis do homologous chromosome pairs align at the center of the cell?
A.) Prophase I
B.) Anaphase I
C.) Telophase I
D.) Metaphase I **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In Metaphase I, the chromosome pairs line up next to each other along the center (equator) of the cell.

### Q526. What crucial event distinguishes Anaphase I of meiosis from Anaphase of mitosis?
A.) Sister chromatids separate.
B.) Chromosomes condense.
C.) Nuclear membrane reforms.
D.) Homologous chromosomes are pulled apart. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In Anaphase I, the pairs of chromosomes are pulled apart by the meiotic spindle, pulling one chromosome to each pole. In meiosis I, the sister chromatids stay together, which is different from mitosis.

### Q527. What is the final outcome of meiosis in males?
A.) One egg cell and three polar bodies.
B.) Four genetically identical sperm cells.
C.) Two diploid spermatids.
D.) Four haploid sperm cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In males, once cytokinesis is complete after meiosis, there are four granddaughter cells, each with half a set of chromosomes (haploid), and all four cells are sperm cells.

### Q528. Which hormone, released from the hypothalamus, controls the release of anterior pituitary gonadotropins?
A.) FSH
B.) LH
C.) Testosterone
D.) GnRH **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The hypothalamus releases gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH), which controls the release of the anterior pituitary gonadotropins, follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) and luteinizing hormone (LH).

### Q529. What is the primary role of Follicle-Stimulating Hormone (FSH) in male reproduction?
A.) Stimulates Leydig cells to release testosterone.
B.) Inhibits sperm production.
C.) Causes growth of germinal epithelium.
D.) Stimulates Sertoli cells to complete sperm development. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — FSH stimulates spermatogenesis by stimulating the Sertoli cells to complete the development of sperms from spermatids.

### Q530. Luteinizing Hormone (LH) in males primarily stimulates which cells to release testosterone?
A.) Sertoli cells
B.) Germinal epithelial cells
C.) Spermatogonia
D.) Leydig cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — LH stimulates Leydig cells (interstitial cells) to release testosterone.

### Q531. Which hormone, produced by Sertoli cells, helps to control the rate of spermatogenesis?
A.) Testosterone
B.) LH
C.) GnRH
D.) Inhibin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Inhibin hormone is produced by the Sertoli cells and serves to control the spermatogenesis at a normal rate.

### Q532. How does testosterone primarily influence LH and FSH production in the anterior pituitary?
A.) It stimulates their production.
B.) It has no effect on their production.
C.) It directly releases them.
D.) It inhibits their production. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Testosterone inhibits LH and FSH production in the anterior pituitary, functioning as a negative feedback mechanism.

### Q533. The female reproductive cycle is primarily divided into which two phases?
A.) Ovulation and Menstruation.
B.) Follicular phase and Luteal phase.
C.) Proliferative phase and Secretory phase.
D.) Ovarian cycle and Uterine cycle. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The female reproductive cycle is primarily divided into two phases: the ovarian cycle (events in ovaries) and the uterine cycle (events in uterus).

### Q534. What is the name of the process where the ripening follicle ejects its oocyte from the ovary each month?
A.) Oogenesis
B.) Fertilization
C.) Menstruation
D.) Ovulation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In adult women, one of the ripening follicles ejects its oocyte from the ovary each month. This event is called ovulation.

### Q535. What structure is formed from the ruptured follicle after ovulation?
A.) Primordial follicle
B.) Mature follicle
C.) Secondary oocyte
D.) Corpus luteum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — After ovulation, the ruptured follicle is transformed into a glandular structure called the corpus luteum.

### Q536. At what stage is the secondary oocyte arrested before ovulation?
A.) Prophase I
B.) Metaphase I
C.) Prophase II
D.) Metaphase II **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In humans, the secondary oocyte arrests in metaphase II, and it is this cell that is ovulated.

### Q537. What happens to an ovulated secondary oocyte if it is not penetrated by sperm?
A.) It completes meiosis II and becomes an ovum.
B.) It is implanted into the uterus.
C.) It transforms into a corpus luteum.
D.) It simply deteriorates. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — If an ovulated secondary oocyte is not penetrated by sperm, it simply deteriorates.

### Q538. What is the primary function of the male reproductive system?
A.) Produce and maintain female gametes.
B.) Regulate body temperature.
C.) Produce digestive enzymes.
D.) Produce and maintain sperms. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The male reproductive system's main function is to produce and maintain sperms.

### Q539. Which of the following is NOT an accessory duct of the male reproductive system?
A.) Epididymis
B.) Rete testis
C.) Vas deferens
D.) Seminiferous tubule **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Seminiferous tubules are part of the testes (gonads), not accessory ducts. Accessory ducts include rete testis, efferent ductules, epididymis, vas deferens, ejaculatory duct, and urethra.

### Q540. Why are the testes located outside the abdominal cavity in the scrotum?
A.) To protect them from injury.
B.) To facilitate blood circulation.
C.) To allow for easier sperm release.
D.) To maintain a lower temperature. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The testes are located outside the abdominal cavity in the scrotum to maintain a temperature of about 35°C, which is optimum for spermatogenesis.

### Q541. Where does spermatogenesis primarily take place within the testes?
A.) Epididymis
B.) Rete testis
C.) Ductus deferens
D.) Seminiferous tubules **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Spermatogenesis, the process of sperm production, occurs in the seminiferous tubules of the testes.

### Q542. Which structure serves as the site for sperm maturation and storage?
A.) Vas deferens
B.) Ejaculatory duct
C.) Urethra
D.) Epididymis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The epididymis is a highly coiled tube located on the outer surface of the testis, where sperms are stored and mature.

### Q543. What is the approximate daily production rate of sperms in a healthy adult male?
A.) 50 million
B.) 100 million
C.) 200 million
D.) 400 million **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Approximately 400 million sperms are produced daily in a healthy adult male.

### Q544. Which accessory gland contributes to semen by forming the ejaculatory duct with the ductus deferens?
A.) Prostate gland
B.) Bulbourethral gland
C.) Cowper's gland
D.) Seminal vesicle **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The seminal vesicle is located at the junction of the sperm duct and ejaculatory duct, and its duct joins the ductus deferens to form the ejaculatory duct.

### Q545. Which gland encircles the urethra just inferior to the bladder in males?
A.) Seminal vesicle
B.) Bulbourethral gland
C.) Epididymis
D.) Prostate gland **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The prostate gland is a single gland that encircles the urethra just inferior to the bladder.

### Q546. What is the role of Sertoli cells in spermatogenesis?
A.) Produce testosterone.
B.) Store mature sperms.
C.) Initiate meiosis I.
D.) Nourish the spermatids. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sertoli cells provide nourishment to the developing spermatids as they differentiate into mature sperms.

### Q547. What is the typical volume of semen ejaculated during intercourse?
A.) 0.5 - 1 ml
B.) 1 - 2 ml
C.) 5 - 10 ml
D.) 2 - 5 ml **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In a normal ejaculation, the volume of semen is 2-5 ml.

### Q548. Which part of the male reproductive system is also known as the urinogenital duct?
A.) Vas deferens
B.) Ejaculatory duct
C.) Epididymis
D.) Urethra **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The urethra in males is also called the urinogenital duct because it carries both urine and semen.

### Q549. The copulatory organ of the male reproductive system is the:
A.) Scrotum
B.) Testis
C.) Epididymis
D.) Penis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The penis is the copulatory organ of the male, designed to deliver sperm into the female reproductive tract.

### Q550. Which of the following is a component of semen besides sperms?
A.) Blood plasma
B.) Gastric juice
C.) Lymph fluid
D.) Secretions from accessory glands **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Semen is a whitish, sticky mixture of sperms and the secretions of the accessory glands.

### Q551. What process involves the elongation, shedding of excess cytoplasm, and formation of a tail in spermatids?
A.) Spermatogenesis
B.) Spermiation
C.) Meiosis
D.) Spermiogenesis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Spermiogenesis is the process where spermatids undergo transformation by elongating, shedding their excess cytoplasm, and forming a tail to become mature sperms.

### Q552. What is the correct sequence of sperm passage through the accessory ducts after leaving the seminiferous tubules?
A.) Epididymis -> Rete testis -> Vas deferens -> Urethra
B.) Vas deferens -> Epididymis -> Rete testis -> Urethra
C.) Efferent ductules -> Vas deferens -> Rete testis -> Epididymis
D.) Rete testis -> Efferent ductules -> Epididymis -> Vas deferens **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — From the seminiferous tubules, sperms pass into the rete testis, then the efferent ductules, which lead into the epididymis, and then the ductus deferens (vas deferens).

### Q553. Which of the following is NOT a main component of the female reproductive system?
A.) Ovaries
B.) Oviducts
C.) Uterus
D.) Prostate gland **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The prostate gland is a component of the male reproductive system, not the female. The main components of the female reproductive system are ovaries, oviducts, uterus, and vagina.

### Q554. What is the primary function of the ovaries?
A.) To transport the ovum to the uterus.
B.) To serve as the site of fertilization.
C.) To receive and nourish a fertilized ovum.
D.) To produce ova and release hormones. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The ovaries are the primary female gonads that produce ova (eggs) and also produce hormones such as estrogen and progesterone.

### Q555. What is contained within each ovarian follicle?
A.) A mature ovum
B.) A corpus luteum
C.) Sperms
D.) An immature egg **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Each of the tiny saclike structures in the ovary, called an ovarian follicle, consists of an immature egg (oocyte) encased by one or more layers of follicle cells.

### Q556. Where does fertilization typically occur in the female reproductive system?
A.) Uterus
B.) Vagina
C.) Ovary
D.) Oviducts **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The oviducts, also known as Fallopian tubes or uterine tubes, provide a site where fertilization occurs.

### Q557. Which part of the uterus is the narrow opening leading into the vagina?
A.) Fundus
B.) Body
C.) Endometrium
D.) Cervix **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cervix is the narrow, neck-like portion of the uterus that projects into the vagina.

### Q558. What is the outermost layer of the uterine wall?
A.) Myometrium
B.) Endometrium
C.) Epithelium
D.) Perimetrium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The uterine wall is composed of three layers: the perimetrium (outermost serous layer), the myometrium (middle layer), and the endometrium (inner layer).

### Q559. Which layer of the uterus is responsible for rhythmic contractions during childbirth?
A.) Perimetrium
B.) Endometrium
C.) Cervix
D.) Myometrium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The myometrium is the thick middle layer of smooth muscle that plays an active role in the delivery of a baby by its rhythmic contractions.

### Q560. What is the primary function of the endometrium?
A.) To produce hormones.
B.) To transport the ovum.
C.) To prevent sperm entry.
D.) To serve as the site of embryo implantation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The endometrium is the inner spongy lining of the uterus where the embryo burrows (implants) and resides for the rest of its development.

### Q561. What is another name for the vagina?
A.) Uterine tube
B.) Womb
C.) Ovarian tube
D.) Birth canal **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vagina is also called the birth canal because it provides a passageway for the delivery of the fetus.

### Q562. At what stage is the primary oocyte arrested during oogenesis until puberty?
A.) Metaphase I
B.) Anaphase II
C.) Telophase II
D.) Prophase I **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — By birth, all the oogonia that are destined to become oocytes have transformed into primary oocytes and have entered into the prophase I of meiosis, where they remain arrested until puberty.

### Q563. What is the significance of the unequal cytoplasmic division during oogenesis?
A.) To produce four equally sized ova.
B.) To facilitate sperm penetration.
C.) To reduce the chromosome number equally.
D.) To ensure the fertilized egg has ample nutrients. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The unequal cytoplasmic divisions that occur during oogenesis ensure that a fertilized egg has ample nutrients for its six- to seven-day journey to the uterus.

### Q564. When does a secondary oocyte complete meiosis II?
A.) Before ovulation.
B.) At birth.
C.) During puberty.
D.) Only if penetrated by sperm. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The secondary oocyte does not complete meiosis II until it is penetrated by a sperm.

### Q565. What happens to the first and second polar bodies produced during oogenesis?
A.) They develop into ova.
B.) They participate in fertilization.
C.) They fuse to form a new cell.
D.) They degenerate and die. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Without nutrient-containing cytoplasm, the polar bodies degenerate and die.

### Q566. Which of the following is NOT a main responsibility of the female reproductive system?
A.) Produce female gametes.
B.) Nurture a developing embryo.
C.) Provide a site for fertilization.
D.) Produce seminal fluid. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Producing seminal fluid is a function of the male reproductive system. The female reproductive system produces gametes, nurtures the embryo, and provides a site for fertilization.

## Inheritance

### Q567. Who is famously known as the 'Father of Genetics' for his foundational work on heredity?
A.) Charles Darwin
B.) James Watson
C.) Francis Crick
D.) Gregor Mendel **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Gregor Mendel conducted pioneering experiments with pea plants that laid the groundwork for modern genetics, hence earning him the title 'Father of Genetics'.

### Q568. The study of heredity and variation of inherited characteristics is called:
A.) Ecology
B.) Evolution
C.) Taxonomy
D.) Genetics **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Genetics is the branch of biology concerned with the study of genes, genetic variation, and heredity in organisms.

### Q569. A specific position on a chromosome occupied by a gene is called a:
A.) Allele
B.) Trait
C.) Character
D.) Locus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A locus is the specific physical location or position of a gene on a chromosome.

### Q570. Different forms of the same gene are known as:
A.) Traits
B.) Chromosomes
C.) Loci
D.) Alleles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Alleles are alternative forms of a gene that are found at the same place on a chromosome.

### Q571. In a monohybrid cross, if a purebred tall pea plant (TT) is crossed with a purebred short pea plant (tt), what will be the genotype of the F1 generation?
A.) TT
B.) tt
C.) A mix of TT and tt
D.) Tt **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — All offspring in the F1 generation will be heterozygous (Tt) because 'T' is dominant over 't'.

### Q572. What is the phenotypic ratio observed in the F2 generation of a monohybrid cross involving complete dominance?
A.) 1:2:1
B.) 9:3:3:1
C.) 1:1
D.) 3:1 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — For a monohybrid cross with COMPLETE DOMINANCE, the F2 phenotypic ratio is typically 3 dominant : 1 recessive.

### Q573. Which of the following terms describes an individual with two identical alleles for a particular gene?
A.) Heterozygous
B.) Hybrid
C.) Dominant
D.) Homozygous **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Homozygous refers to an individual having two identical alleles for a specific gene (e.g., TT or tt).

### Q574. The observable physical characteristics of an organism, determined by its genetic makeup, are called its:
A.) Genotype
B.) Allele
C.) Chromosome
D.) Phenotype **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Phenotype refers to the observable characteristics of an organism, resulting from gene expression and environmental factors.

### Q575. If the genotype of an organism is 'Aa', it is considered to be:
A.) Homozygous dominant
B.) Homozygous recessive
C.) Purebred
D.) Heterozygous **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An individual with two different alleles (Aa) is described as heterozygous.

### Q576. The Law of Segregation states that:
A.) Alleles for different traits assort independently.
B.) Dominant alleles always mask recessive alleles.
C.) Genes located on the same chromosome are always inherited together.
D.) Each individual has two alleles for a trait, which separate during gamete formation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Mendel's Law of Segregation states that during gamete formation, the two alleles for a heritable trait separate so each gamete gets only one allele.

### Q577. A recessive allele expresses its trait:
A.) Only if present with a dominant allele.
B.) Only if in a heterozygous condition.
C.) Either in heterozygous or homozygous form.
D.) Only if in a homozygous condition. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A recessive allele only shows its effect when an organism has two copies of that allele, i.e., is homozygous recessive.

### Q578. What is the genotypic ratio observed in the F2 generation of a monohybrid cross involving complete dominance?
A.) 3:1
B.) 9:3:3:1
C.) 1:1
D.) 1:2:1 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The genotypic ratio is 1 homozygous dominant : 2 heterozygous : 1 homozygous recessive.

### Q579. If a heterozygous tall pea plant (Tt) is self-pollinated, what percentage of the offspring will be short (tt)?
A.) 100%
B.) 75%
C.) 50%
D.) 25% **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The offspring will have a 1:2:1 genotypic ratio, so 1 out of 4 (25%) will be short (tt).

### Q580. In a dihybrid cross, how many different phenotypic combinations are expected in the F2 generation under independent assortment?
A.) 2
B.) 3
C.) 9
D.) 4 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A dihybrid cross produces four distinct phenotypic combinations in the F2 generation in the ratio 9:3:3:1.

### Q581. The phenotypic ratio of 9:3:3:1 is characteristic of which type of cross?
A.) Monohybrid cross
B.) Test cross
C.) Back cross
D.) Dihybrid cross **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The 9:3:3:1 phenotypic ratio is seen in the F2 generation of a dihybrid cross between two heterozygous individuals.

### Q582. Mendel's Law of Independent Assortment states that:
A.) Alleles for the same trait segregate independently.
B.) Dominant alleles are always expressed.
C.) Traits are always inherited together.
D.) Genes located on different chromosomes assort independently during gamete formation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This law explains how genes located on different chromosomes are inherited independently of one another.

### Q583. Which of the following is a limitation of the Law of Independent Assortment?
A.) It only applies to monohybrid crosses.
B.) It does not account for dominant alleles.
C.) It only applies to purebred organisms.
D.) It does not apply to linked genes. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Genes that are located close together on the same chromosome (linked genes) do not assort independently.

### Q584. Dihybrid crosses demonstrate that the inheritance of one trait:
A.) Always influences the inheritance of another trait.
B.) Is completely dependent on the inheritance of another trait.
C.) Is only possible if traits are recessive.
D.) Does not affect the inheritance of another trait, if genes are unlinked. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Dihybrid crosses show that traits controlled by different genes on separate chromosomes assort independently.

### Q585. The usefulness of the Law of Independent Assortment lies in explaining:
A.) Why all offspring are identical.
B.) How recessive traits disappear from a population.
C.) The process of natural selection.
D.) The production of new combinations of traits in offspring. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Independent assortment contributes to genetic diversity by producing new combinations of alleles in offspring.

### Q586. In a cross between two pea plants heterozygous for seed shape (Rr) and seed color (Yy), how many unique gametes can each parent produce?
A.) 1
B.) 2
C.) 3
D.) 4 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Each RrYy parent can produce 4 unique gametes: RY, Ry, rY, and ry due to independent assortment.

### Q587. What is the phenotype ratio in the F2 generation of a dihybrid cross?
A.) 1:1:1:1
B.) 3:1
C.) 4:3
D.) 9:3:3:1 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — 9 both dominant : 3 first dominant, second recessive : 3 first recessive, second dominant : 1 both recessive.

### Q588. If a parent has the genotype AABb, what are the possible gametes it can produce?
A.) AA, Bb
B.) A, B
C.) AABB, AAbb
D.) AB, Ab **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The parent can produce AB and Ab gametes since AA only contributes A, and Bb contributes either B or b.

### Q589. The scope of independent assortment in variation refers to its role in:
A.) Reducing genetic diversity.
B.) Increasing the number of identical offspring.
C.) Ensuring all offspring are purebred.
D.) Creating new combinations of alleles on different chromosomes. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Independent assortment increases genetic variation by mixing alleles of genes on different chromosomes.

### Q590. What is the probability of a coin landing on heads twice in a row?
A.) 1/2
B.) 1/8
C.) 1
D.) 1/4 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The probability of heads in one toss is 1/2. For two tosses: 1/2 × 1/2 = 1/4.

### Q591. In a cross between two individuals, Aa x Aa, what is the probability of producing an 'AA' offspring?
A.) 1/2
B.) 3/4
C.) 1
D.) 1/4 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — From the Punnett square: AA, Aa, Aa, aa → 1 out of 4 chance for AA, which is 1/4.

### Q592. If the probability of inheriting allele 'A' is 1/2 and the probability of inheriting allele 'B' is 1/2, what is the probability of inheriting both 'A' and 'B' (assuming independent assortment)?
A.) 1/2
B.) 1/8
C.) 1
D.) 1/4 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Using the product rule: 1/2 × 1/2 = 1/4 chance of inheriting both A and B.

### Q593. A pedigree chart is a diagram that shows:
A.) The dietary habits of a family.
B.) The migratory patterns of a species.
C.) The evolutionary timeline of an organism.
D.) The inheritance of traits across generations in a family. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A pedigree is used to track the inheritance of traits across generations in a family.

### Q594. If a couple has three children, what is the probability that all three will be girls?
A.) 1/2
B.) 1/4
C.) 1/6
D.) 1/8 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Each child has a 1/2 chance of being a girl. For three girls: 1/2 × 1/2 × 1/2 = 1/8.

### Q595. In incomplete dominance, the heterozygous genotype results in a phenotype that is:
A.) Identical to one of the homozygous parents.
B.) A combination of both parental phenotypes appearing distinctly.
C.) Completely new and unrelated to either parent.
D.) Identical to neither homozygous parent, but an intermediate blend. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In incomplete dominance, neither allele is completely dominant over the other, leading to a heterozygous phenotype that is an intermediate blend.

### Q596. Which of the following is a classic example of incomplete dominance?
A.) Human ABO blood groups
B.) Human MN blood groups
C.) Pea plant height
D.) Flower color in 4 O'clock plants **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Flower color in 4 O'clock plants (Mirabilis jalapa) shows incomplete dominance, producing pink flowers from red and white parents.

### Q597. If a red-flowered 4 O'clock plant (RR) is crossed with a white-flowered 4 O'clock plant (WW), what is the phenotype of the F1 generation?
A.) All red flowers
B.) All white flowers
C.) A mix of red and white flowers
D.) All pink flowers **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Crossing RR and WW in incomplete dominance results in all heterozygous RW offspring, which are pink.

### Q598. What is the genotypic ratio expected in the F2 generation of a cross between two pink-flowered 4 O'clock plants?
A.) 3:1
B.) 9:3:3:1
C.) 1:1
D.) 1:2:1 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RW x RW gives genotypes: 1 RR (red), 2 RW (pink), 1 WW (white) — a 1:2:1 ratio.

### Q599. In incomplete dominance, the phenotypic ratio of the F2 generation for a monohybrid cross is typically:
A.) 3:1
B.) 9:3:3:1
C.) 1:1
D.) 1:2:1 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In incomplete dominance, phenotypes match genotypes in a 1:2:1 ratio.

### Q600. A plant with pink flowers is crossed with a white-flowered plant. If pink is heterozygous (RW) and white is homozygous (WW), what percentage of the offspring will have white flowers?
A.) 0%
B.) 25%
C.) 100%
D.) 50% **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RW x WW → 50% RW (pink), 50% WW (white). So, 50% white flowers.

### Q601. In incomplete dominance, if a character has alleles R (red) and W (white), an individual with genotype RW would exhibit which phenotype?
A.) Red
B.) White
C.) Red and white patches
D.) Pink **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RW shows an intermediate phenotype — pink — in incomplete dominance.

### Q602. Co-dominance is characterized by:
A.) The blending of parental phenotypes in heterozygotes.
B.) The complete masking of one allele by another.
C.) The expression of a trait only in homozygous recessive individuals.
D.) The simultaneous and distinct expression of both alleles in the heterozygote. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In co-dominance, both alleles are fully and distinctly expressed in the heterozygote.

### Q603. Which of the following human blood group systems is a classic example of co-dominance?
A.) Rh blood group
B.) MN blood group
C.) ABO blood group
D.) Both MN and ABO blood groups **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Both MN and ABO (specifically A & B alleles) are co-dominant systems.

### Q604. In the human MN blood group system, an individual with the genotype LMLN will express:
A.) Only M antigen
B.) Only N antigen
C.) Neither M nor N antigens
D.) Both M and N antigens **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — LMLN genotype expresses both M and N antigens due to co-dominance.

### Q605. What is a key difference between incomplete dominance and co-dominance?
A.) In incomplete dominance, only one allele is expressed in phenotype; in co-dominance, both phenotypes are distinctly expressed.
B.) In incomplete dominance, dominant alleles are stronger; in co-dominance, they are weaker.
C.) Incomplete dominance involves multiple genes; co-dominance involves only one.
D.) In incomplete dominance, a blend of phenotypes occurs; in co-dominance, both phenotypes are distinctly expressed. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Incomplete dominance results in blended phenotype; co-dominance results in both traits showing distinctly.

### Q606. If two individuals, both with MN blood type, have children, what are the possible genotypes of their offspring?
A.) Only LMLN
B.) Only LMLM and LNLN
C.) Only LMLM and LMLN
D.) LMLM, LMLN, and LNLN **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — LMLN × LMLN yields LMLM, LMLN, and LNLN — classic 1:2:1 ratio.

### Q607. Which genetic interaction results in a third, distinct phenotype that is not a blend, but shows traits of both alleles?
A.) Complete dominance
B.) Incomplete dominance
C.) Epistasis
D.) Co-dominance **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Co-dominance results in both traits being fully and distinctly expressed.

### Q608. The presence of more than two alleles for a gene in a population is known as:
A.) Incomplete dominance
B.) Co-dominance
C.) Polygenic inheritance
D.) Multiple alleles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Multiple alleles means more than two allele options exist in the gene pool, though each individual inherits only two.

### Q609. The best-known example of multiple alleles in humans is the inheritance of:
A.) Eye color
B.) Height
C.) Sex-linked traits
D.) ABO blood groups **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ABO blood grouping is a classic example of multiple alleles (I<sup>A</sup>, I<sup>B</sup>, i).

### Q610. How many alleles are responsible for determining the ABO blood group in humans at the population level?
A.) 1
B.) 2
C.) 4
D.) 3 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — There are three common alleles — I<sup>A</sup>, I<sup>B</sup>, and i — for ABO blood group determination.

### Q611. An individual with blood type A can have which of the following genotypes?
A.) I B I B or I B i
B.) I A I B
C.) ii
D.) I A I A or I A i **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Blood type A can be either I<sup>A</sup>I<sup>A</sup> (homozygous) or I<sup>A</sup>i (heterozygous).

### Q612. A person with AB blood type has the genotype:
A.) I A I A
B.) I B I B
C.) ii
D.) I A I B **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — AB blood type results from co-dominant alleles I<sup>A</sup> and I<sup>B</sup> being present together.

### Q613. If a child has O blood type (genotype ii), and the mother has A blood type (genotype I<sup>A</sup>i), what must be the father's blood type genotype?
A.) I A I A
B.) I B I B
C.) ii
D.) It must contain at least one 'i' allele (e.g., I A i, I B i, or ii). **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — To produce an 'ii' child, both parents must pass on an 'i'. So the father must carry at least one 'i'.

### Q614. Which allele in the ABO blood group system is recessive to both I<sup>A</sup> and I<sup>B</sup> alleles?
A.) I A
B.) I B
C.) All are co-dominant
D.) i **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The 'i' allele is recessive and only expresses the O phenotype when paired as ii.

### Q615. Which of the following antigens are present on the surface of red blood cells in an individual with B blood type?
A.) A antigens
B.) Both A and B antigens
C.) Neither A nor B antigens
D.) B antigens **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Individuals with blood type B have B antigens on the surface of their red blood cells.

### Q616. A person with AB blood type has which antibodies in their plasma?
A.) Anti-A antibodies
B.) Anti-B antibodies
C.) Both Anti-A and Anti-B antibodies
D.) Neither Anti-A nor Anti-B antibodies **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — AB individuals have both A and B antigens and therefore lack anti-A and anti-B antibodies to prevent agglutination.

### Q617. An individual with O blood type produces which of the following antibodies?
A.) Anti-A only
B.) Anti-B only
C.) Neither Anti-A nor Anti-B
D.) Both Anti-A and Anti-B **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — People with O blood type lack A and B antigens and produce both anti-A and anti-B antibodies.

### Q618. Why is O-negative blood considered the "universal donor"?
A.) It has both A and B antigens.
B.) It has A, B, and Rh antibodies.
C.) It only contains plasma.
D.) It lacks A, B, and Rh antigens. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — O-negative blood lacks A, B, and Rh antigens, minimizing the risk of agglutination in recipients.

### Q619. Why is AB-positive blood considered the "universal recipient"?
A.) It lacks all major antigens.
B.) It has a unique type of hemoglobin.
C.) It is very rare.
D.) It has no antibodies against A, B, or Rh antigens. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — AB-positive individuals can receive any ABO or Rh blood type as they do not have anti-A, anti-B, or anti-Rh antibodies.

### Q620. If a person with A blood type receives a transfusion of B blood type, what will likely happen?
A.) The blood will mix without issue.
B.) The donor's antibodies will attack the recipient's cells.
C.) The recipient's blood type will change to AB.
D.) The recipient's antibodies will attack donor's cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Anti-B antibodies in the recipient will attack B antigens, causing agglutination and a transfusion reaction.

### Q621. Which of the following genotypes represents a person with B blood type?
A.) I A I A
B.) I A I B
C.) ii
D.) I B I B or I B i **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — B blood type results from either I B I B (homozygous) or I B i (heterozygous) genotypes.

### Q622. A couple has blood types A and B. Can they have a child with O blood type?
A.) No, never.
B.) Yes, if the father is homozygous dominant.
C.) Only if father or mother is O.
D.) Yes, if both parents are heterozygous. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — If both A and B parents carry the recessive 'i' allele, their child can inherit ii and be type O.

### Q623. Which blood type lacks both A and B antigens on the surface of red blood cells?
A.) A
B.) B
C.) AB
D.) O **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Type O blood lacks both A and B surface antigens.

### Q624. The presence or absence of which antigen determines if a person's blood type is positive or negative within the Rh system?
A.) A antigen
B.) B antigen
C.) M antigen
D.) D antigen (Rh factor) **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Rh system is based on the D antigen. Its presence makes blood Rh-positive.

### Q625. An Rh-negative individual will develop anti-Rh antibodies if exposed to:
A.) Rh-negative blood
B.) Plasma
C.) White blood cells
D.) Rh-positive blood **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Exposure to Rh-positive blood causes the Rh-negative person to produce anti-Rh antibodies.

### Q626. What is the primary concern for a second Rh-positive fetus carried by an Rh-negative mother if no preventative measures are taken?
A.) The fetus will develop Rh-negative blood.
B.) The mother will become Rh-positive.
C.) The fetus will spontaneously abort due to ABO incompatibility.
D.) Maternal anti-Rh antibodies may cross the placenta and destroy fetal red blood cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — If the mother was sensitized in a previous pregnancy, anti-Rh antibodies can attack the second Rh-positive fetus's RBCs.

### Q627. Hemolytic Disease of the Newborn (Erythroblastosis Fetalis) is most likely to occur when:
A.) Rh-positive mother carries her second or subsequent Rh-negative fetus.
B.) Rh-negative mother carries her first Rh-positive fetus.
C.) Rh-positive mother receives Rh-negative blood.
D.) Rh-negative mother carries her second or subsequent Rh-positive fetus. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This condition arises in Rh-negative mothers with Rh-positive fetuses after sensitization in a prior pregnancy.

### Q628. A common preventative measure to avoid Erythroblastosis Fetalis is the administration of:
A.) Antibiotics to the mother.
B.) Vitamin K to the newborn.
C.) Blood transfusion to the mother during pregnancy.
D.) Rh immunoglobulin (RhoGAM) to the Rh-negative mother. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — RhoGAM prevents sensitization of the Rh-negative mother's immune system to Rh-positive fetal cells.

### Q629. Which of the following is NOT a typical symptom or consequence of severe Erythroblastosis Fetalis in a newborn?
A.) Anemia
B.) Jaundice
C.) Enlarged spleen and liver
D.) Increased red blood cell production **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Increased RBC production is a compensatory response, not a symptom. The condition is marked by anemia and jaundice.

### Q630. The Rh factor is an antigen found on the surface of:
A.) White blood cells
B.) Platelets
C.) Plasma proteins
D.) Red blood cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Rh factor is located on red blood cells and plays a major role in blood compatibility.

### Q631. If a father is Rh-positive (homozygous dominant RR) and the mother is Rh-negative (rr), what is the likelihood of their first child being Rh-positive?
A.) 0%
B.) 25%
C.) 50%
D.) 100% **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — All offspring will be heterozygous (Rr) and thus Rh-positive.

### Q632. Besides ABO and Rh, which other blood group system is an example of co-dominance?
A.) Duffy blood group
B.) Kidd blood group
C.) Kell blood group
D.) MN blood group **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The MN blood group is a classic example of co-dominance where both M and N antigens are expressed.

### Q633. Minor blood group systems, other than ABO and Rh, generally:
A.) Cause severe complications in all blood transfusions.
B.) Are only found in very rare individuals.
C.) Determine blood type compatibility more than ABO and Rh.
D.) Usually do not complicate routine blood transfusions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Minor blood group systems exist but typically do not interfere with standard blood transfusions.

### Q634. The fact that there are more than two hundred minor blood groups indicates:
A.) That only ABO and Rh are important.
B.) That blood transfusions are rarely safe.
C.) That blood types are constantly changing.
D.) A high level of genetic diversity in human blood characteristics. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The existence of over 200 minor blood groups highlights genetic diversity in blood typing.

### Q635. Which of the following best defines polygenic inheritance?
A.) A single gene affecting multiple traits.
B.) Genes located on sex chromosomes.
C.) Genes that blend their effects.
D.) Multiple genes affecting a single trait. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Polygenic inheritance refers to the inheritance of quantitative traits (e.g., height, skin color) controlled by two or more genes, each contributing additively.

### Q636. Human skin color is a classic example of a trait governed by:
A.) Monogenic inheritance
B.) Co-dominance
C.) Sex-linked inheritance
D.) Polygenic inheritance **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Human skin color shows continuous variation, characteristic of polygenic inheritance where multiple genes contribute.

### Q637. Which of the following is a characteristic of polygenic traits?
A.) They exhibit discrete phenotypic categories.
B.) They are always controlled by a single dominant allele.
C.) They show clear Mendelian ratios (e.g., 3:1).
D.) Their expression is often influenced by environmental factors. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Polygenic traits often show continuous variation and are influenced by environmental factors besides genetic contribution.

### Q638. If genes A, B, and C all contribute to the same trait additively, this is an example of:
A.) Epistasis
B.) Incomplete dominance
C.) Pleiotropy
D.) Polygenic inheritance **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Multiple genes contributing additively to a trait indicate polygenic inheritance.

### Q639. The inheritance of grain color in wheat, which can show a range of shades from white to dark red, is an example of:
A.) Mendelian inheritance
B.) Sex-linked inheritance
C.) Lethal alleles
D.) Polygenic inheritance **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Grain color in wheat shows continuous variation controlled by multiple genes, a hallmark of polygenic inheritance.

### Q640. What is epistasis?
A.) When one gene affects multiple, unrelated traits.
B.) When two alleles present at different locus are expressed simultaneously.
C.) When a gene has more than two alleles in a population.
D.) When one gene's expression is masked by another gene at a different locus. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Epistasis is when one gene masks or modifies the expression of another gene at a different locus.

### Q641. The coat color in Labrador retrievers, where the E/e gene determines pigment deposition and the B/b gene determines black/brown, is a classic example of:
A.) Incomplete dominance
B.) Co-dominance
C.) Polygenic inheritance
D.) Epistasis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In Labradors, the E/e gene is epistatic to B/b; ee genotype masks the B/b effect, resulting in yellow coat.

### Q642. In epistasis, the gene that masks or modifies the expression of another gene is called the:
A.) Hypostatic gene
B.) Recessive gene
C.) Modifier gene
D.) Epistatic gene **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The gene that masks/modifies another's expression in epistasis is the epistatic gene.

### Q643. A common modified Mendelian phenotypic ratio observed in recessive epistasis (e.g., in sweet peas for pigment) is:
A.) 9:3:3:1
B.) 12:3:1
C.) 1:2:1
D.) 9:3:4 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Recessive epistasis typically shows a 9:3:4 phenotypic ratio.

### Q644. In the pigment production in sweet peas, if a recessive genotype (cc) prevents the production of color regardless of the other gene's alleles (A/a), this is an example of:
A.) Dominant epistasis
B.) Complementary gene action
C.) Inhibitory gene action
D.) Recessive epistasis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Homozygous recessive cc masks the effect of gene A/a, an example of recessive epistasis.

### Q645. The exchange of genetic material between homologous chromosomes during meiosis is called:
A.) Independent assortment
B.) Segregation
C.) Linkage
D.) Crossing over **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Crossing over during meiosis causes exchange of chromosome segments, increasing genetic variation.

### Q646. How does crossing over affect genetic variation?
A.) It reduces genetic variation by keeping genes linked.
B.) It ensures that all offspring are identical to parents.
C.) It prevents mutation.
D.) It creates new combinations of alleles on chromosomes. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Crossing over breaks linkage and creates new allele combinations, increasing genetic diversity.

### Q647. If two genes are located very far apart on the same chromosome, their inheritance pattern will most closely resemble that of:
A.) Incomplete dominance.
B.) Complete linkage.
C.) Co-dominance.
D.) Independent assortment. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Genes far apart have high recombination frequency and behave like independently assorting genes.

### Q648. Which organism is famously used as a model to study gene linkage and crossing over, particularly concerning traits like wing length and eye color?
A.) Pisum sativum
B.) Homo sapiens
C.) Escherichia coli
D.) Drosophila melanogaster **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Drosophila melanogaster (Fruit fly) is a key genetic model for studying linkage and recombination.

### Q649. In the XX-XY system of sex determination, which chromosome carries the primary genes responsible for maleness?
A.) X chromosome
B.) Autosomes
C.) Both X and Y chromosomes
D.) Y chromosome **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Y chromosome carries the SRY gene responsible for male development.

### Q650. In the XX-XO system of sex determination, What is the genotype for males?
A.) XY
B.) YY
C.) XXY
D.) XO **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Males have one X chromosome and no second sex chromosome (XO).

### Q651. Which term refers to any chromosome that is not a sex chromosome?
A.) Allosome
B.) Holo chromosome
C.) Heterosome
D.) Autosome **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Autosomes are chromosomes not involved in sex determination.

### Q652. A trait that is inherited on a sex chromosome (usually the X chromosome) is called:
A.) Autosomal dominant
B.) Autosomal recessive
C.) Sex-limited
D.) Sex-linked **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sex-linked traits are genes located on sex chromosomes, often X-linked.

### Q653. Which gender is typically more affected by X-linked recessive disorders in humans?
A.) Females
B.) Both equally
C.) Neither gender is affected
D.) Males **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Males express X-linked recessive disorders because they have only one X chromosome.

### Q654. Color blindness in humans is an example of a(n):
A.) Autosomal dominant disorder
B.) Autosomal recessive disorder
C.) Y-linked disorder
D.) X-linked recessive disorder **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Red-green color blindness is an X-linked recessive disorder.

### Q655. Genes that are located only on the Y chromosome and are passed directly from father to son are called:
A.) X-linked genes
B.) Autosomal genes
C.) Sex-influenced genes
D.) Holandric genes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Holandric genes are Y-linked genes passed only from father to son.

### Q656. A trait that appears in both genders but is expressed differently or more frequently in one gender due to hormonal influence is:
A.) Sex-limited
B.) Sex-linked
C.) Holandric
D.) Sex-influenced **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sex-influenced traits are autosomal traits expressed differently based on sex hormones.

### Q657. A trait that is expressed only in one gender, despite being present in both genders' genotypes, is:
A.) Sex-linked
B.) Sex-influenced
C.) Co-dominant
D.) Sex-limited **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sex-limited traits express in only one sex even if gene is present in both.

### Q658. Haemophilia, a blood clotting disorder, is a well-known example of a(n):
A.) Autosomal dominant disorder
B.) Autosomal recessive disorder
C.) Y-linked disorder
D.) X-linked recessive disorder **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Haemophilia is an X-linked recessive disorder, mainly affecting males.

### Q659. If a carrier female for an X-linked recessive disorder (X A X a) has children with an unaffected male (X A Y), what is the probability that a son will be affected?
A.) 0%
B.) 25%
C.) 100%
D.) 50% **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A carrier mother has 50% chance of passing the affected X to a son, who will express the disorder.

### Q660. Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD) is an X-linked recessive disorder. If a mother is a carrier and the father is unaffected, what is the probability that their daughter will be a carrier?
A.) 0%
B.) 25%
C.) 100%
D.) 50% **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Daughters have 50% chance of inheriting the affected X and becoming carriers.

### Q661. In the ZZ-ZW system of sex determination (e.g., in birds), who is having ZW?
A.) Males
B.) Both males and females
C.) Neither one
D.) Females **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In ZZ-ZW system, females are heterogametic (ZW) while males are homogametic (ZZ).

### Q662. Which of the following is true regarding sex-linked traits?
A.) They are always found on the Y chromosome.
