B.) They are inherited independently of sex.
C.) They only affect reproductive organs.
D.) Their expression pattern can differ between males and females. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sex-linked traits often show different expression in males and females due to chromosomal differences.

### Q663. If a trait is controlled by a gene on an autosome, but its expression is only in males, it is an example of a:
A.) Sex-linked trait
B.) Sex-influenced trait
C.) Holandric trait
D.) Sex-limited trait **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sex-limited traits are expressed only in one sex despite being autosomal.

## Immunity

### Q664. The ability of the body to resist damage from foreign substances like microorganisms and harmful chemicals is defined as:
A.) Immune response
B.) Immunology
C.) Resistance
D.) Immunity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Immunity is specifically defined as the ability to resist damage from foreign substances such as microorganisms and harmful chemicals.

### Q665. The body's response to foreign molecules, characterized by the production of antibodies against a specific antigen, is known as:
A.) Immunity
B.) Immunology
C.) Immunization
D.) Immune response **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An immune response is the body's reaction to foreign molecules, including the production of antibodies against a specific antigen.

### Q666. The term "immune" is derived from:
A.) Greek word "immunos"
B.) Roman word "immunitas"
C.) French word "immunité"
D.) Latin word "immunis" **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Word immune is derived from The Latin word "immunis" that means "safe" or "free of burden."

### Q667. Which Greek historian observed that individuals who recovered from a disease often became resistant to subsequent infections of the same disease?
A.) Herodotus
B.) Edward Jenner
C.) Aristotle
D.) Thucydides **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thucydides, a Greek historian, observed this phenomenon more than 2000 years ago.

### Q668. The English country doctor who hypothesized that cowpox conferred protection against smallpox in 1796 was:
A.) Louis Pasteur
B.) Robert Koch
C.) Alexander Fleming
D.) Edward Jenner **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Edward Jenner hypothesized this protective effect of cowpox against smallpox in 1796.

### Q669. Which of the following is considered the first line of defense against microbial attack in the human body?
A.) Phagocytic cells
B.) Inflammation
C.) Specific immune response
D.) External barriers **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The first line of defense consists of external barriers that prevent microbes from entering the body.

### Q670. These two serve as the first line of defense:
A.) Skin and lymphatic system
B.) Mucous membranes and blood
C.) Digestive tract and respiratory tract
D.) Skin and mucous membranes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The skin and the mucous membranes of the digestive and respiratory tracts are the two main external barriers.

### Q671. The two primary layers that make up the human skin are:
A.) Hypodermis and dermis
B.) Epidermis and hypodermis
C.) Keratinocytes and melanocytes
D.) Dermis and epidermis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The skin is composed of two layers: the dermis and the epidermis.

### Q672. Which layer of the skin is described as dense, irregular connective tissue containing nerve endings, hair follicles, glands, and lymphatics?
A.) Epidermis
B.) Hypodermis
C.) Basement membrane
D.) Dermis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The dermis is characterized by dense, irregular connective tissue with various embedded structures.

### Q673. The most abundant cells in the epidermis, responsible for producing keratin, are called:
A.) Melanocytes
B.) Langerhan cells
C.) Keratin fibroblasts
D.) Keratinocytes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Keratinocytes are the primary cells of the epidermis and produce keratin.

### Q674. Which cells in the epidermis contribute to skin color?
A.) Keratinocytes
B.) Langerhan cells
C.) Adipocytes
D.) Melanocytes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Melanocytes are responsible for skin pigmentation.

### Q675. The sebaceous glands are located in:
A.) Epidermis
B.) Hypodermis
C.) Basement membrane
D.) Dermis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The sebaceous glands, located in the dermis, produce an oily, white substance rich in lipids called sebum, which oils the hair and skin surface.

### Q676. The secretions from sweat glands and sebaceous glands contribute to the skin's defense by containing:
A.) Enzymes that digest bacteria
B.) Pyrogens that raise temperature
C.) Antibodies that neutralize toxins
D.) Acids and natural antibiotics **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — These secretions, such as lactic acid, inhibit the growth of bacteria and fungi.

### Q677. In the stomach, hydrochloric acid is secreted by which cells to kill microorganisms?
A.) Zymogen cells
B.) Principal cells
C.) Goblet cells
D.) Oxyntic cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Oxyntic or parietal cells in the stomach are responsible for secreting hydrochloric acid.

### Q678. What is the primary function of the hairs lining the vestibule of the nasal cavity?
A.) To humidify the air
B.) To warm the air
C.) To detect odors
D.) To trap dust particles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hairs in the nasal vestibule serve to trap large particles of dust from the inhaled air.

### Q679. The mucous membrane lining the nasal cavity consists of pseudostratified ciliated columnar epithelium with what type of cells that secrete mucus?
A.) Parietal cells
B.) Zymogen cells
C.) Keratinocytes
D.) Goblet cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Goblet cells in the nasal mucous membrane secrete mucus to trap debris.

### Q680. How are trapped debris and mucus swept posteriorly from the nasal cavity to the pharynx for elimination?
A.) By muscular contractions
B.) By gravitational force
C.) By coughing reflexes
D.) By ciliary action **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cilia on the surface of the mucous membrane sweep mucus and trapped debris towards the pharynx.

### Q681. What is the approximate maximum diameter of particles that typically do NOT enter the lungs through the nose due to the nasal turbulence mechanism?
A.) 1 micrometer
B.) 3 micrometers
C.) 10 micrometers
D.) 6 micrometers **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nasal turbulence mechanism is highly effective, preventing particles larger than 6 micrometers from entering the lungs via the nose.

### Q682. Particles between 1 and 5 micrometers in diameter that manage to bypass the nasal cavity often settle out in the small bronchioles due to:
A.) Air currents
B.) Ciliary action
C.) Mucus trapping
D.) Gravitational precipitation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Smaller particles settle in the bronchioles due to gravity.

### Q683. What happens to particles smaller than 0.5 micrometers that remain suspended in alveolar air?
A.) They are absorbed into the bloodstream.
B.) They are broken down by enzymes.
C.) They trigger an inflammatory response.
D.) They are later expelled by expiration. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Very small particles are expelled from the lungs during exhalation.

### Q684. Which type of white blood cell is characterized by its large size and ability to leave the bloodstream to become macrophages in tissues?
A.) Neutrophils
B.) Lymphocytes
C.) Eosinophils
D.) Monocytes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Monocytes are granular leukocytes that develop into macrophages once they exit the bone marrow and enter tissues.

### Q685. Monocytes originate in the:
A.) Lymph nodes
B.) Spleen
C.) Thymus
D.) Bone marrow **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Monocytes are formed in the bone marrow before circulating in the bloodstream.

### Q686. Once in tissues, monocytes differentiate into:
A.) Neutrophils
B.) Basophils
C.) Plasma cells
D.) Macrophages **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Monocytes mature into macrophages when they migrate from the blood into various tissues.

### Q687. Macrophages are essential for defense due to their ability to:
A.) Produce antibodies
B.) Secrete histamines
C.) Release interferons
D.) Engulf foreign particles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Macrophages are highly phagocytic cells, meaning they engulf and digest cellular debris, foreign substances, and microbes.

### Q688. After engulfing particles, what do macrophages typically do with the residual products?
A.) Store them internally
B.) Convert them into nutrients
C.) Transport them to lymph nodes
D.) Extrude them from the cell **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Macrophages are known to extrude the residual products from their cellular cytoplasm after digestion.

### Q689. Macrophages secrete a protein that aids in the maturation of monocytes into macrophages and plays a role in the inflammatory response. This protein is:
A.) Perforin
B.) Complement protein
C.) Interferon
D.) Interleukin-1 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Macrophages secrete Interleukin-1, which promotes monocyte maturation and contributes to inflammation and fever.

### Q690. The lifespan of monocytes circulating in the blood is approximately:
A.) 2 hours
B.) Several months
C.) Several years
D.) 10-20 days **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Monocytes circulate in the blood for about 10-20 days before migrating into tissues.

### Q691. Fixed macrophages are those that:
A.) Circulate freely in the blood.
B.) Only become active during infection.
C.) Develop into neutrophils.
D.) Remain stationary in specific tissues. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Macrophages can be either free-moving or fixed in tissues, where they perform their phagocytic functions.

### Q692. Which of the following describes neutrophils?
A.) Agranular leukocytes with a long lifespan
B.) Cells that primarily produce antibodies
C.) Phagocytes that only function in the lymphatic system
D.) Granular leukocytes with a multi-lobed nucleus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neutrophils are characterized as granular leukocytes with a multi-lobed nucleus.

### Q693. What is the approximate lifespan of neutrophils in circulation?
A.) Few Weeks
B.) Few Months
C.) Few Years
D.) Few Days **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neutrophils typically have a short lifespan in circulation, often only a few days.

### Q694. What is the primary function of neutrophils in the immune response?
A.) Producing histamine
B.) Releasing heparin
C.) Secreting interferons
D.) Phagocytizing bacteria **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neutrophils are highly motile phagocytes that ingest bacteria and dead cells.

### Q695. Neutrophils are particularly adept at:
A.) Attacking virus-infected cells
B.) Causing allergic reactions
C.) Initiating the adaptive immune response
D.) Moving through tissues **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neutrophils are highly mobile and use pseudopodia to move and engulf targets.

### Q696. Natural Killer (NK) cells are a type of lymphocyte that primarily targets:
A.) Bacteria and fungi
B.) Parasitic worms
C.) Extracellular toxins
D.) Cancerous cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — NK cells are specialized to recognize and kill cells that have been infected by viruses or have become cancerous.

### Q697. How do Natural Killer (NK) cells destroy their target cells?
A.) By engulfing them through phagocytosis
B.) By producing specific antibodies
C.) By releasing histamine
D.) By secreting proteins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — NK cells secrete proteins that insert into the plasma membrane of the target cell, forming pores and leading to its destruction.

### Q698. What is the primary advantage of Natural Killer (NK) cells over specific immune cells?
A.) They produce a wider range of antibodies.
B.) They form memory cells for future infections.
C.) They can only target extracellular pathogens.
D.) They act immediately without prior sensitization. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — NK cells are part of the innate immune system and provide rapid, non-specific defense against infected or cancerous cells without requiring prior exposure to an antigen.

### Q699. The complement system consists of a group of about 20 proteins found in:
A.) Intracellular fluid
B.) Lymph
C.) Cytoplasm
D.) Plasma **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Complement proteins are found in the plasma, which is the fluid component of blood.

### Q700. The activation of the complement system leads to the formation of a protein complex that inserts into the cell membrane of microorganisms, causing them to burst. This complex is called:
A.) Antibody-antigen complex
B.) Major Histocompatibility Complex (MHC)
C.) T-cell receptor
D.) Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Membrane Attack Complex (MAC) is a key component of the complement system that creates holes in microbial membranes.

### Q701. In addition to direct lysis, the complement system also amplifies the:
A.) Adaptive immune response
B.) Antibody production
C.) Red blood cell production
D.) Inflammatory response **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Activated complement proteins contribute to the inflammatory response by attracting phagocytes and promoting vasodilation.

### Q702. Interferons are a group of small proteins that are secreted by cells infected with:
A.) Bacteria
B.) Fungi
C.) Parasites
D.) Viruses **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Interferons are produced and released by host cells in response to the presence of viruses.

### Q703. What is the primary action of interferons in combating viral infections?
A.) They directly kill viruses in the bloodstream.
B.) They activate the complement system to destroy infected cells.
C.) They stimulate antibody production against the virus.
D.) They limit viral replication. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Interferons bind to uninfected cells, stimulating them to produce proteins that inhibit viral protein synthesis, thus preventing viral replication.

### Q704. Which type of interferon is known for enhancing the activity of Natural Killer (NK) cells?
A.) Alpha interferon
B.) Beta interferon
C.) Delta interferon
D.) Gamma interferon **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Gamma interferon (Type II interferon) stimulates the activity of NK cells and macrophages.

### Q705. Interferons are generally classified as a type of:
A.) Hormone
B.) Enzyme
C.) Antibody
D.) Cytokine **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Interferons are a group of signaling proteins, specifically a type of cytokine, that mediate communication between cells.

### Q706. Which of the following is a key mechanism by which interferons limit the spread of a virus?
A.) By directly binding to and neutralizing viral particles.
B.) By preventing the virus from entering host cells.
C.) By stimulating fever to inhibit viral replication.
D.) By inducing uninfected cells to produce antiviral proteins. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Interferons warn neighboring healthy cells, making them resistant to viral infection by prompting the production of antiviral proteins.

### Q707. The overall function of the complement system and interferons is to provide:
A.) Specific, long-term immunity
B.) Antibody-mediated immunity
C.) Cell-mediated immunity
D.) Nonspecific, immediate defense **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Both the complement system and interferons are components of the innate immune system, offering rapid and general defense mechanisms.

### Q708. A key difference between the action of the complement system and interferons is that:
A.) Complement targets viruses directly, while interferons target bacteria.
B.) Interferons activate phagocytes, while complement produces antibodies.
C.) Complement is involved in adaptive immunity, while interferons are part of innate immunity.
D.) Complement proteins primarily lead to cell lysis, while interferons inhibit viral replication within cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The complement system primarily causes direct destruction (lysis) of pathogens, whereas interferons act by preventing viral replication in host cells.

### Q709. The inflammatory response is a localized tissue response to:
A.) Antibody production
B.) Lymphatic blockage
C.) Antiviral activity
D.) Tissue infection **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The inflammatory response is triggered by various forms of tissue injury or infection.

### Q710. A systemic inflammatory response involves changes that occur throughout the body, such as:
A.) Reduced blood flow to infected area
B.) Decreased body temperature
C.) Localized redness only
D.) Increased neutrophil count **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A systemic inflammatory response can include widespread effects like an increase in white blood cell count (leukocytosis), particularly neutrophils.

### Q711. During inflammation, which substance is released by basophils, causing vasodilation and increased vascular permeability?
A.) Prostaglandin
B.) Bradykinin
C.) Serotonin
D.) Histamine **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Histamine is a key mediator released by basophils, leading to the characteristic signs of inflammation.

### Q712. The increased blood flow and plasma leakage into the injured tissue during inflammation are responsible for which two cardinal signs of inflammation?
A.) Pain and loss of function
B.) Heat and pallor
C.) Pus formation and scarring
D.) Swelling and redness **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Increased blood flow causes redness and warmth, while increased vascular permeability leads to fluid leakage, causing swelling (edema).

### Q713. One of the main purposes of the inflammatory response is to:
A.) Produce specific antibodies for the invading pathogen.
B.) Suppress the immune system to prevent autoimmune reactions.
C.) Accelerate blood clotting in distant uninjured areas.
D.) Disinfect and clean injured tissues. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The inflammatory response brings immune cells and antimicrobial substances to the site of injury to eliminate pathogens and clear debris.

### Q714. The accumulation of dead neutrophils, bacteria, and tissue debris at an infection site forms a thick, yellowish fluid called:
A.) Lymph
B.) Plasma
C.) Exudate
D.) Pus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pus is a common outcome of bacterial infections, consisting of dead immune cells, bacteria, and necrotic tissue.

### Q715. A fever is an abnormally high body temperature that can be caused by:
A.) Decreased metabolic rate
B.) Excessive sweating
C.) Low cytokine levels
D.) Brain abnormalities like tumors **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Besides infections, brain abnormalities, like tumors, can also affect the thermoregulatory center and cause fever.

### Q716. Substances that cause fever by acting on the hypothalamus to raise the body's temperature set-point are known as:
A.) Antipyretics
B.) Cytokines
C.) Chemokines
D.) Pyrogens **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pyrogens (e.g., bacterial lipopolysaccharide, toxins, or endogenous pyrogens like IL-1) reset the hypothalamic thermostat to a higher temperature.

### Q717. Which medication is a common antipyretic that reduces fever by inhibiting prostaglandin synthesis in the hypothalamus?
A.) Penicillin
B.) Insulin
C.) Adrenaline
D.) Aspirin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Aspirin is a non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) that acts as an antipyretic by blocking prostaglandin synthesis.

### Q718. One way fever helps the body fight infection is by:
A.) Directly killing all types of bacteria.
B.) Increasing the amount of available iron for bacteria.
C.) Decreasing phagocytic activity.
D.) Reducing the amount of iron available to bacteria. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Fever makes iron less available to bacteria, which require it for growth, and also enhances interferon production, boosting antiviral defense.

### Q719. The third line of defense, or adaptive immunity, is highly dependent on the cells of the:
A.) Circulatory system
B.) Digestive system
C.) Nervous system
D.) Lymphatic system **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lymphatic system, including its cells (lymphocytes), is central to the specific (adaptive) immune response.

### Q720. The effectiveness of the third line of defense relies on the ability of immune cells to:
A.) Non-specifically engulf pathogens.
B.) Produce immediate inflammatory responses.
C.) Generate a fever to inhibit microbial growth.
D.) Recognize and respond specifically to foreign substances. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Adaptive immunity is characterized by its specificity, where immune cells target particular antigens.

### Q721. Foreign substances that elicit an immune response, such as the production of antibodies, are called:
A.) Allergens
B.) Haptens
C.) Pathogens
D.) Antigens **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Antigens are molecules, often foreign, that are recognized by the immune system and can trigger an immune response.

### Q722. A small molecule that is not antigenic by itself but can become antigenic when bound to a larger carrier molecule is called a:
A.) Epitope
B.) Ligand
C.) Superantigen
D.) Hapten **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Haptens are small molecules that require conjugation to a carrier protein to become immunogenic.

### Q723. The immune system primarily distinguishes between 'self' and 'non-self' based on:
A.) The size of the molecules.
B.) Their electrical charge.
C.) Their ability to cause inflammation.
D.) The presence of antigens . **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The immune system identifies foreignness by recognizing specific antigenic determinants on molecules.

### Q724. Which of the following is an example of an exogenous antigen?
A.) A protein from a virus inside a host cell.
B.) A self-protein that has been altered.
C.) A tumor-specific antigen on a cancer cell.
D.) Bacterial toxins released outside cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Exogenous antigens are those that enter the body from the outside, such as bacterial toxins.

### Q725. The type of specific immunity that involves the production of antibodies by B cells is known as:
A.) Cell-mediated immunity
B.) Innate immunity
C.) Non-specific immunity
D.) Humoral immunity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Humoral immunity relies on antibodies circulating in the body fluids (humors).

### Q726. Which type of specific immunity primarily involves T lymphocytes directly attacking infected cells or regulating other immune cells?
A.) Humoral immunity
B.) Antibody-mediated immunity
C.) Innate immunity
D.) Cell-mediated immunity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cell-mediated immunity involves T cells directly engaging with target cells or orchestrating immune responses.

### Q727. Humoral immunity is most effective against:
A.) Intracellular viruses
B.) Cancer cells
C.) Fungal infections within tissues
D.) Extracellular pathogens and their toxins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Antibodies are effective at neutralizing pathogens and toxins circulating in body fluids before they can infect cells.

### Q728. Cell-mediated immunity is crucial for combating:
A.) Bacterial infections in the bloodstream.
B.) Parasitic infestations in the gut.
C.) Allergic reactions to pollen.
D.) Cancer cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cell-mediated immunity, particularly cytotoxic T cells, is essential for identifying and eliminating cells that have become abnormal (e.g., infected or cancerous).

### Q729. Which cells of the immune system are primarily responsible for producing antibodies?
A.) T cells
B.) Macrophages
C.) Neutrophils
D.) B cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — B cells differentiate into plasma cells, which are the primary antibody-producing cells.

### Q730. T lymphocytes mature in the:
A.) Bone marrow
B.) Spleen
C.) Lymph nodes
D.) Thymus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — T cells undergo maturation and selection in the thymus gland.

### Q731. What is the primary role of Helper T cells in the adaptive immune response?
A.) Directly killing infected cells.
B.) Producing antibodies.
C.) Phagocytizing pathogens.
D.) Activating other immune cells. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Helper T cells play a central role in orchestrating both humoral and cell-mediated immune responses by secreting cytokines that activate other immune cells.

### Q732. Cytotoxic T cells primarily recognize and kill target cells that display:
A.) Extracellular antigens.
B.) Antigens presented on MHC class II molecules.
C.) Antibodies on their surface.
D.) Antigens presented on MHC class I molecules. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cytotoxic T cells recognize processed endogenous antigens presented on MHC class I molecules, typically found on all nucleated cells.

### Q733. Memory cells are a crucial component of adaptive immunity because they:
A.) Immediately produce histamine upon antigen exposure.
B.) Phagocytize pathogens more efficiently than naive cells.
C.) Are responsible for non-specific defense mechanisms.
D.) Ensure a rapid response upon re-exposure to the same antigen. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Memory cells provide immunological memory, allowing for a more rapid and robust response upon re-exposure to the same pathogen.

### Q734. Which type of lymphocyte is responsible for immunological surveillance against cancer cells?
A.) B cells
B.) Helper T cells
C.) Plasma cells
D.) Cytotoxic T cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cytotoxic T cells recognize and eliminate tumor cells expressing abnormal antigens.

### Q735. Which of the following cells presents antigens to Helper T cells?
A.) Neutrophils
B.) Basophils
C.) Red blood cells
D.) Macrophages **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Macrophages, dendritic cells, and B cells can function as Antigen-Presenting Cells (APCs) to activate Helper T cells by presenting antigens on MHC class II molecules.

### Q736. Eosinophils are granulocytes primarily involved in defense against:
A.) Bacterial infections
B.) Viral infections
C.) Fungal infections
D.) Allergic reactions **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Eosinophils are associated with allergic responses and defense against helminthic (worm) infections.

### Q737. Basophils play a key role in allergic reactions by releasing:
A.) Antibodies
B.) Cytokines
C.) Perforins
D.) Histamine **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — These cells degranulate and release histamine, which contributes to the symptoms of allergic reactions.

### Q738. The human lymphatic system is composed of lymph vessels, lymphoid masses, lymph nodes, and:
A.) Blood
B.) Plasma
C.) Interstitial fluid
D.) Lymph **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lymphatic system consists of vessels, lymphoid organs, nodes, and the fluid called lymph that flows within it.

### Q739. In addition to the blood circulatory system, what other system is responsible for the transport of materials and returns substances from tissues to blood?
A.) Nervous system
B.) Digestive system
C.) Endocrine system
D.) Lymphatic system **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lymphatic system works in parallel with the blood circulatory system to transport materials and maintain fluid balance.

### Q740. The branches of lymph capillaries found in the villi of the small intestine, responsible for absorbing fatty acids and glycerol, are called:
A.) Arteries
B.) Veins
C.) Capillary beds
D.) Lacteals **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lacteals are specialized lymphatic capillaries in the intestinal villi that absorb digested fats.

### Q741. Fatty acids and glycerol are absorbed into the epithelial cells of villi, where they form triglycerides, which are then coated with proteins to form:
A.) Micelles
B.) Lipoproteins
C.) Fatty acids
D.) Chylomicrons **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chylomicrons are lipoprotein particles formed in the enterocytes that transport dietary lipids to the body.

### Q742. The lymphatic system begins with small vessels called lymph capillaries, which have blind endings in the:
A.) Bloodstream
B.) Intracellular fluid
C.) Digestive tract
D.) Extracellular fluid **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lymph capillaries originate as blind-ended tubules in the Extracellular fluid (interstitial fluid), collecting excess fluid from tissues.

### Q743. Compared to blood capillaries, lymph capillaries are:
A.) Less permeable
B.) Identically permeable
C.) Impermeable to large molecules
D.) More permeable **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lymph capillaries have highly permeable walls, allowing larger molecules and excess fluid to enter easily.

### Q744. When interstitial fluid enters lymph capillaries, it is then referred to as:
A.) Blood plasma
B.) Serum
C.) Chyle
D.) Lymph **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Once interstitial fluid enters the lymphatic capillaries, it becomes lymph.

### Q745. Lymph capillaries join to form larger lymphatic vessels, which eventually converge to form two main lymph ducts: the right lymphatic duct and the:
A.) Jugular duct
B.) Subclavian duct
C.) Carotid duct
D.) Thoracic duct **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The thoracic duct is the largest lymphatic vessel, draining lymph from most of the body.

### Q746. The right lymphatic duct and the thoracic duct both open into:
A.) The aorta
B.) The inferior vena cava
C.) The pulmonary artery
D.) The subclavian veins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — These major lymphatic ducts return lymph to the bloodstream by emptying into the subclavian veins.

### Q747. Which of the following mechanisms helps maintain the flow of lymph through the lymphatic vessels?
A.) Pumping action of the heart
B.) Peristalsis of the lymphatic vessels themselves
C.) Active transport by endothelial cells
D.) Contraction of skeletal muscles and valves **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lymph flow is largely facilitated by skeletal muscle contractions compressing the vessels and one-way valves preventing backflow.

### Q748. One of the key functions of the lymphatic system is to return excess fluid and proteins from the interstitial spaces to the:
A.) Digestive tract
B.) Urinary system
C.) Central nervous system
D.) Blood **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lymphatic system collects excess interstitial fluid and plasma proteins that have leaked from capillaries and returns them to the circulation, maintaining blood volume and pressure.

### Q749. The lymphatic system plays a crucial role in the body's defense by:
A.) Producing red blood cells
B.) Digesting foreign invaders
C.) Transporting oxygen
D.) Filtering lymph and producing lymphocytes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lymph nodes filter lymph, and lymphoid organs produce lymphocytes, which are key immune cells.

### Q750. Small, bean-shaped structures found along lymphatic vessels that filter lymph and contain lymphocytes and macrophages are called:
A.) Tonsils
B.) Spleen
C.) Thymus
D.) Lymph nodes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lymph nodes are strategically located to filter lymph and initiate immune responses.

### Q751. Lymph nodes are typically present in clusters in regions such as the neck, axilla (armpit), and:
A.) Elbow
B.) Knee
C.) Ankle
D.) Groin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Common locations for lymph node clusters include the neck, axilla, and groin.

### Q752. What happens to lymph as it passes through a lymph node?
A.) It picks up more interstitial fluid.
B.) It is directly absorbed into the bloodstream.
C.) It undergoes oxygenation.
D.) It is filtered by lymphocytes and macrophages. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lymph nodes act as filters, removing pathogens and debris from lymph and exposing them to immune cells.

### Q753. Which of the following is considered one of the larger lymphoid masses in the body?
A.) Pancreas
B.) Liver
C.) Kidney
D.) Spleen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The spleen is a major lymphoid organ, alongside the thymus, tonsils, and adenoids.

### Q754. The spleen is primarily responsible for:
A.) Producing digestive enzymes
B.) Absorbing nutrients
C.) Regulating blood sugar
D.) Filtering blood **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The spleen functions to filter blood, remove old red blood cells, and store blood components.

### Q755. Which lymphoid mass is crucial for the maturation of T lymphocytes?
A.) Tonsils
B.) Spleen
C.) Adenoids
D.) Thymus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The thymus is the primary lymphoid organ where T cells mature and differentiate.

### Q756. Tonsils and adenoids are examples of lymphoid masses that primarily produce:
A.) Antibodies
B.) Phagocytes
C.) Histamine
D.) Lymphocytes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Tonsils and adenoids, like other lymphoid tissues, contain and produce lymphocytes, contributing to local immune defense.

### Q757. The collection of lymph nodes, lymph vessels, and lymphoid masses constitutes the system primarily involved in:
A.) Gas exchange
B.) Nutrient absorption
C.) Hormone regulation
D.) Immune surveillance and fluid balance **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lymphatic system's main roles are to maintain fluid homeostasis and provide immune defense.

### Q758. Rheumatoid arthritis is an autoimmune disorder characterized by the immune system attacking:
A.) Its own red blood cells
B.) The myelin sheath of nerves
C.) The insulin-producing cells of the pancreas
D.) The synovial membranes of joints **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Rheumatoid arthritis is a chronic inflammatory disorder that primarily affects the lining of the joints.

### Q759. In rheumatoid arthritis, the body produces autoantibodies that target:
A.) Collagen
B.) Albumin
C.) Fibrinogen
D.) Immunoglobulin G **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A hallmark of rheumatoid arthritis is the presence of rheumatoid factor, which are autoantibodies against the Fc portion of IgG.

### Q760. Which immune cells are found in the synovial fluid of affected joints in rheumatoid arthritis and contribute to the inflammation?
A.) Neutrophils and eosinophils only
B.) B cells and NK cells only
C.) Mast cells and basophils
D.) T cells, plasma cells, and macrophages **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The synovial fluid in rheumatoid arthritis is infiltrated with various immune cells, including T cells, plasma cells (producing autoantibodies), and macrophages.

### Q761. The inflammation and joint damage in rheumatoid arthritis are largely mediated by the release of:
A.) Hormones
B.) Neurotransmitters
C.) Digestive enzymes
D.) Cytokines **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pro-inflammatory cytokines, such as TNF-alpha and IL-6, play a critical role in the pathogenesis of rheumatoid arthritis.

### Q762. Rheumatoid arthritis is classified as an autoimmune disease because:
A.) It is caused by a bacterial infection.
B.) It is a genetic disorder inherited from parents.
C.) It results from a deficiency in antibody production.
D.) Body's immune system mistakenly attacks its own tissues. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Autoimmune diseases occur when the immune system loses its ability to distinguish between self and non-self, leading to an attack on the body's own components.

## Coordination & Control

### Q763. Which of the following defines nervous coordination?
A.) Coordination through chemical messengers.
B.) Regulation of body temperature only.
C.) The study of the structure of the nervous system.
D.) Coordination through electric signals. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Nervous coordination is defined as the system of the body that provides coordination through electric signals among different body parts for the response to a particular stimulus.

### Q764. How do receptors function as transducers?
A.) By generating heat energy from chemical signals.
B.) By converting nerve impulses into light energy.
C.) By transmitting electrical signals without any conversion.
D.) By converting one form of energy into another form. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Receptors act as transducers because they convert one form of energy, such as light energy from a stimulus, into another form, specifically electrochemical energy in the form of a nerve impulse.

### Q765. Which of the following is an example of a receptor acting as a transducer?
A.) Muscles contracting in response to a nerve impulse.
B.) Glands secreting hormones into the bloodstream.
C.) The brain processing sensory information.
D.) Rod and cone cells in the retina. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An example of a receptor acting as a transducer is found in the retina of the eye, where rod and cone cells convert light energy into nerve impulses (electrochemical energy).

### Q766. What are muscles and glands primarily identified as in the context of nervous transmission?
A.) Receptors.
B.) Sensory neurons.
C.) Interneurons.
D.) Effectors. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Muscles and glands are identified as the effectors, which carry out the response to a stimulus after receiving commands from the nervous system.

### Q767. What are the basic structural and functional units of the nervous system?
A.) Glial cells.
B.) Neurotransmitters.
C.) Synapses.
D.) Neurons. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neurons are explicitly stated as the basic structural and functional unit of the nervous system.

### Q768. Which of the following is NOT one of the three basic components of all neurons?
A.) Cell body.
B.) Dendrites.
C.) Axon.
D.) Myelin sheath. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — While a myelin sheath may cover some axons, the three basic components found in all neurons are the cell body, dendrites, and an axon.

### Q769. What is the primary function of dendrites?
A.) To conduct action potentials away from the cell body.
B.) To synthesize proteins for the neuron.
C.) To form the myelin sheath.
D.) To receive stimuli and conduct impulses to the cell body. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The function of the dendrite is to receive stimuli and conduct impulses to the cell body.

### Q770. What structures are responsible for conducting action potentials from the neuron cell body to the presynaptic terminals?
A.) Dendrites.
B.) Cell bodies.
C.) Synaptic vesicles.
D.) Axons. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Axons functionally conduct action potentials from the neuron cell body to the presynaptic terminals, meaning they conduct signals away from the cell body.

### Q771. What is the fatty substance that acts as an insulator around axons?
A.) Neuroglia.
B.) Nissl's granules.
C.) Cytoplasm.
D.) Myelin sheath. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Schwann cells are covered by a fatty substance called myelin sheath that acts as an insulator, making these axons myelinated fibers.

### Q772. What is the non-myelinated part of an axon between two Schwann cells called?
A.) Synaptic cleft.
B.) Presynaptic terminal.
C.) Cell body.
D.) Node of Ranvier. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A non-myelinated part of an axon between two Schwann cells is called a node of Ranvier.

### Q773. Which type of neuron conducts impulses towards the central nervous system from receptors?
A.) Motor neurons.
B.) Interneurons.
C.) Associative neurons.
D.) Sensory neurons. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sensory neurons are responsible for conducting impulses towards the central nervous system from the sensory receptors.

### Q774. Where do interneurons primarily occur?
A.) Entirely within the Peripheral Nervous System.
B.) Between receptors and sensory neurons.
C.) Between motor neurons and effectors.
D.) Entirely within the Central Nervous System. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Interneurons occur entirely within the CNS and convey messages between various parts of the CNS.

### Q775. Which statement accurately describes motor neurons?
A.) They receive stimuli from receptors.
B.) They occur entirely within the CNS.
C.) Their cell body is typically outside the CNS.
D.) They conduct impulses away from the CNS to effectors. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Motor neurons conduct impulses away from the central nervous system, and their terminal branches are connected to an effector.

### Q776. Which factor contributes to a faster velocity of nerve impulse transmission?
A.) Smaller axon diameter.
B.) Longer axon length.
C.) Absence of Schwann cells.
D.) Presence of a myelin sheath. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The myelinated axons transmit information much faster than other neurons, and the velocity of nerve impulse is faster in myelinated neuron fibers due to saltatory conduction.

### Q777. What is saltatory conduction?
A.) The continuous flow of nerve impulses along an unmyelinated axon.
B.) The transmission of impulses across a synaptic cleft.
C.) The slow movement of ions across the axon membrane.
D.) The jumping of nerve impulse from one node of Ranvier to another. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Saltatory conduction is the rapid transmission of a nerve impulse along an axon, resulting from the action potential jumping from one node of Ranvier to another, skipping the myelinated regions of membrane.

### Q778. How does the diameter of a neuron fiber affect the velocity of nerve impulse?
A.) Thicker neuron fibers conduct slower impulses.
B.) Diameter has no effect on impulse velocity.
C.) Only myelination affects impulse velocity, not diameter.
D.) Thicker neuron fibers conduct faster impulses. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thick neuron fibers conduct faster impulses than thin fibers because resistance to electrical current flow is inversely proportional to the cross-sectional area of the conductor, meaning increased thickness leads to decreased resistance.

### Q779. What is the cytoplasm of a neuron cell body characterized by?
A.) Absence of organelles.
B.) High concentration of fatty acids.
C.) Lack of a nucleus.
D.) Presence of Nissl's granules. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cytoplasm of the cell body is characterized by the presence of Nissl's granules, which are groups of ribosomes and rough ER associated with protein synthesis.

### Q780. What type of neurons have three or more processes, typically several dendrites and one axon?
A.) Unipolar neurons.
B.) Bipolar neurons.
C.) Pseudounipolar neurons.
D.) Multipolar neurons. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Multipolar neurons are characterized by having three or more processes, specifically several dendrites and one axon.

### Q781. What is the role of Schwann cells in the peripheral nervous system?
A.) To act as receptors for stimuli.
B.) To conduct impulses between interneurons.
C.) To synthesize neurotransmitters.
D.) To nourish neurons. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Schwann cells are neuroglial cells in the peripheral nervous system that support, protect, and nourish the neurons. They typically wrap around axon fibers and are covered by a myelin sheath.

### Q782. What is a reflex action?
A.) A voluntary response controlled by the brain.
B.) A slow, conscious response to an external change.
C.) A learned behavior pattern.
D.) An involuntary response to a stimulus. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A reflex action is defined as a rapid, involuntary, and unconscious response to a stimulus, occurring almost instantaneously.

### Q783. In a typical reflex arc, what is the role of the interneuron?
A.) To receive stimuli from the external environment.
B.) To transmit impulses from the motor neuron to the effector.
C.) To conduct impulses directly from receptors to effectors.
D.) To act as a link between sensory and motor neurons. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In a reflex arc, the interneuron (also called associative neuron) acts as a link between the sensory neuron and the motor neuron, processing the signal within the CNS.

### Q784. What happens if the sensory neuron in a reflex arc is damaged?
A.) The effector will still respond normally.
B.) Only the motor response will be affected, not sensory perception.
C.) The interneuron will take over the function of the sensory neuron.
D.) The stimulus cannot be received and transmitted to the CNS. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — If the sensory neuron is damaged, the stimulus cannot be successfully received by the CNS and thus no reflex action will occur.

### Q785. Which component of a reflex arc carries impulses away from the central nervous system to the muscles or glands?
A.) Receptor.
B.) Sensory neuron.
C.) Interneuron.
D.) Motor neuron. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Motor neurons conduct impulses away from the central nervous system to the muscles or glands (effectors).

### Q786. The withdrawal reflex, where a hand is pulled away from a hot object, involves which type of neurons?
A.) Only sensory neurons.
B.) Only motor neurons.
C.) Only interneurons.
D.) Sensory, inter, and motor neurons. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The withdrawal reflex is a classic example of a reflex arc involving all three types of neurons: sensory (detects heat), interneuron (processes in spinal cord), and motor (activates muscles to withdraw).

### Q787. Where is the cell body of a sensory neuron typically located in a reflex arc?
A.) Within the spinal cord (CNS).
B.) In the effector organ (e.g., muscle).
C.) In the brain.
D.) In the PNS. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cell body of a sensory neuron is generally located in the dorsal root ganglion, which is part of the Peripheral Nervous System, outside the CNS.

### Q788. Which of the following is an effector in a reflex arc?
A.) Skin.
B.) Spinal cord.
C.) Dorsal root ganglion.
D.) Gland. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Effectors are the structures that respond to the nerve impulse, such as muscles contracting or glands secreting.

### Q789. A knee-jerk reflex is an example of what type of reflex arc?
A.) A complex reflex involving the brain.
B.) A polysynaptic reflex.
C.) A conditioned reflex.
D.) A monosynaptic reflex. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The knee-jerk reflex is a monosynaptic reflex, meaning it involves only one synapse between the sensory and motor neuron, without an interneuron.

### Q790. What is the correct sequence of components in a reflex arc?
A.) Effector → Motor neuron → Interneuron → Sensory neuron → Receptor.
B.) Sensory neuron → Receptor → Interneuron → Motor neuron → Effector.
C.) Receptor → Motor neuron → Interneuron → Sensory neuron → Effector.
D.) Receptor → Sensory neuron → Interneuron → Motor neuron → Effector. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The typical pathway of a reflex arc is: Receptor detects stimulus, Sensory neuron transmits to CNS, Interneuron processes (in CNS), Motor neuron transmits to effector, Effector produces response.

### Q791. What is a nerve impulse defined as?
A.) A purely chemical signal.
B.) A mechanical vibration within the neuron.
C.) A static electrical charge on the neuron membrane.
D.) An electrochemical change. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A nerve impulse is defined as an electrochemical change which travels along the length of a neuron.

### Q792. What is the state of a neuron membrane when it is at its resting potential?
A.) Depolarized.
B.) Repolarized.
C.) Hyperpolarized.
D.) Polarized. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When a neuron is at its resting potential, it is in a polarized state, meaning there is a difference in electrical charge across the membrane.

### Q793. Which of the following ions has a higher concentration inside the neuron cell during the resting membrane potential?
A.) Sodium ions (Na+).
B.) Chloride ions (Cl-).
C.) Calcium ions (Ca2+).
D.) Potassium ions (K+). **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — During the resting membrane potential, there is a higher concentration of potassium ions (K+) inside the cell compared to the outside.

### Q794. What is the primary role of the Sodium-Potassium (Na+/K+) pump in maintaining the resting membrane potential?
A.) To allow Na+ and K+ to freely diffuse across the membrane.
B.) To equally exchange Na+ and K+ ions across the membrane.
C.) To only pump K+ ions out of the cell.
D.) To actively transport 3 Na+ ions out and 2 K+ ions into the cell. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Na+/K+ pump actively transports 3 Na+ ions out of the cell and 2 K+ ions into the cell, which significantly contributes to maintaining the negative resting membrane potential.

### Q795. What contributes to the negative charge inside the neuron during resting potential, in addition to the Na+/K+ pump?
A.) High concentration of positive ions.
B.) Leakage of Na+ ions into the cell.
C.) Active transport of positive ions into the cell.
D.) Presence of negative ions in the cell. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The presence of negatively charged organic ions (proteins, amino acids) inside the cytoplasm, which cannot easily cross the membrane, contributes to the overall negative charge inside.

### Q796. What is the immediate event that occurs when a stimulus reaches the threshold level, leading to an action potential?
A.) K+ channels open, and K+ rushes out.
B.) Cl- channels open, and Cl- rushes in.
C.) The Na+/K+ pump stops working.
D.) Na+ channels open, and Na+ rushes in. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When a stimulus reaches the threshold level, voltage-gated Na+ channels open rapidly, causing Na+ ions to rush into the cell, leading to depolarization.

### Q797. What is the term for the rapid influx of sodium ions that causes the inside of the neuron membrane to become positive?
A.) Repolarization.
B.) Hyperpolarization.
C.) Resting potential.
D.) Depolarization. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Depolarization is the phase during an action potential where the rapid influx of Na+ ions causes the inside of the membrane to become positive relative to the outside.

### Q798. During the repolarization phase of an action potential, which ion movement is primarily responsible for restoring the negative charge inside the cell?
A.) Influx of Na+ ions.
B.) Influx of Cl- ions.
C.) Efflux of Ca2+ ions.
D.) Efflux of K+ ions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Repolarization occurs as voltage-gated K+ channels open, allowing K+ ions to rush out of the cell, making the inside of the membrane negative again.

### Q799. What is hyperpolarization?
A.) A state where the membrane potential is less negative than the resting potential.
B.) The initial depolarization phase of an action potential.
C.) The point at which the membrane potential reaches zero.
D.) A brief period when the membrane potential becomes more negative than the resting potential. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hyperpolarization is a brief period after repolarization where the membrane potential becomes even more negative than the resting potential, due to the slow closing of K+ channels.

### Q800. What is the minimum strength of a stimulus required to generate an action potential?
A.) Sub-threshold stimulus.
B.) Suprathreshold stimulus.
C.) Refractory stimulus.
D.) Threshold stimulus. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A threshold stimulus is the minimum strength of a stimulus required to generate an action potential.

### Q801. If a stimulus is applied that is too weak to cause a significant change in membrane potential, it is referred to as a:
A.) Threshold stimulus.
B.) Suprathreshold stimulus.
C.) Weak-threshold stimulus.
D.) Sub-threshold stimulus. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A sub-threshold stimulus is one that is too weak to reach the threshold potential and therefore does not generate an action potential.

### Q802. What is the refractory period of a neuron?
A.) The time during which the neuron is constantly firing impulses.
B.) The time it takes for an impulse to travel across a synapse.
C.) The duration of the resting membrane potential.
D.) The time during which a neuron cannot generate another action potential. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The refractory period is a brief period immediately following an action potential during which the neuron is unable to generate another action potential, ensuring one-way propagation.

### Q803. In which type of neuron fiber does saltatory conduction occur, leading to faster impulse transmission?
A.) Non-myelinated fibers.
B.) Dendrites.
C.) Cell bodies.
D.) Myelinated fibers. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Saltatory conduction, which involves the action potential "jumping" between Nodes of Ranvier, occurs only in myelinated fibers, significantly increasing the speed of impulse transmission.

### Q804. How does the diameter of an axon influence the velocity of nerve impulse conduction?
A.) Larger diameter axons conduct impulses slower.
B.) Smaller diameter axons conduct impulses faster.
C.) Axon diameter has no impact on conduction velocity.
D.) Larger diameter axons conduct impulses faster. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Larger diameter axons conduct impulses faster because their larger cross-sectional area offers less resistance to the flow of local currents, allowing depolarization to spread more rapidly.

### Q805. The "All-or-None Law" applies to:
A.) The strength of a neurotransmitter.
B.) The magnitude of the resting membrane potential.
C.) The number of dendrites on a neuron.
D.) The generation of an action potential. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The "All-or-None Law" states that if a stimulus reaches threshold, an action potential will occur with full strength, or it won't occur at all if the stimulus is sub-threshold. The magnitude of the action potential is independent of the stimulus strength once threshold is reached.

### Q806. Which ion channels are crucial for the repolarization phase of an action potential?
A.) Voltage-gated Na+ channels.
B.) Ligand-gated Na+ channels.
C.) Mechanically-gated ion channels.
D.) Voltage-gated K+ channels. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The opening of voltage-gated K+ channels and the subsequent efflux of K+ ions are primarily responsible for the repolarization phase, bringing the membrane potential back towards resting levels.

### Q807. During which phase of an action potential is the membrane most permeable to sodium ions?
A.) Resting potential.
B.) Repolarization.
C.) Hyperpolarization.
D.) Depolarization. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — During depolarization, voltage-gated sodium channels open, causing a large influx of Na+ ions, making the membrane highly permeable to sodium.

### Q808. The initial segment of the axon where action potentials are typically generated is called the:
A.) Dendrite.
B.) Synaptic knob.
C.) Cell body.
D.) Axon hillock. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The axon hillock is the specialized region where the axon originates from the cell body, and it is typically where the summation of graded potentials occurs to trigger an action potential.

### Q809. What maintains the resting membrane potential of a neuron?
A.) The passive diffusion of ions only.
B.) The continuous influx of Na+ ions.
C.) The complete impermeability of the membrane to all ions.
D.) The active transport of ions by the Na+/K+ pump and the selective permeability of the membrane to K+ ions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The resting membrane potential is maintained by both the active transport of ions via the Na+/K+ pump (pumping 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in) and the higher permeability of the membrane to K+ ions through leak channels, leading to a net efflux of K+ and a negative internal charge.

### Q810. What is the specialized junction where a nerve impulse is transmitted from one neuron to another, or from a neuron to an effector cell?
A.) Node of Ranvier.
B.) Axon hillock.
C.) Dendrite.
D.) Synapse. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A synapse is the specialized junction or point of contact where an impulse is transmitted from one neuron to another or to an effector organ like a muscle or gland.

### Q811. Which part of a synapse is located at the end of the presynaptic axon and contains synaptic vesicles?
A.) Synaptic cleft.
B.) Postsynaptic membrane.
C.) Dendrite.
D.) Synaptic knob. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The synaptic knob (or terminal) is the swollen end of the presynaptic axon, which contains synaptic vesicles filled with neurotransmitters.

### Q812. What is the narrow gap between the presynaptic and postsynaptic neurons called?
A.) Axon hillock.
B.) Node of Ranvier.
C.) Myelin sheath.
D.) Synaptic cleft. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The synaptic cleft is the narrow space that separates the presynaptic membrane from the postsynaptic membrane.

### Q813. What are the chemical messengers stored in synaptic vesicles and released into the synaptic cleft?
A.) Hormones.
B.) Enzymes.
C.) Ions.
D.) Neurotransmitters. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neurotransmitters are chemical substances stored in synaptic vesicles within the presynaptic terminal, which are released into the synaptic cleft to transmit signals.

### Q814. What is the essential role of Calcium (Ca2+) ions in synaptic transmission?
A.) To depolarize the postsynaptic membrane directly.
B.) To activate enzymes that break down neurotransmitters.
C.) To repolarize the presynaptic membrane.
D.) To trigger exocytosis of synaptic vesicles. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When an action potential reaches the presynaptic terminal, voltage-gated Ca2+ channels open, and the influx of Ca2+ ions triggers the exocytosis of synaptic vesicles, releasing neurotransmitters into the cleft.

### Q815. Upon release, what do neurotransmitters bind to on the postsynaptic membrane?
A.) Voltage-gated ion channels.
B.) Sodium-Potassium pumps.
C.) Myelin sheath.
D.) Receptor proteins. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neurotransmitters diffuse across the synaptic cleft and bind to specific receptor proteins on the postsynaptic membrane, causing a change in its permeability to ions.

### Q816. Which type of neurotransmitter increases the likelihood of an action potential in the postsynaptic neuron?
A.) Inhibitory neurotransmitter.
B.) Modulatory neurotransmitter.
C.) Regulatory neurotransmitter.
D.) Excitatory neurotransmitter. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Excitatory neurotransmitters cause a depolarization of the postsynaptic membrane, making it more likely for an action potential to be generated.

### Q817. Which type of neurotransmitter decreases the likelihood of an action potential in the postsynaptic neuron?
A.) Excitatory neurotransmitter.
B.) Modulatory neurotransmitter.
C.) Facilitatory neurotransmitter.
D.) Inhibitory neurotransmitter. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Inhibitory neurotransmitters cause hyperpolarization or stabilization of the resting potential of the postsynaptic membrane, making it less likely for an action potential to be generated.

### Q818. The process of neurotransmitter removal from the synaptic cleft is crucial for:
A.) Ensuring prolonged postsynaptic excitation.
B.) Preventing the presynaptic neuron from firing.
C.) Increasing the sensitivity of postsynaptic receptors.
D.) Allowing for precise and rapid control of synaptic transmission. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Neurotransmitters must be quickly removed from the synaptic cleft (by enzymatic degradation or reuptake) to ensure that the postsynaptic neuron is not continuously stimulated or inhibited, allowing for precise and rapid control over neural signaling.

### Q819. What is the direct effect of a neurotransmitter binding to its receptor on the postsynaptic membrane?
A.) It causes the release of more neurotransmitters from the presynaptic terminal.
B.) It triggers the production of new action potentials in the presynaptic neuron.
C.) It directly synthesizes ATP for the postsynaptic neuron.
D.) It opens or closes ion channels. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The binding of a neurotransmitter to its receptor directly causes ion channels to open or close, leading to a change in the postsynaptic membrane potential (either depolarization for excitation or hyperpolarization for inhibition).

### Q820. The human nervous system is broadly divided into which two main parts?
A.) Somatic and Autonomic Nervous System.
B.) Sympathetic and Parasympathetic Nervous System.
C.) Brain and Spinal Cord.
D.) Central and Peripheral Nervous System. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The human nervous system consists of the central nervous system (CNS) and the peripheral nervous system (PNS).

### Q821. Which part of the nervous system serves as the coordinating center and lies in the midline of the body?
A.) Peripheral Nervous System (PNS).
B.) Autonomic Nervous System.
C.) Somatic Nervous System.
D.) Central Nervous System (CNS). **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The CNS is identified as the coordinating center and lies in the midline of the body, comprising the brain and spinal cord.

### Q822. What is the primary function of the Peripheral Nervous System (PNS)?
A.) To process and integrate sensory information.
B.) To control reflex activities only.
C.) To generate thoughts and emotions.
D.) To transmit information. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The PNS transmits information from receptors to the CNS and transmits orders and commands from the CNS to effectors.

### Q823. Which division of the human nervous system controls voluntary movements?
A.) Autonomic Nervous System.
B.) Sympathetic Division.
C.) Parasympathetic Division.
D.) Somatic Nervous System. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Somatic Nervous System controls voluntary movements.

### Q824. The "Fight or Flight" response is primarily mediated by which division of the Autonomic Nervous System?
A.) Somatic Nervous System.
B.) Central Nervous System.
C.) Parasympathetic Division.
D.) Sympathetic Division. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Sympathetic Division of the Autonomic Nervous System is responsible for the "Fight or Flight" response.

### Q825. Which division of the Autonomic Nervous System is primarily associated with "Rest and Digest" functions?
A.) Sympathetic Division.
B.) Somatic Nervous System.
C.) Peripheral Nervous System.
D.) Parasympathetic Division. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Parasympathetic Division of the Autonomic Nervous System controls involuntary responses and is associated with "Rest and Digest" activities.

### Q826. What are the two main components of the Central Nervous System (CNS)?
A.) Sensory neurons and motor neurons.
B.) Somatic and Autonomic systems.
C.) Cranial nerves and spinal nerves.
D.) Brain and Spinal cord. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Central Nervous System (CNS) consists of the Brain and Spinal Cord.

### Q827. The brain is responsible for all of the following EXCEPT:
A.) Receiving and processing sensory information.
B.) Initiating responses.
C.) Storing memories and generating thoughts.
D.) Directly controlling reflex activities without spinal cord involvement. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The brain receives and processes sensory information, initiates responses, stores memories, and generates thoughts and emotions. Reflex activities are primarily controlled by the spinal cord.

### Q828. What is the primary function of the spinal cord?
A.) Generating complex thoughts.
B.) Producing hormones.
C.) Filtering blood.
D.) Conducting signals to and from the brain. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The spinal cord conducts signals to and from the brain and controls reflex activities.

### Q829. Which of the following best describes the function of cranial and spinal nerves?
A.) They are part of the Central Nervous System.
B.) They primarily process sensory information.
C.) They only control involuntary responses.
D.) They connect the CNS to the rest of the body. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cranial and spinal nerves are components of the Peripheral Nervous System (PNS) that connect the CNS to receptors and effectors throughout the body.

### Q830. Which of the following is categorized as a narcotic drug affecting the nervous system?
A.) Caffeine.
B.) Nicotine.
C.) Alcohol.
D.) Heroine. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Heroine is a narcotic drug, which affects the nervous system by acting on opiate receptors, producing feelings of euphoria and pain relief.

### Q831. Which of the following is a common withdrawal symptom associated with alcohol addiction?
A.) Increased appetite.
B.) Enhanced coordination.
C.) Improved sleep.
D.) Convulsions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Convulsions are explicitly mentioned as a withdrawal symptom of alcohol, indicating the severe impact of alcohol dependence on the nervous system.

### Q832. Drug addiction is primarily characterized by:
A.) Occasional use of drugs for recreational purposes.
B.) The ability to easily stop drug use at any time.
C.) Taking drugs only under medical supervision.
D.) A psychological and physiological dependence on a drug. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Drug addiction is characterized by both psychological dependence (craving the drug) and physiological dependence (withdrawal symptoms upon cessation), meaning the body has adapted to the drug's presence.

### Q833. What is drug tolerance?
A.) An individual's ability to resist the effects of a drug.
B.) The immediate adverse reaction to a drug.
C.) The complete elimination of a drug from the body.
D.) The reduced effect of a drug over time. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Drug tolerance refers to the phenomenon where, with repeated exposure, the body adapts to a drug, requiring increasingly larger doses to produce the same initial effect.

### Q834. Cannabis is classified as:
A.) A stimulant.
B.) A depressant.
C.) A narcotic.
D.) A hallucinogen. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cannabis (Marijuana) is classified as a hallucinogen, affecting perception and mood.

### Q835. Which of the following is a vascular disorder of the nervous system?
A.) Meningitis.
B.) Brain tumor.
C.) Alzheimer's disease.
D.) Stroke. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Stroke is a vascular disorder of the nervous system, typically caused by a disruption of blood flow to the brain, leading to brain damage.

### Q836. Which infectious disorder of the nervous system involves inflammation of the brain membranes?
A.) Stroke.
B.) Alzheimer's disease.
C.) Headache.
D.) Meningitis. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Meningitis is an infectious disease of the nervous system characterized by inflammation of the meninges, the protective membranes surrounding the brain and spinal cord.

### Q837. Which diagnostic test uses magnetic fields and radio waves to create detailed images of the brain and spinal cord?
A.) EEG.
B.) CT scan.
C.) X-ray.
D.) MRI. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Magnetic Resonance Imaging (MRI) is a diagnostic test that utilizes strong magnetic fields and radio waves to produce detailed images of organs and soft tissues, including the brain and spinal cord.

### Q838. Which neurodegenerative disorder is characterized by progressive memory loss and cognitive decline, primarily affecting older adults?
A.) Brain tumor.
B.) Meningitis.
C.) Headache.
D.) Alzheimer's disease. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Alzheimer's disease is a common neurodegenerative disorder primarily affecting older adults, characterized by progressive memory loss, cognitive decline, and changes in behavior.

### Q839. An Electroencephalogram (EEG) is primarily used to:
A.) Visualize brain tumors.
B.) Identify structural abnormalities in the spinal cord.
C.) Measure blood flow to the brain.
D.) Detect and record the electrical activity of the brain. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An Electroencephalogram (EEG) is a diagnostic tool that measures and records the electrical activity of the brain, useful in diagnosing conditions like epilepsy and sleep disorders.

### Q840. What is the primary role of hormones in the body?
A.) To produce electrical signals for rapid communication.
B.) To provide structural support to tissues.
C.) To transport oxygen throughout the bloodstream.
D.) To act as chemical messengers that regulate various bodily functions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hormones are chemical messengers secreted by endocrine glands that travel through the bloodstream to target cells, regulating a wide range of physiological processes.

### Q841. How does the endocrine system primarily coordinate body cells?
A.) Through direct nerve innervation of all cells.
B.) By generating electrical impulses.
C.) Through mechanical force.
D.) By secreting hormones into the bloodstream. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The endocrine system coordinates most body cells by secreting hormones (chemical messengers) directly into the bloodstream, which then travel to target cells.

### Q842. Which type of gland secretes its products directly into the bloodstream?
A.) Exocrine gland.
B.) Digestive gland.
C.) Salivary gland.
D.) Endocrine gland. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Endocrine glands are ductless glands that secrete hormones directly into the bloodstream, which then transports them to target cells or organs.

### Q843. Which type of gland secretes its products into ducts?
A.) Endocrine gland.
B.) Pituitary gland.
C.) Thyroid gland.
D.) Exocrine gland. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Exocrine glands secrete their products, such as digestive enzymes or sweat, into ducts that carry them to a specific external or internal surface.

### Q844. Which of the following is an example of an exocrine gland?
A.) Thyroid gland.
B.) Pancreas.
C.) Adrenal gland.
D.) Salivary gland. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Salivary glands are exocrine glands that secrete saliva through ducts into the mouth. The pancreas has both endocrine (insulin, glucagon) and exocrine (digestive enzymes) functions, but for this question, salivary gland is a clear example of a solely exocrine function.

### Q845. Which of the following hormone types is derived from cholesterol and is lipid-soluble?
A.) Protein hormones.
B.) Peptide hormones.
C.) Catecholamines.
D.) Steroid hormones. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Steroid hormones (e.g., testosterone, estrogen) are lipid-soluble hormones derived from cholesterol.

### Q846. Which chemical nature characterizes hormones like insulin and growth hormone?
A.) Steroid.
B.) Amine.
C.) Fatty acid derivative.
D.) Protein. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Insulin and growth hormone are examples of protein or peptide hormones, which are composed of amino acids.

### Q847. Which of the following hormones typically utilizes a "second messenger system" to exert its effects on target cells?
A.) Testosterone.
B.) Cortisol.
C.) Estrogen.
D.) Insulin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Protein (and peptide) hormones, being water-soluble, cannot directly cross the cell membrane. They bind to receptors on the cell surface and activate a second messenger system, such as cAMP, to trigger intracellular responses. Insulin is a protein hormone.

### Q848. How do steroid hormones typically exert their action on target cells?
A.) By binding to receptors on the cell surface and activating a second messenger.
B.) By modifying existing proteins in the cytoplasm.
C.) By breaking down ATP to release energy.
D.) By directly activating genes within the cell nucleus. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Steroid hormones are lipid-soluble and can directly diffuse across the cell membrane. They bind to intracellular receptors (in the cytoplasm or nucleus) and the hormone-receptor complex then acts directly on DNA to activate or repress gene transcription, leading to protein synthesis.

### Q849. Epinephrine are derived from which type of molecule?
A.) Lipids.
B.) Proteins.
C.) Carbohydrates.
D.) Amino acids. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Catecholamines (like epinephrine and norepinephrine) are amine hormones derived from amino acids (specifically tyrosine).

### Q850. Which hormone characteristic allows it to easily pass through the cell membrane to reach intracellular receptors?
A.) Water solubility.
B.) Large molecular size.
C.) Charged nature.
D.) Lipid solubility. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hormones that are lipid-soluble (like steroid hormones and thyroid hormones) can readily diffuse across the lipid bilayer of the cell membrane to bind to receptors inside the cell.

### Q851. Cyclic AMP (cAMP) often acts as a second messenger for which type of hormone?
A.) Steroid hormones.
B.) Thyroid hormones.
C.) Hormones that bind to nuclear receptors.
D.) Peptide hormones. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cyclic AMP (cAMP) is a common second messenger activated when water-soluble protein/peptide hormones bind to their receptors on the cell surface.

### Q852. When a protein hormone binds to its receptor on the cell membrane, what is the immediate effect inside the cell?
A.) Direct gene activation.
B.) Synthesis of new proteins.
C.) Breakdown of the cell membrane.
D.) Enzyme Activation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The binding of a protein hormone to its cell surface receptor typically activates an enzyme (like adenylate cyclase) that then produces a second messenger, such as cAMP, inside the cell.

### Q853. Which of the following is an example of an amino acid derivative hormone?
A.) Aldosterone.
B.) Growth hormone.
C.) Oxytocin.
D.) Thyroxine. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thyroxine (T4) is an amine hormone derived from the amino acid tyrosine, similar to catecholamines, but with different synthesis and action mechanisms.

### Q854. What is the primary advantage of the second messenger system for water-soluble hormones?
A.) It allows direct interaction with DNA.
B.) It permits the hormone to enter the nucleus without a carrier.
C.) It converts the hormone into a steroid.
D.) It amplifies the original signal. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The second messenger system provides signal amplification, meaning a single hormone molecule binding to its receptor can lead to the production of many second messenger molecules, resulting in a large and rapid cellular response.

### Q855. Which part of the brain is considered the "master control center" for the endocrine system?
A.) Pituitary gland.
B.) Thyroid gland.
C.) Adrenal gland.
D.) Hypothalamus. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The hypothalamus is described as the "master control center" because it plays a crucial role in regulating the anterior pituitary gland and influencing various endocrine functions.

### Q856. The hypothalamus produces releasing and inhibiting factors that primarily control the secretions of which gland?
A.) Thyroid gland.
B.) Posterior pituitary gland.
C.) Adrenal medulla.
D.) Anterior pituitary gland. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The hypothalamus produces various releasing hormones (e.g., GnRH, TRH) and inhibiting hormones (e.g., GHIH, PIH) that regulate the secretion of hormones from the anterior pituitary gland.

### Q857. Which two hormones are produced by the hypothalamus but stored and released by the posterior pituitary gland?
A.) Growth Hormone and Prolactin.
B.) TSH and ACTH.
C.) Insulin and Glucagon.
D.) ADH and Oxytocin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Antidiuretic Hormone (ADH) and Oxytocin are neurohormones produced by neurosecretory cells in the hypothalamus, but they are transported down axons and stored in the posterior pituitary gland until their release.

### Q858. What is the primary function of ADH (Antidiuretic Hormone)?
A.) To stimulate growth.
B.) To stimulate milk production.
C.) To regulate metabolism.
D.) To promote water reabsorption in the kidneys. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ADH (Vasopressin) primarily acts on the kidneys to increase water reabsorption, thus conserving body water and concentrating urine.

### Q859. Which hormone, produced by the hypothalamus and released by the posterior pituitary, plays a key role in uterine contractions during childbirth and milk ejection?
A.) Prolactin.
B.) Follicle-Stimulating Hormone (FSH).
C.) Luteinizing Hormone (LH).
D.) Oxytocin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Oxytocin is known for its role in stimulating uterine contractions during labor and promoting milk ejection during breastfeeding.

### Q860. Where is the pituitary gland primarily located?
A.) In the neck, anterior to the trachea.
B.) On top of the kidneys.
C.) In the pancreas.
D.) At the base of the brain. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pituitary gland is a pea-sized gland located at the base of the brain, beneath the hypothalamus, to which it is functionally and structurally connected.

### Q861. How many lobes does the pituitary gland typically have?
A.) One.
B.) Two.
C.) Four.
D.) Three. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pituitary gland consists of three lobes: the anterior lobe, the posterior lobe, and the median (intermediate) lobe.

### Q862. Which lobe of the pituitary gland is often referred to as the "master gland"?
A.) Posterior lobe.
B.) Median lobe.
C.) All lobes contribute equally.
D.) Anterior lobe. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The anterior lobe of the pituitary gland is often called the "master gland" because it produces and secretes several hormones that regulate the function of other endocrine glands, such as the thyroid, adrenal cortex, and gonads.

### Q863. Which hormone secreted by the anterior pituitary stimulates overall body growth, especially bone and muscle?
A.) TSH (Thyroid Stimulating Hormone).
B.) ACTH (Adrenocorticotropic Hormone).
C.) Prolactin.
D.) STH (Somatotropic Hormone). **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Growth Hormone (GH), also known as Somatotropic Hormone (STH), is secreted by the anterior pituitary and promotes overall body growth, particularly in bones and muscles.

### Q864. What is the condition caused by the oversecretion of Growth Hormone (GH) in adults?
A.) Dwarfism.
B.) Gigantism.
C.) Cretinism.
D.) Acromegaly. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Acromegaly is a disorder resulting from the oversecretion of GH in adults, causing abnormal growth of bones in the face, hands, and feet after epiphyseal plates have closed.

### Q865. Undersecretion of STH (Somatotropic Hormone) in children can lead to which condition?
A.) Gigantism.
B.) Acromegaly.
C.) Graves' disease.
D.) Dwarfism. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Undersecretion of Growth Hormone (GH) during childhood leads to dwarfism, characterized by stunted growth.

### Q866. Which anterior pituitary hormone stimulates the thyroid gland to produce and secrete its hormones?
A.) ACTH.
B.) FSH.
C.) LTH.
D.) TSH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thyroid Stimulating Hormone (TSH), also known as thyrotropin, is secreted by the anterior pituitary and stimulates the thyroid gland to produce and release thyroid hormones.

### Q867. Which two gonadotropic hormones are secreted by the anterior pituitary?
A.) ADH and Oxytocin.
B.) Insulin and Glucagon.
C.) Prolactin and MSH.
D.) FSH and LH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The anterior pituitary secretes two gonadotropic hormones: Follicle-Stimulating Hormone (FSH) and Luteinizing Hormone (LH), which regulate the function of the gonads.

### Q868. What is the primary function of Prolactin secreted by the anterior pituitary?
A.) To stimulate adrenal cortex.
B.) To regulate water balance.
C.) To control metabolic rate.
D.) To stimulate milk production. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Prolactin (LTH - Luteotropic Hormone) primarily stimulates and maintains milk production after childbirth.

### Q869. Which hormone is secreted by the median lobe of the pituitary gland?
A.) ACTH.
B.) ADH.
C.) Oxytocin.
D.) MSH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The median (intermediate) lobe of the pituitary gland secretes Melanocyte Stimulating Hormone (MSH).

### Q870. Which of the following hormones is released from the posterior pituitary but is actually produced in the hypothalamus?
A.) Growth Hormone.
B.) TSH.
C.) Prolactin.
D.) ADH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ADH (Antidiuretic Hormone) and Oxytocin are produced by neurosecretory cells in the hypothalamus and then transported to and stored in the posterior pituitary for release.

### Q871. Diabetes insipidus is a disorder caused by the undersecretion of which hormone?
A.) Insulin.
B.) Oxytocin.
C.) Thyroxine.
D.) ADH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Diabetes insipidus is a condition characterized by excessive urination and thirst, caused by the undersecretion of ADH (Vasopressin) from the posterior pituitary, leading to impaired water reabsorption by the kidneys.

### Q872. What is the primary function of Oxytocin?
A.) To stimulate growth.
B.) To regulate blood glucose levels.
C.) To control stress response.
D.) To stimulate uterine contractions. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Oxytocin's main functions include stimulating strong uterine contractions during labor and promoting milk ejection ("let-down") during breastfeeding.

### Q873. If a person has abnormally low levels of ADH, what would be a likely symptom?
A.) Decreased urine production.
B.) Increased water retention.
C.) Low blood pressure.
D.) Increased urine output. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Low levels of ADH would result in the kidneys reabsorbing less water, leading to increased urine output and potentially dehydration.

### Q874. What is the general term for the hormones secreted by the anterior pituitary that influence the activity of other endocrine glands?
A.) Neurotransmitters.
B.) Local hormones.
C.) Steroid hormones.
D.) Tropic hormones. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Many hormones from the anterior pituitary are "tropic hormones" (or tropins) because they target and stimulate other endocrine glands to secrete their own hormones (e.g., TSH on thyroid, ACTH on adrenal cortex).
