### Q875. Where is the thyroid gland located?
A.) At the base of the brain.
B.) On top of the kidneys.
C.) Posterior to the stomach.
D.) In the neck, anterior to the trachea. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The thyroid gland is located in the neck, anterior to the trachea, below the larynx.

### Q876. Which two hormones, containing iodine, are primarily responsible for regulating the body's metabolic rate?
A.) Calcitonin and PTH.
B.) Insulin and Glucagon.
C.) Epinephrine and Norepinephrine.
D.) Thyroxine and Triiodothyronine. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thyroxine (T4) and Triiodothyronine (T3) are the two iodine-containing hormones produced by the thyroid gland that play a crucial role in regulating basal metabolic rate.

### Q877. What is the primary function of Calcitonin, secreted by the thyroid gland?
A.) To increase blood calcium levels.
B.) To regulate metabolic rate.
C.) To stimulate growth.
D.) To decrease blood calcium levels. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Calcitonin is involved in calcium homeostasis; it works to decrease blood calcium levels by inhibiting osteoclast activity and promoting calcium deposition in bones.

### Q878. Graves' disease is a condition characterized by hyperthyroidism. Which of the following symptoms is commonly associated with it?
A.) Weight gain and sluggishness.
B.) Intolerance to cold and constipation.
C.) Decreased heart rate and lethargy.
D.) Weight loss and increased metabolic rate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Graves' disease is associated with hyperthyroidism (overactive thyroid), which leads to symptoms like increased metabolic rate, weight loss, heat intolerance, and nervousness.

### Q879. Cretinism, a condition characterized by stunted physical and mental growth in children, is caused by:
A.) Hyperthyroidism.
B.) Oversecretion of growth hormone.
C.) Lack of ADH.
D.) Hypothyroidism. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cretinism is caused by severe hypothyroidism (underactive thyroid) during infancy or early childhood, leading to impaired physical and mental development.

### Q880. Where are the parathyroid glands typically located?
A.) On the anterior surface of the thyroid gland.
B.) On top of the adrenal glands.
C.) Within the pancreas.
D.) Embedded in the posterior surface of the thyroid gland. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The parathyroid glands are usually four small glands embedded in the posterior surface of the thyroid gland.

### Q881. What is the main hormone secreted by the parathyroid glands?
A.) Calcitonin.
B.) Thyroxine.
C.) ACTH.
D.) PTH. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The parathyroid glands secrete Parathyroid Hormone (PTH), which is the primary regulator of calcium levels in the blood.

### Q882. What is the primary function of Parathyroid Hormone?
A.) To lower blood glucose levels.
B.) To decrease blood calcium levels.
C.) To stimulate metabolism.
D.) To increase blood calcium levels. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — PTH acts to increase blood calcium levels by stimulating osteoclasts to break down bone, enhancing calcium reabsorption in the kidneys, and promoting the synthesis of vitamin D, which aids in intestinal calcium absorption.

### Q883. The endocrine functions of the pancreas are carried out by specialized clusters of cells called:
A.) Acini.
B.) Pancreatic ducts.
C.) Gastric pits.
D.) Islets of Langerhans. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The endocrine cells of the pancreas are organized into clusters called Islets of Langerhans, which produce hormones.

### Q884. Which hormone is secreted by the beta cells of the Islets of Langerhans?
A.) Glucagon.
B.) Somatostatin.
C.) Pancreatic polypeptide.
D.) Insulin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Insulin, secreted by the beta cells of the Islets of Langerhans, acts to lower blood glucose levels by promoting glucose uptake by cells and its conversion to glycogen.

### Q885. Which hormone raises blood glucose levels?
A.) Insulin.
B.) Somatostatin.
C.) Amylin.
D.) Glucagon. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Glucagon, secreted by the alpha cells of the Islets of Langerhans, raises blood glucose levels primarily by stimulating the liver to release stored glucose (glycogenolysis) and produce new glucose (gluconeogenesis).

### Q886. When blood glucose levels are high, which hormone is released to bring them back to normal?
A.) Glucagon.
B.) Adrenaline.
C.) Cortisol.
D.) Insulin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Insulin is released in response to high blood glucose levels to facilitate glucose uptake by cells and lower blood sugar.

### Q887. What type of feedback mechanism controls the secretion of both insulin and glucagon to maintain blood glucose homeostasis?
A.) Positive feedback.
B.) Neural feedback.
C.) Hormonal feedback.
D.) Negative feedback. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The secretion of both insulin and glucagon is regulated by a negative feedback mechanism to maintain stable blood glucose levels. When glucose is high, insulin is released to lower it; when glucose is low, glucagon is released to raise it, thus opposing the initial change.

### Q888. Which two main subdivisions make up the adrenal gland?
A.) Anterior and Posterior.
B.) Alpha and Beta.
C.) Inner and Outer.
D.) Cortex and Medulla. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The adrenal gland is divided into two main parts: the outer adrenal cortex and the inner adrenal medulla.

### Q889. Which part of the adrenal gland secretes catecholamines like Epinephrine (Adrenaline) and Norepinephrine (Noradrenaline)?
A.) Adrenal cortex.
B.) Zona glomerulosa.
C.) Zona fasciculata.
D.) Adrenal medulla. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The adrenal medulla is responsible for secreting the catecholamines Epinephrine and Norepinephrine, which are involved in the "fight or flight" response.

### Q890. Which hormone, secreted by the adrenal cortex, is a glucocorticoid?
A.) Aldosterone.
B.) Epinephrine.
C.) Norepinephrine.
D.) Cortisol. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cortisol is a glucocorticoid hormone produced by the adrenal cortex that plays a vital role in metabolism, immune response, and the body's response to stress.

### Q891. Which mineralocorticoid hormone, secreted by the adrenal cortex, is crucial for regulating sodium and potassium balance and blood pressure?
A.) Cortisol.
B.) Adrenaline.
C.) Androgen.
D.) Aldosterone. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Aldosterone, a mineralocorticoid from the adrenal cortex, is essential for maintaining electrolyte balance by regulating sodium and potassium levels in the blood, thereby influencing blood pressure.

### Q892. Androgens, which are male sex hormones, are also produced in small amounts by which part of the adrenal gland?
A.) Adrenal medulla.
B.) Pituitary gland.
C.) Pancreas.
D.) Adrenal cortex. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The adrenal cortex also produces small amounts of androgens, which are male sex hormones, in both males and females.

### Q893. What is the primary male sex hormone produced by the testes?
A.) Estrogen.
B.) Progesterone.
C.) Inhibin.
D.) Testosterone. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Testosterone is the primary male sex hormone secreted by the interstitial cells of the testes.

### Q894. Which hormone secreted by the ovaries is primarily responsible for the development of female secondary sex characteristics and the regulation of the menstrual cycle?
A.) Testosterone.
B.) Progesterone.
C.) Inhibin.
D.) Estrogens. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Estrogens are the female sex hormones primarily produced by the ovaries, responsible for the development of female secondary sex characteristics and various aspects of the menstrual cycle.

### Q895. Which ovarian hormone is mainly secreted by the corpus luteum after ovulation and is crucial for preparing and maintaining the uterus for pregnancy?
A.) Estrogen.
B.) Testosterone.
C.) FSH.
D.) Progesterone. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Progesterone is produced by the corpus luteum (and later the placenta during pregnancy) and is vital for preparing the uterine lining for implantation and maintaining pregnancy.

### Q896. What is the primary function of Inhibin, produced by both testes and ovaries?
A.) To stimulate FSH secretion.
B.) To stimulate LH secretion.
C.) To stimulate estrogen production.
D.) To inhibit FSH secretion from the pituitary. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Inhibin, secreted by Sertoli cells in the testes and granulosa cells in the ovaries, acts to inhibit the secretion of Follicle-Stimulating Hormone (FSH) from the anterior pituitary.

### Q897. Undersecretion of progesterone during the menstrual cycle may lead to:
A.) Development of fibroids.
B.) Increased chance of pregnancy.
C.) Male sterility.
D.) Early menstruation. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Undersecretion of progesterone during the menstrual cycle decreases the chance of pregnancy and may cause early menstruation or, if pregnancy occurs, may lead to miscarriage.

### Q898. Which hormone is primarily secreted by the Pineal Gland and helps regulate circadian rhythm?
A.) Thyroxin.
B.) Thymosin.
C.) Renin.
D.) Melatonin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pineal gland secretes Melatonin, a hormone that plays a crucial role in regulating the body's circadian rhythms, including sleep-wake cycles.

### Q899. The Thymus gland secretes hormones called Thymosins, which are vital for the development and maturation of which type of immune cell?
A.) B lymphocytes.
B.) Macrophages.
C.) Plasma cells.
D.) T lymphocytes. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thymosins, secreted by the thymus gland, are essential for the maturation and differentiation of T lymphocytes (T cells), which are crucial components of the immune system.

### Q900. The heart produces hormones known as Natriuretic Peptides. What is their primary role?
A.) To increase heart rate.
B.) To stimulate red blood cell production.
C.) To increase water reabsorption in kidneys.
D.) To lower blood pressure. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The heart secretes natriuretic peptides (e.g., ANP), which primarily act to lower blood pressure and blood volume by promoting sodium and water excretion by the kidneys.

### Q901. Which hormone is produced by the kidneys and stimulates the production of red blood cells in the bone marrow?
A.) Renin.
B.) Calcitriol.
C.) Aldosterone.
D.) Erythropoietin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Erythropoietin (EPO) is a hormone secreted by the kidneys in response to low oxygen levels, which stimulates the bone marrow to produce more red blood cells.

### Q902. Which active form of Vitamin D is produced by the kidneys and is essential for calcium absorption in the intestine?
A.) Calcitonin.
B.) Parathyroid Hormone.
C.) Vitamin D2.
D.) Calcitriol. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The kidneys convert an inactive form of Vitamin D into its active form, Calcitriol, which is crucial for the absorption of calcium from the digestive tract.

### Q903. Adipose tissue (fat tissue) secretes a hormone that plays a role in regulating appetite and metabolism. What is this hormone called?
A.) Ghrelin.
B.) Insulin.
C.) Gastrin.
D.) Leptin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Adipose tissue produces and secretes Leptin, a hormone that signals satiety to the brain, helping to regulate energy balance and metabolism.

### Q904. The digestive tract produces numerous hormones that regulate digestion and absorption. Which of the following is an example of a hormone produced by the stomach that stimulates gastric acid secretion?
A.) Secretin.
B.) Cholecystokinin (CCK).
C.) Somatostatin.
D.) Gastrin. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Gastrin is a hormone produced by the stomach that stimulates the secretion of gastric acid (HCl) by the parietal cells, playing a key role in digestion.

## Digestion

### Q905. The digestive system consists of the GI tract and what other group of organs?
A.) Visceral support organs
B.) Major endocrine organs
C.) Abdominal cavity organs
D.) Accessory digestive organs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The digestive system is divided into the GI tract (a continuous tube) and accessory organs like the liver and pancreas that aid the process.

### Q906. What is the correct anatomical sequence of the small intestine's segments?
A.) Ileum, Jejunum, Duodenum
B.) Jejunum, Ileum, Duodenum
C.) Duodenum, Ileum, Jejunum
D.) Duodenum, Jejunum, Ileum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The correct order is Duodenum (first), Jejunum (middle), and Ileum (last).

### Q907. The vermiform appendix is a small, blind tube attached to which structure?
A.) Sigmoid colon
B.) Stomach pylorus
C.) Gallbladder
D.) Cecum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vermiform appendix is attached to the cecum, which is the first part of the large intestine.

### Q908. Which opening marks the junction between the esophagus and the stomach?
A.) Pyloric opening
B.) Ileocecal junction
C.) Oral fauces
D.) Gastro-oesophageal opening **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This opening, also called the cardiac opening, is where the esophagus joins the stomach.

### Q909. From the lumen outward, what is the correct order of the GI tract's four layers?
A.) Serosa, Muscularis, Submucosa, Mucosa
B.) Submucosa, Muscularis, Mucosa, Serosa
C.) Muscularis, Mucosa, Serosa, Submucosa
D.) Mucosa, Submucosa, Muscularis, Serosa **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The correct sequence from the inside out is Mucosa, Submucosa, Muscularis, and Serosa.

### Q910. What is the primary function of the stratified squamous epithelium lining the mouth?
A.) Secretion of enzymes
B.) Absorption of nutrients
C.) Detection of taste
D.) Protection from abrasion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The multiple layers of this epithelium are ideal for resisting the physical wear and tear from chewing.

### Q911. What is the fleshy projection that hangs from the soft palate?
A.) Epiglottis
B.) Palatine tonsil
C.) Larynx
D.) Uvula **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The uvula is the projection from the soft palate that helps block the nasal cavity during swallowing.

### Q912. The common hepatic duct and the cystic duct merge to form which duct?
A.) Pancreatic duct
B.) Hepatic portal vein
C.) Right hepatic duct
D.) Common bile duct **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The common bile duct is formed by the union of the liver's common hepatic duct and the gallbladder's cystic duct.

### Q913. What is the main digestive function of the liver?
A.) Insulin secretion
B.) Enzyme production
C.) Chyme storage
D.) Bile production **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The liver's primary role in digestion is to produce bile, which is essential for fat emulsification.

### Q914. Salivary amylase begins the chemical digestion of which macromolecule?
A.) Proteins
B.) Lipids
C.) Nucleic acids
D.) Carbohydrates **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Salivary amylase specifically targets starch (a carbohydrate) and breaks it down into smaller sugars.

### Q915. What is the main purpose of mastication (chewing)?
A.) Begin protein digestion
B.) Mix food with stomach acid
C.) Absorb food nutrients
D.) Increase food surface area **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chewing breaks large food particles into smaller ones, providing more surface area for digestive enzymes to act upon.

### Q916. What is the term for the chewed, moistened ball of food that is swallowed?
A.) Chyme
B.) Micelle
C.) Faeces
D.) Bolus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A bolus is the mass of chewed food that is pushed to the back of the throat for swallowing.

### Q917. Which structure elevates to block the nasal cavity during swallowing?
A.) Epiglottis
B.) Larynx
C.) Tongue
D.) Soft palate **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Muscles raise the soft palate to close off the nasopharynx, preventing food from entering the nasal passages.

### Q918. Which flap of cartilage covers the airway during swallowing?
A.) Uvula
B.) Soft palate
C.) Pharynx
D.) Epiglottis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The epiglottis folds down over the larynx to ensure food travels down the esophagus and not the trachea.

### Q919. What are the rhythmic, wave-like muscular contractions that move food through the GI tract?
A.) Segmentation
B.) Mastication
C.) Deglutition
D.) Peristalsis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Peristalsis is the coordinated muscular wave that propels food forward through the digestive tube.

### Q920. Which stomach cells are responsible for secreting hydrochloric acid (HCl)?
A.) Chief cells
B.) Goblet cells
C.) Endocrine cells
D.) Parietal cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Parietal (or oxyntic) cells are specialized epithelial cells in the stomach that secrete HCl and intrinsic factor.

### Q921. Chief (zymogenic) cells in the stomach secrete which inactive enzyme?
A.) Trypsinogen
B.) Gastrin
C.) Mucus
D.) Pepsinogen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chief cells secrete pepsinogen, which is the inactive precursor to the protein-digesting enzyme pepsin.

### Q922. What is a primary function of hydrochloric acid in the stomach?
A.) Begin carbohydrate digestion
B.) Emulsify lipids
C.) Absorb vitamin B12
D.) Kill bacteria and activate pepsin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — HCl creates a low pH environment that kills most ingested bacteria and converts inactive pepsinogen into active pepsin.

### Q923. What is the semi-fluid, acidic mixture of food and gastric secretions in the stomach called?
A.) Bolus
B.) Bile
C.) Faeces
D.) Chyme **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ingested food and stomach secretions mix to form a semi-fluid material called chyme.

### Q924. Which substance is essential for the absorption of vitamin B12 in the ileum?
A.) Gastrin
B.) Mucin
C.) Secretin
D.) Intrinsic factor **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Intrinsic factor, secreted by parietal cells, binds with vitamin B12 to allow its absorption.

### Q925. How is the stomach's epithelial wall protected from its own acid?
A.) By a keratinized outer layer
B.) By rapid water secretion
C.) By the presence of bile salts
D.) By a thick mucus layer **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A thick layer of viscous and alkaline mucus protects the stomach wall from the damaging effect of acid and pepsin.

### Q926. What is the optimal pH for the enzymatic activity of pepsin?
A.) Around 4.5
B.) Around 6.5
C.) 7.0 or more
D.) 3.0 or less **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pepsin functions effectively in the highly acidic conditions of the stomach, with peak activity at a very low pH.

### Q927. The low pH of the stomach inactivates which enzyme from the oral cavity?
A.) Pepsin
B.) Lipase
C.) Trypsin
D.) Salivary amylase **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Salivary amylase, which works at a neutral pH, is denatured by the strong acidity of the stomach.

### Q928. Where does the greatest amount of nutrient absorption occur?
A.) Stomach
B.) Large intestine
C.) Esophagus
D.) Small intestine **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The small intestine, with its massive surface area, is the site where most digestion and absorption takes place.

### Q929. Which two segments of the small intestine are the major sites of nutrient absorption?
A.) Jejunum and Ileum
B.) Ileum and Cecum
C.) Duodenum and Ileum
D.) Duodenum and Jejunum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explicitly states that the duodenum and jejunum are the primary locations for absorbing digested nutrients.

### Q930. The presence of acidic chyme in the duodenum stimulates the release of which hormone?
A.) Gastrin
B.) Insulin
C.) Cholecystokinin (CCK)
D.) Secretin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Secretin is released in response to acid and signals the pancreas to secrete bicarbonate to neutralize the chyme.

### Q931. The arrival of fats in the duodenum initiates the release of which two hormones?
A.) Secretin and Gastrin
B.) Insulin and Glucagon
C.) Somatostatin and Pepsin
D.) GIP and Cholecystokinin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Fats and lipids trigger the release of both gastric inhibitory peptide (GIP) and cholecystokinin (CCK).

### Q932. What is the primary role of bile salts in digestion?
A.) Digest proteins
B.) Neutralize chyme
C.) Break down starches
D.) Emulsify fats **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Bile salts break down large fat masses into smaller droplets, a process called emulsification, increasing the surface area for enzymes.

### Q933. What is the main function of the gallbladder?
A.) Produce bile
B.) Produce digestive enzymes
C.) Detoxify chemicals
D.) Store and concentrate bile **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The gallbladder stores bile from the liver and concentrates it by absorbing water and electrolytes.

### Q934. The bile pigment bilirubin is formed from the breakdown of what molecule?
A.) Glycogen
B.) Albumin
C.) Cholesterol
D.) Hemoglobin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When old red blood cells are broken down, the heme portion of hemoglobin is converted into bilirubin.

### Q935. What is the process of converting excess glucose into glycogen in the liver called?
A.) Glycogenolysis
B.) Gluconeogenesis
C.) Glycolysis
D.) Glycogenesis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Glycogenesis (glycogen formation) is the synthesis of glycogen from glucose molecules for storage.

### Q936. Which organ synthesizes blood clotting proteins like prothrombin and fibrinogen?
A.) Pancreas
B.) Bone marrow
C.) Spleen
D.) Liver **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The liver synthesizes most plasma proteins, including the essential factors for the blood coagulation cascade.

### Q937. In the liver, toxic ammonia is converted into which less toxic substance?
A.) Uric acid
B.) Creatinine
C.) Bilirubin
D.) Urea **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The liver's urea cycle combines ammonia with CO2 to form urea, which is safely transported in the blood.

### Q938. What is the main function of bicarbonate ions in pancreatic juice?
A.) Emulsify fats
B.) Digest proteins
C.) Activate enzymes
D.) Neutralize acidic chyme **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Bicarbonate raises the pH of acidic chyme from the stomach, creating an optimal environment for pancreatic enzymes.

### Q939. The inactive enzyme trypsinogen is activated to trypsin by what?
A.) Pepsin
B.) Chymotrypsin
C.) Lipase
D.) Enterokinase **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Enterokinase, an enzyme on the brush border of the small intestine, activates trypsinogen to start the protease cascade.

### Q940. Which pancreatic enzyme continues the digestion of polysaccharides?
A.) Pancreatic lipase
B.) Trypsin
C.) Nuclease
D.) Pancreatic amylase **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pancreatic amylase continues to break down starches and glycogen into smaller sugars in the small intestine.

### Q941. Why are proteases like trypsin secreted from the pancreas in an inactive form?
A.) To conserve metabolic energy
B.) To function in an acidic pH
C.) To allow for longer storage
D.) To prevent pancreatic self-digestion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — If secreted in their active forms, these enzymes would digest the proteins of the pancreatic cells, causing autodigestion.

### Q942. What are the final absorbable products of carbohydrate digestion?
A.) Polysaccharides
B.) Disaccharides
C.) Amino acids
D.) Monosaccharides **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Polysaccharides must be broken down into their simplest units, monosaccharides (like glucose), to be absorbed.

### Q943. What are the tiny structures formed by bile salts that ferry lipids to intestinal cells?
A.) Chylomicrons
B.) Lipoproteins
C.) Lacteals
D.) Micelles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Micelles are microscopic aggregates that solubilize lipids and facilitate their contact with the absorptive cells.

### Q944. Inside intestinal cells, absorbed fats are packaged with proteins into what structures?
A.) Micelles
B.) Free fatty acids
C.) HDLs
D.) Chylomicrons **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Chylomicrons are lipoprotein particles assembled within intestinal cells to transport dietary lipids into the lymphatic system.

### Q945. Unlike other nutrients, absorbed fats in chylomicrons first enter the:
A.) Hepatic portal vein
B.) Blood capillaries
C.) Gallbladder
D.) Lymphatic system **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Due to their large size, chylomicrons are transported via lymphatic vessels (lacteals), bypassing the liver initially.

### Q946. Where does the final breakdown of dipeptides and tripeptides into amino acids occur?
A.) In the intestinal lumen
B.) In the stomach
C.) In the liver
D.) Inside intestinal epithelial cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Dipeptides and tripeptides are absorbed into intestinal cells, where intracellular enzymes complete their digestion.

### Q947. By which process is water primarily absorbed from the digestive tract?
A.) Active transport
B.) Facilitated diffusion
C.) Endocytosis
D.) Osmosis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water follows the movement of absorbed solutes, moving down its concentration gradient via osmosis.

### Q948. What are the primary functions of the large intestine?
A.) Protein digestion
B.) Nutrient absorption
C.) Enzyme secretion
D.) Water absorption and feces formation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The large intestine absorbs water and electrolytes from chyme to form solid feces for elimination.

### Q949. What stimulus directly initiates the defecation reflex?
A.) Contraction of the stomach
B.) Hormones from the duodenum
C.) A conscious decision
D.) Stretching of the rectal wall **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The distention of the rectum's wall by feces stimulates stretch receptors, triggering the reflex.

### Q950. Which anal sphincter is under voluntary, conscious control?
A.) Internal anal sphincter
B.) Pyloric sphincter
C.) Ileocecal sphincter
D.) External anal sphincter **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The external anal sphincter is made of skeletal muscle, which allows for conscious control over defecation.

### Q951. Bacteria in the colon synthesize which essential, fat-soluble vitamin?
A.) Vitamin C
B.) Vitamin D
C.) Vitamin B12
D.) Vitamin K **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Some bacteria in the intestine synthesize vitamin K, which is then passively absorbed in the colon.

### Q952. Which stomach hormone stimulates secretion from both parietal and chief cells?
A.) Secretin
B.) Cholecystokinin
C.) Insulin
D.) Gastrin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Gastrin acts on the stomach to powerfully stimulate the secretion of both hydrochloric acid and pepsinogen.

### Q953. What are the primary stimuli for the greatest volume of gastric secretion?
A.) The sight and smell of food
B.) Chyme entering the duodenum
C.) Stress and other emotions
D.) Stomach distention and amino acids **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The presence of food, causing distention and introducing peptides, is the main trigger for the gastric phase of secretion.

### Q954. Cholecystokinin (CCK) stimulates the pancreas to release a secretion rich in what?
A.) Bicarbonate ions
B.) Water
C.) Mucus
D.) Digestive enzymes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — CCK is the main stimulus for the acinar cells of the pancreas to secrete their enzymatic component.

### Q955. The bacterium *Helicobacter pylori* is the most important factor in which disorder?
A.) Food poisoning
B.) Dyspepsia
C.) Appendicitis
D.) Peptic ulcer disease **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that H. pylori accounts for 90% of duodenal ulcers and 70% of gastric ulcers.

### Q956. Which eating disorder involves binging followed by self-induced vomiting or purging?
A.) Anorexia nervosa
B.) Obesity
C.) Dyspepsia
D.) Bulimia nervosa **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Bulimia nervosa is characterized by bouts of overeating followed by compensatory behaviors like purging.

### Q957. What is the medical term for incomplete or imperfect digestion causing abdominal discomfort?
A.) Ulcer
B.) Food poisoning
C.) Peritonitis
D.) Dyspepsia **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Dyspepsia is the general term for indigestion and its associated symptoms.

### Q958. Blockage of the common bile duct would most severely impair the digestion of what?
A.) Proteins
B.) Carbohydrates
C.) Nucleic acids
D.) Lipids **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Bile is essential for emulsifying fats. Without it, fat digestion by lipase would be extremely inefficient.

### Q959. Which organ produces both digestive enzymes and acid-neutralizing bicarbonate?
A.) Liver
B.) Stomach
C.) Small intestine
D.) Pancreas **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pancreas secretes both an enzymatic component and an aqueous bicarbonate component into the duodenum.

### Q960. A hiatal hernia involves a portion of the stomach protruding through the:
A.) Pyloric sphincter
B.) Ileocecal valve
C.) Abdominal wall
D.) Oesophageal hiatus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This condition involves the widening of the opening in the diaphragm for the esophagus.

### Q961. What is the primary function of the ileocecal sphincter?
A.) Control bile release
B.) Control stomach emptying
C.) Control chyme flow to the illeum
D.) Control chyme flow to the cecum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This sphincter regulates the passage of material from the ileum to the large intestine.

### Q962. A chylomicron, which is mostly lipid, would be classified as having what type of density?
A.) High density (HDL)
B.) Low density (LDL)
C.) Intermediate density
D.) Extremely very low density **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Because lipids are less dense than proteins, a particle made almost entirely of lipid has a very low density.

### Q963. Which hormone primarily stimulates the pancreas to release a bicarbonate-rich secretion?
A.) Gastrin
B.) Cholecystokinin
C.) Gastric inhibitory peptide
D.) Secretin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Secretin is the principal hormone that stimulates pancreatic duct cells to secrete bicarbonate.

### Q964. An inability to produce intrinsic factor would lead to a deficiency in which vitamin?
A.) Vitamin K
B.) Vitamin C
C.) Vitamin A
D.) Vitamin B12 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Intrinsic factor is absolutely required for the absorption of Vitamin B12 in the ileum.

### Q965. The liver can synthesize Vitamin A from which precursor molecule?
A.) Amino acids
B.) Fatty acids
C.) Cholesterol
D.) Carotene **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that the synthesis of Vitamin A from carotene takes place in the liver.

### Q966. The reversal of peristalsis, known as antiperistalsis, can result in what action?
A.) Diarrhea
B.) Absorption
C.) Constipation
D.) Vomiting **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Antiperistalsis is the physiological mechanism that forcefully moves stomach contents upward, leading to vomiting.

### Q967. Where does the final digestion of proteins into single amino acids occur?
A.) In the pancreatic juice
B.) In the gastric juice
C.) Within the liver cells
D.) At the intestinal brush border **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Peptidases bound to the microvilli (brush border) complete the breakdown of small peptides.

### Q968. Why is defecation an automatic reflex in infants?
A.) Lack of control over the internal anal sphincter
B.) Lack of control over the ileocecal sphincter
C.) Lack of control over the rectal muscles
D.) Lack of control over the external anal sphincter **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Voluntary control of the skeletal muscle of the external anal sphincter is not yet developed in infants.

### Q969. Which substance synthesized by the liver acts as an anticoagulant?
A.) Fibrinogen
B.) Prothrombin
C.) Vitamin K
D.) Heparin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Heparin, produced by the liver, prevents blood from clotting inside blood vessels.

### Q970. The Nobel Prize in 2005 recognized the discovery of which bacterium's role in ulcers?
A.) Salmonella enterica
B.) Campylobacter jejuni
C.) Escherichia coli
D.) Helicobacter pylori **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Barry Marshall and Robin Warren won the Nobel Prize for demonstrating that *H. pylori* is responsible for most peptic ulcers.

### Q971. What are the three major pairs of salivary glands?
A.) Buccal, lingual, and palatine
B.) Gastric, duodenal, and intestinal
C.) Hepatic, cystic, and pancreatic
D.) Parotid, submandibular, and sublingual **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The parotid, submandibular, and sublingual glands are the three major pairs that produce the bulk of saliva.

### Q972. What is the primary function of the gastric folds known as rugae?
A.) Increase absorptive area
B.) Secrete hydrochloric acid
C.) Anchor the stomach wall
D.) Allow stomach expansion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The rugae are folds that flatten out as the stomach fills, allowing it to increase its volume to accommodate a meal.

### Q973. What type of epithelium covers the surface of an intestinal villus?
A.) Stratified squamous
B.) Simple cuboidal
C.) Transitional
D.) Simple columnar **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The villus is covered by a single layer of simple columnar epithelial cells, specialized for absorption.

### Q974. The endocrine function of the pancreas is performed by which structures?
A.) Acini cells
B.) Kupffer cells
C.) Ductal cells
D.) Pancreatic islets **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pancreatic islets (of Langerhans) are the distinct clusters of endocrine cells within the pancreas.

### Q975. The stomach's muscularis layer contains a third layer of what type of muscle?
A.) Skeletal muscle
B.) Diagonal muscle
C.) Transverse muscle
D.) Oblique muscle **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The stomach's three smooth muscle layers (longitudinal, circular, and oblique) allow for complex churning movements.

### Q976. Besides stimulating the pancreas, cholecystokinin (CCK) also causes what action?
A.) Stomach acid secretion
B.) Hepatic glucose production
C.) LES sphincter relaxation
D.) Gallbladder contraction **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — CCK is the main hormone for gallbladder contraction, ensuring bile is released when fat is in the duodenum.

### Q977. Immunoglobulin and lysozyme in saliva contribute to what function?
A.) Digesting starch
B.) Lubricating food
C.) Neutralizing acids
D.) Preventing bacterial infection **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Immunoglobulins (antibodies) and lysozyme (an antibacterial enzyme) provide a first line of defense in the mouth.

### Q978. How is ingested cellulose handled by the human digestive system?
A.) It is digested by pancreatic amylase
B.) It is absorbed in the large intestine
C.) It is broken down by stomach acid
D.) It passes through largely undigested **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Humans lack the enzyme cellulase, so cellulose serves as dietary fiber and passes through mostly undigested.

### Q979. The 'brush border' of the small intestine is formed by what structures?
A.) Villi
B.) Goblet cells
C.) Lacteals
D.) Microvilli **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Microvilli are microscopic projections on absorptive cells that form the brush border, increasing surface area.

### Q980. Anorexia nervosa is a serious eating disorder primarily characterized by what?
A.) Binging and purging
B.) Excessive food intake
C.) Imperfect digestion
D.) Fear of becoming obese **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The core feature of anorexia nervosa is a distorted body image and an intense fear of gaining weight, leading to severe food restriction.

### Q981. The removal of amino groups from amino acids, or deamination, occurs in which organ?
A.) Kidneys
B.) Small intestine
C.) Stomach
D.) Liver **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The liver is the primary site for deamination, processing amino acids for energy use.

### Q982. Which pancreatic cells produce the exocrine secretion known as pancreatic juice?
A.) Islets of Langerhans
B.) Parietal cells
C.) Hepatocytes
D.) Acini cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The acinar cells are the exocrine cells of the pancreas that synthesize and secrete digestive enzymes.

### Q983. Gluconeogenesis in the liver refers to the synthesis of glucose from what?
A.) The breakdown of glycogen
B.) Fructose and galactose
C.) The breakdown of starch
D.) Non-carbohydrate sources **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Gluconeogenesis ('new glucose formation') creates glucose from sources like amino acids and glycerol.

### Q984. What is the primary digestive function of the pharynx?
A.) Secrete mucus and amylase
B.) Begin protein digestion
C.) Absorb water and electrolytes
D.) Act as a passageway for the bolus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pharynx is a muscular tube that propels the bolus from the oral cavity to the esophagus during swallowing.

### Q985. What is the straight, muscular tube that precedes the anal canal?
A.) Sigmoid colon
B.) Appendix
C.) Descending colon
D.) Rectum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The rectum is the terminal section of the colon that stores feces before defecation.

### Q986. What are the main products of lipid digestion by pancreatic lipase?
A.) Amino acids
B.) Monosaccharides
C.) Bile salts and cholesterol
D.) Free fatty acids and glycerol **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pancreatic lipase breaks down triacylglycerols into their main components: free fatty acids and glycerol.

### Q987. What are the gentle, 20-second interval contractions in the stomach called?
A.) Segmentation contractions
B.) Mass movements
C.) Tonic contractions
D.) Mixing waves **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — These gentle waves mix ingested material with gastric secretions, distinct from stronger peristaltic waves.

### Q988. Which group of vitamins is stored in the liver?
A.) C and most B vitamins
B.) A and C only
C.) K and B6 only
D.) A, D, E, K, and B12 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text lists the fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K) and vitamin B12 as being stored by hepatocytes.

### Q989. Food poisoning from *Salmonella* often occurs due to what?
A.) Excessive stomach acid
B.) An autoimmune reaction
C.) Self-induced vomiting
D.) Cross-contamination from raw meat **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text highlights the risk of liquid from defrosting meat containing bacteria contaminating other foods or utensils.

### Q990. What is the medical term for having an abnormal amount of body fat?
A.) Anorexia nervosa
B.) Dyspepsia
C.) Jaundice
D.) Obesity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Obesity is the term for having excess body fat and is linked to disorders like diabetes and cardiovascular disease.

### Q991. Where are the phagocytic Kupffer cells that destroy old red blood cells located?
A.) Spleen
B.) Pancreas
C.) Small intestine
D.) Liver **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Kupffer cells are the resident macrophages of the liver sinusoids.

### Q992. What is the main role of the lower esophageal sphincter?
A.) Regulate stomach emptying
B.) Initiate swallowing
C.) Mix the bolus with mucus
D.) Prevent acid reflux **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This sphincter remains contracted to prevent acidic stomach contents from backing up into the esophagus.

### Q993. What is the term for gases produced by bacterial action in the colon?
A.) Haustra
B.) Faeces
C.) Chyme
D.) Flatus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text defines flatus as the gases produced by microorganisms in the colon.

### Q994. Which enzymes in pancreatic juice digest DNA and RNA?
A.) Amylase and lipase
B.) Trypsin and chymotrypsin
C.) Pepsin and gastrin
D.) Deoxyribonucleases and ribonucleases **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — These enzymes specifically break down DNA and RNA, respectively, into their component nucleotides.

### Q995. Hyperplastic obesity is a condition characterized by what?
A.) An increase in fat cell size
B.) Uneven fat distribution
C.) A decreased metabolic rate
D.) A greater number of fat cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hyperplastic obesity is defined as having a greater than normal number of fat cells.

## Biotechnology

### Q996. Which of these is a primary method used in biotechnology to produce vaccines?
A.) Extracting antibodies from recovered patients
B.) Weakening a virus by exposing it to heat
C.) Amplifying the entire viral genome using PCR
D.) Synthesizing a specific antigen using a cloned gene **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text lists three methods for vaccine development, one of which is the 'synthesis of an antigen with the help of a cloned gene'.

### Q997. What marketable vaccine is explicitly mentioned as being a product of transgenic bacteria?
A.) Polio vaccine
B.) MMR vaccine
C.) Tetanus vaccine
D.) Hepatitis B vaccine **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explicitly lists the 'hepatitis B vaccine' as a biotechnology product produced by bacteria that is now on the market.

### Q998. In vaccine development, what is the role of monoclonal antibodies?
A.) To act as the main ingredient in the vaccine
B.) To deliver a cloned gene into bacteria
C.) To diagnose the disease before vaccination
D.) To prepare a pure antigen for the vaccine **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that one method of vaccine development is the 'preparation of a pure antigen using a specific monoclonal antibody'.

### Q999. Besides complete antigens, what smaller synthesized molecules can also be used as vaccines?
A.) Plasmids
B.) Liposomes
C.) Protoplasts
D.) Peptides **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text mentions that the 'synthesis of peptides to be used as vaccines' is one of the three main biotechnological approaches.

### Q1000. The creation of an antigen from a cloned gene is a direct application of what technology?
A.) Polymerase Chain Reaction
B.) Gene Therapy
C.) DNA Sequencing
D.) Recombinant DNA technology **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Producing a protein (antigen) from a foreign gene inserted into a host is the central principle of recombinant DNA technology.

### Q1001. What is the main function of a DNA probe in disease diagnosis?
A.) To cut the patient's DNA at specific sites
B.) To replicate a segment of DNA millions of times
C.) To carry a therapeutic gene into a patient's cells
D.) To find and bind with a complementary DNA strand **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A probe is a single-stranded nucleotide sequence designed to hybridize (pair) with a specific piece of DNA, allowing for its detection.

### Q1002. According to the text, PCR can be used as a tool to diagnose which conditions?
A.) A patient's blood type and pH levels
B.) Antibody concentrations after vaccination
C.) Blood sugar levels and nutritional deficiencies
D.) Viral infections, genetic disorders, and cancer **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explicitly lists that PCR can be used to 'diagnose viral infections, genetic disorders, and cancer'.

### Q1003. The unique patterns of DNA fragments used in DNA fingerprinting are known as:
A.) cDNA
B.) Plasmids
C.) Palindromic Sequences
D.) RFLPs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text defines 'restriction fragment length polymorphism (RFLPs)' as the unique collection of different sized fragments that exists between individuals.

### Q1004. What technique allows for the analysis of a minuscule DNA sample from a crime scene?
A.) Gene therapy
B.) Anther culture
C.) Gel electrophoresis
D.) Polymerase Chain Reaction **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text highlights that PCR is used to amplify DNA several times, making it possible to analyze even very small samples.

### Q1005. How are DNA probes made detectable in a diagnostic test?
A.) They are stained with a colored protein dye
B.) They naturally glow when bound to DNA
C.) They are attached to large metal particles
D.) They are made radioactive **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text clearly states that a probe is 'either radioactive or fluorescent' to make its location detectable.

### Q1006. Which of these is NOT listed as a product of genetic engineering for disease treatment?
A.) Human insulin
B.) Human growth hormone
C.) Antithrombin III
D.) Quinine **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Quinine is mentioned as a product of plant cell suspension cultures, but not as a product of genetic engineering for disease treatment in the same context as the others.

### Q1007. What is the term for using transgenic farm animals to produce pharmaceuticals?
A.) Gene therapy
B.) Bioleaching
C.) Cloning
D.) Gene pharming **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explicitly defines 'gene pharming' as 'the use of transgenic farm animals to produce pharmaceuticals'.

### Q1008. What is the most common approach in gene therapy for correcting a faulty gene?
A.) Removing the faulty gene entirely from all cells
B.) Using specific drugs to reactivate the faulty gene
C.) Swapping the abnormal gene with a normal one
D.) Inserting a normal gene into a nonspecific location **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that the most common approach is when a 'normal gene may be inserted into a nonspecific location within the genome to replace a non-functional gene'.

### Q1009. What enzyme is deficient in patients with SCID who undergo *ex vivo* gene therapy?
A.) DNA ligase
B.) Taq polymerase
C.) Restriction endonuclease
D.) Adenosine deaminase **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text clearly identifies that children with SCID lack the enzyme 'adenosine deaminase (ADA)'.

### Q1010. What is a 'vector' in gene therapy?
A.) A protein product made by the new gene
B.) The bone marrow stem cells that receive the gene
C.) The faulty gene that is being replaced
D.) A carrier molecule, like a virus, to deliver a gene **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text defines a vector as a 'carrier molecule' used to deliver the therapeutic gene, with a virus being the most common example.

### Q1011. What is the purpose of creating 'frost-minus' bacteria?
A.) To increase the fertility of frozen soil
B.) To act as a novel type of insecticide
C.) To clean up oil spills in cold climates
D.) To protect plants from ice crystal formation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains these bacteria were changed from 'frost-plus to frost-minus' to prevent the formation of ice crystals on plants.

### Q1012. Which tool of biotechnology is known as 'molecular scissors'?
A.) DNA ligase
B.) Plasmids
C.) DNA polymerase
D.) Restriction endonucleases **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explicitly names restriction enzymes (endonucleases) as the 'molecular scissors' used to cut DNA.

### Q1013. What is a 'genomic library'?
A.) A digital database of all known protein sequences
B.) A single chromosome carefully preserved from an organism
C.) A stock of a single cloned gene with millions of copies
D.) A collection of clones containing an organism's entire genome **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This is the precise definition provided in the text: a collection of clones containing particular segments of DNA from a source.

### Q1014. What are 'sticky ends'?
A.) The telomeres at the ends of a chromosome
B.) The primers used to start a PCR reaction
C.) The circular shape of a bacterial plasmid
D.) Complementary single-stranded ends of cut DNA **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text describes sticky ends as single-stranded projected ends that result from a staggered cut by a restriction enzyme.

### Q1015. How is the gene for cystic fibrosis delivered in the *in vivo* therapy trial described?
A.) By injecting a viral vector into the blood
B.) By transplanting genetically modified lung cells
C.) By using a particle gun on the respiratory tract
D.) By spraying liposomes into the patient's nostrils **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This is the exact *in vivo* method detailed in the text, where liposomes carrying the gene are sprayed into the patient's nostrils.

### Q1016. Which of these is NOT a stated application of PCR?
A.) Diagnosing viral infections
B.) Identifying criminals in forensic labs
C.) Studying evolutionary history
D.) Producing therapeutic proteins like insulin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Producing proteins requires gene cloning and expression in a host system (like bacteria), not just PCR. PCR makes copies of DNA, it does not synthesize proteins.

### Q1017. Gene therapy for familial hypercholesterolemia targets which problem?
A.) A lack of the ADA enzyme in immune cells
B.) A faulty CFTR gene in lung cells
C.) A defective blood clotting factor gene
D.) Liver cells lacking a cholesterol receptor **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains this condition develops when 'liver cells lack a receptor for removing cholesterol from the blood'.

### Q1018. A gene inserted into corn roots to protect against pests codes for what product?
A.) A growth hormone
B.) A frost-preventing protein
C.) A herbicide resistance enzyme
D.) An insect toxin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states the gene codes for an 'insect toxin', which protects the plant's roots from insects.

### Q1019. What is a 'protoplast'?
A.) A mature pollen grain for anther culture
B.) An undifferentiated mass of plant cells
C.) An encapsulated 'artificial seed'
D.) A plant cell with its cell wall removed **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This is the correct definition given in the text. Removing the cell wall makes it easier to introduce foreign DNA.

### Q1020. The Taq polymerase used in PCR is extracted from what organism?
A.) A common bacteriophage
B.) The intestinal bacterium *E. coli*
C.) A species of baking yeast
D.) The bacterium *Thermus aquaticus* **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explicitly names *Thermus aquaticus*, a bacterium from hot springs, as the source of the temperature-tolerant Taq polymerase.

### Q1021. Besides a plasmid, what else can be used as a vector to insert a gene into bacteria?
A.) A liposome
B.) A microneedle
C.) A plant protoplast
D.) DNA from a bacterial virus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains that the DNA of bacterial viruses, like the lambda phage, can be used as a vector to carry foreign genes into bacteria.

### Q1022. What was the main scientific significance of cloning the sheep Dolly from an adult cell?
A.) Showing asexual reproduction is more efficient
B.) Creating a method to produce industrial bioplastics
C.) Confirming any 2n nucleus could be put into a sheep egg
D.) Proving adult cell genes can be reprogrammed for full development **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The success with Dolly showed that all genes of a mature, specialized adult cell could be turned on again to direct full development, a feat previously thought impossible.

### Q1023. What is the benefit of making crops resistant to a broad-spectrum herbicide?
A.) It makes the crops grow twice as fast
B.) It enhances the flavor of the final food product
C.) It makes the crops produce their own fertilizer
D.) It allows for killing weeds without harming the crop **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains that if crops are resistant and weeds are not, 'the herbicide can be used to kill the weeds,' which simplifies weed control.

### Q1024. Which of the following was a primary goal of the Human Genome Project?
A.) To cure all known genetic diseases
B.) To create a perfect human clone
C.) To prove all human DNA is identical
D.) To construct a genetic map of each chromosome **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text lists constructing a 'genetic map of the human genome' as the first primary goal.

### Q1025. How is a foreign gene typically introduced when creating a transgenic animal?
A.) By injecting it into an adult animal's bloodstream
B.) By feeding it to the host mother during pregnancy
C.) By adding it to the animal's milk after birth
D.) By injecting it into donor eggs before fertilization **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text and accompanying diagram show that 'DNA containing the gene of interest is injected into donor eggs' as a key step.

### Q1026. The pest-resistance gene known as the Bt gene comes from what source?
A.) A virus
B.) A fungus
C.) A different pest-resistant plant
D.) A bacterium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text identifies the 'bacterium Bacillus thuringiensis' as the source of the Bt genes that code for insect toxins.

### Q1027. What is the function of DNA ligase?
A.) It cuts plasmid DNA at specific sites
B.) It acts as a carrier for a foreign gene
C.) It synthesizes a new strand of DNA
D.) It seals foreign DNA into a vector **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text describes DNA ligase as 'molecular glue' that joins the gene of interest with the vector DNA by forming phosphodiester linkages.

### Q1028. What is a key advantage of using meristem culture for plant propagation?
A.) The resulting plants are haploid
B.) The resulting plants are always genetically different
C.) It is the best method for cereal grains
D.) The resulting plants are virus-free **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that a key advantage is that the 'meristem, unlike other portions of a plant, is virus free, therefore the plants produced are also virus free'.

### Q1029. What happened when a tobacco plant was engineered with the firefly luciferase gene?
A.) It became resistant to all common insects
B.) It grew to three times its normal size
C.) It developed the ability to grow in salty soil
D.) It glowed when sprayed with luciferin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text uses this as a classic example where the expression of the foreign gene resulted in the adult plants glowing when sprayed with the substrate luciferin.

### Q1030. What does the term 'totipotency' describe?
A.) The process of crossing different plant varieties
B.) A specific type of palindromic DNA sequence
C.) The uptake of foreign DNA by a host cell
D.) The ability of a single plant cell to form a whole organism **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text defines 'totipotency' as the phenomenon where plant cells 'have the capacity to regenerate into whole plants'.

### Q1031. Why are bacterial cells treated with calcium chloride before introducing a plasmid?
A.) To kill any untransformed bacteria
B.) To activate the DNA ligase enzyme
C.) To make the plasmid radioactive
D.) To make their membranes more permeable **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that bacterial cells are treated with calcium chloride to 'make them more permeable' for the uptake of the recombinant plasmid.

### Q1032. Which product is used to treat the blood clotting disorder hemophilia?
A.) Insulin
B.) Human growth hormone
C.) Tissue plasminogen activator
D.) Haemophilia factor VIII **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text lists 'haemophilia factor VIII' as one of the biotechnology products produced by transgenic bacteria for treatment.

### Q1033. In DNA analysis, what does gel electrophoresis do?
A.) It cuts DNA into many small fragments
B.) It makes millions of copies of DNA fragments
C.) It transfers DNA fragments onto a membrane
D.) It separates DNA fragments based on their length **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains that during gel electrophoresis, 'the fragments can be separated according to their lengths (molecular weight or size)'.

### Q1034. What is the initial chromosomal state of plants produced via anther culture?
A.) Virus-free
B.) Identical to the parent
C.) Herbicide-resistant
D.) Haploid **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that anther culture begins with haploid tube cells from pollen grains, which develop into 'haploid embryos' and then haploid plants.

### Q1035. Which was the first human chromosome to be fully sequenced?
A.) Chromosome 1
B.) X chromosome
C.) Y chromosome
D.) Chromosome 22 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that the 'DNA sequence of human chromosome no. 22...was completed in 1999', making it the first to be seen in its entirety.

### Q1036. What is a 'callus'?
A.) The initial tissue piece used in plant culture
B.) A plant cell with its cell wall removed
C.) A somatic embryo ready for germination
D.) An undifferentiated group of dividing plant cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text defines a 'callus' as an 'undifferentiated group of cells' produced when cultured cells begin dividing.

### Q1037. Who is credited with isolating the first restriction enzyme in 1970?
A.) Kary B. Mullis
B.) Frederick Sanger
C.) Gottlieb Haberlandt
D.) Hamilton O. Smith **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text credits 'Hamilton O. Smith, at Johns Hopkins University,' with isolating the first restriction enzyme in 1970.

### Q1038. Which of these therapeutic products is made by transgenic plants, not bacteria?
A.) Insulin
B.) Human growth hormone
C.) Tissue plasminogen activator
D.) Antibodies for treating genital herpes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text specifies that this antibody is made by transgenic 'soybeans' (a plant), not bacteria.

### Q1039. Why are 'suicide genes' sometimes added to bacteria used for bioremediation?
A.) To make them work faster under pressure
B.) To protect them from environmental toxins
C.) To help them reproduce more quickly in the field
D.) To make them self-destruct after their job is done **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains this is a safety feature that caused the bacteria 'to self-destruct when the job had been accomplished'.

### Q1040. How is paternity determined using DNA fingerprinting?
A.) The child's DNA bands must be a perfect match to the father's
B.) The father must have more DNA bands than the mother
C.) The child must have no bands in common with the father
D.) The child's DNA contains a mix of bands from both parents **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text and image explanation show that the child's fingerprint is a composite, with half of the markers coming from the mother and half from the father.

### Q1041. What is the function of a particle gun in plant biotechnology?
A.) To inject therapeutic genes into human patients
B.) To rapidly sequence a plant's DNA sample
C.) To eliminate agricultural pests using metal pellets
D.) To shoot DNA-coated particles into a plant callus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text describes this device as a method to introduce DNA into a plant tissue culture callus by bombarding it with DNA-coated microscopic metal particles.

### Q1042. The gene for which hormone has been used to create larger transgenic fish?
A.) Luciferase
B.) Antithrombin III
C.) An insect toxin
D.) Bovine growth hormone **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that the gene for 'bovine growth hormone' has been used to produce larger fishes, cows, pigs, rabbits, and sheep.

### Q1043. What ecological concern is mentioned regarding transgenic fish?
A.) They are not safe for human consumption
B.) They reproduce much too slowly in captivity
C.) They require a highly specialized and expensive diet
D.) They could disrupt natural ecosystems if they escape **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text specifically mentions the 'concern that they will upset or destroy natural ecosystems,' leading to them being kept in ponds with no escape route.

### Q1044. What type of bond initially forms between complementary 'sticky ends'?
A.) Covalent bond
B.) Ionic bond
C.) Disulphide bond
D.) Hydrogen bond **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The complementary single-stranded 'sticky ends' initially associate via weak hydrogen bonds between the base pairs. DNA ligase then forms permanent covalent bonds.

### Q1045. What is the potential benefit of using cell suspension cultures to produce chemicals?
A.) It allows chemicals to be synthesized from petroleum
B.) It is a process that requires no genetic engineering
C.) It reduces the need to import chemicals
D.) It eliminates the need to farm the source plants **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text proposes that this method would make it 'no longer be necessary to farm plants for the purpose of acquiring the chemicals they produce'.

### Q1046. What special nucleotide terminates DNA synthesis in the Sanger sequencing method?
A.) Ribonucleotides (NTPs)
B.) Deoxyribonucleotides (dNTPs)
C.) Radioactive nucleotides
D.) Dideoxynucleotides (ddNTPs) **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains that the Sanger method is based on 'dideoxynucleosides triphosphates (ddNTP's)' which prevent the addition of further nucleotides, thus terminating the chain.

### Q1047. What is the main benefit of automated DNA sequencing?
A.) It requires no enzymes like DNA polymerase
B.) It uses significantly less DNA than manual methods
C.) It can only be used for sequencing viral DNA
D.) It greatly improves the quality and speed of sequencing **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that 'automatic sequencing machines have greatly improved the quality as well as the speed of the sequencing process'.

### Q1048. When using *Agrobacterium* as a vector, where is the foreign gene first placed?
A.) Directly into the plant's chromosome
B.) Into the plant's mitochondria
C.) Into the cell wall of the plant
D.) Into the bacterium's plasmid **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains that in this technique, 'foreign DNA is inserted into the plasmid of the bacterium Agrobacterium,' which then infects the plant cell.

### Q1049. To create salt-tolerant plants, a gene for a channel protein was used to sequester Na+ ions into which organelle?
A.) The nucleus
B.) The chloroplast
C.) The cytoplasm
D.) The vacuole **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains that the channel protein transports Na+ 'across a vacuole membrane,' isolating the salt inside the vacuole to prevent it from interfering with metabolism.

### Q1050. What is the natural purpose of restriction enzymes within bacteria?
A.) To repair mutations in their own DNA
B.) To assist in bacterial conjugation
C.) To synthesize messenger RNA
D.) To defend against infecting viruses **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that restriction enzymes 'appear to serve as host-defence role because they chop up and inactivate... the DNA of infecting viruses'.

### Q1051. What defines a 'palindromic sequence' in DNA?
A.) It consists only of A and T bases
B.) It is always over 100 base pairs long
C.) It is a sequence that cannot be cut by enzymes
D.) Its nucleotides are arranged symmetrically in reverse order **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — This is the definition given in the text, describing a sequence of four to eight base pairs where nucleotides are arranged symmetrically in reverse order.

### Q1052. Why are bone marrow stem cells ideal for *ex vivo* gene therapy?
A.) They are the easiest cells to remove from the body
B.) They are the only cells affected by most genetic diseases
C.) They do not have a nucleus, which simplifies the process
D.) They divide to create a lasting supply of corrected cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explains that stem cells are preferred 'because they divide to produce more cells with same genes,' ensuring a continuous supply of corrected immune cells.

### Q1053. How did biotechnology improve the production of phenylalanine for aspartame?
A.) By engineering plants to produce it in their leaves
B.) By using gene therapy to help humans synthesize it
C.) By extracting it from the milk of transgenic animals
D.) By engineering bacteria for more efficient production **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text gives this as an example where biochemists isolated and altered genes so that 'various bacteria could be genetically engineered to produce phenylalanine'.

### Q1054. What is complementary DNA (cDNA)?
A.) The non-coding strand of a DNA double helix
B.) A short piece of DNA used as a PCR primer
C.) DNA found exclusively within bacterial plasmids
D.) DNA synthesized from an mRNA template **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text defines cDNA as 'The DNA formed by this process' (synthesis from mRNA via reverse transcriptase).

### Q1055. Bioleaching by engineered bacteria is used for what industrial purpose?
A.) Producing biodegradable plastics
B.) Cleaning up environmental oil spills
C.) Removing sulfur from coal before burning
D.) Extracting metals from low-grade ore **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text explicitly mentions that genetic engineering may 'enhance the ability of bacteria to extract copper, uranium and gold from low grade sources'.

### Q1056. What is the function of the enzyme reverse transcriptase?
A.) It cuts DNA at specific palindromic sequences
B.) It joins two DNA fragments together
C.) It synthesizes RNA from a DNA template
D.) It synthesizes DNA from an mRNA template **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The text states that the 'synthesis of gene from mRNA is carried out by reverse transcriptase enzymes'.

## Support & Movement

### Q1057. Which of the following best describes the primary need for support in living organisms?
A.) To enhance sensory perception.
B.) To facilitate reproduction.
C.) To regulate internal temperature.
D.) To resist gravity and external forces. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Support in living organisms is necessary to uphold and sustain the body against gravity and other external forces.

### Q1058. What was a significant evolutionary development that increased the need for greater support in living organisms?
A.) The transition from unicellular to multicellular forms.
B.) The development of photosynthesis.
C.) The shift from asexual to sexual reproduction.
D.) The increase in size of organisms. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — As living organisms increased in size through evolution, the need for support became greater, especially when they colonized land.

### Q1059. What is the scientific study of bones and cartilage called?
A.) Myology
B.) Histology
C.) Neurology
D.) Osteology **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The study of bones and cartilage is called osteology.

### Q1060. An individual bone is composed of a variety of tissues. Which of the following is NOT typically found in an individual bone?
A.) Bone tissue
B.) Cartilage
C.) Nerve tissue
D.) Muscle tissue **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An individual bone is composed of bone tissue, cartilage, fibrous connective tissue, blood, and nerve tissue.

### Q1061. In a long bone, the broad terminal parts are called the:
A.) Diaphysis
B.) Periosteum
C.) Endosteum
D.) Epiphysis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The terminal broad parts of a long bone are called the epiphysis.

### Q1062. Which part of a long bone is also known as the shaft and contains a central cavity filled with yellow bone marrow?
A.) Epiphysis
B.) Periosteum
C.) Spongy bone
D.) Diaphysis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The middle part along the length of the bone is called the diaphysis or shaft, which contains a central cavity filled by yellow bone marrow.

### Q1063. The outer connective tissue layer around a bone is known as the:
A.) Endosteum
B.) Compact bone
C.) Spongy bone
D.) Periosteum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The outer connective tissue around the bone is called periosteum.

### Q1064. Which of the following bone structures primarily contains red bone marrow in its spaces?
A.) Compact bone
B.) Periosteum
C.) Yellow bone marrow
D.) Spongy bone **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The red bone marrow is found in the spaces of spongy bone.

### Q1065. What is the primary function of osteoblasts?
A.) To destroy bone tissue.
B.) To maintain healthy bone tissue.
C.) To regulate calcium release to blood.
D.) To synthesize and secrete unmineralized ground substance. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Osteoblasts are bone-forming cells that synthesize and secrete unmineralized ground substance.

### Q1066. What do osteoblasts become once they are surrounded by the bone matrix?
A.) Osteoclasts
B.) Chondrocytes
C.) Fibroblasts
D.) Osteocytes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Once the osteoblasts are surrounded by matrix, they become osteocytes.

### Q1067. What is a key function of osteocytes in healthy bone tissue?
A.) Breaking down bone for calcium release.
B.) Synthesizing new bone matrix.
C.) Forming cartilage.
D.) Secreting enzymes and influencing bone mineral content. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Osteocytes maintain healthy bone tissue by secreting enzymes and influencing bone mineral content. They also regulate calcium release from bone tissue to blood.

### Q1068. Which type of bone cell is responsible for bone resorption?
A.) Osteoblasts
B.) Osteocytes
C.) Chondrocytes
D.) Osteoclasts **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Osteoclasts are bone-destroying cells that perform bone resorption, breaking down bone and depositing calcium and phosphate into the blood.

### Q1069. The work of osteoclasts is important for which of the following processes?
A.) Cartilage formation.
B.) Muscle contraction.
C.) Nerve tissue development.
D.) Bone growth and repair. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The work of osteoclasts is important to the growth and repair of bone.

### Q1070. How does cartilage compare to bone in terms of strength?
A.) Cartilage is stronger than bone.
B.) Cartilage is as strong as bone.
C.) Strength comparison varies greatly by type.
D.) Cartilage is not as strong as bone. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cartilage is not as strong as bone.

### Q1071. The flexibility of cartilage is primarily due to its matrix being:
A.) Densely packed with minerals.
B.) Composed of dead cells.
C.) Highly vascularized.
D.) Containing collagenous and elastic fibers. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cartilage is more flexible than bone because its matrix is gel-like and contains many collagenous and elastic fibers.

### Q1072. What is the dense layer of collagen fibers covering the cartilage matrix called?
A.) Periosteum
B.) Endosteum
C.) Lacunae
D.) Perichondrium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cartilage matrix is covered by a dense layer of collagen fibers called perichondrium.

### Q1073. The living cells of cartilage are known as:
A.) Osteocytes
B.) Osteoblasts
C.) Fibrocytes
D.) Chondrocytes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The living cells of cartilage are called chondrocytes.

### Q1074. How do chondrocytes primarily receive their nutrient supply?
A.) Through direct blood vessels.
B.) Via nerve tissue.
C.) Through direct contact with bone.
D.) By diffusion. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cartilage does not contain blood vessels, and the chondrocytes are supplied by diffusion.

### Q1075. Why does cartilage heal very slowly?
A.) It lacks nerve tissue.
B.) It has a very rigid matrix.
C.) It is constantly under pressure.
D.) It lacks blood vessels. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cartilage heals very slowly because it does not contain blood vessels.

### Q1076. Which type of cartilage is found at the ends of long bones, in the nose, larynx, and trachea?
A.) Fibrocartilage
B.) Elastic cartilage
C.) Reticular cartilage
D.) Hyaline cartilage **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hyaline cartilage is found at the ends of long bones and in the nose, at the larynx and trachea.

### Q1077. Where is fibrocartilage typically found in the adult human body?
A.) Ear flaps and epiglottis.
B.) Ends of long bones.
C.) Nose and trachea.
D.) Disks between vertebrae. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Fibrocartilage is found in the disks located between the vertebrae and the cartilage of the knee.

### Q1078. In comparison to cartilage, collagen fibers in bone are:
A.) Loosely packed.
B.) Absent.
C.) Randomly arranged.
D.) Densely packed. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — collagen in bone is densely packed, while in cartilage it is loosely packed.

### Q1079. Which of the following cell types is characteristic of cartilage but not bone?
A.) Osteoblasts
B.) Osteocytes
C.) Osteoclasts
D.) Chondrocytes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — chondrocytes are the cell types found in cartilage, while bone has osteoblasts, osteocytes, and osteoclasts.

### Q1080. What is a key difference regarding blood vessels between bone and cartilage?
A.) Both bone and cartilage have abundant blood vessels.
B.) Cartilage has blood vessels, but bone does not.
C.) Neither bone nor cartilage has blood vessels.
D.) Bone has blood vessels, but cartilage does not. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — blood vessels are present in bone but absent in cartilage.

### Q1081. Which substance is deposited in bone but not in cartilage?
A.) Collagen
B.) Water
C.) Protein fibers.
D.) Minerals. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — bone deposits minerals such as calcium, carbonates, and phosphates, while there is no deposition of minerals in cartilage.

### Q1082. What is the external covering of bone called, which differs from that of cartilage?
A.) Perichondrium
B.) Endosteum
C.) Joint capsule
D.) Periosteum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Table 16.1 indicates that bone is covered by periosteum, while cartilage is covered by perichondrium.

### Q1083. How many bones are typically found in the human skeletal system?
A.) 106
B.) 306
C.) 406
D.) 206 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The human skeletal system consists of 206 bones.

### Q1084. The human skeletal system is primarily divided into two main divisions. What are they?
A.) Upper and Lower skeleton.
B.) Anterior and Posterior skeleton.
C.) Cranial and Post-cranial skeleton.
D.) Axial and Appendicular skeleton. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The human skeletal system is primarily divided into axial skeleton and appendicular skeleton.

### Q1085. Which of the following is included in the axial skeleton?
A.) Pelvic girdle
B.) Forelimbs
C.) Hind limbs
D.) Rib cage **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Axial skeleton includes skeletal parts along the central axis of the body, such as the skull, vertebral column, and rib cage.

### Q1086. How many bones are present in the human head?
A.) 8
B.) 14
C.) 33
D.) 29 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The head contains 29 bones.

### Q1087. How many cranial bones form the cranium?
A.) 6
B.) 14
C.) 29
D.) 8 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — There are 8 cranial bones that form the cranium (brain box).

### Q1088. Which of the following are paired cranial bones?
A.) Frontal and Occipital
B.) Ethmoid and Sphenoid
C.) Vomer and Mandible
D.) Parietal and Temporal **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Out of the 8 cranial bones, two are paired: parietal bones (left and right) and temporal bones (left and right).

### Q1089. Which of the following is an unpaired cranial bone?
A.) Parietal
B.) Temporal
C.) Lacrimal
D.) Frontal **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Four cranial bones are unpaired: frontal bone, occipital bone, ethmoid bone, and sphenoid bone.

### Q1090. How many facial bones are there in the human skull?
A.) 8
B.) 12
C.) 22
D.) 14 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Facial bones are fourteen in number.

### Q1091. Which of the following is a paired facial bone?
A.) Mandible
B.) Vomer
C.) Hyoid
D.) Maxilla **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The paired bones of the face include lacrimal, zygomatic, nasal bones, inferior nasal concha, maxilla, and palatine.

### Q1092. Which of the following is an unpaired facial bone?
A.) Zygomatic
B.) Nasal
C.) Palatine
D.) Mandible **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The unpaired bones of the face are mandible and vomer.

### Q1093. The three pairs of middle ear ossicles are:
A.) Femur, Tibia, Fibula
B.) Humerus, Ulna, Radius
C.) Carpals, Metacarpals, Phalanges
D.) Malleus, Incus, Stapes **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Three pairs of middle ear ossicles are malleus, incus, and stapes.

### Q1094. What is a unique characteristic of the hyoid bone?
A.) It articulates with the sternum.
B.) It forms part of the cranium.
C.) It is a paired bone.
D.) It does not articulate with any other head bone. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The hyoid bone is a small single bone that lies at the base of the skull below the tongue and does not articulate with any other bone of the head.

### Q1095. How many vertebrae typically make up the human vertebral column?
A.) 24
B.) 26
C.) 35
D.) 33 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vertebral column in human beings consists of thirty-three vertebrae.

### Q1096. How many cervical vertebrae are there in the human vertebral column?
A.) 5
B.) 12
C.) 4
D.) 7 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — There are 7 cervical vertebrae.

### Q1097. Which of the following is the first cervical vertebra?
A.) Axis
B.) Sacrum
C.) Coccyx
D.) Atlas **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The atlas is the first cervical vertebra.

### Q1098. Which characteristic is typical of thoracic vertebrae?
A.) They are found in the neck region.
B.) They are present in the abdominal region.
C.) They are fused to form the sacrum.
D.) They are rib-carrying vertebrae. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thoracic vertebrae are rib-carrying vertebrae having large spinous processes and are found in the chest region.

### Q1099. How many lumbar vertebrae are typically found in the human vertebral column?
A.) 7
B.) 12
C.) 4
D.) 5 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — There are 5 lumbar vertebrae.

### Q1100. The sacrum is formed by the fusion of how many vertebrae?
A.) 4
B.) 7
C.) 12
D.) 5 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sacral vertebrae are five fused vertebrae forming the sacrum.

### Q1101. Which bones articulate with the sacrum to form the back of the pelvis?
A.) Ischial bones
B.) Pubic bones
C.) Femur bones
D.) Iliac bones **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The sacrum articulates with the iliac bones of the hip bone to form the back of the pelvis.

### Q1102. How many coccygeal vertebrae are fused in adults to form the coccyx?
A.) 2
B.) 3
C.) 5
D.) 4 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Coccygeal vertebrae or coccyx are four vertebrae fused in adults.

### Q1103. How many pairs of ribs make up the rib cage?
A.) 7
B.) 10
C.) 14
D.) 12 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The rib cage consists of twelve pairs of ribs.

### Q1104. Which ribs attach directly to the sternum?
A.) Floating ribs
B.) False ribs
C.) Both false and floating ribs
D.) True ribs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The seven pairs of ribs that attach directly to the sternum are called true ribs.

### Q1105. Which rib pairs are classified as false ribs?
A.) 1st-7th pairs
B.) 11th and 12th pairs
C.) All ribs that attach indirectly to the sternum
D.) 8th, 9th, and 10th pairs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The 8th, 9th, and 10th pairs are called false ribs, as these three pairs of ribs are attached to the sternum by means of common costal cartilage.

### Q1106. Which rib pairs are known as floating ribs?
A.) 1st-7th pairs
B.) 8th, 9th, and 10th pairs
C.) Only the 12th pair
D.) 11th and 12th pairs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The 11th and 12th pairs of ribs are known as floating ribs because they do not attach to the sternum.

### Q1107. The appendicular skeleton includes skeletal parts present in:
A.) The skull and vertebral column.
B.) The rib cage and sternum.
C.) The axial skeleton.
D.) Appendages (arms and legs). **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The appendicular skeleton includes those skeletal parts which are present in appendages (arms and legs).

### Q1108. The pectoral girdle consists of a pair of clavicles and a pair of:
A.) Humerus
B.) Pelvic bones
C.) Sternum
D.) Scapula **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pectoral girdle consists of a pair of clavicles and a pair of scapula.

### Q1109. Which of the following bones is NOT part of the upper limb (forelimb)?
A.) Humerus
B.) Ulna
C.) Carpals
D.) Femur **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The upper limb or forelimb consists of humerus, ulna, radius, carpals, metacarpals, and phalanges. Femur is a lower limb bone.

### Q1110. The spherical head of the humerus fits into which cavity?
A.) Acetabulum
B.) Pubic symphysis
C.) Lacunae
D.) Glenoid cavity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The humerus is a long bone, the end of which has a spherical head, which fits into the glenoid cavity.

### Q1111. The radius bone is located on which side of the forearm?
A.) Inner side
B.) Posterior side
C.) Superior side
D.) Outer side **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The radius is a long, outer bone of the forearm (on the thumb side).

### Q1112. How many short bones make up the carpals, forming the wrist?
A.) 5
B.) 7
C.) 10
D.) 8 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Carpals consist of two rows of eight short bones forming the wrist.

### Q1113. Which bones make up the palm of the hand?
A.) Carpals
B.) Phalanges
C.) Tarsals
D.) Metacarpals **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Metacarpals consist of five bones making up the palm of the hand.

### Q1114. How many phalanges does the human thumb possess, compared to other fingers?
A.) Three, same as other fingers.
B.) Four, more than other fingers.
C.) One, fewer than other fingers.
D.) Two, while other fingers have three. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Each finger possesses three phalanges except the thumb which comprises two phalanges.

### Q1115. The pelvic girdle is made up of three units that form the coxa. These units are the ileum, ischium, and:
A.) Femur
B.) Sacrum
C.) Tibia
D.) Pubis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The pelvic girdle is made up of three units: the ileum, ischium, and pubis, which form the coxa.

### Q1116. Which of the following bones is part of the hind limb?
A.) Ulna
B.) Radius
C.) Clavicle
D.) Patella **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lower limb or hind limb consists of femur, patella, tibia, fibula, tarsal, metatarsal, and phalanges.

### Q1117. What is the definition of a joint or articulation?
A.) A place where muscles attach to bones.
B.) The rigid connection between two bones.
C.) The fluid-filled sac in a synovial capsule.
