D.) A place where two bones or bone and cartilage come together. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A joint or articulation is a place where two bones or bone and cartilage come together.

### Q1118. What is the scientific study of the structure and function of joints called?
A.) Osteology
B.) Myology
C.) Neurology
D.) Arthrology **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The scientific study of the structure and function of joints is called arthrology.

### Q1119. Which characteristic defines fibrous joints?
A.) They are freely moveable.
B.) They are united by cartilage.
C.) They contain synovial fluid.
D.) Bones are connected with no joint cavity. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In fibrous joints, adjacent bones are directly connected by fibrous connective tissue, and there is no joint cavity between them.

### Q1120. An example of a fibrous joint is found in the:
A.) Knee
B.) Pubic symphysis
C.) Elbow
D.) Skull sutures **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Fibrous joints are found between most bones of the skull, called sutures.

### Q1121. Which statement is true regarding cartilaginous joints?
A.) They are freely moveable.
B.) They always have a wide gap between bones.
C.) They are covered by a synovial membrane.
D.) They are united by cartilage and lack a joint cavity. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — At a cartilaginous joint, the adjacent bones are united by cartilage, and these types of joints lack a joint cavity.

### Q1122. What type of movement do cartilaginous joints generally allow?
A.) Freely moveable.
B.) No movement.
C.) Full rotation.
D.) Little movement. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cartilaginous joints allow little movement.

### Q1123. Which of the following is an example of a cartilaginous joint?
A.) The shoulder joint.
B.) The knee joint.
C.) The ankle joint.
D.) The intervertebral disc. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Examples of cartilaginous joints include costal cartilages that attach ribs to the sternum, and the pubic symphysis and intervertebral disc.

### Q1124. What type of movement is characteristic of synovial joints?
A.) Immoveable
B.) Slightly moveable
C.) Limited to one plane
D.) Freely moveable **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Synovial joints are freely moveable joints.

### Q1125. What is secreted by the inner lining of the synovial membrane within a synovial joint capsule?
A.) Collagen
B.) Fibrous tissue
C.) Hyaline cartilage
D.) Synovial fluid **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The inner lining of the synovial membrane secretes synovial fluid.

### Q1126. Which of the following is an example of a synovial joint?
A.) Skull suture
B.) Pubic symphysis
C.) Rib attachment to sternum
D.) Hinge joint **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Examples of synovial joints include hinge joint, pivot joint, ball-and-socket joint, and gliding joint.

### Q1127. What is the medical term for joint replacement?
A.) Osteology
B.) Arthroscopy
C.) Myectomy
D.) Arthroplasty **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Joint replacement is called arthroplasty.

### Q1128. Artificial joints are commonly composed of metal in combination with modern:
A.) Rubber
B.) Ceramics
C.) Glass
D.) Plastics **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Artificial joints are usually composed of metal (e.g., stainless steel, titanium alloys) in combination with modern plastics (e.g., high-density polythene, silastic or elastomer).

### Q1129. How do the skeleton and muscular systems together maintain bipedal posture?
A.) The skeleton alone provides all necessary support.
B.) Muscular systems work independently of the skeleton.
C.) Bipedal posture is maintained only through passive balance.
D.) Muscular systems works along with the skeleton. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The skeleton and muscular systems maintain bipedal posture, with certain muscles acting as posture muscles working in opposition to gravity, and the pelvis structure also permitting upright posture.

### Q1130. Each intervertebral disc consists of an inner semifluid part called the nucleus pulposus and an outer ring of fibrocartilage called the:
A.) Periosteum
B.) Synovial membrane
C.) Medullary cavity
D.) Annulus fibrosus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Each intervertebral disc consists of nucleus pulposus and annulus fibrosus, which is the strong outer ring of fibrocartilage.

### Q1131. What is the primary function of the nucleus pulposus in an intervertebral disc?
A.) To firmly hold vertebrae together.
B.) To secrete synovial fluid.
C.) To prevent bone-to-bone contact.
D.) To provide elasticity and compressibility. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The nucleus pulposus is an inner semifluid which acts as a rubber ball to give a disc its elasticity and compressibility.

### Q1132. The strong outer ring of fibrocartilage that holds together successive vertebrae is the:
A.) Nucleus pulposus
B.) Joint capsule
C.) Ligament
D.) Annulus fibrosus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Annulus fibrosus is the strong outer ring of fibrocartilage, which holds together successive vertebrae.

### Q1133. What is spondylosis characterized by?
A.) Inflammation of joints.
B.) Reduced bone mass.
C.) Pain in the leg due to nerve pressure.
D.) Immobility and fusion of a vertebral joint. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Spondylosis is the immobility and fusion of a vertebral joint.

### Q1134. Sciatica refers to symptoms such as pain, weakness, numbness, or tingling in the leg. What is its direct cause?
A.) Inflammation of the knee joint.
B.) Metabolic disorder affecting joints.
C.) Degeneration of articular cartilage.
D.) Injury to or pressure on the sciatic nerve. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sciatica is caused by injury to or pressure on the sciatic nerve.

### Q1135. Which of the following is a common cause of sciatica?
A.) Gouty arthritis
B.) Osteoporosis
C.) Rheumatoid arthritis
D.) Slipped disc **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Common causes of sciatica include a slipped disc, pelvic injury or fracture, and tumors.

### Q1136. What are typical symptoms of arthritis?
A.) Increased bone density.
B.) Improved joint flexibility.
C.) Numbness in limbs.
D.) Pain after walking. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The typical symptoms of arthritis include pain after walking (which may later occur even at rest), creaking sounds in the joint, and difficulty in getting up from a chair.

### Q1137. Which type of arthritis is a progressive disease where articular cartilages gradually soften and disintegrate?
A.) Rheumatoid arthritis
B.) Gouty arthritis
C.) Septic arthritis
D.) Osteoarthritis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Osteoarthritis is a progressive disease in which the articular cartilages gradually soften and disintegrate.

### Q1138. Osteoarthritis commonly affects which joints?
A.) Skull sutures and fibrous joints.
B.) Elbow and shoulder joints only.
C.) Joints of the fingers and toes only.
D.) Knee, hip, and intervertebral joints. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Osteoarthritis affects the knee, hip, and intervertebral joints.

### Q1139. What is the underlying cause of rheumatoid arthritis?
A.) Nutritional deficiency.
B.) Excessive uric acid deposition.
C.) Physical trauma to the joint.
D.) An autoimmune disorder. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Rheumatoid arthritis is the result of an autoimmune disorder in which the synovial membrane becomes inflamed due to a faulty immune system.

### Q1140. Gouty arthritis results from a metabolic disorder leading to the deposition of which crystals in the joints?
A.) Calcium phosphate crystals.
B.) Oxalate crystals.
C.) Collagen crystals.
D.) Sodium urate crystals. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Gouty arthritis results from a metabolic disorder in which sodium urate crystals are deposited in the joints.

### Q1141. Which joint is most commonly affected by gouty arthritis?
A.) Knee joint
B.) Hip joint
C.) Elbow joint
D.) Big toe joint **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The most common joint affected by gouty arthritis is the joint of the big toe.

### Q1142. Osteoporosis is characterized by:
A.) Increased bone mass and decreased fracture risk.
B.) Inflammation of joints.
C.) Softening of articular cartilages.
D.) Reduced bone mass and an increased risk of fracture. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Osteoporosis is a common disease characterized by reduced bone mass and an increased risk of fracture.

### Q1143. Why is adequate calcium intake in teenage years and twenties a preventive action against osteoporosis?
A.) It prevents joint inflammation.
B.) It promotes cartilage regeneration.
C.) It directly reduces osteoclast activity.
D.) It increases peak bone mass. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Adequate calcium intake in teenage years and twenties increases peak bone mass, offering greater tolerance for bone loss later in life, because a greater bone mass before the onset of osteoporosis provides more resilience.

### Q1144. What is the medical term for a broken bone?
A.) Sprain
B.) Dislocation
C.) Contusion
D.) Fracture **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A fracture is the medical term for a broken bone.

### Q1145. Bone fractures occur when the physical force exerted on the bone is:
A.) Less than the bone's strength.
B.) Equal to the bone's strength.
C.) Applied slowly over time.
D.) Stronger than the bone itself. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Fractures occur when the physical force exerted on the bone is stronger than the bone itself.

### Q1146. In which type of fracture does the skin remain intact, without any penetration by the bone ends?
A.) Compound fracture
B.) Complicated fracture
C.) Stress fracture
D.) Closed fracture **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Simple fracture or closed fractures are those in which the skin is intact.

### Q1147. What characterizes a compound fracture?
A.) The skin is intact over the fracture site.
B.) It damages adjacent internal organs.
C.) It is a minor crack in the bone.
D.) The bone ends penetrate the skin and form a wound. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — If the bone ends penetrate the skin and form a wound, it is called a compound fracture or open fracture.

### Q1148. When a fracture damages adjacent internal organs, it is classified as a:
A.) Simple fracture
B.) Compound fracture
C.) Hairline fracture
D.) Complicated fracture **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When a fracture damages the adjacent organs, it is called a complicated fracture.

### Q1149. What is the very first step in the repair process of a simple bone fracture?
A.) Bony callus formation.
B.) Bone remodeling.
C.) Fibrocartilaginous callus formation.
D.) Haematoma. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The repair process of a simple fracture takes place in four major steps, starting with haematoma or clot formation.

### Q1150. During haematoma formation at a fracture site, what happens to the blood vessels in the bone and surrounding tissues?
A.) They constrict to limit blood flow.
B.) They become stronger and reinforce the bone.
C.) They form new connections immediately.
D.) They are torn, leading to haemorrhage. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When a bone breaks, blood vessels in the bone, and perhaps in surrounding tissues, are torn and haemorrhage.

### Q1151. Following the formation of a haematoma at a fracture site, what happens to the bone cells deprived of nutrition?
A.) They rapidly multiply.
B.) They become active in repair immediately.
C.) They convert into blood cells.
D.) They die. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Soon after haematoma formation, bone cells deprived of nutrition die.

### Q1152. After a fracture and haematoma formation, the tissue at the fracture site typically becomes:
A.) Stronger and rigid.
B.) Numb and discolored.
C.) Warm and dry.
D.) Swollen and inflamed. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — As a result of the haematoma and dying bone cells, the tissue at the site becomes swollen, painful, and inflamed.

### Q1153. A mass of clotted blood that forms at a fracture site is specifically called a:
A.) Callus
B.) Plaque
C.) Cyst
D.) Haematoma **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — As a result of torn blood vessels and haemorrhage, a haematoma, a mass of clotted blood, forms at the fracture site.

## Body Fluids & Circulation

### Q1154. What is the primary function of the human heart?
A.) Gas exchange
B.) Nutrient absorption
C.) Waste filtration
D.) Pumping blood **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The heart functions as a pump and is responsible for the circulation of blood through the blood vessels.

### Q1155. The human heart is best described as a:
A.) Solid, muscular organ
B.) Glandular, epithelial organ
C.) Spongy, cartilaginous organ
D.) Hollow, fibromuscular organ **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The human heart is described as a hollow, fibromuscular organ.

### Q1156. What is the anatomical term for the blunt, rounded point of the heart cone?
A.) Base
B.) Septum
C.) Atrium
D.) Apex **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The blunt, rounded point of the heart cone is called the apex.

### Q1157. The Greek name for the heart, is:
A.) Cor
B.) Vena
C.) Pulmo
D.) Cardia **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The Greek name for the heart is 'cardia', from which the adjective 'cardiac' is derived.

### Q1158. Which of the following is responsible for producing the pressure that makes blood flow through blood vessels?
A.) Relaxation of the heart
B.) Contraction of blood vessels
C.) Gravity
D.) Forceful contraction of the heart **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The heart produces the pressure responsible for making blood flow through the blood vessels by contracting forcefully.

### Q1159. In which body cavity is the heart primarily located?
A.) Abdominal cavity
B.) Pelvic cavity
C.) Cranial cavity
D.) Thoracic cavity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The heart is located in the thoracic cavity between the lungs.

### Q1160. The middle portion of the thoracic cavity, formed by the heart, trachea, esophagus, and associated structures, is called the:
A.) Pleural cavity
B.) Pericardial cavity
C.) Diaphragm
D.) Mediastinum **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The heart, trachea, oesophagus, and associated structures form a middle portion called mediastinum.

### Q1161. What is the function of the pericardial fluid?
A.) Lubricates heart valves
B.) Nourishes cardiac muscle
C.) Prevents blood clot formation
D.) Reduces friction **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pericardial fluid is secreted between the membranes of the pericardium and reduces friction between the heart wall and surrounding tissues when the heart is beating.

### Q1162. How many chambers does the human heart consist of?
A.) Two
B.) Three
C.) Five
D.) Four **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The heart consists of four chambers: two atria and two ventricles.

### Q1163. The groove that separates the atria from the ventricles on the surface of the heart is known as the:
A.) InterAtrioventricular groove
B.) Interventricular groove
C.) Coronary sulcus
D.) Atrioventricular groove **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The atria and ventricles are separated from each other on the surface of the heart by an atrioventricular groove or sulcus.

### Q1164. Which layer of the heart wall is primarily composed of cardiac muscle cells and is responsible for its ability to contract?
A.) Epicardium
B.) Endocardium
C.) Pericardium
D.) Myocardium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The thick middle layer of the heart, the myocardium, is composed of cardiac muscle cells and is responsible for the ability of the heart to contract.

### Q1165. The surface of the heart chambers that allows blood to move easily is the:
A.) Epicardium
B.) Myocardium
C.) Pericardium
D.) Endocardium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The smooth inner surface of the heart chambers is the endocardium, which allows blood to move easily through the heart.

### Q1166. What is the approximate ratio of the thickness of the right ventricle wall to the left ventricle wall?
A.) 1:1
B.) 1:2
C.) 3:1
D.) 1:3 **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The right ventricle has thinner walls than the left ventricle in a ratio of 1:3.

### Q1167. Which of the following veins directly empties deoxygenated blood into the right atrium?
A.) Pulmonary vein
B.) Aorta
C.) Pulmonary artery
D.) Superior vena cava **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The right atrium receives three veins: the superior vena cava, the inferior vena cava, and the coronary sinus.

### Q1168. The interatrial septum separates which two chambers of the heart?
A.) Right atrium and right ventricle
B.) Left atrium and left ventricle
C.) Two ventricles
D.) Two atria **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The two atria are separated from each other by the interatrial septum.

### Q1169. Which valve is located between the right atrium and the right ventricle?
A.) Bicuspid valve
B.) Mitral valve
C.) Aortic semilunar valve
D.) Tricuspid valve **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The atrioventricular valve between the right atrium and the right ventricle has three cusps and is called the tricuspid valve.

### Q1170. The bicuspid valve is also known as the:
A.) Tricuspid valve
B.) Pulmonary valve
C.) Aortic valve
D.) Mitral valve **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The atrioventricular valve between the left atrium and left ventricle has two cusps and is therefore called the bicuspid or mitral valve.

### Q1171. What is the strong connective tissue that attach papillary muscles to the cusps of atrioventricular valves?
A.) Tendons
B.) Ligaments
C.) Myofibrils
D.) Chordae tendineae **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Papillary muscles are attached by thin, strong connective tissue strings called chordae tendineae to the cusps of the atrioventricular valves.

### Q1172. Which valves prevent blood from flowing back into the ventricles from the aorta and pulmonary trunk?
A.) Atrioventricular valves
B.) Tricuspid valve
C.) Bicuspid valve
D.) Semilunar valves **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The aorta and pulmonary trunk possess aortic and pulmonary semilunar valves, which consist of cusps that meet to block blood flow.

### Q1173. The thickest layer of the heart wall, responsible for contraction, is the:
A.) Endocardium
B.) Epicardium
C.) Pericardium
D.) Myocardium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The thick middle layer of the heart, the myocardium, is composed of cardiac muscle cells and is responsible for the ability of the heart to contract.

### Q1174. Deoxygenated blood from the body first enters which chamber of the heart?
A.) Left atrium
B.) Left ventricle
C.) Right ventricle
D.) Right atrium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The superior vena cava and the inferior vena cava, both carrying deoxygenated blood, enter the right atrium.

### Q1175. From the right atrium, blood passes through which valve to enter the right ventricle?
A.) Bicuspid valve
B.) Mitral valve
C.) Aortic semilunar valve
D.) Tricuspid valve **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The right atrium sends blood through an atrioventricular valve (the tricuspid valve) to the right ventricle.

### Q1176. Where does the right ventricle pump blood to?
A.) Aorta
B.) Left atrium
C.) Body proper
D.) Lungs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The right ventricle sends blood through the pulmonary semilunar valve into the pulmonary trunk and the two pulmonary arteries to the lungs.

### Q1177. Oxygenated blood from the lungs first enters which chamber of the heart?
A.) Right atrium
B.) Right ventricle
C.) Left ventricle
D.) Left atrium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Four pulmonary veins, carrying oxygenated blood from the lungs, enter the left atrium.

### Q1178. The left ventricle pumps blood into which major artery to be distributed to the body proper?
A.) Pulmonary artery
B.) Superior vena cava
C.) Pulmonary trunk
D.) Aorta **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The left ventricle sends blood through the aortic semilunar valve into the aorta to the body proper.

### Q1179. What is the term for the contraction phase of a heart chamber?
A.) Diastole
B.) Refraction
C.) Repolarization
D.) Systole **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Systole is the contraction phase, which forces blood out of the chamber.

### Q1180. What is the term for the relaxation phase of a heart chamber?
A.) Systole
B.) Depolarization
C.) Contraction
D.) Diastole **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Diastole is the relaxation phase, during which the chamber fills with blood.

### Q1181. Approximately how long does the atrial systole last during a cardiac cycle?
A.) 0.3 second
B.) 0.4 second
C.) 0.8 second
D.) 0.1 second **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Atrial systole lasts for about 0.1 second, during which the atria contract and pump blood into the ventricles.

### Q1182. What event causes the "Lubb" sound of the heart?
A.) Closing of semilunar valves
B.) Opening of atrioventricular valves
C.) Contraction of atria
D.) Closing of atrioventricular valves **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The "Lubb" sound is produced by the closing of the tricuspid and bicuspid (mitral) valves at the beginning of ventricular systole.

### Q1183. What event causes the "Dubb" sound of the heart?
A.) Closing of atrioventricular valves
B.) Opening of semilunar valves
C.) Contraction of ventricles
D.) Closing of semilunar valves **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The "Dubb" sound is produced by the closing of the aortic and pulmonary semilunar valves at the beginning of ventricular diastole.

### Q1184. Which component of the heart's conducting system is known as the natural pacemaker?
A.) Atrioventricular (AV) node
B.) Bundle of His
C.) Purkinje fibers
D.) Sinoatrial (SA) node **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The sinoatrial (SA) node acts as the heart's natural pacemaker, initiating the electrical impulse for contraction.

### Q1185. Where is the Sinoatrial node located?
A.) Near the tricuspid valve
B.) In the wall of the left atrium
C.) In the interventricular septum
D.) In the upper part of the right atrial wall **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The SA node is located in the upper part of the right atrial wall, near the opening of the superior vena cava.

### Q1186. What is the primary function of the Atrioventricular node?
A.) To initiate ventricular contraction directly
B.) To send impulses to the atria
C.) To regulate blood pressure
D.) To delay the impulse from the SA node **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The AV node delays the electrical impulse from the SA node, allowing the atria to fully contract and empty blood into the ventricles before ventricular contraction begins.

### Q1187. After the AV node, the electrical impulse travels down which structure within the interventricular septum?
A.) Purkinje fibers
B.) Coronary sinus
C.) Moderator band
D.) Bundle of His **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — From the AV node, the impulse travels to the bundle of His (atrioventricular bundle), which extends into the interventricular septum.

### Q1188. Which components rapidly conduct the electrical impulse throughout the ventricular muscle, causing coordinated ventricular contraction?
A.) Chordae tendineae
B.) Papillary muscles
C.) Coronary arteries
D.) Purkinje fibers **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The bundle of His divides into right and left bundle branches, which then branch into Purkinje fibers that spread the impulse rapidly throughout the ventricular walls, causing them to contract.

### Q1189. The inherent rhythmicity of the heartbeat originates from:
A.) Nervous stimulation from the brain
B.) Hormonal regulation
C.) Blood pressure changes
D.) Specialized cardiac muscle cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The heartbeat originates from within the heart itself, specifically from specialized cardiac muscle cells that form the conducting system.

### Q1190. What is the average duration of one cardiac cycle at rest?
A.) 0.1 second
B.) 0.3 second
C.) 0.5 second
D.) 0.8 second **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A complete cardiac cycle, including both systole and diastole, typically lasts about 0.8 seconds at rest.

### Q1191. Which part of the autonomic nervous system generally decreases heart rate?
A.) Sympathetic nervous system
B.) Somatic nervous system
C.) Enteric nervous system
D.) Parasympathetic nervous system **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The parasympathetic nervous system, via the vagus nerve, generally decreases heart rate.

### Q1192. An increase in body temperature typically has what effect on heart rate?
A.) Decreases heart rate
B.) No effect on heart rate
C.) Causes irregular heart rate
D.) Increases heart rate **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An increase in body temperature generally increases heart rate.

### Q1193. Which hormone can increase heart rate and force of contraction during stressful situations?
A.) Insulin
B.) Glucagon
C.) Thyroxine
D.) Adrenaline **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Adrenaline, secreted by the adrenal medulla, acts to increase heart rate and the force of cardiac contraction.

### Q1194. What does an Electrocardiogram record?
A.) Blood pressure changes in the heart
B.) Mechanical contractions of the heart
C.) Volume of blood pumped by the heart
D.) Electrical activity of the heart **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An ECG is a graphic record of the electrical current generated by the heart.

### Q1195. The P wave on an ECG represents:
A.) Ventricular depolarization
B.) Ventricular repolarization
C.) Atrial repolarization
D.) Atrial depolarization **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The P wave represents atrial depolarization, which leads to atrial contraction.

### Q1196. The QRS complex on an ECG primarily represents:
A.) Atrial repolarization
B.) Atrial depolarization
C.) Ventricular repolarization
D.) Ventricular depolarization **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The QRS complex represents ventricular depolarization, which leads to ventricular contraction.

### Q1197. What event in the heart is indicated by the T wave on an ECG?
A.) Atrial contraction
B.) Atrial relaxation
C.) Ventricular contraction
D.) Ventricular relaxation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The T wave represents ventricular repolarization, which corresponds to the relaxation of the ventricles.

### Q1198. The interval between the beginning of the P wave and the beginning of the QRS complex (P-R interval) indicates:
A.) Atrial contraction time
B.) Ventricular relaxation time
C.) Time for ventricular repolarization
D.) Time taken for impulse to travel from atria to ventricles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The P-R interval represents the time taken for the electrical impulse to travel from the atria through the AV node and bundle of His to the ventricles.

### Q1199. A prolonged P-R interval on an ECG could indicate a problem with the:
A.) Ventricular muscle
B.) SA node activity
C.) Aortic valve
D.) AV node activity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A prolonged P-R interval suggests a delay in impulse conduction through the AV node.

### Q1200. What might a depressed or elevated S-T segment on an ECG signify?
A.) Normal heart rhythm
B.) Atrial fibrillation
C.) Valve dysfunction
D.) Myocardial infarction **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Deviations in the S-T segment are often indicative of myocardial ischemia (reduced blood flow to the heart muscle) or myocardial infarction (heart attack).

### Q1201. An ECG can be used to diagnose which of the following?
A.) High cholesterol levels
B.) Blood clots in veins
C.) High blood pressure
D.) Abnormal heart rhythms **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — ECGs are crucial for detecting and diagnosing various abnormal heart rhythms or arrhythmias.

### Q1202. The segment between the end of the S wave and the beginning of the T wave (S-T segment) represents:
A.) Atrial systole
B.) Ventricular repolarization
C.) Atrial diastole
D.) Ventricular systole plateau phase **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The S-T segment represents the period when the ventricles are fully depolarized and contracting, corresponding to the plateau phase of the ventricular action potential.

### Q1203. Which part of the ECG is typically hidden by the larger QRS complex?
A.) P wave
B.) T wave
C.) Ventricular depolarization
D.) Atrial repolarization **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Atrial repolarization occurs simultaneously with ventricular depolarization and is usually obscured by the much larger QRS complex.

### Q1204. Which type of blood vessel carries blood away from the heart?
A.) Veins
B.) Venules
C.) Capillaries
D.) Arteries **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Arteries are blood vessels that carry blood away from the heart.

### Q1205. Which layer of an artery wall is primarily composed of elastic and connective tissues, allowing it to stretch and recoil?
A.) Tunica intima
B.) Tunica media
C.) Endothelium
D.) Tunica externa **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The outermost layer, tunica externa (adventitia), is made of elastic and connective tissues, enabling arteries to stretch and recoil.

### Q1206. The smallest arteries that regulate blood flow into capillary beds are called:
A.) Veins
B.) Venules
C.) Aorta
D.) Arterioles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Arteries branch into smaller vessels called arterioles, which are important in regulating blood flow into capillary beds.

### Q1207. What is the primary function of capillaries?
A.) Transporting the blood under high pressure
B.) Returning blood to the heart
C.) Storing blood
D.) Exchange of substances between blood and tissues **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Capillaries are the sites of exchange of nutrients, oxygen, carbon dioxide, and waste products between the blood and tissue fluids.

### Q1208. Which type of blood vessel has valves?
A.) Arteries
B.) Arterioles
C.) Capillaries
D.) Veins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Veins contain valves, especially in the limbs, to prevent the backflow of blood against gravity.

### Q1209. Where is the rate of blood flow slowest in the circulatory system?
A.) Aorta
B.) Large arteries
C.) Veins
D.) Capillaries **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The total cross-sectional area of capillaries is much larger than that of arteries or veins, causing the blood flow to be slowest in the capillaries, facilitating efficient exchange of substances.

### Q1210. What is the main reason for the rapid blood flow in arteries?
A.) Broad lumen
B.) Large total cross-sectional area
C.) Contraction of surrounding muscles
D.) Narrow lumen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Arteries have a relatively narrow lumen and receive blood directly from the heart under high pressure, leading to a rapid flow rate.

### Q1211. Which vessels are formed when venules merge together?
A.) Arterioles
B.) Arteries
C.) Capillaries
D.) Veins **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Capillaries unite to form venules, and venules then merge to form veins.

### Q1212. The smooth inner layer of all blood vessels is called the:
A.) Tunica externa
B.) Tunica media
C.) Elastic lamina
D.) Endothelium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The tunica intima, the innermost layer of blood vessels, consists of a smooth lining of endothelium that minimizes friction to blood flow.

### Q1213. What happens to the blood flow rate as blood moves from the aorta to the capillaries?
A.) It increases significantly
B.) It remains constant
C.) It fluctuates unpredictably
D.) It decreases significantly **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Blood flow rate decreases as blood moves from the aorta to the arteries, arterioles, and significantly slows down in the capillaries due to the vast increase in total cross-sectional area.

### Q1214. What is blood pressure?
A.) The force exerted by blood on the heart walls
B.) The volume of blood in the arteries
C.) The rate of blood flow
D.) The force exerted by blood on the vessel walls **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Blood pressure is the force exerted by the blood against the walls of blood vessels.

### Q1215. What is the term for the maximum pressure in the arteries during ventricular contraction?
A.) Diastolic pressure
B.) Pulse pressure
C.) Mean arterial pressure
D.) Systolic pressure **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Systolic pressure is the maximum pressure in the arteries during ventricular systole (contraction).

### Q1216. What is the term for the minimum pressure in the arteries during ventricular relaxation?
A.) Systolic pressure
B.) Pulse pressure
C.) Mean arterial pressure
D.) Diastolic pressure **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Diastolic pressure is the minimum pressure in the arteries during ventricular diastole (relaxation).

### Q1217. A healthy average blood pressure reading for an adult is often cited as:
A.) 100/70 mmHg
B.) 140/90 mmHg
C.) 90/60 mmHg
D.) 120/80 mmHg **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An average blood pressure for a healthy adult is approximately 120/80 mmHg.

### Q1218. Which instrument is commonly used to measure blood pressure?
A.) Stethoscope
B.) Electrocardiogram (ECG)
C.) Pulse oximeter
D.) Sphygmomanometer **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Blood pressure is typically measured using a sphygmomanometer.

### Q1219. Which of the following factors would generally lead to an *increase* in blood pressure?
A.) Decrease in cardiac output
B.) Decrease in peripheral resistance
C.) Increased elasticity of arteries
D.) Increase in blood volume **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — An increase in blood volume directly increases the pressure within the blood vessels, leading to higher blood pressure.

### Q1220. How does vasoconstriction affect peripheral resistance and thus blood pressure?
A.) Decreases resistance, decreases pressure
B.) Increases resistance, decreases pressure
C.) Decreases resistance, increases pressure
D.) Increases resistance, increases pressure **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Vasoconstriction (narrowing of blood vessels) increases peripheral resistance, which in turn leads to an increase in blood pressure.

### Q1221. Hypertension is defined as persistently high blood pressure, often starting at readings equal to or greater than:
A.) 100/70 mmHg
B.) 120/80 mmHg
C.) 160/90 mmHg
D.) 140/90 mmHg **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hypertension is characterized by a resting blood pressure of 140/90 mmHg or higher.

### Q1222. What effect does increased blood viscosity have on blood pressure?
A.) Decreases blood pressure
B.) No effect on blood pressure
C.) Stabilizes blood pressure
D.) Increases blood pressure **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Increased blood viscosity (thickness) makes it harder for blood to flow, thus increasing the resistance and leading to higher blood pressure.

### Q1223. When measuring blood pressure, what sound is listened for with a stethoscope that indicates blood flow returning through the artery?
A.) Lubb Dubb sounds
B.) Murmur sounds
C.) Bruit sounds
D.) Korotkoff sounds **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When measuring blood pressure, Korotkoff sounds are the sounds heard with a stethoscope as blood flow returns through the partially compressed artery, indicating systolic and diastolic pressures.

### Q1224. What is the term for the formation of a blood clot inside a blood vessel?
A.) Hemorrhage
B.) Embolism
C.) Aneurysm
D.) Thrombosis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Thrombosis is the formation of a blood clot, known as a thrombus, inside a blood vessel, obstructing the flow of blood through the circulatory system.

### Q1225. A piece of a thrombus that breaks off and travels through the bloodstream is called a/an:
A.) Plaque
B.) Hematoma
C.) Aneurysm
D.) Embolus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — If a piece of the thrombus breaks off and travels in the bloodstream, it is called an embolus, which can block a smaller vessel elsewhere.

### Q1226. Chest pain caused by insufficient blood flow to the heart muscle is known as:
A.) Myocardial infarction
B.) Heart failure
C.) Arrhythmia
D.) Angina pectoris **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Angina pectoris is severe chest pain resulting from a reduced oxygen supply to the heart muscle.

### Q1227. A "heart attack" is medically termed as:
A.) Angina pectoris
B.) Bradycardia
C.) Tachycardia
D.) Myocardial infarction **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A heart attack, or myocardial infarction, occurs when blood flow to a part of the heart is blocked, usually by a blood clot.

### Q1228. What condition results when the heart cannot pump enough blood to meet the body's needs?
A.) Arrhythmia
B.) Angina pectoris
C.) Stroke
D.) Heart failure **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Heart failure is a condition where the heart is unable to pump sufficient blood to supply the body's needs.

### Q1229. Irregular heart rhythms are collectively known as:
A.) Bradycardia
B.) Tachycardia
C.) Dysrhythmias
D.) Arrhythmias **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Arrhythmias are disorders of heart rate and rhythm.

### Q1230. Which diagnostic test involves injecting a dye and using X-rays to visualize blood vessels?
A.) ECG
B.) Echocardiography
C.) Stress test
D.) Angiography **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Angiography involves injecting a contrast dye into blood vessels and taking X-ray images to visualize blockages or abnormalities.

### Q1231. What non-invasive diagnostic tool uses sound waves to create images of the heart's structure and function?
A.) ECG
B.) Angiography
C.) CT scan
D.) Echocardiography **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Echocardiography uses ultrasound (sound waves) to produce images of the heart, showing its size, structure, and pumping function.

### Q1232. A treadmill test where the heart's electrical activity is monitored during exercise is typically called a/an:
A.) Echocardiogram
B.) Angiogram
C.) ECG
D.) Stress test **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A stress test typically involves walking on a treadmill or riding a stationary bicycle while hooked to an ECG machine, to see how the heart performs under stress.

### Q1233. Which surgical procedure uses a balloon-tipped catheter to widen narrowed or blocked coronary arteries?
A.) Bypass surgery
B.) Valve replacement
C.) Pacemaker implantation
D.) Angioplasty **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Angioplasty is a procedure to widen narrowed or obstructed blood vessels, often using a balloon catheter.

### Q1234. What is the common name for the surgical procedure that reroutes blood around a blocked artery using a healthy blood vessel from another part of the body?
A.) Angioplasty
B.) Stent placement
C.) Atherectomy
D.) Bypass surgery **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery, commonly known as bypass surgery, involves creating new pathways for blood flow around blocked arteries.

### Q1235. A key lifestyle modification for preventing cardiovascular disorders is:
A.) Increasing intake of saturated fats
B.) Sedentary lifestyle
C.) Smoking
D.) Regular physical activity **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Regular physical activity is a crucial lifestyle modification that helps prevent cardiovascular disorders.

### Q1236. What is the medical term for persistently high blood pressure?
A.) Hypotension
B.) Tachycardia
C.) Bradycardia
D.) Hypertension **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hypertension is the medical term for persistently high blood pressure.

### Q1237. A common symptom of hypotension is:
A.) Severe chest pain
B.) Shortness of breath at rest
C.) Swelling in the legs
D.) Dizziness **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hypotension (low blood pressure) can cause symptoms like dizziness, lightheadedness, or fainting due to insufficient blood flow to the brain.

### Q1238. What is a significant risk factor for developing hypertension?
A.) Low sodium diet
B.) Regular exercise
C.) Maintaining a healthy weight
D.) High intake of saturated fats **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — High intake of saturated fats and cholesterol contributes to atherosclerosis, which can lead to hypertension.

### Q1239. The fluid that circulates within the lymphatic system is called:
A.) Plasma
B.) Serum
C.) Interstitial fluid
D.) Lymph **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lymphatic system transports a fluid called lymph, which is formed from interstitial fluid.

### Q1240. What is the primary function of the lymphatic system?
A.) Pumping blood to the lungs
B.) Transporting oxygen to tissues
C.) Absorbing nutrients from the digestive system
D.) Returning excess interstitial fluid to the blood **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The lymphatic system collects excess tissue fluid, filters it, and returns it to the bloodstream, and also plays a crucial role in immunity.

### Q1241. Lymphatic capillaries differ from blood capillaries by being:
A.) Impermeable to large molecules
B.) Less permeable to fluid
C.) Containing red blood cells
D.) Closed at one end **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lymphatic capillaries are blind-ended (closed at one end) vessels that collect interstitial fluid.

### Q1242. Which organ of the lymphatic system is responsible for filtering blood and removing old red blood cells?
A.) Thymus
B.) Tonsil
C.) Lymph node
D.) Spleen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The spleen filters blood, removes old and damaged red blood cells, and plays a role in the immune system.

### Q1243. The spleen functions as a reservoir for:
A.) Lymph
B.) Bile
C.) Digestive enzymes
D.) Blood **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The spleen serves as a blood reservoir, capable of storing a significant amount of blood.

## Plant Functions

### Q1244. Which of the following elements is classified as a macronutrient required by plants?
A.) Zinc
B.) Molybdenum
C.) Boron
D.) Potassium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Macronutrients are required in relatively large quantities (greater than 0.05% of dry weight). Potassium is a macronutrient, while zinc, molybdenum, and boron are micronutrients, needed in trace amounts.

### Q1245. The technique most useful for determining if an element is essential for plant growth is:
A.) Soil analysis
B.) Foliar application
C.) Crop rotation
D.) Hydroponics **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hydroponics allows plants to be grown in a controlled environment with specific mineral solutions, enabling precise determination of an element's essentiality by observing growth in its absence.

### Q1246. A plant showing stunted growth and purplish discoloration of leaves, particularly older ones, is likely deficient in:
A.) Nitrogen
B.) Iron
C.) Magnesium
D.) Phosphorus **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Phosphorus deficiency often results in stunted growth and a distinctive purplish coloration, especially in older leaves, due to its role in energy transfer and nucleic acid synthesis.

### Q1247. Which micronutrient is essential for chlorophyll synthesis but is not a constituent of the chlorophyll molecule itself?
A.) Magnesium
B.) Manganese
C.) Copper
D.) Iron **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Iron is crucial for the synthesis of chlorophyll, but it does not become a part of the chlorophyll molecule's structure. Magnesium, in contrast, is a central atom in the chlorophyll molecule.

### Q1248. Carnivorous plants, like the pitcher plant, primarily obtain which nutrient from consuming insects?
A.) Carbon
B.) Oxygen
C.) Water
D.) Nitrogen **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Carnivorous plants typically thrive in nitrogen-poor soils. They supplement their nitrogen requirements by trapping and digesting insects and other small organisms.

### Q1249. Which of these is a typical characteristic of a plant suffering from nitrogen deficiency?
A.) Yellowing of younger leaves
B.) Necrosis of leaf margins
C.) Deep green coloration of all leaves
D.) Yellowing of older leaves **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Nitrogen is a mobile element in plants. When deficient, it is re-mobilized from older leaves to support new growth, leading to a general yellowing or chlorosis of the older foliage first.

### Q1250. Which element is a component of amino acids and nucleic acids, and its deficiency leads to stunted growth and chlorosis?
A.) Calcium
B.) Molybdenum
C.) Boron
D.) Sulfur **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sulfur is an essential component of several amino acids (methionine, cysteine) and is therefore crucial for protein synthesis. Its deficiency can cause stunted growth and yellowing of younger leaves.

### Q1251. The primary purpose of growing plants in hydroponic solutions is to:
A.) Increase plant resistance to pests
B.) Enhance flavor and aroma of produce
C.) Reduce the need for artificial lighting
D.) Control the availability of specific mineral nutrients **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Hydroponics provides a controlled environment where the precise composition of mineral nutrients supplied to the plant roots can be manipulated, allowing for experimental determination of nutrient requirements.

### Q1252. During the daytime, which gas primarily enters the plant leaf for the process of photosynthesis?
A.) Oxygen
B.) Nitrogen
C.) Water vapor
D.) Carbon dioxide **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plants take in carbon dioxide from the atmosphere through stomata for use as a reactant in photosynthesis during daylight hours.

### Q1253. Which of the following structures is responsible for regulating the opening and closing of stomata?
A.) Epidermal cells
B.) Mesophyll cells
C.) Companion cells
D.) Guard cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Guard cells are specialized epidermal cells that surround each stoma. Their turgor changes regulate the size of the stomatal pore, thereby controlling gas exchange.

### Q1254. The primary site for gaseous exchange in the submerged parts of aquatic plants is typically through their:
A.) Stomata
B.) Lenticels
C.) Roots
D.) General body surface **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Submerged aquatic plants often lack stomata or lenticels in their submerged parts, and gaseous exchange occurs directly through the diffusion across their general body surface.

### Q1255. Which process in plants releases oxygen as a by-product?
A.) Respiration
B.) Transpiration
C.) Absorption
D.) Photosynthesis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Photosynthesis utilizes carbon dioxide and water to produce glucose and oxygen. Oxygen is released into the atmosphere as a by-product of the light-dependent reactions.

### Q1256. Stomata are typically more numerous on the lower epidermis of a dicot leaf. This arrangement primarily helps to:
A.) Increase light absorption
B.) Enhance nutrient uptake
C.) Improve structural support
D.) Reduce water loss **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Placing more stomata on the lower surface, which is less exposed to direct sunlight and wind, helps to minimize water loss through transpiration while still allowing for sufficient gas exchange.

### Q1257. The opening of stomata is primarily regulated by the influx and efflux of which ion into and out of the guard cells?
A.) Sodium ions (Na+)
B.) Calcium ions (Ca2+)
C.) Chloride ions (Cl-)
D.) Potassium ions (K+) **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The active transport of potassium ions into guard cells increases their solute concentration, leading to water uptake by osmosis, increased turgor pressure, and subsequent stomatal opening.

### Q1258. During periods of water stress, plants typically:
A.) Keep stomata wide open to increase water uptake
B.) Increase the number of stomata on their leaves
C.) Promote gaseous exchange through lenticels
D.) Close stomata to conserve water **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Under water stress, plants close their stomata to reduce transpiration and prevent excessive water loss, even though this limits carbon dioxide uptake for photosynthesis.

### Q1259. Small pores on the woody stems and roots of plants that allow for some gaseous exchange are called:
A.) Stomata
B.) Hydathodes
C.) Cuticles
D.) Lenticels **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Lenticels are small, raised pores found on the bark of woody stems and some roots, facilitating gaseous exchange between the internal tissues and the atmosphere. Stomata are on leaves, hydathodes release water, and cuticles are a protective layer.

### Q1260. Which of the following conditions generally promotes the closing of stomata?
A.) High light intensity
B.) High humidity
C.) Low carbon dioxide concentration inside the leaf
D.) Water deficit **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When a plant experiences a water deficit (e.g., drought conditions), abscisic acid is produced, which signals the guard cells to lose turgor and close the stomata, conserving water.

### Q1261. What is the primary role of the intercellular air spaces within the mesophyll layer of a leaf?
A.) Photosynthesis
B.) Water storage
C.) Structural support
D.) Facilitating the diffusion of gases **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The extensive network of intercellular air spaces within the spongy mesophyll allows for efficient diffusion of carbon dioxide from the stomata to the photosynthetic cells and the diffusion of oxygen and water vapor out of the leaf.

### Q1262. The primary structures in roots responsible for the absorption of water and minerals are:
A.) Root caps
B.) Vascular bundles
C.) Lateral roots
D.) Root hairs **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Root hairs are slender extensions of epidermal cells that greatly increase the surface area of the root, making them highly efficient at absorbing water and dissolved minerals from the soil.

### Q1263. Water tends to move from an area of:
A.) Higher solute concentration to lower solute concentration
B.) Lower water potential to higher water potential
C.) Lower pressure to higher pressure
D.) Higher water potential to lower water potential **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water movement in plants, including absorption by roots, occurs down a water potential gradient, meaning from an area where water potential is higher (less negative) to an area where it is lower (more negative).

### Q1264. In the apoplast pathway, water moves primarily through the:
A.) Cytoplasm and plasmodesmata
B.) Vacuoles of root cells
C.) Cell membranes
D.) Cell walls and intercellular spaces **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The apoplast pathway involves water moving through the non-living parts of the root, specifically the cell walls and the intercellular spaces, without crossing any cell membranes until it reaches the endodermis.

### Q1265. The symplast pathway involves water movement through the:
A.) Cell walls only
B.) Intercellular spaces only
C.) Xylem vessels
D.) Cytoplasm connected by plasmodesmata **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In the symplast pathway, water moves from cell to cell by passing through the cytoplasm of cells, connected via microscopic channels called plasmodesmata.

### Q1266. Which part of the root acts as a selective barrier, forcing water and solutes to enter the symplast pathway before reaching the vascular cylinder?
A.) Epidermis
B.) Cortex
C.) Pericycle
D.) Endodermis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The endodermis, with its Casparian strips, forms a waterproof barrier that forces water and dissolved solutes to move from the apoplast into the symplast before they can enter the stele (vascular cylinder).

### Q1267. Casparian strips are composed mainly of:
A.) Cellulose and pectin
B.) Hemicellulose and chitin
C.) Proteins and lipids
D.) Lignin and suberin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Casparian strips are waxy bands within the cell walls of endodermal cells, impregnated with hydrophobic substances like lignin and suberin, which make them impermeable to water.

### Q1268. The movement of water directly across the cell membranes and cytoplasm of individual cells, not relying on plasmodesmata or cell walls, is known as the:
A.) Apoplast pathway
B.) Symplast pathway
C.) Mass flow pathway
D.) Transmembrane pathway **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The transmembrane pathway involves water repeatedly entering and exiting cells by crossing the plasma membranes, thereby moving through the cell cytoplasm and vacuoles.

### Q1269. A high concentration of solutes inside root hair cells compared to the soil water leads to water absorption primarily by:
A.) Active transport
B.) Facilitated diffusion
C.) Mass flow
D.) Osmosis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Root hair cells maintain a lower (more negative) water potential than the surrounding soil water due to a higher concentration of solutes. This water potential gradient drives the passive movement of water into the root hairs via osmosis.

### Q1270. Which factor contributes to the water potential of a plant cell?
A.) Only solute potential
B.) Only pressure potential
C.) Temperature and light intensity
D.) Both solute potential and pressure potential **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Water potential (Ψ) is a measure of the free energy of water. It is determined by the combined effects of solute potential (Ψs, due to dissolved solutes) and pressure potential (Ψp, due to turgor pressure).

### Q1271. The process by which mineral ions are taken up by root cells against their concentration gradient is:
A.) Diffusion
B.) Osmosis
C.) Facilitated diffusion
D.) Active transport **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Mineral ions are often present in lower concentrations in the soil than inside root cells. To absorb these ions, plants utilize active transport, which requires energy (ATP) to move ions against their concentration gradient.

### Q1272. The upward movement of water and dissolved minerals from the roots to the aerial parts of the plant is called:
A.) Translocation
B.) Guttation
C.) Imbibition
D.) Ascent of sap **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ascent of sap specifically refers to the transport of water and inorganic solutes upwards through the xylem from the roots to the leaves. Translocation is for organic solutes in phloem.

### Q1273. Which of the following contributes to root pressure?
A.) Transpiration from leaves
B.) Cohesion of water molecules
C.) Adhesion of water to xylem walls
D.) Active accumulation of solutes in the root xylem **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Root pressure is generated by the active transport of mineral ions into the xylem of roots, which lowers the water potential within the xylem, causing water to move in by osmosis and build up pressure.

### Q1274. The primary driving force for the ascent of sap in tall trees, according to the cohesion-tension theory, is:
A.) Root pressure
B.) Capillary action in xylem
C.) Guttation
D.) Transpiration pull **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cohesion-tension theory proposes that the evaporation of water from leaves (transpiration) creates a negative pressure or 'pull' that draws water upwards through the xylem vessels due to the cohesive properties of water molecules.

### Q1275. The property of water molecules to stick to each other through hydrogen bonds is called:
A.) Adhesion
B.) Imbibition
C.) Surface tension
D.) Cohesion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cohesion refers to the attractive forces between like molecules. In water, these are strong hydrogen bonds that allow water molecules to form a continuous column in the xylem.

### Q1276. The property of water molecules to stick to the inner surfaces of xylem vessels is called:
A.) Cohesion
B.) Osmosis
C.) Transpiration
D.) Adhesion **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Adhesion refers to the attractive forces between different types of molecules. Water molecules adhere to the hydrophilic (water-attracting) walls of the xylem vessels, helping to counteract the pull of gravity.

### Q1277. Which type of xylem cell is narrower and longer, with tapered ends and pits, contributing to water transport and support?
A.) Sieve tubes
B.) Companion cells
C.) Parenchyma cells
D.) Tracheids **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Tracheids are elongated, narrow, dead cells with tapered ends and pits in their walls, forming continuous pathways for water transport in xylem. Sieve tubes and companion cells are part of phloem.

### Q1278. Xylem vessels are characterized by:
A.) Living cells with cytoplasm
B.) Transporting sugars from leaves
C.) Lacking lignin in their walls
D.) Being continuous tubes formed from dead cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Xylem vessels are formed from a series of dead, hollow cells (vessel elements) arranged end-to-end, forming continuous tubes. Their walls are typically lignified, providing structural support.

### Q1279. The transpiration stream is driven by:
A.) Pressure exerted by root hairs
B.) The difference in water potential between the soil and the root
C.) Active pumping of water by xylem parenchyma
D.) The evaporation of water from the stomata **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The evaporation of water from the moist surfaces within the leaf (through stomata) creates a negative pressure (tension) that pulls the continuous column of water up through the xylem, establishing the transpiration stream.

### Q1280. The opening of stomata is primarily caused by an increase in:
A.) Turgor pressure in epidermal cells
B.) Water potential in surrounding air
C.) Solute concentration in subsidiary cells
D.) Turgor pressure in guard cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When guard cells absorb water, their turgor pressure increases, causing them to bow outwards and open the stomatal pore.

### Q1281. Which of the following ions actively accumulate in guard cells, leading to stomatal opening?
A.) Sodium ions (Na+)
B.) Chloride ions (Cl-)
C.) Calcium ions (Ca2+)
D.) Potassium ions (K+) **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The active influx of potassium ions into guard cells significantly lowers their water potential, drawing water into the cells by osmosis and increasing turgor, which leads to stomatal opening.

### Q1282. Light typically promotes stomatal opening because it triggers:
A.) An increase in CO2 concentration inside the leaf
B.) The breakdown of starch into sugar in guard cells
C.) The synthesis of abscisic acid
D.) A decrease in CO2 concentration inside the leaf **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Light drives photosynthesis, which consumes CO2. A decrease in internal CO2 concentration signals the stomata to open to allow more CO2 uptake. Light also directly promotes potassium ion uptake.

### Q1283. During stomatal closing, guard cells primarily:
A.) Actively pump water into subsidiary cells
B.) Synthesize more sugars
C.) Increase their solute potential
D.) Lose turgor due to water efflux **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Stomatal closing occurs when guard cells lose water, leading to a decrease in their turgor pressure. This happens as potassium ions move out of the guard cells, causing water to follow by osmosis.

### Q1284. Which hormone plays a significant role in promoting stomatal closure under water stress conditions?
A.) Auxin
B.) Gibberellin
C.) Cytokinin
D.) Abscisic Acid **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Abscisic Acid (ABA) is a stress hormone that is produced in response to water deficit. It signals guard cells to close stomata rapidly to conserve water.

### Q1285. The uneven thickening of the cell walls of guard cells contributes to:
A.) Their ability to store starch
B.) Their resistance to pathogen entry
C.) Their role in photosynthesis
D.) Their flexibility to change shape **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Guard cells have unevenly thickened walls, with the inner wall (facing the stoma) being thicker. This structural feature causes them to bow outwards when turgid, facilitating stomatal opening.

### Q1286. The primary sugar transported by the phloem is:
A.) Glucose
B.) Fructose
C.) Starch
D.) Sucrose **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sucrose is the most common form of sugar transported in the phloem because it is a non-reducing sugar, making it less reactive and more stable for long-distance transport.

### Q1287. Which of the following phloem cells are enucleated at maturity and primarily responsible for conducting sugars?
A.) Companion cells
B.) Phloem parenchyma
C.) Sclereids
D.) Sieve tube elements **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sieve tube elements are living cells that form the continuous tubes of the phloem, but they lose their nucleus at maturity, allowing for efficient sugar transport.

### Q1288. The 'source' in the pressure flow hypothesis refers to the plant part where:
A.) Water is absorbed from the soil
B.) Transpiration occurs at a high rate
C.) Minerals are assimilated
D.) Sugars are produced or stored in high concentration **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A source is typically a photosynthesizing leaf or a storage organ (like a root tuber) that is exporting sugars because it has an excess.

### Q1289. According to the pressure flow hypothesis, sugar is actively loaded into sieve tubes at the source, which leads to:
A.) Water diffusing out of the sieve tubes
B.) A decrease in pressure within the sieve tubes
C.) Sugar moving from sieve tubes to companion cells
D.) Water moving into the sieve tubes by osmosis **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Active loading of sucrose into the sieve tube elements at the source increases their solute concentration, lowering water potential and causing water to move in from the xylem by osmosis, thus building up turgor pressure.

### Q1290. Which cells are closely associated with sieve tube elements and provide metabolic support for loading and unloading sugars?
A.) Tracheids
B.) Vessel elements
C.) Epidermal cells
D.) Companion cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Companion cells are specialized parenchyma cells that are metabolically very active and are intimately associated with sieve tube elements, playing a crucial role in loading and unloading sugars into and out of the sieve tubes.

### Q1291. The 'sink' in the pressure flow hypothesis refers to a plant part where:
A.) Excess water is released
B.) Photosynthesis is highest
C.) Only minerals are transported
D.) Sugars are stored **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — A sink is a non-photosynthetic part of the plant or a storage organ (e.g., growing root, fruit, flower) that requires sugars and actively removes them from the phloem.

### Q1292. The process of water loss in the form of water vapor from the aerial parts of a plant, primarily through stomata, is called:
A.) Guttation
B.) Imbibition
C.) Respiration
D.) Transpiration **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Transpiration is the evaporative loss of water from plants, mainly from leaves through stomata, and it is a major process of water regulation.

### Q1293. The exudation of xylem sap, often seen as droplets on leaf margins in humid conditions, is known as:
A.) Transpiration
B.) Osmosis
C.) Plasmolysis
D.) Guttation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Guttation is the process where water is released as liquid droplets from the hydathodes (specialized pores) on leaf margins, typically occurring when transpiration rates are low and root pressure is high.

### Q1294. Which of the following is a primary way plants regulate their temperature?
A.) Increasing the rate of photosynthesis
B.) Decreasing mineral uptake
C.) Increasing root hair surface area
D.) Transpirational cooling **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Similar to sweating in animals, the evaporation of water during transpiration dissipates heat from the leaf surface, effectively cooling the plant.

### Q1295. Plants store many waste products, such as oxalic acid crystals, in which cellular component to prevent harm?
A.) Nucleus
B.) Mitochondria
C.) Chloroplasts
D.) Vacuoles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Plant cells often isolate and store various metabolic waste products, including toxic substances and crystals (like calcium oxalate), within their large central vacuoles, effectively compartmentalizing them away from active metabolism.

### Q1296. Which adaptation helps xerophytes (plants in dry environments) to reduce water loss?
A.) Numerous stomata on the upper epidermis
B.) Large, broad leaves
C.) Extensive shallow root systems
D.) Sunken stomata and thick cuticles **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sunken stomata create a humid microenvironment that reduces the water potential gradient between the leaf and the outside air, while thick cuticles provide a waxy, impermeable barrier, both significantly reducing water loss.

### Q1297. The closing of stomata on hot, dry days is an example of a plant's response to maintain:
A.) Optimal light absorption
B.) Carbon dioxide uptake
C.) Sugar production
D.) Water balance **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Stomatal closure in response to dry conditions is a critical homeostatic mechanism that allows the plant to conserve water, even at the cost of reduced photosynthesis.

### Q1298. In deciduous trees, the shedding of leaves in autumn can be considered a mechanism for:
A.) Increasing nutrient absorption
B.) Enhancing photosynthesis in winter
C.) Promoting new branch growth
D.) Excreting accumulated waste products **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Many plants accumulate metabolic waste products, sometimes in high concentrations, within their leaves. By shedding leaves seasonally, deciduous trees effectively remove these accumulated wastes from their system.

### Q1299. In herbaceous plants, the primary means of providing mechanical support is:
A.) Extensive lignified xylem
B.) Thickened sclerenchyma fibers
C.) Presence of cork cambium
D.) Turgor pressure within cells **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Turgor pressure, the pressure exerted by the cell contents against the cell wall, keeps herbaceous plant cells firm and provides rigidity, preventing wilting.

### Q1300. The rigid outer layer of plant cells that provides structural support and protection is the:
A.) Cell membrane
B.) Cytoplasm
C.) Vacuolar membrane
D.) Cell wall **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The plant cell wall, primarily composed of cellulose, is a strong, rigid layer outside the cell membrane that provides structural support, maintains cell shape, and protects the cell.

### Q1301. Which plant tissue, characterized by highly lignified cell walls, provides significant mechanical strength and support to the plant body?
A.) Parenchyma
B.) Collenchyma
C.) Epidermis
D.) Sclerenchyma **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Sclerenchyma tissue is composed of cells with thick, lignified secondary walls that provide strong structural support, particularly in mature plant parts.

### Q1302. The dead, hollow cells with lignified walls that contribute to both water transport and mechanical support in plants are primarily found in:
A.) Phloem
B.) Cortex
C.) Pith
D.) Xylem **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Xylem tissue contains vessel elements and tracheids, which are dead at maturity, hollow, and have lignified walls. These features enable them to transport water efficiently and provide substantial structural support to the plant.

### Q1303. Loss of turgor pressure in a plant cell will most directly lead to:
A.) Increased rate of photosynthesis
B.) Enhanced nutrient uptake
C.) Accelerated cell division
D.) Wilting of leaves and stems **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When plant cells lose water, their turgor pressure drops, and they become flaccid. This reduction in rigidity causes the non-woody parts of the plant, like leaves and herbaceous stems, to droop or wilt.

### Q1304. The primary meristematic tissue responsible for increasing the length of a plant's roots and shoots is the:
A.) Lateral meristem
B.) Vascular cambium
C.) Cork cambium
D.) Apical meristem **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Apical meristems are located at the tips of roots and shoots and are responsible for primary growth, which is the increase in length of the plant body.

### Q1305. Which type of growth results in an increase in the girth or thickness of a plant stem or root?
A.) Primary growth
B.) Apical growth
C.) Longitudinal growth
D.) Secondary growth **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Secondary growth, characteristic of woody plants, leads to an increase in diameter and is facilitated by lateral meristems such as the vascular cambium and cork cambium.

### Q1306. The vascular cambium is a type of lateral meristem responsible for producing:
A.) Epidermis and cortex
B.) Root hairs and root cap
C.) Leaves and flowers
D.) Xylem and phloem **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vascular cambium is a cylindrical meristem that produces secondary xylem (wood) to the inside and secondary phloem to the outside, contributing to the thickening of stems and roots.

### Q1307. Which tissue system is produced by the cork cambium during secondary growth?
A.) Vascular tissue system
B.) Ground tissue system
C.) Meristematic tissue system
D.) Dermal tissue system **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The cork cambium (phellogen) is a lateral meristem that produces the periderm, which replaces the epidermis as the protective outer layer in plants undergoing secondary growth. The periderm includes cork (phellem) and phelloderm.

### Q1308. Primary growth in plants occurs at:
A.) Only the stem tip
B.) Only the root tip
C.) Along the entire length of the stem
D.) Both root and stem tips **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Primary growth, which involves elongation, occurs at the apical meristems found at both the tips of the roots and the tips of the shoots (stems).

### Q1309. Growth rings in tree stems are formed due to the seasonal activity of the:
A.) Apical meristem
B.) Cork cambium
C.) Intercalary meristem
D.) Vascular cambium **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vascular cambium's activity fluctuates with seasons, producing larger, thin-walled xylem cells in spring wood and smaller, thick-walled xylem cells in summer wood, leading to the formation of visible annual growth rings.

### Q1310. The irreversible increase in the size of a plant organ or the entire plant is defined as:
A.) Differentiation
B.) Development
C.) Morphogenesis
D.) Growth **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Growth in plants refers to an irreversible increase in size, typically involving an increase in cell number, cell size, and accumulation of dry mass.

### Q1311. The process by which cells become specialized in structure and function is called:
A.) Growth
B.) Morphogenesis
C.) Reproduction
D.) Differentiation **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Differentiation is the process where unspecialized cells (e.g., from meristems) develop into specialized cell types with distinct structures and functions (e.g., xylem, phloem, epidermal cells).

### Q1312. The sum of all changes that an organism undergoes from its origin to its maturity, including growth and differentiation, is termed:
A.) Morphogenesis
B.) Senescence
C.) Vernalization
D.) Development **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Development in plants encompasses all the progressive and irreversible changes that occur during its life cycle, from germination through maturity and reproduction, including growth, differentiation, and various responses to environmental cues.

### Q1313. Which of the following is an example of a developmental process in plants, rather than solely growth?
A.) Increase in root length
B.) Widening of a tree trunk
C.) Increase in leaf surface area
D.) Formation of a mature flower from a bud **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — While growth is involved, the transformation of a vegetative bud into a reproductive structure like a flower involves complex changes in gene expression and cell differentiation, which falls under the broader concept of development. The other options are primarily examples of growth.

### Q1314. Which plant hormone is primarily responsible for promoting cell elongation in shoots and plays a key role in phototropism and gravitropism?
A.) Gibberellin
B.) Cytokinin
C.) Ethylene
D.) Auxin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Auxins are a class of plant hormones that promote cell elongation, particularly in shoots, and are crucial for various growth responses like phototropism (growth towards light) and gravitropism (growth in response to gravity).

### Q1315. Which plant hormone is known for breaking seed dormancy and promoting stem elongation and fruit development?
A.) Abscisic Acid
B.) Ethylene
C.) Auxin
D.) Gibberellin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Gibberellins are plant hormones that play significant roles in breaking dormancy in seeds and buds, promoting stem elongation, and influencing fruit development and flowering.

### Q1316. Which plant hormone primarily promotes cell division and differentiation, often found in actively growing tissues like roots and embryos?
A.) Ethylene
B.) Auxin
C.) Abscisic Acid (ABA)
D.) Cytokinin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Cytokinins are a class of plant hormones that stimulate cell division (cytokinesis) and differentiation, working in conjunction with auxins to regulate various aspects of plant growth and development.

### Q1317. The plant hormone often associated with stress responses, dormancy, and stomatal closure is:
A.) Gibberellin
B.) Cytokinin
C.) Ethylene
D.) Abscisic Acid **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Abscisic Acid (ABA) is a plant hormone that primarily acts as a growth inhibitor, playing crucial roles in initiating and maintaining dormancy in seeds and buds, and promoting stomatal closure under water stress.

### Q1318. Which gaseous plant hormone promotes fruit ripening and senescence in plants?
A.) Auxin
B.) Gibberellin
C.) Cytokinin
D.) Ethylene **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ethylene is a unique plant hormone that exists as a gas. It is well-known for its role in promoting fruit ripening, leaf abscission (shedding), and senescence (aging).

### Q1319. The apical dominance, where the growth of lateral buds is inhibited by the apical bud, is primarily maintained by which hormone produced in the shoot tip?
A.) Cytokinin
B.) Gibberellin
C.) Ethylene
D.) Auxin **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Auxin produced by the apical meristem in the shoot tip is responsible for maintaining apical dominance, suppressing the growth of lateral (axillary) buds.

### Q1320. The growth response of a plant part towards or away from a light source is called:
A.) Geotropism
B.) Hydrotropism
C.) Thigmotropism
D.) Phototropism **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Phototropism is the directional growth of a plant organ in response to a light stimulus, typically seen as shoots growing towards light and roots growing away from it.

### Q1321. The positive gravitropic response of roots ensures that they grow:
A.) Towards light
B.) Away from water
C.) Along the soil surface
D.) Downwards into the soil **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Gravitropism (also called geotropism) is growth in response to gravity. Roots exhibit positive gravitropism, meaning they grow downwards, anchoring the plant and seeking water and nutrients.

### Q1322. A plant that flowers only when the day length is shorter than a critical period is known as a:
A.) Long-day plant
B.) Day-neutral plant
C.) Intermediate-day plant
D.) Short-day plant **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Short-day plants (SDP) initiate flowering when the night period is longer than a critical length, meaning the day length is shorter than a critical period.

### Q1323. The requirement of a cold period to stimulate flowering in some plants is known as:
A.) Photoperiodism
B.) Thigmotropism
C.) Stratification
D.) Vernalization **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Vernalization is the process by which a plant's flowering is promoted or accelerated by exposure to a period of low temperatures (cold treatment), often required for temperate zone plants.

## Hemostasis

### Q1324. A kangaroo rat is an example of an animal exhibiting anhydrobiosis. What is the primary source of the water it uses for survival?
A.) Absorbing moisture from the air at night
B.) Drinking large amounts of water when available
C.) Storing water in specialized sacs in the body
D.) Water produced as a by-product of metabolism **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Kangaroo rats derive about 90% of their water from the cellular oxidation of carbohydrates found in the seeds they eat. This characteristic is called anhydrobiosis.

### Q1325. Which of the following nitrogenous wastes requires the most water for safe excretion and is therefore common in aquatic animals?
A.) Uric Acid
B.) Urea
C.) Creatinine
D.) Ammonia **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Ammonia is highly toxic and requires about 500 ml of water per gram of nitrogen to be diluted to a non-toxic level, making it suitable only for animals with constant access to water.

### Q1326. The blood vessel that carries filtered blood away from the glomerulus is the:
A.) Afferent arteriole
B.) Renal vein
C.) Peritubular capillary
D.) Efferent arteriole **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The efferent arteriole arises from the glomeruli and carries blood away from it after filtration has occurred.

### Q1327. What causes the high filtration pressure in the glomerulus?
A.) Direct connection of the renal artery to the aorta.
B.) Active pumping by the Bowman's capsule.
C.) High solute concentration in the blood plasma.
D.) A narrower efferent arteriole than the afferent arteriole **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The diameter of the efferent arteriole is about half that of the afferent arteriole, creating a bottleneck that significantly increases blood pressure within the glomerulus, which drives filtration.

### Q1328. A patient is diagnosed with pyelonephritis. Which part of the urinary tract is infected?
A.) Urethra
B.) Urinary bladder
C.) Ureters
D.) Kidneys **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Pyelonephritis is the medical term for an infection of the kidneys. An infection of the urethra is urethritis, and an infection of the bladder is cystitis.

### Q1329. The countercurrent multiplier mechanism primarily serves to:
A.) Filter large proteins from blood.
B.) Secrete drugs and toxins.
C.) Reabsorb glucose and amino acids.
D.) Create a gradient for water reabsorption. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The countercurrent multiplier mechanism, involving the loop of Henle and vasa recta, achieves the reabsorption of about 99.5% of water from the filtrate by creating a concentration gradient.

### Q1330. A patient with chronic kidney failure undergoes haemodialysis. The dialysate fluid used in the dialyzer should contain:
A.) A high concentration of urea and waste products.
B.) No dissolved substances at all (pure distilled water).
C.) The patient's own filtered blood plasma.
D.) Normal plasma concentrations of essential substances like glucose and salts, but no urea. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The dialysate absorbs waste by-products from the blood by creating a concentration gradient. To prevent the loss of essential substances, the fluid must have normal concentrations of minerals and electrolytes, but it contains no waste products.

### Q1331. Which of the following is considered a physiological response to hyperthermia (high body temperature)?
A.) Vasoconstriction
B.) Shivering
C.) Piloerection
D.) Sweating **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — When body temperature rises above 37°C (hyperthermia), one of the main physiological responses is sweating. Evaporation of the sweat produces a cooling effect.

### Q1332. A person has a kidney transplant. To prevent the rejection of the new kidney, they are prescribed medications like cyclosporine. What is the primary function of these medications?
A.) To increase the rate of urine production by the new kidney.
B.) To lower the patient's blood pressure to protect the new organ.
C.) To destroy any bacteria that could infect the new kidney.
D.) To suppress the recipient's immune system. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Patients are required to take medications such as cyclosporine to suppress their immune system in order to prevent rejection of the transplanted kidney.

### Q1333. The vasa recta are specialized capillaries associated with which part of the nephron?
A.) Glomerulus
B.) Proximal and distal convoluted tubules
C.) Bowman's capsule
D.) Loop of Henle **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — The vasa recta are a specialized part of the peritubular capillaries that course into the medulla along with the loops of Henle.

### Q1334. The ESWL procedure treats kidney stones by:
A.) Surgically removing the stone.
B.) Vaporizing the stone with a laser.
C.) Dissolving the stone with medication.
D.) Using external shock waves. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — In ESWL, an instrument called a lithotripter generates shock waves from outside the patient's body, which are focused on the stone to break it into small pieces.

### Q1335. A person is dehydrated. Which hormonal changes would you expect to see?
A.) Decreased ADH and decreased aldosterone
B.) Increased ADH and decreased aldosterone
C.) Decreased ADH and increased aldosterone
D.) Increased ADH and increased aldosterone **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — During dehydration, anti-diuretic hormone (ADH) promotes water reabsorption from the descending limb and collecting duct, while aldosterone promotes the transport of sodium chloride out of the ascending limb, which also aids water retention.

### Q1336. Tubular secretion is a vital process for:
A.) Recovering essential nutrients like glucose from the filtrate.
B.) Creating the high pressure needed for filtration.
C.) Reabsorbing water under the influence of ADH.
D.) Removing waste products and excess ions from the blood into the filtrate. **(answer)**
**Answer: D** — Tubular secretion is the process by which certain substances, like ammonium and hydrogen ions, are secreted by tubular epithelial cells into the filtrate to maintain the pH of urine.
